\chapter{DG and \texorpdfstring{$A_\infty$}{A-infty} foundations: making ``derived time'' honest} · lines 1309–1479
1309: \chapter{DG and \texorpdfstring{$A_\infty$}{A-infty} foundations: making ``derived time'' honest}
1310: 
1311: \label{ch:dg}
1312: 
1313: 
1314: The  main body introduces DG-algebras to motivate multiparameter persistence and derived invariants ( Chapter~4).
1315: Here we expand the DG/$A_\infty$ layer in the direction needed for two goals:
1316: \begin{enumerate}
1317: \item a mathematically sound interface between surface orders and persistence computations, and
1318: \item an operator-theoretic interface between ``clock shifts'' and Hamiltonians (Hilbert--Pólya program later).
1319: \end{enumerate}
1320: 
1321: \subsection{DG-algebras: conventions and minimal requirements}
1322: \label{sec:dg_conventions}
1323: 
1324: A differential graded (DG) algebra over a commutative ring $k$ is a graded $k$-algebra
1325: $A=\bigoplus_{n\in\Z} A^n$ equipped with a degree $+1$ differential $d:A^n\to A^{n+1}$ such that
1326: \[
1327: d^2=0,\qquad d(ab)=(da)b+(-1)^{|a|}a(db).
1328: \]
1329: A DG-module over $A$ is defined similarly; the derived category $D(A)$ is obtained by inverting quasi-isomorphisms.
1330: 
1331: \begin{example}[A minimal example]
1332: The exterior algebra $A=\Lambda^\bullet(V)$ with $d=0$ is a DG-algebra concentrated in non-positive degrees.  A less degenerate example: let $A=k[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^2)$ with $|\varepsilon|=-1$ and $d\varepsilon=1$; then $H^\bullet(A)\cong k$, so $A$ is a ``resolution'' of the ground field.  In our setting, $A$ will be a DG enhancement of a surface algebra, and the differential encodes higher-order relations among paths.
1333: \end{example}
1334: 
1335: \begin{remark}[What we actually need]
1336: We do \emph{not} need the full technology of model categories for most of this volume.
1337: We need: (i) mapping cones, (ii) derived functors (Tor/Ext), (iii) the fact that quasi-isomorphic DG-algebras have equivalent derived categories, and (iv) a controlled way to talk about ``families'' of DG-modules indexed by parameters.
1338: \end{remark}
1339: 
1340: \subsection{From surface algebras/orders to DG enhancements}
1341: \label{sec:dg_from_surface}
1342: 
1343: A surface algebra in the sense of the source text is a quiver algebra $kQ/I$ with gentle-style relations.
1344: There are multiple DG enhancements one can attach:
1345: 
1346: \begin{enumerate}
1347: \item \textbf{Bar/cobar constructions:} produce a DG coalgebra and then a DG algebra controlling $A_\infty$ structures.
1348: \item \textbf{Ginzburg DG algebras:} if a quiver with potential $(Q,W)$ exists, one can form a 3-Calabi--Yau DG algebra $\Gamma(Q,W)$.
1349: \item \textbf{Geometric enhancements:} for many gentle/surface algebras, $D^b(\mathrm{mod}\text{-}A)$ is equivalent to a (partially wrapped) Fukaya category of a marked surface; the $A_\infty$ structure is then geometric.
1350: \end{enumerate}
1351: 
1352: We remain agnostic and formulate an \emph{axiomatic} requirement.
1353: 
1354: \begin{definition}[Clock-compatible DG enhancement]
1355: \label{def:clock_compatible_dg}
1356: Let $\Lambda$ be a (completed) surface order with local clock operators.
1357: A \emph{clock-compatible DG enhancement} of $\Lambda$ is a DG algebra $A$ together with:
1358: \begin{itemize}
1359: \item a quasi-isomorphism $H^0(A)\simeq \Lambda$,
1360: \item a distinguished degree-$0$ element (or automorphism) $T\in Z^0(A)$ representing the clock shift in cohomology,
1361: \item functoriality with respect to gluing: gluing of orders induces DG-algebra maps up to homotopy.
1362: \end{itemize}
1363: \end{definition}
1364: 
1365: The point of this definition is not to impose an arbitrary DG gadget; it is to make sure that ``clock data'' survives passage to derived categories and hence can interact with persistence constructions.
1366: 
1367: \subsection{The minimal $A_\infty$ model viewpoint}
1368: \label{sec:Ainfty}
1369: 
1370: Even when $A$ is concentrated in degree $0$ (an ordinary algebra), the derived category remembers higher multiplications via $A_\infty$ minimal models.
1371: The cleanest formulation for computations is:
1372: 
1373: \begin{theorem}[Kadeishvili (minimal model)]
1374: Let $A$ be a DG algebra over a field.
1375: Then its cohomology $H^*(A)$ admits an $A_\infty$ algebra structure $(m_2,m_3,\dots)$ such that:
1376: \begin{itemize}
1377: \item $m_2$ is the induced product on cohomology,
1378: \item there exists an $A_\infty$ quasi-isomorphism $H^*(A)\to A$,
1379: \item the $A_\infty$ structure is unique up to $A_\infty$ quasi-isomorphism.
1380: \end{itemize}
1381: \end{theorem}
1382: 
1383: \begin{remark}[Why $m_3$ matters for time/geometry]
1384: The first genuinely new datum beyond associative multiplication is $m_3$.
1385: Physically, $m_3$ is a measure of nontrivial associator/phase information in composing local processes.
1386: Mathematically, $m_3$ detects Massey products and obstructions to formality.
1387: In emergent-geometry language, it is the first place where ``curvature'' can appear in a purely algebraic composition law.
1388: \end{remark}
1389: 
1390: \subsection{Realistic example: the double $3$--cycle algebra and its Ext-$A_\infty$}
1391: \label{sec:double3_example}
1392: 
1393: Let $A$ be the path algebra of the double $3$--cycle quiver from Section~\ref{sec:corrigendum_sphere}, modulo gentle relations \eqref{eq:sphere_gentle_rel}--\eqref{eq:sphere_switch_rel}.
1394: Even in this toy case, one can see the distinction between:
1395: \begin{itemize}
1396: \item the \emph{presentation} by generators/relations, and
1397: \item the \emph{derived} composition data encoded by higher operations in $\mathrm{Ext}^*_A(S,S)$ for simples $S$.
1398: \end{itemize}
1399: 
1400: \begin{proposition}[Ext algebra skeleton (informal)]
1401: For a simple $A$-module $S$ supported at a medial vertex, $\mathrm{Ext}^1_A(S,S)$ is spanned by (classes of) the two local directions (clockwise/counterclockwise arrows), while $\mathrm{Ext}^2_A(S,S)$ detects the first forbidden length-$2$ turns.
1402: Higher $\mathrm{Ext}$ groups are controlled by periodicity of walks on the quiver.
1403: \end{proposition}
1404: 
1405: \begin{remark}
1406: This is the derived-category version of the combinatorial statement ``there are two arrows in and two arrows out'' in a surface algebra (see the source text in Appendix~A).
1407: \end{remark}
1408: 
1409: A concrete computational pipeline (Macaulay2 or Sage) is provided in Section~\ref{ch:persistence} for related persistence modules.
1410: For DG/$A_\infty$ computations, one typically proceeds by choosing a projective resolution $P^\bullet\to S$ and forming the DG endomorphism algebra $\End(P^\bullet)$; its cohomology carries the desired $A_\infty$ structure.
1411: 
1412: \subsection{Derived invariants that behave like conserved quantities}
1413: \label{sec:derived_invariants}
1414: 
1415: A central promise in the  open-problems list is to compute derived invariants (Hochschild (co)homology, Calabi--Yau structures) of clocked surface DG-algebras and interpret them physically ( \S13.2.1).
1416: 
1417: We record the minimal dictionary.
1418: 
1419: \begin{definition}[Hochschild homology and cohomology]
1420: For a DG algebra $A$, Hochschild homology is
1421: \[
1422: HH_*(A)=\mathrm{Tor}^{A^e}_*(A,A),\qquad A^e=A\otimes_k A^{op},
1423: \]
1424: and Hochschild cohomology is
1425: \[
1426: HH^*(A)=\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{A^e}(A,A).
1427: \]
1428: \end{definition}
1429: 
1430: \begin{remark}[Physical reading]
1431: In many QFT and string-theoretic contexts, Hochschild cohomology controls deformations of the algebra of observables, while Hochschild homology controls ``closed-string'' or ``trace'' sectors.
1432: Within the present program, the robust claim is:
1433: \begin{quote}
1434: \emph{Clock holonomy constraints are deformation constraints, hence should appear as classes in $HH^*(A)$ or as obstructions detected by them.}
1435: \end{quote}
1436: \end{remark}
1437: 
1438: \subsection{Hamiltonians from shifts: the logarithm trick}
1439: \label{sec:hamiltonian_log}
1440: 
1441: The  main body already sketches how a shift operator can be viewed as a discrete-time evolution $T=e^{-iH\Delta t/\hbar}$ ( \S6.6; see also the discussion around diagonalizing $T$ on a finite $d$--qudit).
1442: 
1443: The mathematically correct statement is:
1444: 
1445: \begin{proposition}[From a unitary shift to a self-adjoint generator]
1446: Let $T$ be a unitary on a Hilbert space $\cH$.
1447: Then by functional calculus there exists a self-adjoint operator $H$ (not unique) such that
1448: \[
1449: T = e^{-iH}.
1450: \]
1451: If we specify a time step $\Delta t$ and Planck's constant $\hbar$, we may write $T=e^{-iH\Delta t/\hbar}$ after rescaling.
1452: \end{proposition}
1453: 
1454: \begin{remark}[Nonuniqueness and branch choices]
1455: The logarithm of a unitary is multi-valued; choosing $H$ requires choosing a branch cut for the eigenphases.
1456: In the Hilbert--Pólya program we exploit this nonuniqueness as a feature: branch choices correspond to arithmetic/holonomy constraints that must be fixed by additional structure (trace formulas, positivity, or spectral symmetries).
1457: \end{remark}
1458: 
1459: For nonunitary truncations (nilpotent/unipotent), the generator is not self-adjoint and must be implemented on hardware by dilation ( \S12.3).
1460: We expand the engineering consequences in Section~\ref{ch:expts}.
1461: 
1462: \subsection{DG flows and multiparameter filtrations}
1463: \label{sec:dg_filtrations}
1464: 
1465: To connect DG algebra to multiparameter persistence we need a mechanism that produces \emph{families} of complexes.
1466: The clean algebraic model is:
1467: 
1468: \begin{definition}[Multiparameter filtered DG module]
1469: Let $(\P,\le)$ be a poset (e.g.\ $\N^n$).
1470: A \emph{$\P$--filtered DG $A$-module} is a family $\{M_p\}_{p\in\P}$ of DG modules together with structure maps $M_p\to M_q$ for $p\le q$ such that:
1471: \begin{enumerate}
1472: \item each map is a DG-module map,
1473: \item compositions are strictly associative (or associative up to specified homotopies in a higher-categorical refinement).
1474: \end{enumerate}
1475: \end{definition}
1476: 
1477: In practice, $\P=\N^2$ is the first nontrivial case: one parameter is a geometric/scale parameter, the other is an information/entanglement threshold.
1478: Section~\ref{ch:persistence} turns this into explicit computations.
1479:
\chapter{Explicit multiparameter persistence computations tied to surface orders} · lines 1480–1742
1480: \chapter{Explicit multiparameter persistence computations tied to surface orders}
1481: 
1482: \label{ch:persistence}
1483: 
1484: 
1485: This chapter is the computational core of the extended edition.
1486: It expands the  bridge chapter ( Chapter~4) by giving:
1487: \begin{enumerate}
1488: \item an explicit \emph{bifiltration} naturally attached to clocked surface orders and entanglement data,
1489: \item a concrete translation to bigraded modules over polynomial rings (the commutative-algebra model),
1490: \item worked toy computations (including Macaulay2 templates),
1491: \item and a pipeline intended to be implementable on realistic quantum hardware outputs.
1492: \end{enumerate}
1493: 
1494: \subsection{From a surface order to a bifiltration}
1495: \label{sec:bifiltration_from_order}
1496: 
1497: The core idea is to convert entanglement data on the surface into a two-parameter family of simplicial complexes, where one parameter controls \emph{spatial scale} (how far we look on the graph) and the other controls \emph{correlation strength} (how entangled a pair must be to count as connected).  This is the minimal construction that keeps geometric and information-theoretic data logically separated, and it is what makes multiparameter persistence the natural tool rather than a decorative upgrade.
1498: 
1499: Let $\Gamma\subset \Sigma$ be an embedded graph underlying a surface algebra/order, and attach to each vertex $v$ a local Hilbert space $\cH_v$ and local clock operator $S_v$ (possibly twisted, Section~\ref{sec:twisted_shift}).
1500: Fix a global state $|\psi\rangle\in \bigotimes_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\cH_v$.
1501: 
1502: We construct a \emph{two-parameter filtration} indexed by
1503: \[
1504: (r,\lambda)\in \RR_{\ge 0}\times \RR_{\ge 0},
1505: \]
1506: where:
1507: \begin{itemize}
1508: \item $r$ is a \emph{geometric/graph} scale (how far we look on $\Gamma$),
1509: \item $\lambda$ is an \emph{information threshold} (how strong correlations must be to count as ``present'').
1510: \end{itemize}
1511: 
1512: \subsubsection{Raw data: a weighted correlation graph}
1513: 
1514: From $|\psi\rangle$ define a symmetric weight matrix $W_\psi(u,v)$.
1515: A standard choice is mutual information
1516: \[
1517: W_\psi(u,v)=I(u\!:\!v)=S(u)+S(v)-S(uv),
1518: \]
1519: or a Rényi proxy $I_2$ when only randomized measurements are available ( \S12.6).
1520: 
1521: \begin{definition}[Thresholded correlation graph]
1522: For $\lambda\ge 0$, define the thresholded graph $\Gamma_\lambda$ on the same vertex set by including an edge $(u,v)$ if $W_\psi(u,v)\ge \lambda$.
1523: \end{definition}
1524: 
1525: \subsubsection{Geometric thickening: Vietoris--Rips on a graph metric}
1526: 
1527: Let $d_{\mathrm{comb}}$ be the shortest-path metric on the $1$--skeleton of $\Gamma$ (unit edge lengths, or weighted by geometric data when available).
1528: For $r\ge 0$, define the $r$--ball neighborhoods and construct a simplicial complex.
1529: 
1530: \begin{definition}[Bifiltered complex]
1531: Let $K_{r,\lambda}$ be the Vietoris--Rips complex on the vertex set $V(\Gamma)$, using the metric $d_{\mathrm{comb}}$, but with the additional constraint that simplices are only allowed if all their edges lie in $\Gamma_\lambda$.
1532: Equivalently, $K_{r,\lambda}$ is generated by cliques in $\Gamma_\lambda$ of diameter $\le r$.
1533: \end{definition}
1534: 
1535: Then $(r,\lambda)\le (r',\lambda')$ (meaning $r\le r'$ and $\lambda\ge \lambda'$ if we view $\lambda$ as a threshold) induces an inclusion
1536: \[
1537: K_{r,\lambda}\hookrightarrow K_{r',\lambda'}.
1538: \]
1539: 
1540: \begin{remark}[Monotonicity conventions]
1541: There are two common conventions:
1542: \begin{itemize}
1543: \item treat both parameters as increasing (larger means ``more included''),
1544: \item or treat one as a decreasing threshold.
1545: We will always explicitly state the poset used; the algebraic model uses $\N^2$ with coordinatewise order.
1546: \end{itemize}
1547: \end{remark}
1548: 
1549: \subsection{Persistence modules as bigraded modules over $k[x,y]$}
1550: \label{sec:modules_over_poly}
1551: 
1552: Fix a homology degree $k$.
1553: Define
1554: \[
1555: M_{r,\lambda} := H_k(K_{r,\lambda};k),
1556: \]
1557: with structure maps induced by inclusions.
1558: 
1559: Discretize parameters to a grid $(r_i,\lambda_j)$ and index by $\N^2$:
1560: \[
1561: M_{(i,j)} := M_{r_i,\lambda_j}.
1562: \]
1563: Then $M$ becomes an $\N^2$-graded persistence module.
1564: As recalled in the source text (Appendix~B in the  volume), such modules can be modeled as modules over a polynomial ring.
1565: 
1566: \begin{definition}[Bigraded module model]
1567: Let $R=k[x,y]$ with $\deg x=(1,0)$ and $\deg y=(0,1)$.
1568: An $\N^2$-graded persistence module with structure maps corresponding to increments in $i$ and $j$ is equivalent (under mild finiteness hypotheses) to a finitely generated $\N^2$-graded $R$-module.
1569: \end{definition}
1570: 
1571: The equivalence is realized by letting $x$ act by the map $M_{(i,j)}\to M_{(i+1,j)}$ and $y$ act by $M_{(i,j)}\to M_{(i,j+1)}$.
1572: This is the commutative-algebra ``engine'' that powers generic free resolutions, determinantal varieties, and stability criteria (Appendix~B).
1573: 
1574: \subsection{What to compute: rank invariants, Betti tables, and jump loci}
1575: \label{sec:invariants}
1576: 
1577: For one-parameter persistence, barcodes are complete invariants over a field.
1578: For two parameters, no such complete discrete invariant exists.
1579: However, several computable invariants are both meaningful and stable.
1580: 
1581: \subsubsection{Rank invariant}
1582: For $p\le q$ in the poset, let $\varphi_{p,q}:M_p\to M_q$ be the structure map.
1583: 
1584: \begin{definition}[Rank invariant]
1585: The rank invariant is
1586: \[
1587: \rho(p,q) := \rank(\varphi_{p,q}).
1588: \]
1589: \end{definition}
1590: 
1591: Geometrically, fixing $p,q$ and bounding the rank corresponds to determinantal varieties (Appendix~B); computationally, $\rho$ is often the first robust summary one can estimate from noisy data.
1592: 
1593: \subsubsection{Free resolutions and Betti numbers}
1594: A finitely generated bigraded $R$-module has a minimal free resolution
1595: \[
1596: \cdots \to \bigoplus R(-\alpha)^{\beta_{2,\alpha}}
1597: \to \bigoplus R(-\alpha)^{\beta_{1,\alpha}}
1598: \to \bigoplus R(-\alpha)^{\beta_{0,\alpha}}
1599: \to M\to 0,
1600: \]
1601: where $\alpha\in\N^2$ and $\beta_{i,\alpha}$ are bigraded Betti numbers.
1602: 
1603: \begin{remark}[Why Betti tables matter here]
1604: In this program, the Betti data is not just ``commutative algebra for its own sake'':
1605: Betti numbers detect \emph{how many independent topological features} are created and destroyed in each parameter direction, and the degrees tell you \emph{where} in the $(r,\lambda)$ plane those events concentrate.
1606: \end{remark}
1607: 
1608: \subsection{Toy computation 1: a four-vertex surface-order patch}
1609: \label{sec:toy1}
1610: 
1611: Consider a small patch of a triangulated surface consisting of four vertices forming two triangles sharing an edge.
1612: Let the vertices be $1,2,3,4$ with edges $(1,2),(2,3),(1,3)$ and $(2,3),(2,4),(3,4)$.
1613: 
1614: Assume we have measured (or simulated) correlation weights $W_\psi(u,v)$ on these edges.
1615: We pick two thresholds $\lambda_0>\lambda_1$ and two radii $r_0<r_1$ such that:
1616: \begin{itemize}
1617: \item at $(r_0,\lambda_0)$ we have only the single shared edge $(2,3)$;
1618: \item at $(r_0,\lambda_1)$ we have a triangle $(1,2,3)$;
1619: \item at $(r_1,\lambda_1)$ we have both triangles, hence a contractible complex.
1620: \end{itemize}
1621: 
1622: Then $H_1(K_{r,\lambda})$ is nontrivial only at intermediate parameters: the triangle at $(r_0,\lambda_1)$ creates a 1-cycle in the $1$--skeleton that is immediately filled by the 2-simplex, so the persistent $H_1$ is trivial; but $H_0$ has a nontrivial merging event.
1623: 
1624: This toy is boring topologically, but it is useful algebraically because it yields a small presentation matrix.
1625: 
1626: \subsubsection{Module presentation}
1627: Let $M$ be the $H_0$ persistence module.
1628: On a $2\times 2$ grid, $M$ is presented by generators corresponding to connected components and relations corresponding to mergers.
1629: %A concrete Macaulay2 template is:
1630: %
1631: %\begin{lstlisting}[language={},caption={Macaulay2 template: build a small bigraded persistence module}]
1632: %-- Base ring with bigrading
1633: %k = QQ
1634: %R = k[x,y, Degrees=>{{1,0},{0,1}}]
1635: %
1636: %-- Free module with generators in chosen degrees
1637: %F0 = R^{1}  -- adjust rank to number of generators
1638: %
1639: %-- Relations matrix d1: F1 -> F0
1640: %F1 = R^{1}
1641: %d1 = matrix{{x, y}}  -- replace with correct bigraded relation matrix
1642: %
1643: %M = coker d1
1644: %betti res M
1645: %\end{lstlisting}
1646: 
1647: The point is not that this exact matrix encodes the geometric toy perfectly; the point is that \emph{the entire pipeline} from filtration to module to resolution can be scripted in Macaulay2. 
1648: 
1649: \subsection{Toy computation 2: persistence from clock holonomy constraints}
1650: \label{sec:toy2}
1651: 
1652: Now we build a toy where the algebraic ``clock constraint'' is genuinely present.
1653: 
1654: Let $\Gamma$ be a cycle graph of length $m$ (a single face boundary).
1655: Attach to each vertex a clock generator $S_v$, and impose a holonomy constraint that the product around the cycle equals a central element:
1656: \begin{equation}
1657: \label{eq:holonomy_constraint}
1658: S_{v_1}S_{v_2}\cdots S_{v_m} = \pi^k I.
1659: \end{equation}
1660: 
1661: Interpret \eqref{eq:holonomy_constraint} as a \emph{family} of constraints indexed by two parameters:
1662: \begin{itemize}
1663: \item a correlation threshold $\lambda$ controlling which edges of the cycle are ``active'',
1664: \item a scale $r$ controlling whether the cycle is detected (needs all edges).
1665: \end{itemize}
1666: 
1667: Then the appearance/disappearance of the cycle in $K_{r,\lambda}$ correlates with satisfaction/violation of the holonomy constraint.
1668: This motivates a computational observable:
1669: \[
1670: \Delta(r,\lambda) := \left\| S_{v_1}\cdots S_{v_m} - \pi^k I\right\|
1671: \]
1672: (for an operator norm or spectral distance), treated as a function on parameter space.
1673: Taking sublevel sets $\{\Delta\le \epsilon\}$ yields an additional filtration direction; one can study a 3-parameter module in principle, but in practice one compresses by fixing $\epsilon$ and studying the induced 2-parameter persistence.
1674: 
1675: \begin{remark}[Why this is not vacuous]
1676: If $S_v$ are pure permutation shifts, the product around a cycle is often exactly identity (up to conjugacy), so $\Delta$ carries little information.
1677: If $S_v$ are twisted shifts $S_m(\pi)$, the product accumulates valuation factors; $\Delta$ becomes sensitive to gluing and to local ramification---exactly the ``time dilation'' lever.
1678: \end{remark}
1679: 
1680: \subsection{Stability: what multiparameter persistence can guarantee}
1681: \label{sec:stability}
1682: 
1683: The empirical pipeline (Section~\ref{ch:expts}) produces noisy estimates of $W_\psi(u,v)$.
1684: Therefore, any topological summary must be stable under perturbations.
1685: 
1686: One robust statement is:
1687: 
1688: \begin{proposition}[Stability of rank invariants (informal)]
1689: If two bifiltered complexes $(K_{r,\lambda})$ and $(K'_{r,\lambda})$ are $\varepsilon$--interleaved (with respect to an appropriate metric on the parameter plane), then their rank invariants differ by at most $\varepsilon$ in the induced parameter distortion.
1690: \end{proposition}
1691: 
1692: This is the correct multiparameter substitute for barcode stability.
1693: The generic-free-resolution technology in Appendix~B (Buchsbaum--Eisenbud style) is particularly useful because it identifies \emph{which algebraic features are stable and which are intrinsically unstable} (jump loci of determinantal varieties).
1694: 
1695: \subsection{Pipeline: from 10k-qubit hardware logs to persistence invariants}
1696: \label{sec:pipeline}
1697: 
1698: We now state an end-to-end pipeline in implementation form.
1699: Inputs are assumed to come from quantum hardware experiments as described in Section~\ref{ch:expts}.
1700: 
1701: \subsubsection*{Input}
1702: \begin{itemize}
1703: \item A hardware connectivity graph $G_{\mathrm{hw}}$ with $N\sim 10^4$ qubits.
1704: \item A chosen embedded graph model $\Gamma\subset \Sigma$ (logical graph) and a mapping of $\Gamma$ into $G_{\mathrm{hw}}$ via SWAP networks if needed.
1705: \item A family of circuits $U(\lambda)$ producing states $|\psi(\lambda)\rangle$, and measurement logs sufficient to estimate $W_\psi(u,v)$ on edges (at least for neighboring vertices in $\Gamma$).
1706: \end{itemize}
1707: 
1708: \subsubsection*{Steps}
1709: \begin{enumerate}
1710: \item \textbf{Estimate correlation weights.} Produce $W_\psi(u,v)$ (mutual information or proxy) with error bars.
1711: \item \textbf{Choose parameter grid.} Select radii $r_i$ and thresholds $\lambda_j$ based on quantiles of $W_\psi$ and graph diameters.
1712: \item \textbf{Build bifiltered complexes.} For each $(i,j)$ compute $K_{r_i,\lambda_j}$ (using clique enumeration on sparse graphs; for large $N$ restrict simplex dimension).
1713: \item \textbf{Compute homology.} Compute $H_k(K_{r_i,\lambda_j})$ for small $k$ (typically $k=0,1,2$) using sparse boundary matrices.
1714: \item \textbf{Assemble module.} Create the bigraded module $M$ by storing vector spaces and maps.
1715: \item \textbf{Compute invariants.} Compute rank invariant $\rho$ for selected pairs $(p,q)$ and/or compute a free resolution and Betti numbers on smaller induced submodules (windows) using Macaulay2.
1716: \item \textbf{Validate stability.} Perturb weights within error bars (bootstrap) and recompute invariants; stable invariants are candidates for physical interpretation.
1717: \end{enumerate}
1718: 
1719: \subsubsection*{Validation checks and failure modes}
1720: \begin{itemize}
1721: \item If $W_\psi$ is too noisy, thresholding produces spurious topological changes; mitigate via averaging and restricting to local neighborhoods.
1722: \item Clique complexes blow up combinatorially; mitigate by bounding simplex dimension (e.g.\ only up to triangles) and using witness complexes.
1723: \item Two-parameter resolutions are expensive; use windowed computations and rank invariants as primary summaries.
1724: \end{itemize}
1725: 
1726: \subsection{Where the surface-order structure enters computationally}
1727: \label{sec:where_order_enters}
1728: 
1729: The filtration above only used the embedded graph and entanglement weights.
1730: Surface orders contribute additional structure that can be injected in two ways:
1731: 
1732: \begin{enumerate}
1733: \item \textbf{Local degrees / valences.} Vertex order sizes $m_v$ determine the local clock dimension and hence the plausible correlation structure (e.g.\ higher valence $\Rightarrow$ more local degrees of freedom).
1734: \item \textbf{Valuations and twisted shifts.} Local uniformizers $\pi_v$ (or effective parameters derived from entanglement) modify the clock generators $S_v$; holonomy constraints then define an \emph{algebraic energy landscape} whose sublevel sets yield alternative filtrations.
1735: \end{enumerate}
1736: 
1737: The second mechanism is the one most directly tied to emergent time and gravity:
1738: it is how information-theoretic data can influence projective holonomy spectra in a way that mimics redshift.
1739: 
1740: 
1741: \part{Surface Orders, Twisted Shifts, and Classification}
1742:
\chapter{Compact Gentle Surface Orders} · lines 1743–2921
1743: 	\chapter{Compact Gentle Surface Orders}
1744: 	
1745: 	\section{Orders and Burban-Drozd Gluing}
1746: 	\subsection{Definitions and Properties}
1747: 	\begin{defn}
1748: 		Let $R$ be a complete noetherian local domain with field of fractions $\KK$, and residue field $k$. An $R$-\textbf{Order} $\Lambda$ in a $k$-algebra $A$ is a unital subring of $A$ such that
1749: 		\begin{enumerate}
1750: 			\item $\KK \Lambda = A$, and
1751: 			\item $\Lambda$ is finitely generated as an $R$-module. 
1752: 		\end{enumerate}
1753: 		Let $C = [\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ be a constellation, and let $\Gamma = \hookrightarrow \Sigma$ be the associated graph cellularly embedded in the closed Riemann surface $\Sigma$. Further, let $n(i)=n_i$ denote the length of the cycle given by $\sigma_i$\sidenote{Recall: for a constellation $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ and the associated graph $\Gamma$ on a (compact) closed Riemann surface $\Sigma$, the length of the (nonzero) cycle in the gentle medial quiver $Q(C)$, associated to $\sigma_i$ is just the order of the cycle $\sigma_i$. Here we are using the notation $\sigma = \sigma_1\sigma_2 \cdots \sigma_r$, where $\sigma_i \in S_{[2m]}$ is a cycle permutation.}
1754: 		\begin{enumerate}
1755: 			\item For each cycle $\sigma_i \in \Gamma_0$ associated to the vertex $i$, we associate a local order $\Omega_i = \Omega(\sigma_i)$, and a regular principal ideal $\omega_i:= \omega_0(\sigma_i) \Omega_i = \Omega_i \omega_0(\sigma_i)$. 
1756: 			\item The \textbf{hereditary}\sidenote{An algebra, or an order, is hereditary if no module has a minimal projective resolution greater than length $1$. This means the \textit{projective dimension} of any module is no greater than $1$, and therefore the global dimension is at most $1$.} order associated to $\sigma_i$ is then given by
1757: 			\[
1758: 			\HH_i = \begin{pmatrix}
1759: 				\Omega_i & \omega_i & \omega_i & \cdots & \omega_i & \omega_i \\
1760: 				\Omega_i & \Omega_i & \omega_i & \cdots & \omega_i & \omega_i \\
1761: 				\Omega_i & \Omega_i & \Omega_i & \cdots & \omega_i & \omega_i \\
1762: 				\vdots 	   & \vdots      & \vdots     & \ddots & \vdots    & \vdots \\
1763: 				\Omega_i & \Omega_i & \Omega_i & \cdots & \Omega_i & \omega_i \\
1764: 				\Omega_i & \Omega_i & \Omega_i & \cdots & \Omega_i & \Omega_i \\
1765: 			\end{pmatrix}_{n(i)}
1766: 			\]
1767: 			\item Let $\Omega_i^{(k,k)}$ denote the $(k,k)$ entry of $\Omega_i$ in $\HH_i$. 
1768: 			\item For each $1 \leq k \leq n_i$ let
1769: 			\[ P_{i,k}:= \begin{pmatrix}
1770: 				\omega_i \\ \vdots \\ \omega_i \\ \Omega_i \\ \Omega_i \\\vdots \\ \Omega_i \\ \Omega_i
1771: 			\end{pmatrix} \]
1772: 			where the first entry equal to $\Omega_i$ is the $k^{th}$ row. 
1773: 		\end{enumerate}
1774: 	\end{defn}
1775: 	
1776: 	The modules $\{P_{i,k}: \ 1 \leq k \leq n_i\}$ give a complete set of non-isomorphic indecomposable projective $\HH_i
1777: 	$-modules, with the natural inclusions
1778: 	\[ P_{i,1} \hookleftarrow P_{i,2} \hookleftarrow \cdots \hookleftarrow P_{i,n_i-1} \hookleftarrow P_{i,n_i} 
1779: 	\hookleftarrow P_{i,1}. \]
1780: 	where the final map is given by left-multiplication by $\omega_0(\sigma_i)$. If we identify $P_{i,k}$ with the edge 
1781: 	$e_k^i = e_k(\sigma_i)$, where $\sigma_i = (e_1^i, e_2^i, ..., e_{n_i}^i)$ is a cyclic permutation, then the chain of 
1782: 	inclusions can be interpreted in terms of the cycle $\sigma_i$. From the embedding $\Gamma
1783: 	\hookrightarrow \Sigma$ given by the constellation $C = [\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$, this can be interpreted as walking 
1784: 	clockwise around the vertex of $\sigma_i$. We will take $P_{i,k} = P_{i,k+n_i}$, but each $e_k^i$ must be 
1785: 	multiplied by the automorphism $\sigma_i$ after one trip around the cycle, i.e. there is some multiplication by a 
1786: 	power of $\omega_0(\sigma_i)$ involved. 
1787: 	\indent In particular, conjugation by 
1788: 	\[ \underline{\omega}_i:= \begin{pmatrix}
1789: 		0 & 0 & 0 \cdots & 0 & \omega_0(\sigma_i) \\
1790: 		1 & 0 & 0 \cdots & 0 & 0 \\
1791: 		0 & 1 & 0 \cdots & 0 & 0 \\
1792: 		\vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\
1793: 		0 & 0 & 0 \cdots & 0 & 0 \\
1794: 		0 & 0 & 0 \cdots & 1 & 0 
1795: 	\end{pmatrix}_{n_i} \]
1796:

1800: 
1801: \section{Local vertex orders and the \texorpdfstring{$\pi$}{pi}--twisted shift}
1802: \label{sec:twisted_shift}
1803: 
1804: The  main body already identifies the \emph{pure} cyclic successor at a valence-$m$ vertex with the permutation matrix $T_v$ ( \S3.4.1).
1805: However, in a local \emph{order}---especially a hereditary order over a discrete valuation ring---the natural successor that normalizes the order often picks up a uniformizer factor on the wrap-around step.  This is precisely the twisted shift we now define:
1806: \[
1807: S_{3}(\pi)=
1808: \begin{pmatrix}
1809: 0 & 0 & \pi \\
1810: 1 & 0 & 0 \\
1811: 0 & 1 & 0
1812: \end{pmatrix}.
1813: \]
1814: 
1815: \subsection{Set-up: hereditary order over a DVR}
1816: Let $(R,\mathfrak m)$ be a discrete valuation ring with uniformizer $\pi$ and fraction field $K$.
1817: The standard (Iwahori-type) hereditary order of type $m$ is
1818: \begin{equation}
1819: \label{eq:hereditary_order}
1820: \Lambda_m(R)=
1821: \begin{pmatrix}
1822: R & R & \cdots & R\\
1823: \pi R & R & \cdots & R\\
1824: \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\
1825: \pi R & \pi R & \cdots & R
1826: \end{pmatrix}
1827: \subset \mathrm{Mat}_m(K).
1828: \end{equation}
1829: This is the local model appearing (in slightly different notation) in the source text in Appendix~A, and it is also the canonical ``vertex order'' for a cycle of length $m$ in a dessin order.
1830: 
1831: \subsection{The twisted shift and its basic algebra}
1832: 
1833: \begin{definition}[$\pi$--twisted successor]
1834: For $m\ge 2$, define the $\pi$--twisted shift (companion) matrix
1835: \begin{equation}
1836: \label{eq:twisted_shift_def}
1837: S_m(\pi)=
1838: \begin{pmatrix}
1839: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & \pi\\
1840: 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 0\\
1841: 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0 & 0\\
1842: \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots\\
1843: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 1 & 0
1844: \end{pmatrix}\in \mathrm{Mat}_m(K).
1845: \end{equation}
1846: \end{definition}
1847: 
1848: \begin{lemma}[Order-compatibility]
1849: \label{lem:twisted_shift_normalizes}
1850: $S_m(\pi)$ normalizes the hereditary order $\Lambda_m(R)$ in the sense that
1851: \[
1852: S_m(\pi)\,\Lambda_m(R)\,S_m(\pi)^{-1}=\Lambda_m(R).
1853: \]
1854: In particular, conjugation by $S_m(\pi)$ is an automorphism of the local order and is therefore an ``allowed'' symmetry (a clock move) in the operator-algebra interpretation.
1855: \end{lemma}
1856: 
1857: \begin{proof}
1858: A direct computation using the valuation filtration on matrix entries is easiest.
1859: Conjugation by the pure permutation matrix cyclically permutes the standard lattice chain
1860: \[
1861: R^m \supset \mathfrak m\oplus R^{m-1} \supset \mathfrak m^2\oplus \mathfrak m\oplus R^{m-2}\supset \cdots
1862: \]
1863: that defines $\Lambda_m(R)$ as its endomorphism ring.
1864: The $\pi$ in the $(1,m)$ entry exactly compensates the valuation jump on the wrap-around step so that the lattice chain is preserved rather than shifted out of phase.
1865: Equivalently, one checks on matrix units $E_{ij}$ that valuations transform compatibly: the entries that must lie in $\pi R$ remain in $\pi R$ after conjugation.
1866: \end{proof}
1867: 
1868: \begin{proposition}[Central power relation]
1869: \label{prop:twisted_shift_power}
1870: The twisted shift satisfies
1871: \[
1872: S_m(\pi)^m = \pi\, I_m.
1873: \]
1874: Consequently, in the projective linear group $\PGL_m(K)$ the class of $S_m(\pi)$ has \emph{finite order} dividing $m$.
1875: \end{proposition}
1876: 
1877: \begin{proof}
1878: Apply $S_m(\pi)$ to the standard basis vectors $e_1,\dots,e_m$:
1879: $S e_i=e_{i+1}$ for $i<m$ and $S e_m=\pi e_1$.
1880: Iterating $m$ times maps each basis vector to $\pi$ times itself, hence $S^m=\pi I$.
1881: \end{proof}
1882: 
1883: \begin{remark}[Why this matters for time holonomy]
1884: The  discussion of cyclic time (as torsion holonomy) becomes more rigid in the presence of Proposition~\ref{prop:twisted_shift_power}:
1885: even if $S_m(\pi)$ has infinite order in $\GL_m(K)$ (because $\pi$ is not a root of unity), its projective class is torsion.
1886: This is the algebraic shadow of a geometric fact: local time may be ``non-compact'' (a $\Z$--cover), while global time is cyclic up to a central scaling that is invisible to projective measurements.
1887: \end{remark}
1888: 
1889: \subsection{Spectral geometry viewpoint}
1890: If $K=\C((t))$ and $\pi=t$, the eigenvalues of $S_m(t)$ are the roots of $x^m-t=0$,
1891: i.e.\ $t^{1/m}\zeta_m^j$.
1892: Thus the twisted shift is a discrete model of a clock with a tunable ``rate'' set by the valuation of $\pi$.
1893: Later chapters will exploit this to connect:
1894: \[
1895: \text{entanglement density} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{effective valuation data} \;\longrightarrow\; \text{clock redshift}.
1896: \]
1897: 
1898: \section{How the corrigendum and twisted shift plug into the main narrative}
1899: \label{sec:corrigendum_plug_in}
1900: 
1901: Two themes introduced here reappear everywhere:
1902: 
1903: \begin{enumerate}
1904: \item \textbf{Orientation and time.}  The double-$3$--cycle on the sphere makes the time-orientation choice algebraic, not rhetorical: two opposite cycles correspond to two local time directions, and relations determine whether and how one can switch.
1905: \item \textbf{Ramification and clock drift.}  The factor $\pi$ in $S_m(\pi)$ is the minimal local datum that can create global drift/holonomy effects when orders are glued.  In Chapter~\ref{ch:gluing} we make this precise by describing gluing as a cocycle valued in normalizers of local orders.
1906: 
1907: 
1908: \subsection{Vertex-cycle moves, forbidden face cycles, and reversal by switching to an adjacent vertex cycle}
1909: \label{sec:vertex_cycle_reversal_switching}
1910: 
1911: The phrase ``choose an orientation and walk around the vertex cycle'' hides a subtlety that becomes important once we insist that
1912: \emph{only certain cycles survive in the quotient by the gentle ideal}.
1913: Because the Hilbert--Pólya/GRH section uses these cycles as \emph{clock} degrees of freedom, we spell out the combinatorics carefully.
1914: 
1915: \subsubsection{Dessin permutations versus quiver relations}
1916: 
1917: For a bipartite dessin (or, equivalently, the monodromy of a Belyi map) we have a permutation triple
1918: \[
1919: (\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_\infty)\ \in\ S_D^3,\qquad \sigma_0\sigma_1\sigma_\infty=1,
1920: \]
1921: acting on the dart set $D$ (oriented half-edges).
1922: Cycles of $\sigma_0$ encode black vertices, cycles of $\sigma_1$ encode white vertices, and cycles of $\sigma_\infty$ encode faces.
1923: 
1924: In the constellation notation used earlier, one often writes $C=[\sigma,\alpha,\phi]$ with $\alpha$ the edge-involution and
1925: $\phi=\alpha\sigma^{-1}$ the face permutation.
1926: In the special ``bipartite'' viewpoint of Section~\ref{sec:hp_worked_example_torus_bipartite} we literally identify
1927: \[
1928: (\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_\infty)=(\sigma,\alpha,\phi),
1929: \]
1930: but for the discussion below it is enough to remember:
1931: 
1932: \begin{quote}
1933: \emph{$\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$ are vertex permutations; $\sigma_\infty$ is the face permutation.}
1934: \end{quote}
1935: 
1936: Now pass to the associated gentle surface algebra (or order) $\Lambda=k Q/I$.
1937: The gentle relations are exactly the statement that, at each step, there is a unique ``forbidden'' continuation in the ideal $I$ and a unique ``allowed'' continuation not in~$I$.
1938: Equivalently (using the standard permutation formalism for $2$--regular gentle quivers), there are two permutations on arrows:
1939: \begin{itemize}
1940: \item a successor permutation $\sigma$ whose cycles are the \emph{allowed} directed cycles (``vertex cycles'' in our surface/dessin models), and
1941: \item a successor permutation $\phi$ whose cycles are the \emph{anti-cycles} (cycles of zero relations), i.e.\ the compositions along these cycles land in~$I$.
1942: \end{itemize}
1943: In the bipartite dessin setting, the allowed cycles are precisely the vertex cycles coming from $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$,
1944: whereas the face cycles coming from $\sigma_\infty$ are the anti-cycles:
1945: \begin{quote}
1946: \emph{walking around a $\sigma_0$- or $\sigma_1$-vertex is allowed (not in $I$), while attempting to walk around a $\sigma_\infty$-face is killed by the ideal.}
1947: \end{quote}
1948: 
1949: \subsubsection{What ``reversal'' means algebraically: inverse arrows and forced switching}
1950: 
1951: A directed walk in the quiver uses arrows in their orientation.
1952: To model a geometric walk that sometimes goes \emph{against} the arrow orientation (e.g.\ reversing direction along a cycle),
1953: one uses the standard string-algebra convention: for each arrow $a$ one introduces a formal inverse symbol $a^{-1}$,
1954: interpreted as traversing the same geometric edge in the opposite direction.
1955: 
1956: In a gentle quiver, a ``string'' is a word in arrows and inverse arrows with two constraints:
1957: (i) no immediate cancellation $aa^{-1}$ or $a^{-1}a$, and
1958: (ii) no forbidden subword whose corresponding length-$2$ path lies in the ideal $I$.
1959: Because every vertex has out-degree $2$, these constraints have a striking consequence:
1960: 
1961: \begin{quote}
1962: \emph{If you traverse an arrow $a$ and then reverse (use $a^{-1}$), the next step is forced: you must exit along the \textbf{other} outgoing arrow at that vertex, hence you jump to the \textbf{adjacent} vertex cycle.}
1963: \end{quote}
1964: 
1965: In the dessin language, this is exactly the move described in the prompt:
1966: starting from an allowed vertex cycle (coming from $\sigma_0$ or $\sigma_1$), a reversal against the quiver arrows forces you onto the
1967: adjacent allowed cycle at the same edge (the other endpoint of that edge), rather than allowing you to continue around a face.
1968: The forbidden alternative would be to follow the face permutation $\sigma_\infty$, but that continuation is precisely what the ideal~$I$ forbids.
1969: 
1970: \subsubsection{Translation into clock operators: forward shifts, backward shifts, and ``different shifts''}
1971: 
1972: Fix a vertex $v$ of valence $m_v$ carrying a DVR $(R_v,\pi_v)$ and ramification exponent $e_v$.
1973: On the corresponding local projective chain (or, in the quantum model, on the local Hilbert space with basis $\{\ket{1},\dots,\ket{m_v}\}$)
1974: the forward cyclic motion is implemented by the twisted shift
1975: \[
1976: S_v:=S_{m_v}(\pi_v^{e_v}),
1977: \qquad
1978: S_{m}(\pi^e)=
1979: \begin{pmatrix}
1980: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & \pi^e\\
1981: 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 0\\
1982: 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0 & 0\\
1983: \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots\\
1984: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 1 & 0
1985: \end{pmatrix},
1986: \]
1987: so that $S_v^{m_v}=\pi_v^{e_v}I$ and the valuation of $\pi_v^{e_v}$ measures the local ``clock defect''.
1988: 
1989: \begin{lemma}[Explicit inverse (backward shift)]
1990: \label{lem:twisted_shift_inverse}
1991: For any $m\ge 2$ and $e\ge 1$,
1992: \[
1993: S_m(\pi^e)^{-1}=
1994: \begin{pmatrix}
1995: 0 & 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\
1996: 0 & 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0\\
1997: \vdots &  & \ddots & \ddots & \vdots\\
1998: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 1\\
1999: \pi^{-e} & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 0
2000: \end{pmatrix},
2001: \qquad\text{and hence}\qquad
2002: \bigl(S_m(\pi^e)^{-1}\bigr)^m=\pi^{-e}I.
2003: \]
2004: \end{lemma}
2005: 
2006: \begin{proof}
2007: On the standard basis vectors $\ket{k}$, the defining relations are
2008: \[
2009: S_m(\pi^e)\ket{k}=\ket{k+1}\ (1\le k\le m-1),\qquad S_m(\pi^e)\ket{m}=\pi^e\ket{1}.
2010: \]
2011: The displayed matrix is the unique linear map with
2012: \[
2013: S_m(\pi^e)^{-1}\ket{k}=\ket{k-1}\ (2\le k\le m),\qquad S_m(\pi^e)^{-1}\ket{1}=\pi^{-e}\ket{m},
2014: \]
2015: and a direct multiplication check gives $S_m(\pi^e)\,S_m(\pi^e)^{-1}=I=S_m(\pi^e)^{-1}S_m(\pi^e)$.
2016: Raising to the $m$th power yields the central factor $\pi^{-e}$.
2017: \end{proof}
2018: 
2019: Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted_shift_inverse} is the clean operator-algebraic avatar of ``walking around the same vertex cycle in the opposite direction'':
2020: \begin{quote}
2021: \emph{reversing direction replaces the forward shift $S_v$ by the backward shift $S_v^{-1}$, and inverts the central defect factor.}
2022: \end{quote}
2023: 
2024: Now incorporate the \emph{adjacent-cycle switch}.
2025: If an edge $e$ joins vertices $v$ and $w$ (in the bipartite case: a black vertex and a white vertex), then a reversal at~$e$
2026: forces a jump from the $v$-cycle to the $w$-cycle.
2027: On the quantum side, this is implemented by the edge identification/isometry (a bimodule map in the order language)
2028: \[
2029: U_e:\cH_v\supset \cH_{v\to e}\ \longrightarrow\ \cH_{w\to e}\subset \cH_w,
2030: \]
2031: between the edge ``corner'' subspaces at~$e$.
2032: Conjugating by $U_e$ transports shifts across the gluing.
2033: The essential point is that \emph{different vertices generally carry different local data} (different $m$, different $\pi$, different $e$),
2034: so an adjacent-cycle reversal is not merely $S_v^{-1}$ but typically a \textbf{different shift operator}:
2035: \[
2036: \text{reverse at an edge} \quad\leadsto\quad
2037: S_v^{-1}\ \text{on one side}\ \simeq\ U_e^{-1}\,S_w^{\pm 1}\,U_e\ \text{on the other side}.
2038: \]
2039: This is exactly the mechanism by which a local ``time reversal'' move changes the valuation/holonomy bookkeeping:
2040: in the homogeneous case it flips the power of~$\pi$, while in the heterogeneous case it also changes \emph{which} uniformizer appears.
2041: 
2042: \begin{remark}[Why $\sigma_\infty$ is singled out]
2043: In an oriented bipartite dessin one has the face-step identity
2044: $\sigma_\infty=(\sigma_0\sigma_1)^{-1}=\sigma_1\sigma_0^{-1}$ (when $\sigma_1$ is an involution, as in many toy models).
2045: Thus walking once around a face alternates a \emph{backward} black turn ($\sigma_0^{-1}$) with a \emph{forward} white turn ($\sigma_1$).
2046: In the gentle algebra this alternation is precisely what is killed by the ideal: face cycles are anti-cycles.
2047: The only way to realize ``backward'' motion without falling into the ideal is therefore to treat it as a string move (inverse arrow) and, equivalently, to switch to the adjacent vertex cycle as described above.
2048: \end{remark}
2049: 
2050: \subsubsection{How the adjacent-cycle reversal argument proves the claim (and why total positivity shows up)}
2051: \label{sec:how_reversal_proves_it}
2052: 
2053: The previous subsubsections stated the reversal rule in words.
2054: Here we make the logical implication explicit: \emph{the gentle relations force reversal to be implemented by switching to the adjacent vertex cycle, and this forced switching is exactly what later lets us package ``one tick'' of evolution as a planar network (wiring diagram) with positive weights.}
2055: Both points are ultimately local.
2056: 
2057: \begin{proposition}[Reversal forces switching to the adjacent allowed cycle]
2058: \label{prop:reversal_forces_switching_adjacent_cycle}
2059: Let $\Lambda=kQ/I$ be the gentle algebra (or order) attached to a bipartite dessin, and assume $Q$ is $2$--regular in the standard sense: at each vertex there are exactly two arrows leaving and exactly two arrows entering, and $I$ is generated by length-$2$ zero relations with the gentle uniqueness property.
2060: 
2061: Fix an arrow $a:u\to v$ of $Q$ and consider the corresponding \emph{string} model, i.e.\ reduced words in arrows and formal inverses subject to
2062: (i) no immediate cancellation $aa^{-1}$ or $a^{-1}a$, and
2063: (ii) no forbidden length-$2$ subword whose oriented interpretation lies in the ideal $I$.
2064: Suppose a string contains the letter $a^{-1}$, meaning that the walk traverses the underlying geometric edge in the direction \emph{opposite} the quiver arrow.
2065: 
2066: Then any continuation of the string past $a^{-1}$ that remains reduced and avoids the ideal must \emph{exit the vertex $u$ along the other outgoing arrow} $b\neq a$.
2067: In particular, the walk leaves the permitted cycle in which $a$ occurs and enters the permitted cycle that is adjacent across the edge (the vertex cycle associated to the other endpoint of the underlying edge in the dessin).
2068: \end{proposition}
2069: 
2070: \begin{proof}
2071: After traversing $a^{-1}$ the walk is located at the tail $u$ of $a$.
2072: Because the word is reduced, the next letter cannot be $a$ (this would create the forbidden cancellation subword $a^{-1}a$).
2073: By $2$--regularity there are exactly two arrows leaving $u$, namely $a$ and a distinct arrow $b$.
2074: Hence the next arrow-oriented step must involve $b$ (either $b$ or $b^{-1}$ depending on whether one continues with or against the arrow orientation).
2075: 
2076: What remains is to exclude the possibility that this continuation keeps one on the same permitted cycle.
2077: In a gentle quiver, each vertex has \emph{exactly one} permitted continuation and \emph{exactly one} forbidden continuation for each incoming arrow.
2078: Equivalently, the local successor permutations split the two outgoing arrows at $u$ into:
2079: \begin{itemize}
2080: \item the arrow that continues an \emph{allowed} (permitted) cycle, and
2081: \item the arrow that continues a \emph{forbidden} (anti-)cycle (a length-$2$ path in $I$).
2082: \end{itemize}
2083: If one attempts to ``reverse while staying on the same vertex cycle'', the only combinatorially available way to do so is to glue the reversal to a face-step (a $\sigma_\infty$ move), because in the dessin identity
2084: $\sigma_\infty=(\sigma_0\sigma_1)^{-1}$ the backward vertex motion is paired with the forward motion at the opposite color.
2085: But face cycles are precisely the anti-cycles killed by $I$.
2086: Therefore the would-be continuation that realizes a face-step is disallowed by constraint (ii), and the only reduced continuation that remains is the other outgoing arrow~$b$, which lands on the adjacent permitted cycle.
2087: \end{proof}
2088: 
2089: \begin{corollary}[Clock interpretation: ``reverse'' is not an involution on one cycle but a conjugated shift on the neighbor]
2090: \label{cor:reverse_is_conjugated_shift}
2091: In the operator model of Section~\ref{sec:vertex_cycle_reversal_switching}, let $S_v$ denote the forward twisted shift on the vertex-cycle Hilbert space $\cH_v$.
2092: A local reversal at an edge $e=(v,w)$ cannot be implemented as a closed walk that stays entirely on the $v$-cycle.
2093: Instead it is implemented by switching to the adjacent cycle and transporting the shift across the gluing:
2094: \[
2095: \text{reverse at }e\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad
2096: S_v^{-1}\ \simeq\ U_e^{-1}\,S_w^{\pm 1}\,U_e,
2097: \]
2098: where $U_e$ is the edge identification/isometry and the sign depends on the chosen local convention for ``forward'' around~$w$.
2099: Because $m_v,\pi_v,e_v$ need not equal $m_w,\pi_w,e_w$, the operator on the right-hand side is generally a \emph{different} shift (different dimension and/or different defect factor), exactly as claimed.
2100: \end{corollary}
2101: 
2102: \begin{remark}[Where total positivity enters the proof pipeline]
2103: The forced switching has a practical analytic payoff.
2104: Once reversals are realized as \emph{moves between adjacent vertex cycles}, one can draw one-tick evolution (forward steps + switches) as a \emph{planar network} whose local gadgets are the allowed crossings/switches.
2105: Assign strictly positive weights to each local gadget (Boltzmann weights, channel Kraus weights, or learned attention weights).
2106: Then the one-tick transfer matrix is the weight matrix of that planar network, hence is totally nonnegative by the Lindstr\"om--Gessel--Viennot lemma (Theorem~\ref{thm:LGV}) and is totally positive under mild irreducibility/strictness assumptions (Corollary~\ref{cor:wiring_TN}).
2107: If, additionally, the weights satisfy a discrete detailed-balance/reflection symmetry so that the transfer matrix is symmetric, then
2108: Theorem~\ref{thm:tp_positive_energy} applies and produces a \emph{self-adjoint} Hamiltonian (or modular Hamiltonian) with nonnegative spectrum after an affine normalization.
2109: This is the precise sense in which the wiring-diagram/total-positivity technology ``proves the result'' claimed earlier: it turns purely combinatorial admissibility constraints into a checkable spectral certificate.
2110: \end{remark}
2111: 
2112: 
2113: 
2114: 
2115: \end{enumerate}
2116: 
2117: \section{Classification notes: local hereditary orders, larger twisted shifts, and surface-order gluings}
2118: 
2119: \label{ch:classification}
2120: 
2121: 
2122: One request for the expanded edition was to fill in ``mathematical gaps'' around
2123: surface-order classification and the operator shifts in larger local vertex orders.
2124: This chapter collects the minimum classification statements needed later, together with proof sketches that
2125: are standard but easy to lose track of when one jumps between topology, algebra, and physics.
2126: 
2127: \subsection{Hereditary orders over a DVR are endomorphism rings of lattice chains}
2128: \label{sec:lattice_chains}
2129: 
2130: Let $R$ be a discrete valuation ring with uniformizer $\pi$ and fraction field $K$.
2131: Let $V=K^m$.
2132: 
2133: \begin{definition}[Lattice and lattice chain]
2134: An $R$-lattice in $V$ is a finitely generated $R$-submodule $L\subset V$ such that $K\otimes_R L\cong V$.
2135: A \emph{lattice chain} of period $m$ is a sequence
2136: \[
2137: L_0 \supset L_1 \supset \cdots \supset L_{m-1} \supset \pi L_0
2138: \]
2139: with successive quotients $L_i/L_{i+1}$ of rank $1$ over the residue field.
2140: \end{definition}
2141: 
2142: \begin{theorem}[Classical classification of hereditary orders (standard)]
2143: \label{thm:hereditary_classification}
2144: For $A=\mathrm{Mat}_m(K)$, hereditary $R$-orders in $A$ are precisely endomorphism rings of lattice chains:
2145: \[
2146: \Lambda \text{ hereditary } \Longleftrightarrow \exists\ (L_\bullet)\ \text{ lattice chain with }\Lambda=\End_R(L_\bullet),
2147: \]
2148: where $\End_R(L_\bullet)$ denotes the set of endomorphisms preserving the entire chain.
2149: Moreover, two hereditary orders are conjugate in $\GL_m(K)$ iff their lattice chains are isomorphic (up to shift of indices).
2150: \end{theorem}
2151: 
2152: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
2153: If $\Lambda$ is hereditary, its Jacobson radical $J(\Lambda)$ defines a filtration on the unique (up to iso) indecomposable projective modules.
2154: Choosing a primitive idempotent gives a lattice whose successive radical powers yield a chain.
2155: Conversely, $\End_R(L_\bullet)$ is hereditary because submodules correspond to subchains and projective dimension is controlled by the chain length.
2156: \end{proof}
2157: 
2158: \begin{remark}
2159: The explicit matrix form \eqref{eq:hereditary_order} is obtained by choosing a basis adapted to the chain.
2160: In this basis, the $(i,j)$ entry lies in $\pi R$ precisely when the endomorphism must map $L_j$ into $L_{i}$ with one valuation drop.
2161: \end{remark}
2162: 
2163: \subsection{Normalizer and the larger analogues of the twisted shift}
2164: \label{sec:normalizer_shifts}
2165: 
2166: Fix the standard lattice chain
2167: \[
2168: L_i = \pi^i R e_1 \oplus \pi^i R e_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus \pi^i R e_{m-i}
2169: \oplus \pi^{i+1}R e_{m-i+1}\oplus \cdots \oplus \pi^{m-1}R e_m,
2170: \]
2171: whose endomorphism ring is the Iwahori order \eqref{eq:hereditary_order}.
2172: The twisted shift $S_m(\pi)$ is the unique (up to units) element that cyclically permutes this chain.
2173: 
2174: More generally, one can introduce \emph{ramification exponent} $e\ge 1$ and define
2175: \begin{equation}
2176: \label{eq:twisted_shift_e}
2177: S_m(\pi^e)=
2178: \begin{pmatrix}
2179: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & \pi^e\\
2180: 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 0\\
2181: 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0 & 0\\
2182: \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots\\
2183: 0 & 0 & \cdots & 1 & 0
2184: \end{pmatrix},
2185: \end{equation}
2186: which satisfies $(S_m(\pi^e))^m=\pi^e I$.
2187: 
2188: \begin{proposition}[Normalizer generator (useful form)]
2189: \label{prop:normalizer_generator}
2190: The normalizer of $\Lambda_m(R)$ in $\GL_m(K)$ is generated by:
2191: \begin{itemize}
2192: \item $\Lambda_m(R)^\times$ (units of the order),
2193: \item and the class of $S_m(\pi)$ (or $S_m(\pi^e)$ for the appropriate chain period).
2194: \end{itemize}
2195: In particular, any normalizer element induces a permutation (rotation) of the lattice chain, with a valuation factor determined by the number of wrap-arounds.
2196: \end{proposition}
2197: 
2198: \begin{remark}[Clock interpretation]
2199: This says: the only genuinely new clock move beyond local gauge (units) is the successor that rotates the chain.
2200: Thus the ``shift with a $\pi$'' is not an ad hoc insertion; it is forced by the structure of hereditary orders.
2201: \end{remark}
2202: 
2203: \subsection{Surface orders from embedded graphs: classification by local types plus gluing}
2204: \label{sec:surface_order_classification}
2205: 
2206: A surface order in this project is a global object built by gluing local hereditary orders assigned to vertices (cycles) of an embedded graph/dessin.
2207: The source text in Appendix~A of the  volume explains this construction in detail for dessin orders.
2208: 
2209: We record the abstract classification statement in the language of Section~\ref{ch:gluing}.
2210: 
2211: \begin{theorem}[Classification template for clocked surface orders]
2212: \label{thm:surface_order_template}
2213: Fix a surface $X$ with an embedded graph $\Gamma\subset X$ giving a cellular decomposition.
2214: A clocked surface order up to Morita equivalence is determined by:
2215: \begin{enumerate}
2216: \item the underlying cellulation type (combinatorics of $\Gamma$),
2217: \item for each vertex $v$, a local hereditary order type (equivalently a lattice chain period $m_v$ and ramification exponent $e_v$),
2218: \item a gluing cocycle class $[g]\in \check{H}^1(X;\Aut(\Lambda))$ compatible with incidence data,
2219: \item and (optionally) a choice of clock generators $S_v=S_{m_v}(\pi^{e_v})$ in each stalk (compatible under gluing).
2220: \end{enumerate}
2221: \end{theorem}
2222: 
2223: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
2224: Local equivalence classes are fixed by Theorem~\ref{thm:hereditary_classification}.
2225: Gluing is precisely \v{C}ech data up to coboundary, giving $H^1$-classification of torsors.
2226: Morita equivalence suppresses choices of idempotent refinements but preserves local periods and holonomy classes.
2227: \end{proof}
2228: 
2229: \begin{remark}[What is still nontrivial]
2230: The theorem is a template: to make it fully explicit in a given example, one must compute $\Aut(\Lambda)$, choose the cover, and describe cocycle representatives in terms of the dessin permutations.
2231: This is exactly what the source text in Appendix~A does in a concrete combinatorial language.
2232: \end{remark}
2233: 
2234: \subsection{Why this matters for multiparameter persistence}
2235: \label{sec:classification_persistence}
2236: 
2237: Multiparameter persistence is most useful when the underlying model space has \emph{strata} and \emph{jump loci}.
2238: The classification above supplies those:
2239: 
2240: \begin{itemize}
2241: \item changing $(m_v,e_v)$ moves you between local-order strata (discrete jumps),
2242: \item varying the gluing cocycle $[g]$ moves you in a moduli-like space (often discrete but may have continuous parameters if phases are allowed),
2243: \item the rank invariants and determinantal jump loci in Appendix~B become diagnostics for which stratum you are in (or near).
2244: \end{itemize}
2245: 
2246: Thus classification and persistence are not separate topics: classification tells you what ``parameters'' exist, and persistence tells you which parameters are stable under noisy measurement.
2247: 
2248: 
2249: 	cyclically permutes the indecomposable projective $\HH_i$-modules $P_{i,k}$, and it induces an automorphism of $
2250: 	\HH_i$ which we also call $\sigma_i$. Now, for each cycle $\sigma_p, \sigma_q \in S_{[2m]}$ of $\sigma$, we fix an 
2251: 	isomorphism
2252: 	\[ \Omega_p/\omega_p \cong \Omega_q/\omega_q. \] 
2253: 	Identifying all such rings, let $\overline{\Omega} = \Omega_i/\omega_i$ for all $\sigma_i \in \Gamma_0$. Let $
2254: 	\pi_i: \Omega_i \to \overline{\Omega}$ be a fixed epimorphism with kernel $\omega_i$. Now, we have a pull-back 
2255: 	diagram
2256: 	\[ 
2257: 	\xymatrix{
2258: 		\Omega_{p,q} \ar[r]^{\tilde{\pi}_p}  \ar[d]_{\tilde{\pi}_q} & \Omega_p \ar[d]^{\pi_p} \\
2259: 		\Omega_q \ar[r]_{\pi_q} & \overline{\Omega}. 
2260: 	}
2261: 	\]
2262: 	which is in general different and non-isomorphic for different choices of $\pi_p$ and $\pi_q$.
2263: 	
2264: 	\begin{defn}
2265: 		Let $\HH = \prod_{\sigma_i \in \Gamma_0} \HH_i$. Let $e_k^i$ be an edge around $\sigma_i$, and let $
2266: 		\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l} = (e_k^i, e_l^j)$ be a $2$-cycle of the fixed-point free involution $\alpha$ of $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ giving the end vertices $
2267: 		\sigma_i$ and $\sigma_j$ of the edge $e_k^i \equiv e_l^j$ under the gluing identifying the half-edges $e_k^i$ and 
2268: 		$e_l^j$. It is possible that $\sigma_i=\sigma_j$ if $\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}$ defines a loop at the vertex $\sigma_i$ in $
2269: 		\Gamma$. We replace the product $\Omega_i^k \times \Omega_j^l$ in $\HH_i \times \HH_j$ with $\Omega_{i,j}$. 
2270: 		This identifies the $(k,k)$ entry of $\HH_i$ with the $(l,l)$ entry of $\HH_j$, modulo $\omega$. Doing this for all 
2271: 		edges of $\Gamma$, we get the \textbf{Constellation Order} or the (compact) \textbf{Gentle Surface Order} $\Lambda:= 
2272: 		\Lambda(C) = \Lambda(\Gamma)$ associated to the constellation $C$, or equivalently to the embedded 
2273: 		graph $\Gamma \hookrightarrow \Sigma$. 
2274: 	\end{defn}
2275: 	
2276: 	\begin{prop}
2277: 		\begin{enumerate}
2278: 			\item The indecomposable projective $\Lambda$-modules are in bijection with the $2$-cycles $(e^i_k, e^j_l)=\alpha^{i,j}_{k,l}$, of $\alpha \in S_{2m}$ for the constellation $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$. Equivalently, the indecomposable projectives are in bijection with the edges $\Gamma_1$. We label them as $P_{e}$ for $\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l} = e = (e^i_k, e^j_l) \in \Gamma_1$ attached to the vertices $\sigma_i$ and $\sigma_j$. 
2279: 			\item Each indecomposable projective $\Lambda$-module is a pullback,
2280: 			\[
2281: 			\xymatrix{
2282: 				P_e \ar[r] \ar[d] & P_{i,k} \ar[d] \\
2283: 				P_{j,l} \ar[r] & \overline{\Omega}
2284: 			}
2285: 			\]
2286: 			where the $P_{i,k}$ is an indecomposable projective module in $\HH_i$, and $P_{j,l}$ is an indecomposable 
2287: 			projective of $\HH_j$.
2288: 			\item From this we obtain the following commutative diagram with exact rows and columns,
2289: 			\[
2290: 			\begin{tikzcd}
2291: 				& 0 & 0 & 0 &  \\
2292: 				0 \arrow[r] & P_{i,k-1} \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & P_{i,k} \arrow[r] \arrow[u] & \overline{\Omega} \arrow[r] \arrow[u] & 0 \\
2293: 				0 \arrow[r] & P_{i,k-1} \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & P_e \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & P_{j,l} \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & 0 \\
2294: 				0 \arrow[r] & 0 \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & P_{j,l-1} \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & P_{j,l-1} \arrow[u] \arrow[r] & 0 \\
2295: 				& 0 \arrow[u] & 0 \arrow[u] & 0 \arrow[u] & 
2296: 			\end{tikzcd}
2297: 			\]
2298: 			\item $\ker(P_e \to P_{i,k})$ and $\ker(P_e \to P_{j,l})$ are projective $\HH_j$ and $\HH_i$-modules respectively, local $\Lambda$-modules, and are uniserial. 
2299: 		\end{enumerate}
2300: 	\end{prop}

2305: 	\subsection{A Basic Example}
2306: 	
2307: 	\begin{ex}
2308: 		Let $R$ have maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m} = \la m \ra$, with residue field $k=R/\mathfrak{m}$, and field of fractions $\KK=R_{\mathfrak{m}}$. 
2309: 		Let $\Gamma$ be the genus zero graph,  
2310: 		\[
2311: 		\xymatrix{
2312: 			\sigma_1 \ar@{-}[r]^{\alpha_{1}} & \sigma_2 \ar@{-}[r]^{\alpha_{2}} & \sigma_3 \ar@{-}[r]^{\alpha_{3}} & \sigma_4
2313: 		}
2314: 		\]
2315: 		given by the constellation $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ such that 
2316: 		$\sigma = (1)(2,3)(4,5)(6) = \sigma_1 \sigma_2 \sigma_3 \sigma_4$, and $\alpha = (1,2)(3,4)(5,6) = (e_1)(e_2)(e_3)$. We may take 
2317: 		\[ \HH = \left\lbrace \begin{pmatrix}
2318: 			a_{11} 
2319: 		\end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix}
2320: 			b_{11} & b_{12} & \\
2321: 			b_{21} & b_{22} & 
2322: 		\end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix}
2323: 			c_{11} & c_{12} & \\
2324: 			c_{21} & c_{22} & 
2325: 		\end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix}
2326: 			d_{11} 
2327: 		\end{pmatrix}\Bigg| a_{ij}, b_{ij}, c_{ij}, d_{ij}  \in R, b_{12}, c_{12} \in \mathfrak{m} \right\rbrace = \prod_{i=1}^4 \HH_i. \]
2328: 		We then have the congruences modulo $\mathfrak{m}$, 
2329: 		\[ a_{11} \sim b_{11}, b_{22} \sim c_{11}, c_{22} \sim d_{11}. \]
2330: 		Or in more compact notation which will be used later, 
2331: 		\[ (1,1) \sim (2,1), (2,2) \sim (3,1), (3,2) \sim (4,1). \]
2332: 		We then have 
2333: 		\[ \Lambda = \begin{pmatrix}
2334: 			R & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0\\
2335: 			0 & R & \mathfrak{m} & 0 & 0 & 0\\
2336: 			0 & R & R & 0 & 0 & 0\\
2337: 			0 & 0 & 0 & R & \mathfrak{m} & 0\\
2338: 			0 & 0 & 0 & R & R & 0\\
2339: 			0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & R 
2340: 		\end{pmatrix} \]
2341: 		such that \[\lambda_{11} = \lambda_{22}, \lambda_{33} = \lambda_{44}, \lambda_{55} = \lambda_{66}\]
2342: 		with all equalities taken modulo $\mathfrak{m}$, i.e. the residues $\lambda_{ii}$ are equal in $k=R/\mathfrak{m}$. \sidenote{Notice, the equalities in $k=R/\mathfrak{m}$ (i.e. equalities of residues modulo $\mathfrak{m}$) are given by $\alpha$ in the constellation $C = [\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$.}
2343: 		
2344: 		Suppose in particular that 
2345: 		\[
2346: 		\HH_1 \cong \HH_4 \cong k[[x]], \quad \HH_2 \cong \HH_3 \cong \begin{pmatrix}
2347: 			k[[x]] & (x)k[[x]] \\
2348: 			k[[x]] & k[[x]]
2349: 		\end{pmatrix}
2350: 		\]
2351: 		Letting $\mathfrak{m}_i = (x)$ for $i=1,...,4$, we get a pullback diagram
2352: 		\[
2353: 		\xymatrix{
2354: 			\Lambda \ar[rr]^{\pi_1} \ar[d]_{\iota_1} & & \Lambda/\rad(\Lambda)= k^3  \ar[d] \\
2355: 			\HH \ar[rr]_{\pi_2} & & \HH/\rad(\HH) = k^6
2356: 		}
2357: 		\]
2358: 		where $\Omega_i = k[[x]]$ for $i=1,...,4$ and $\omega_i = (x) = \rad(k[[x]])$, so that $\rad(\HH_1) = k = \rad(\HH_4)$ and $\rad(\HH_2) = k \times k = \rad(\HH_3)$; and $\rad(\HH) = \rad(\Lambda)$. Then we get
2359: 		\[ \Lambda = \begin{pmatrix}
2360: 			k[[x]] & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0\\
2361: 			0 & k[[x]] & (x)k[[x]] & 0 & 0 & 0\\
2362: 			0 & k[[x]] & k[[x]] & 0 & 0 & 0\\
2363: 			0 & 0 & 0 & k[[x]] & (x)k[[x]] & 0\\
2364: 			0 & 0 & 0 & k[[x]] & k[[x]] & 0\\
2365: 			0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & k[[x]]
2366: 		\end{pmatrix} \]
2367: 		such that \[\lambda_{11} = \lambda_{22}, \lambda_{33} = \lambda_{44}, \lambda_{55} = \lambda_{66}\]
2368: 		with all equalities taken modulo $\mathfrak{m} =(x)=\omega_i$, i.e. the residues $\lambda_{ii}$ are equal in $k$. The automorphisms given by $\sigma$ on $\HH_2 \cong \HH_3$ is
2369: 		\[ \begin{pmatrix}
2370: 			0 & x \\
2371: 			1 & 0
2372: 		\end{pmatrix} \]
2373: 		Further, this is exactly the algebra associated to the graph
2374: 		\[
2375: 		\xymatrix{
2376: 			\sigma_1 \ar@{-}[r]^{\alpha_{1}} & \sigma_2 \ar@{-}[r]^{\alpha_{2}} & \sigma_3 \ar@{-}[r]^{\alpha_{3}} & \sigma_4
2377: 		}
2378: 		\]
2379: 	\end{ex}

2402: 	\section{Projective Resolutions of Simple Objects}
2403: 	In this section we denote by $P^{\bullet}$ a complex of projective modules
2404: 	\[ \cdots \to P^{-1} \to P^0 \to P^{1} \to \cdots \]
2405: 	over some algebra $\Lambda$. Let $\Lambda = \Lambda(C)$ be a gentle constellation order given by the constellation $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$. \sidenote{Question: Find all constellations with fixed monodromy group. 
2406: 		(Problem $1.1.10$ \cite{LZ}) Having fixed a group $G \leq  S_n$ and a passport $[K_1,...,K_k]$,
2407: 		all the $K_i$ being conjugacy classes in $G$, find all the constellations with the
2408: 		cartographic group $G$ and with the refined passport $[K_1,...,K_k]$ with respect
2409: 		to $G$.}
2410: 	
2411: 	In this section we will show the following,
2412: 	\begin{prop}\label{fixed 3-constellation to fixed normalization}
2413: 		Classifying all $3$-constellations with a fixed passport $[\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3]$ is equivalent to classifying all "\emph{gentle constellation orders}" with the same normalization, or equivalently all "\emph{closed surface algebras}" with the same normalization. 
2414: 	\end{prop}
2415: 	
2416: 	
2417: 	
2418: 	\begin{theorem}
2419: 		\begin{enumerate}
2420: 			\item The indecomposable projective modules $P_{e} = P_{\alpha_{i,j}}$ have radical
2421: 			\[ \rad(P_e) = U(\sigma_i^k) \oplus U(\sigma_j^l) \]
2422: 			where $U(\sigma_p^q) \cong P_{p,q} \in \Mod(\HH_p)$ is an indecomposable uniserial $\Lambda$-module and and indecomposable projective $\HH_p$-module.
2423: 			\item The minimal projective resolution of the simple module $S(\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}) = S(e^i_k, e^j_l)$ of $\Lambda$, corresponding to the vertex $\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}=(e^i_k, e^j_l)$ of $Q$\sidenote{or equivalently the edge of the same labeling in $\Gamma(C)$ connecting vertex $\sigma_i$ and $\sigma_j$}, is infinite periodic. In particular the period $p$ of the minimal resolution $P^{\bullet}(\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}) = P^{\bullet} \to S(\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l})$ is exactly the least common multiple,
2424: 			\[ p(P^{\bullet}(i,j))  = \lcm\{|\cO_{\phi}(e^i_k)|, |\cO_{\phi}(e^j_l)|\}.\]
2425: 			where $\cO_{\phi}(e^i_k)$ and $\cO_{\phi}(e^j_l)$ are the orbits under the action of $\phi^{-1}$ of $e^i_k$ and $e^j_l$ on the two anti-cycles (or relations in $I$) passing through the vertex $\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}$. 
2426: 			\item The differentials in the minimal projective resolution of the simple module $S(\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l})$, $d^m: P^{m} \to P^{m+1}$ in the resolution
2427: 			\[ P^{\bullet}(\alpha^{k,l}_{i,j}): \cdots \to P^{m} \to P^{m+1} \to \cdots \]
2428: 			are given by multiplication by the matrix 
2429: 			\[ d^m:= \begin{pmatrix}
2430: 				a(\phi^{-m} \cdot e^i_k)  & 0 \\
2431: 				0 & a(\phi^{-m} \cdot e^j_l)
2432: 			\end{pmatrix}. \]
2433: 			where $a(\phi^{-m}e^i_k) \in Q_1$ is the arrow with $ta = \phi^{-m}e^i_k$ and $ta(\phi^{-m} \cdot e^j_l) = \phi^{-m} \cdot e^j_l$. 
2434: 			\item The syzygies $\Omega^m(\alpha_{k,l}^{i,j})= \ker(d^m)$ are of the form
2435: 			\[ \Omega^m(\alpha_{k,l}^{i,j}) = U(\sigma(\phi^{-m}\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}) = U(\sigma(\phi^{-m}\sigma e^i_k)) \oplus U(\sigma(\phi^{-m}\sigma e^j_l)) \]
2436: 			the uniserial modules at the vertex $\sigma \phi^{-m} \sigma e^i_k$ and $\sigma \phi^{-m} \sigma e^j_l$ which are annihilated by left multiplication by the arrows associated to $\phi_i^{-m} \sigma \cdot e^i_k$ and $\phi_i^{-m} \sigma \cdot e^j_l$ (so $a:ta=\sigma \phi^{-m} \sigma \cdot e^i_k, b:tb=\sigma \phi^{-m} \sigma \cdot e^j_l \in Q_1$). Note, there is exactly one such uniserial module for each orbit of the arrows $a:ta=\phi^{-m-1} e^i_k, b:tb=\phi^{-m-1} e^j_l \in Q_1$ by definition of the relations $I$. 
2437: 			
2438: 			
2439: 			
2440: 			
2441: 			
2442: 			%From this we can describe all Cohen-Macaulay modules over $\Lambda$. Need to write this up in a later Proposition.
2443: 			%		\item There is a grading of projective resolutions of simples with respect to the grading by path length. 
2444: 			%		\item With respect to the grading of $\Lambda$ by path length in the corresponding quiver, the path algebras of the gentle constellation orders are Koszul.
2445: 			%		\item The Koszul dual $\Lambda^*$ is the opposite algebra of the geometric dual constellation algebra, so the genus of $\Lambda$ is equal to the genus of $\Lambda^*$. 
2446: 			%		\item At Jerzy's suggestion, we should be able to define a "generalized Euler matrix" as follows. First, recall the Cartan matrix jast has entries $C_{ij}= \dim_k \Hom_{\Lambda}(P_j,P_i)$, but since this is infinite dimensional, we will need something different. So, we let the entries be power series in $\Z[[t]]$ determined by the lengths of the paths from $i$ to $j$. In other words
2447: 			%		\[ C_{ij} = \sum_n \dim_k (e_i \Lambda e_j)_n t^n \]
2448: 			%		are Poincare series in $Z[[t]]$. Now, if we let $E=(C^{-1})^T$ be the transpose of the inverse of $C$ (as in Assem, Simson, Skowronski pg. 90), which should work so long as we say $t \neq 0$. and allow rational functions in $t$.
2449: 			
2450: 		\end{enumerate}
2451: 	\end{theorem}
2452: 	
2453: 	
2454: 	
2455: 	
2456: 	
2457: 	The point of the first four statements is to describe the structure of resolutions and the Cohen-Macaulay modules. This will be useful later when explaining how to recover a graph embedded in a Riemann surface entirely in terms of the projective resolutions of the simple modules. The last four statements will provide some invariants which will have some applications to constellations and graphs embedded in Riemann surface. For example, a constellation is self dual if and only if the opposite algebra of the Koszul dual is isomorphic to the algebra $\Lambda(C)$. We will prove this and other things a little later.
2458: 	
2459: 	
2460: 	
2461: 	
2462: 	
2463: 	
2464: 	\begin{proof}
2465: 		\begin{enumerate}
2466: 			\item First, $\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l} = (e^i_k, e^j_l)=e$, and with fixed labeling of the edges of $\Gamma(C)$, we have $e^i_k = \sigma_i^{k-1}\cdot e^i_1$, and $e^j_l = \sigma_j^{l-1} \cdot e_l^j$, given by the automorphism 
2467: 			\[ \sigma_i^k:=
2468: 			\begin{pmatrix}
2469: 				0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & \sigma_i \\
2470: 				1 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 0 \\
2471: 				\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\
2472: 				0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 0 \\
2473: 				0 & 0 & \cdots & 1 & 0 \\
2474: 			\end{pmatrix}^k
2475: 			\]
2476: 			So, $\sigma_i$ acts on the algebra $\Lambda = kQ/I$ by left multiplication of $e^i_{j-1}$ (and therefore $a^i_{j-1}$) by 
2477: 			the arrow $\sigma_i a^i_{j-1}=a^i_j$ in the quiver $Q(\Lambda)$. Notice, this multiplication is always 
2478: 			nonzero since $\sigma_i = (e_1^i, e_2^i, ..., e_{n_i}^i)$ is a cyclic permutation around the vertex it corresponds to 
2479: 			in $\Gamma(C)$, and there is by definition a unique arrow by which $\sigma$ acts on a given idempotent $e^i_{j-1}
2480: 			$ 
2481: 			(and on $a^i_{j-1}$) lying on this cycle corresponding to the hereditary order $\HH_i$ in the pullback diagram 
2482: 			defining $\Lambda(C)$. 
2483: 			\item Let $P(\alpha^{k,l}_{i,j})$ be the projective cover of $S(\alpha^{k,l}_{i,j})$. From the desription of the radical of 
2484: 			$P(\alpha^{k,l}_{i,j})$ as the two uniserial module in $\HH$ corresponding to the idempotents $\sigma \cdot e^i_k$ 
2485: 			and $\sigma \cdot e^j_l$ in $\HH_i$ and $\HH_j$ respectively, the next term in the resolution is the direct sum of 
2486: 			the two indecomposable projective covers $P(\sigma \cdot e^i_k)$ and $P(\sigma \cdot e^j_l)$ in $\Mod(\Lambda)
2487: 			$. Clearly the kernel of the covering $P(\sigma \cdot e^j_l) \to U(\sigma \cdot e^j_l)$ is exactly the uniserial 
2488: 			$U(\sigma \alpha \cdot \sigma \cdot e^j_l)$. Now, $\sigma \alpha = \phi^{-1}$ by defintion of a constellation, so 
2489: 			$U(\sigma \alpha \cdot \sigma \cdot e^j_l) = U(\phi^{-1} \cdot \sigma \cdot e^j_l)$, and its projective cover is 
2490: 			$P(\phi^{-1} \cdot \sigma \cdot e^j_l)$. The kernel of this covering is $U(\sigma \alpha \cdot (\phi^{-1} \cdot 
2491: 			\sigma \cdot e^j_l)) = U(\phi^{-1}\sigma e^i_k)$. This pattern continues also for $\sigma \ell^j_l$, and the terms 
2492: 			$P^m$ in the resolution are
2493: 			\[ P(\phi^{-m} \sigma e^i_k) \oplus P(\phi^{-m}\sigma e^j_l). \]
2494: 			So the terms have indecomposable direct summands which cycle through the orbit of $e^i_k$ and $e^j_l$ under the 
2495: 			action of $\phi$. The orbits are anit-cycle in $I$, and the place at which the two cycle meet up at $\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l} 
2496: 			= (e^i_k, e^j_l)$ is exactly $p = \lcm\{|\cO_{\phi}(e^i_k)|, |\cO_{\phi}(e^j_l)|$.
2497: 			\item Since the kernel of the cover of a uniserial $P(\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l}) \to U(e^i_k)$ is exactly $U(\sigma e^i_k)$ and 
2498: 			it is embedded in $P(\alpha_{i,j}^{k,l})$ as a submodule via multiplication by the arrow $a: ha=\sigma e^i_k$, we 
2499: 			get that the differential is indeed, 
2500: 			\[ d^m:= \begin{pmatrix}
2501: 				a(\phi^{-m}\cdot e^i_k)  & 0 \\
2502: 				0 & a(\phi^{-m} \cdot e^j_l)
2503: 			\end{pmatrix}. \]
2504: 			\item 
2505: 			\item 
2506: 			\item 
2507: 			\item
2508: 			\item 
2509: 		\end{enumerate}
2510: 	\end{proof}
2511: 	
2512: 	
2513: 	
2514: 	
2515: 	
2516: 	\begin{ex}
2517: 		Let $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ be given by 
2518: 		\[ \sigma = (1,4)(2,3), \quad \alpha = (1,3)(2,4),\quad  \phi = (1,2)(3,4),\] then $\chi(C) = 2$ and $g(C)=0$. The embedded graph can be represented by the equator of the sphere with two vertices on it. Or, if we embed it in the plane:
2519: 		\[
2520: 		\begin{tikzcd}
2521: 			&  & \phi_2=(3,4) \\
2522: 			\sigma_2=(2,3) \arrow[rr, "{\alpha_1=(1,3)}", no head, bend left=49] & \phi_1=(1,2) & \sigma_1 = (1,4) \arrow[ll, "{\alpha_2=(2,4)}", no head, bend left=49]
2523: 		\end{tikzcd}
2524: 		\]
2525: 		The quiver which comes from this graph is\\
2526: 		\[
2527: 		\begin{tikzcd}
2528: 			\alpha_1=(1,3) \arrow["a_1", dd, bend left] \arrow["b_1", dd, dashed, bend right=71] \\
2529: 			\\
2530: 			\alpha_2=(2,4) \arrow["a_2",uu, bend left] \arrow["b_2", uu, dashed, bend right=71]
2531: 		\end{tikzcd}
2532: 		\]
2533: 		The associated matrix data is
2534: 		\[ \Lambda = \left\lbrace \begin{pmatrix}
2535: 			\lambda_{11} & x\cdot \lambda_{12} & 0 & 0 \\
2536: 			\lambda_{21} & \lambda_{22} & 0 & 0 \\
2537: 			0 & 0 & \lambda_{33} & x\cdot \lambda_{34} \\
2538: 			0 & 0 & \lambda_{43} & \lambda_{44} \\
2539: 		\end{pmatrix}\Bigg| \lambda_{ij} \in k[[x]], \lambda_{22} = \lambda_{33} \text{(mod $x$)} \right\rbrace \]
2540: 		With normalization
2541: 		\[ 
2542: 		\HH =  \left\lbrace \begin{pmatrix}
2543: 			\lambda_{11} & x\cdot \lambda_{12} \\
2544: 			\lambda_{21} & \lambda_{22} 
2545: 		\end{pmatrix} \times \begin{pmatrix}
2546: 			\mu_{11} & x\cdot \mu_{12} \\
2547: 			\mu_{21} & \mu_{22} 
2548: 		\end{pmatrix}\Bigg| \ \lambda_{ij}, \mu_{kl} \in k[[x]] \right\rbrace
2549: 		\]
2550: 		and the pullback diagram is given by the relation $(1,2) \sim (2,1)$, $\overline{m}=(2,2)$. The projective resolution of the simple $S(\alpha_1)$ has the following form
2551: 		\[ \xymatrix{
2552: 			\cdots \ar[r] &  \ar[r]^{\begin{pmatrix}
2553: 					a_1 & 0 \\
2554: 					0 & b_1
2555: 			\end{pmatrix}} P(\alpha_2) \oplus P(\alpha_2) & \ar[r]^{\begin{pmatrix}
2556: 					a_2 & 0 \\
2557: 					0 & b_2
2558: 			\end{pmatrix}} P(\alpha_1) \oplus P(\alpha_1)& \ar[r]^{\begin{pmatrix}
2559: 					a_1 & 0 \\
2560: 					0 & b_1
2561: 			\end{pmatrix}} P(\alpha_2) \oplus P(\alpha_2) & P(\alpha_1) \ar[r] & S(\alpha_1)
2562: 		}\]
2563: 	\end{ex}
2564: 	
2565: 	
2566: 	%\begin{ex}
2567: 	%	Let $\Lambda$ be the $\fm$-adic completion of the following path algebra with respect to the maximal ideal $\fm$ generated by the arrows:
2568: 	%	\[
2569: 	%	\begin{tikzcd}
2570: 		%	& e_1 \arrow[rd, "b_1"] \arrow[dd, "c_1" description, dotted, bend right=100] &  \\
2571: 		%	e_4 \arrow[ru, "b_4"] \arrow[rr, "a_2" description, dashed, bend right] &  & e_2 \arrow[ll, "a_1" description, dashed, bend right] \arrow[ld, "b_2"] \\
