2579: 	
2580: 	\section{Recovering the Surface}
2581: 	Give the list of projective resolutions of the simple objects, we may completely recover the constellation $C$, and therefore the Riemann surface and cellularly embedded graph. In fact, we don't need any other information. Neither the surface algebra, nor the medial quiver are needed to recover $C = [\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$, only the projective resolutions of the simples. 
2582: 	
2583: 	
2584: 	
2585: 	
2586: 	\section{The Graded Hilbert Function}
2587: %-----------------------------------------------------------------
2588: %  Added content: Graded Hilbert function / Hilbert series viewpoint
2589: %-----------------------------------------------------------------
2590: 
2591: \noindent
2592: In many parts of this manuscript we work with \emph{completed} surface algebras/orders (completion at the arrow ideal, or at a maximal ideal $\mathfrak m$ in a DVR).
2593: Strictly speaking, a completion is \emph{filtered} rather than graded.
2594: Nevertheless, for all of the path-counting and determinantal arguments (Cartan determinants, cycle zeta functions, and eventually the Hilbert--Pólya determinant convergence),
2595: the relevant object is the \emph{associated graded} algebra/module, which is graded by path length (or by a chosen arrow-weight grading).
2596: This is the correct setting for Hilbert functions and Hilbert series.
2597: 
2598: \subsection{Path-length grading and the Hilbert series of an algebra/module}
2599: Let $k$ be a field and let $A=kQ/I$ be a bound quiver algebra.
2600: Write $J\subset A$ for the arrow ideal (the ideal generated by the images of arrows).
2601: Then $A$ is naturally \emph{filtered} by the powers $J^n$.
2602: The associated graded algebra is
2603: \[
2604: \gr_J(A)\ :=\ \bigoplus_{n\ge 0} J^n/J^{n+1}.
2605: \]
2606: When $I$ is homogeneous for the path-length grading (true for the gentle/surface families where $I$ is generated by length-$2$ relations),
2607: $\gr_J(A)$ agrees with the usual path-length graded quotient.
2608: In either case, the graded pieces measure the number of admissible paths of a given length.
2609: 
2610: \begin{definition}[Hilbert function and Hilbert series]
2611: Let $M=\bigoplus_{n\ge 0} M_n$ be a $\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$-graded $k$-vector space (e.g.\ $M=\gr_J(A)$ or a graded $A$-module).
2612: The \emph{Hilbert function} is
2613: \[
2614: H_M(n)\ :=\ \dim_k(M_n),
2615: \]
2616: and the \emph{Hilbert series} is the formal power series
2617: \[
2618: \Hilb_M(q)\ :=\ \sum_{n\ge 0} H_M(n)\,q^n\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}[[q]].
2619: \]
2620: \end{definition}
2621: 
2622: \begin{remark}[Why Hilbert series are the right ``partition functions'' here]
2623: If one interprets path length as an energy/complexity (as we do later in the transfer-operator and modular-Hamiltonian constructions),
2624: then $\Hilb_M(q)$ is literally a discrete partition function with fugacity $q$.
2625: Replacing $q$ by $e^{-s}$ or by $p^{-s}$ is exactly the analytic continuation move that connects path-counting to Dirichlet series and Euler products.
2626: \end{remark}
2627: 
2628: \subsection{Matrix-valued Hilbert series and the $q$-Cartan matrix}
2629: In noncommutative settings with many primitive idempotents, it is often more informative to keep track of \emph{where} paths start and end.
2630: 
2631: Let $Q_0=\{1,\dots,n\}$ and let $\{e_i\}_{i\in Q_0}$ be the primitive orthogonal idempotents of $A$.
2632: Then $A$ decomposes as a matrix of bimodules:
2633: \[
2634: A\ \cong\ \bigoplus_{i,j\in Q_0} e_i A e_j.
2635: \]
2636: If $A$ is graded (or replaced by $\gr_J(A)$), each $e_iAe_j$ is graded.
2637: 
2638: \begin{definition}[$q$-Cartan matrix / matrix Hilbert series]
2639: The \emph{$q$-Cartan matrix} of a graded bound quiver algebra $A$ is the $n\times n$ matrix
2640: \[
2641: C_A(q)\ :=\ \bigl(C_{ij}(q)\bigr)_{i,j\in Q_0},
2642: \qquad
2643: C_{ij}(q)\ :=\ \sum_{m\ge 0} \dim_k\!\bigl((e_iAe_j)_m\bigr)\,q^m.
2644: \]
2645: Equivalently, $C_{ij}(q)$ is the generating function counting admissible paths from $j$ to $i$ by length.
2646: \end{definition}
2647: 
2648: \begin{remark}[Recovering the ordinary Cartan matrix]
2649: If $A$ is finite-dimensional and one forgets the grading, then the ordinary Cartan matrix $C_A$ is recovered by evaluation at $q=1$:
2650: \[
2651: (C_A)_{ij}=\dim_k(e_iAe_j)=C_{ij}(1).
2652: \]
2653: In general, $C_A(q)$ is defined as a formal power series matrix (or a rational function matrix) even when $A$ is infinite-dimensional.
2654: \end{remark}
2655: 
2656: \subsection{Scalar Hilbert series from the $q$-Cartan matrix}
2657: The Hilbert series of the whole algebra is the sum of all matrix entries:
2658: \[
2659: \Hilb_A(q)\ =\ \sum_{m\ge 0}\dim_k(A_m)\,q^m
2660: \ =\ \sum_{i,j\in Q_0} C_{ij}(q)
2661: \ =\ \mathbf{1}^\top C_A(q)\,\mathbf{1},
2662: \]
2663: where $\mathbf{1}$ denotes the all-ones column vector.
2664: More generally, the Hilbert series of an indecomposable projective $P_j=e_jA$ is
2665: \[
2666: \Hilb_{P_j}(q)\ =\ \sum_{i\in Q_0} C_{ij}(q).
2667: \]
2668: 
2669: \subsection{Rationality for gentle/surface families}
2670: For gentle and locally gentle algebras, admissible paths form a regular language, and the corresponding generating functions are rational.
2671: This rationality is made \emph{completely explicit} in terms of oriented cycles by the Bessenrodt--Holm determinant formula for weighted (and hence graded) Cartan matrices
2672: (Theorem~\ref{thm:BH_weighted_cartan_det}, and Corollary~\ref{cor:qcartan_det_simple} below in the specialized $q$-grading).
2673: In later chapters this is the key mechanism that lets us treat ``cycle sums'' and ``Euler products'' inside a single determinantal package.
2674: 
2675: 
2676: 	
2677: 	
2678: 	
2679: 	
2680: 	
2681: 	
2682: 	
2683: 	\section{The Generalized Cartan Matrix}
2684: %-----------------------------------------------------------------
2685: %  Added content: generalized / weighted / graded Cartan matrices
2686: %-----------------------------------------------------------------
2687: 
2688: \noindent
2689: The Cartan matrix is the fundamental ``path-counting'' invariant of a bound quiver algebra, and in the surface-order setting it is the bridge between:
2690: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
2691: \item combinatorics of permitted/forbidden cycles on the embedded graph,
2692: \item representation theory (projectives, simples, and resolutions), and
2693: \item determinants that factor over cycles (zeta/Euler product analogies).
2694: \end{enumerate}
2695: 
2696: \subsection{Ordinary Cartan matrix and composition multiplicities}
2697: Let $A$ be a finite-dimensional $k$-algebra with primitive orthogonal idempotents $\{e_i\}_{i\in Q_0}$ and indecomposable projectives $P_i=e_iA$.
2698: Let $S_i=P_i/\rad(P_i)$ be the corresponding simple modules.
2699: 
2700: \begin{definition}[Cartan matrix]
2701: The (ordinary) \emph{Cartan matrix} of $A$ is
2702: \[
2703: C_A\ :=\ \bigl(c_{ij}\bigr)_{i,j\in Q_0},
2704: \qquad
2705: c_{ij}\ :=\ [P_j:S_i],
2706: \]
2707: the multiplicity of $S_i$ as a composition factor of $P_j$.
2708: Equivalently,
2709: \[
2710: c_{ij}=\dim_k\Hom_A(P_i,P_j)=\dim_k(e_iAe_j).
2711: \]
2712: \end{definition}
2713: 
2714: In a bound-quiver presentation $A=kQ/I$, the entry $c_{ij}$ is literally the number of admissible paths from $j$ to $i$ (counted with multiplicity in case of relations), because $e_iAe_j$ is spanned by paths from $j$ to $i$ that survive modulo~$I$.
2715: 
2716: \subsection{Graded (or filtered) refinements: the $q$-Cartan matrix}
2717: When $A$ is graded (or replaced by $\gr_J(A)$), one refines the Cartan matrix to keep track of the grading.
2718: 
2719: \begin{definition}[$q$-Cartan matrix]
2720: For a graded algebra $A=\bigoplus_{m\ge 0}A_m$ with idempotents $e_i\in A_0$, define
2721: \[
2722: C_A(q)\ :=\ \bigl(C_{ij}(q)\bigr)_{i,j},
2723: \qquad
2724: C_{ij}(q)=\sum_{m\ge 0}\dim_k\!\bigl((e_iAe_j)_m\bigr)\,q^m.
2725: \]
2726: This is the same object defined in the previous section, viewed now as a \emph{graded Cartan matrix}.
2727: \end{definition}
2728: 
2729: \begin{remark}[Why this deserves to be called ``generalized'' in the surface-order setting]
2730: If one works with completed surface orders over a DVR $R$ with uniformizer $\pi$, then the arrow-ideal filtration and the $\pi$-adic filtration are intertwined.
2731: One therefore obtains \emph{multi-graded} Cartan data, e.g.\ a matrix of formal power series in $q$ (path length) and $\pi$ (valuation/ramification depth).
2732: For the present manuscript we mostly keep a single grading variable and treat the rest as parameters inside the weights $w(\alpha)$ of arrows.
2733: \end{remark}
2734: 
2735: \subsection{Weighted Cartan matrices and the cycle-product determinant}
2736: The key combinatorial input for our later determinant/trace identities is the explicit cycle factorization of $\det C_A(q)$ for locally gentle algebras.
2737: 
2738: To make the specialization transparent, recall the Bessenrodt--Holm formula (Theorem~\ref{thm:BH_weighted_cartan_det}):
2739: for a weighted locally gentle quiver $(Q,I,w)$,
2740: \[
2741: \det C_Q^w(x)
2742: =
2743: \frac{\prod_{C\in \mathrm{ZC}(Q)} \Bigl(1-(-1)^{\ell(C)}\,w(C)\Bigr)}
2744: {\prod_{C\in \mathrm{IC}(Q)} \Bigl(1-w(C)\Bigr)}.
2745: \]
2746: Setting all arrow weights equal to $q$ (path-length grading) yields the following useful specialization.
2747: 
2748: \begin{corollary}[$q$-Cartan determinant for locally gentle algebras \cite{BessenrodtHolm2005WeightedLocallyGentle}]
2749: \label{cor:qcartan_det_simple}
2750: Let $(Q,I)$ be a locally gentle quiver and $A=kQ/I$ equipped with the path-length grading.
2751: Then
2752: \begin{equation}
2753: \label{eq:qcartan_det_simple}
2754: \det C_A(q)
2755: =
2756: \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{C\in \mathrm{ZC}(Q)} \Bigl(1-(-q)^{\ell(C)}\Bigr)}
2757: {\displaystyle\prod_{C\in \mathrm{IC}(Q)} \Bigl(1-q^{\ell(C)}\Bigr)}.
2758: \end{equation}
2759: \end{corollary}
2760: 
2761: \begin{remark}[Determinants, Hilbert series, and cycle zeta functions]
2762: Equation~\eqref{eq:qcartan_det_simple} is the algebraic version of a dynamical zeta function:
2763: a determinant becomes a product over primitive oriented cycles.
2764: In later chapters we use exactly this structural fact when matching:
2765: \[
2766: \log\det(I-T)\ =\ -\sum_{n\ge 1}\frac{1}{n}\Tr(T^n)
2767: \qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad
2768: \log L(s,\varpi)\ =\ \sum_{p,k\ge 1} \frac{a_\varpi(p^k)}{k}\,p^{-ks}.
2769: \]
2770: \end{remark}
2771: 
2772: 
2773: 	
2774: 	
2775: 	
2776: 	
2777: 	
2778: 	\section{The Generalized Euler Form}
2779: %-----------------------------------------------------------------
2780: %  Added content: Euler form inner product and its relation to Cartan/Hilbert series
2781: %-----------------------------------------------------------------
2782: 
2783: \noindent
2784: The Euler form is the bilinear pairing that controls both:
2785: (i) the weights of quiver semi-invariants (Schofield determinants), and
2786: (ii) the inversion relation between Cartan data and Ext data.
2787: Because the manuscript uses both \emph{dimension vectors} (representation theory) and \emph{operator traces} (physics),
2788: it is worth writing the Euler form carefully and in a form that can be used computationally.
2789: 
2790: \subsection{Euler form via Ext groups (derived-category definition)}
2791: Let $A$ be a finite-dimensional $k$-algebra.
2792: For $A$-modules $M,N$ of finite projective dimension (e.g.\ when $A$ has finite global dimension, or when one restricts to suitable subcategories),
2793: define the \emph{Euler form}
2794: \begin{equation}
2795: \label{eq:euler_ext_definition}
2796: \langle M,N\rangle_A\ :=\ \sum_{t\ge 0}(-1)^t\,\dim_k \Ext_A^t(M,N).
2797: \end{equation}
2798: This depends only on the classes $[M],[N]$ in the Grothendieck group $K_0(A)$ and extends by bilinearity to a pairing
2799: \[
2800: \langle -,-\rangle_A:K_0(A)\times K_0(A)\to \mathbb{Z}.
2801: \]
2802: 
2803: \begin{remark}[When $A$ has infinite global dimension]
2804: Gentle and special-biserial algebras often have infinite global dimension.
2805: Nevertheless, the Euler form \eqref{eq:euler_ext_definition} is still meaningful on any full subcategory where the Ext groups are finite-dimensional and eventually vanish (e.g.\ perfect complexes, or modules admitting eventually periodic resolutions where one works with a graded/refined Euler series).
2806: For the Hilbert-series computations below we will primarily use the \emph{graded} (or $q$-)Euler form, which is defined as a formal series and does not require vanishing in finite degree.
2807: \end{remark}
2808: 
2809: \subsection{Euler matrix and the inverse Cartan relation (finite global dimension case)}
2810: Assume for the moment that $A$ has finite global dimension.
2811: Let $\{S_i\}_{i\in Q_0}$ be the simple modules and $\{P_i\}_{i\in Q_0}$ the indecomposable projectives.
2812: Write $C_A$ for the Cartan matrix.
2813: Define the Euler matrix $E_A$ by
2814: \[
2815: (E_A)_{ij}\ :=\ \langle S_i,S_j\rangle_A.
2816: \]
2817: Then one has the classical relation
2818: \begin{equation}
2819: \label{eq:euler_is_inverse_cartan}
2820: E_A\ =\ (C_A^{-1})^\top,
2821: \qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad
2822: \langle S_i,S_j\rangle_A\ =\ (C_A^{-1})_{ji}.
2823: \end{equation}
2824: Equivalently, in the $K_0$-basis of projectives one has $\langle P_i,S_j\rangle_A=\delta_{ij}$.
2825: 
2826: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
2827: Take a finite projective resolution of $S_i$,
2828: $0\to P_m\to\cdots\to P_0\to S_i\to 0$, apply $\Hom_A(-,S_j)$, and take alternating sums of dimensions.
2829: In matrix form this expresses the identity matrix as the product of the Cartan matrix with the Euler matrix, which is \eqref{eq:euler_is_inverse_cartan}.
2830: \end{proof}
2831: 
2832: \subsection{Bound quivers and an explicit dimension-vector formula}
2833: When $A=kQ/I$ is given by a bound quiver presentation with relations generated by paths of length~$2$ (the gentle/surface regime),
2834: the Euler form can be written directly on dimension vectors.
2835: Let $\mathbf{d}=(d_i)_{i\in Q_0}$ and $\mathbf{e}=(e_i)_{i\in Q_0}$ be dimension vectors.
2836: 
2837: \begin{definition}[Combinatorial Euler form for a bound quiver]
2838: Assume $(Q,I)$ has relations generated by a set $R$ of minimal relations, each a linear combination of paths with fixed start and end.
2839: Define the bilinear form
2840: \begin{equation}
2841: \label{eq:euler_bound_quiver}
2842: \langle \mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}\rangle_{(Q,I)}
2843: \ :=\
2844: \sum_{i\in Q_0} d_i e_i
2845: \;-\;
2846: \sum_{a:i\to j\in Q_1} d_i e_j
2847: \;+\;
2848: \sum_{r:i\Rightarrow j\in R} d_i e_j,
2849: \end{equation}
2850: where $r:i\Rightarrow j$ means that the relation $r$ starts at $i$ and ends at $j$.
2851: \end{definition}
2852: 
2853: \begin{remark}[Interpretation]
2854: For quivers without relations ($I=0$) this reduces to the usual Euler/Ringel form
2855: $\langle\mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}\rangle=\sum_i d_i e_i-\sum_{a:i\to j} d_i e_j$.
2856: The extra $+\sum_{r} d_i e_j$ term is the first correction in the presence of relations, and is exact when the algebra has global dimension~$\le 2$ (which covers many gentle cases of interest).
2857: More generally, higher syzygies contribute further correction terms; in the graded setting one keeps track of them via the $q$-Euler series below.
2858: \end{remark}
2859: 
2860: \subsection{Graded/$q$-Euler form and inversion of the $q$-Cartan matrix}
2861: For Koszul graded algebras (and in particular for locally gentle algebras with the natural path-length grading \cite{BessenrodtHolm2005WeightedLocallyGentle}),
2862: one can package the entire Ext tower into a generating series.
2863: 
2864: \begin{definition}[$q$-Euler matrix]
2865: Let $A$ be a graded algebra and let $S_i$ be the graded simples.
2866: Define the $q$-Euler matrix
2867: \[
2868: E_A(q)\ :=\ \bigl(E_{ij}(q)\bigr)_{i,j},
2869: \qquad
2870: E_{ij}(q):=\sum_{t\ge 0}(-1)^t\,\dim_k\Ext_A^t(S_i,S_j)\,q^t.
2871: \]
2872: \end{definition}
2873: 
2874: When $A$ is Koszul, one has the standard Koszul inversion relation
2875: \begin{equation}
2876: \label{eq:q_euler_cartan_inverse}
2877: C_A(q)\,E_A(-q)^\top\ =\ I,
2878: \qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
2879: E_A(q)\ =\bigl(C_A(-q)^{-1}\bigr)^\top.
2880: \end{equation}
2881: Thus the $q$-Cartan matrix is the matrix Hilbert series of projectives, while the $q$-Euler matrix is the matrix Poincar\'e series of Ext between simples.
2882: 
2883: \begin{remark}[Hilbert series from Euler form computations]
2884: Equation~\eqref{eq:q_euler_cartan_inverse} is precisely the computational principle requested in the ``expanded'' instructions:
2885: \emph{once the Euler form/Ext series is known (e.g.\ from explicit projective resolutions of simples), one can recover the graded Cartan data and hence the Hilbert series by matrix inversion, and conversely.}
2886: In the gentle/surface setting, explicit periodic resolutions make $E_A(q)$ rational, hence $C_A(q)$ and $\Hilb_A(q)$ are rational as well.
2887: \end{remark}
2888: 
2889: \subsection{Euler form and weights of determinantal semi-invariants}
2890: Finally, we record the Euler-form identity that explains why the pairing is indispensable in the invariant theory used later.
2891: 
2892: Let $A=kQ/I$ and fix a dimension vector $\mathbf{d}$.
2893: For any $A$-module $M$ of dimension vector $\mathbf{m}$, the Schofield/Derksen--Weyman theory associates a character (weight)
2894: \[
2895: \theta_M\in \mathbb{Z}^{Q_0}\cong X^\ast(G_\mathbf{d}),
2896: \qquad
2897: \theta_M(i):=\langle \mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_i\rangle_{(Q,I)},
2898: \]
2899: where $\mathbf{e}_i$ is the $i$th standard basis vector.
2900: The dimension-match condition for the determinantal semi-invariant $c^M$ (Definition~\ref{def:det_semi_inv_projective}) is exactly the vanishing of the Euler pairing
2901: \[
2902: \langle \mathbf{m},\mathbf{d}\rangle_{(Q,I)}=0,
2903: \]
2904: and the resulting determinant $c^M$ lies in the weight space $\SI(A,\mathbf{d})_{\theta_M}$.
2905: This is the conceptual reason that the Euler form sits at the intersection of:
2906: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
2907: \item determinantal invariant theory (semi-invariants as determinants),
2908: \item toric degeneration (weights become lattice points), and
2909: \item the arithmetic transfer-operator model (weights become prime-power phases).
2910: \end{enumerate}
2911:

2922: 	\chapter{Examples: Double Simple Cycle Algebras}
2923: 	Although it is not phrased in these terms, in \cite{Kraskiewicz-Weyman} Kraskiewicz and Weyman study a class of finite dimensional gentle algebras $A(n)$ given by the following pullback diagram with $\mathbb{A}_n$ the equioriented type $\mathbb{A}$ quiver with $|Q_0|=n$. 
2924: 	\[ \xymatrix{
2925: 		A(n) \ar[rr]^{\pi_1} \ar[d]_{\iota_1} & & \ttop(A(n)) \ar[d]^{\iota_2} \\
2926: 		k\mathbb{A}_n \times k\mathbb{A}_n \ar[rr]_{\pi_2} & & \ttop (k\mathbb{A}_n \times k\mathbb{A}_n)
2927: 	} \]
2928: 	The maps $\pi_1, \pi_2$ are the projections onto $\ttop(A(n))= A(n)/\rad(A(n)) = \bigoplus_{i=1}^n P(i)/\rad(P(i)) \cong \prod_{i=1}^n k$, $\ttop(k\mathbb{A}_n \times k\mathbb{A}_n) = k\mathbb{A}_n/\rad(k\mathbb{A}_n) \times k\mathbb{A}_n/\rad(k\mathbb{A}_n) \cong \prod_{i=1}^n k \times \prod_{i=1}^n k$. The maps $\iota_1, \iota_2$ are idempotent embeddings given by $e_i \mapsto e_i'+e_i''$, where $e_i$ is the $i^{th}$ primitive idempotent of the algebra $A(n)$, $e_i'$ is the primitive idempotent in the first copy of $k\mathbb{A}_n$ corresponding to the vertex $i \in \mathbb{A}_{n,0}$, and $e_i''$ is the $i^{th}$ idempotent of the second copy. We would like to study a class of algebras closely related to these algebras. Let $\tilde{\mathbb{A}}_n$ be the cyclicly oriented quiver with $n$ arrows and vertices. For $n=0$ (mod $2$), let $\Lambda(n)$ be given by the matrix algebra
2929: 	\[
2930: 	\Lambda(n): =  \begin{pmatrix}
2931: 		\lambda_{11} & (x)\lambda_{12} & \cdots & (x)\lambda_{1,n-1} & (x)\lambda_{1,n}\\
2932: 		\lambda_{11} & \lambda_{12} & \cdots & (x)\lambda_{1,n-1} & (x)\lambda_{1,n}\\
2933: 		\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\
2934: 		\lambda_{n-1,1} & \lambda_{n-1,2} & \cdots & \lambda_{1,n-1} & (x)\lambda_{1,n}\\
2935: 		\lambda_{n1} & \lambda_{n2} & \cdots & \lambda_{n,n-1} & \lambda_{n,n}
2936: 	\end{pmatrix} \times 
2937: 	\begin{pmatrix}
2938: 		\mu_{11} & (x)\mu_{12} & \cdots & (x)\mu_{1,n-1} & (x)\mu_{1,n}\\
2939: 		\mu_{11} & \mu_{12} & \cdots & (x)\mu_{1,n-1} & (x)\mu_{1,n}\\
2940: 		\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\
2941: 		\mu_{n-1,1} & \mu_{n-1,2} & \cdots & \mu_{1,n-1} & (x)\mu_{1,n}\\
2942: 		\mu_{n1} & \mu_{n2} & \cdots & \mu_{n,n-1} & \mu_{n,n}
2943: 	\end{pmatrix} ,\]
2944: 	where $\lambda_{ij}, \mu_{ij} \in k[[x]], \lambda_{ii}=\mu_{ii}$ (mod $x$), which is given by the pullback diagram
2945: 	\[
2946: 	\xymatrix{

2948: 		\bH(n) \ar[r]_{\pi_2} & \bH(n)/\rad(\bH(n)) 
2949: 	}

2955: 		\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\

2965: 	\end{pmatrix} ,\]
2966: 	with $\lambda_{ij}, \mu_{ij} \in k[[x]]$. 
2967: 	Then, $\rad(\bH(n)) = \rad(\Lambda(n))$, and $\iota_1$ and $\iota_2$ are given by radical embeddings with mapping on the idempotents:
2968: 	\[ \Lambda(n)/\rad(\Lambda(n)) \ni e_{ii} \mapsto e_{ii}'+e_{ii}'' \in \bH(n)/\rad(\bH(n)). \]

2980: 	\[ \bH(n) = \begin{pmatrix}
2981: 		\lambda_{11} & (x)\lambda_{12} & \cdots & (x)\lambda_{1,n-1} & (x)\lambda_{1,n}\\
2982: 		\lambda_{11} & \lambda_{12} & \cdots & (x)\lambda_{1,n-1} & (x)\lambda_{1,n}\\

2987: 	We call $\bH(n)$ the \textbf{normalization} of the order $\Lambda(n)$. We will call 
2988: 	$\Lambda(n)$ a \textbf{double simple cycle order}. In general, we may replace $k[[x]]

2997: 		& 5 \arrow[lu, no head, dotted, bend left] & 4 \arrow[l, bend left] \arrow[l, dashed] & 
2998: 	\end{tikzcd}
2999: 	\]
3000: 	where the relations $I=\la a_{i+1}a_i, b_{i+1}b_i \ra_{i =1}^n$ is given by composition of two arrows of the same color. We then have a pullback diagram
3001: 	\[

3014: 	\subsection{The Riemann Surfaces}
3015: 	Let $\tilde{A}(n) = kQ/I$ be a double simple cycle algebra of length $n$. Then $I = \la a_{i+1}a_i, b_{i+1}b_i \ra_{i 
3016: 		\in \ZZ/n\ZZ}$. In particular, there are two cycles, up to cyclic permutation, "$R$" and "$B$" of length $n$, such that 
3017: 	any two consecutive arrows lies in $I$, and there is a single unique non-zero cycle (not in $I$) of length $2n$, 
3018: 	\[ C = (a_1b_2a_3b_4 \cdots b_1a_2b_3a_4b_5 \cdots a_{n-1}b_n) \] 
3019: 	up to a cyclic permutation of the arrows if $n$ is even, and there are two
3020: 	\[ C_1 = (a_1b_2a_3 \cdots a_{n-1}b_n), \quad C_2 = (b_1a_2b_3 \cdots b_{n-1}a_n) \]
3021: 	if $n$ is odd. Let $\varphi = \varphi_R \varphi_B \in S_{Q_1}$ be two cyclic permutations in the permutation group $S_{Q_1} \cong S_{2n}$. Let $\varphi_R = (a_1,a_2,...,a_n) \mapsto  (1,2,...,n)$ and $\varphi_B = (b_1, b_2, ..., b_n) \mapsto (n+1, n+2, ..., 2n)$ given by identifying $Q_1$ with $[2n]$ by fixing a labeling of the arrows. Let 
3022: 	\[
3023: 	\sigma =
3024: 	\begin{cases}
3025: 		(a_1b_2a_3b_4 \cdots b_1a_2b_3a_4b_5 \cdots a_{n-1}b_n) \quad \text{if} \ |Q_0|=2k+1 \\
3026: 		(a_1,b_2,a_3,..., b_{n-2},a_{n-1},b_n)(b_1,a_2,b_3,...,a_{n-2}b_{n-1}a_n) \quad \text{if}\ |Q_0|=2k
3027: 	\end{cases}
3028: 	\]
3029: 	In the above cases we may identify, 
3030: 	\[ (a_1b_2a_3b_4 \cdots b_1a_2b_3a_4b_5 \cdots a_{n-1}b_n) \mapsto (1, n+1, 2, n+2, ..., n, 2n) \in S_{2n} \]
3031: 	and
3032: 	\begin{align*}
3033: 		(a_1,b_2,a_3,..., b_{n-2},a_{n-1},b_n)&(b_1,a_2,b_3,...,a_{n-2}b_{n-1}a_n) \\
3034: 		&\mapsto (1,n+2,3,...,n-1,2n)(n+1,2,n+3,...,2n-1,n) \in S_{2n}.
3035: 	\end{align*}
3036: 	
3037: 	for $k=0,1,2,...$; finally, let $\alpha = (1,n+1)(2,n+2) \cdots (n,2n)$. If we restrict $I$ to $R$ or $B$, we have an algebra $\Lambda_R = kR/I \cong k \mathbb{\tilde{A}}_n/\rad(k \mathbb{\tilde{A}}_n)^2 \cong kB/I = \Lambda_B$. We may then view $\alpha$ as the gluing that identifies $ta_i' \leftrightarrow tb_i'$, where $a_i' \in \Lambda_R$ and $b_i' \in \Lambda_B$ are arrows in the respective algebras. It is not hard to verify $\sigma \alpha = \varphi^{-1}$, and thus the triple $[\sigma, \alpha, \varphi]$ may be seen as a combinatorial embedding of a graph into a Riemann surface. This map $\Gamma(n) \hookrightarrow X$ has either one vertex if $n$ is even, or two vertices if $n$ is odd, corresponding to $\sigma$. It has two faces, corresponding to $\varphi_R$ and $\varphi_B$; it also has exactly $n=|Q_0|$ edges corresponding to $\alpha$. Thus we may compute the surface associated to the algebra $\tilde{A}(n)$ via
3038: 	\[ \chi(\tilde{A}(n)) = |\sigma|-|\alpha|+|\varphi| = \begin{cases}
3039: 		2-2k \quad \text{if} \ |Q_0|=n=2k+1 \\
3040: 		4-2k \quad \text{if} \ |Q_0| = n = 2k
3041: 	\end{cases}. \]
3042: 	for $k=0,1,2,...$, and $n \geq 1$, so that we have the genus function 
3043: 	\[ g(\tilde{A}(n)) = \begin{cases}
3044: 		k \quad \text{if} \ n=2k+1 \\
3045: 		k-1 \quad \text{if} \ n=2k
3046: 	\end{cases} \]
3047:

\chapter{Background on Representation Theory of General Linear Groups} · lines 3174–3268
3174: 		\chapter{Background on Representation Theory of General Linear Groups}
3175: 		
3176: 		
3177: 		\section{Young Tableaux and Schur Functors}\label{Young Tableaux}
3178: 		A \textbf{partition} $\lambda \vdash m$, of some nonnegative integer $m$ is a sequence of nonincreasing numbers $\lambda = (\lambda_1, ..., \lambda_s)$ such that $\sum_i \lambda_i = m$. We define a \textbf{Young diagram} corresponding to a partition $\lambda = (\lambda_1, ..., \lambda_s) \vdash m$, as the diagram with $\lambda_i$ boxes in the $i^{th}$ row. We identify partitions with their Young diagrams and speak of the two interchangeably. For a free module $E$ over a commutative ring $K$ with ordered basis $\{e_1, ..., e_n\}$, we associate a filling of the diagram $\lambda$ by integers $\{1, ..., n\}$ to an element in the module
3179: 		\[ \bigwedge^{\lambda_1}E \otimes \cdots \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_s}E \]
3180: 		as follows: If in row $i$ and box $j$ of $\lambda$ we have the integers $t(i, j)$ for $j=1, ..., \lambda_i$, we associate the element
3181: 		\[ e_{t(1,1)} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{t(1,\lambda_1)} \otimes  \cdots \otimes e_{t(s,1)} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{t(s, \lambda_s)}.\]
3182: 		We define such a filling to be a \textbf{tableaux}. A tableaux is \textbf{standard} if its rows are strictly increasing, and its columns are nondecreasing. 
3183: 		
3184: 		Fix a free module $E$ of rank $n$ over a commutative ring $K$. Let $\lambda = ( \lambda_1, ..., \lambda_s) \vdash m$ be a partition. We define the module
3185: 		\[ L_{\lambda}E = \bigwedge^{\lambda_1} \otimes \cdots \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_s}E/R(\lambda, E) \]
3186: 		where the submodule $R(\lambda, E)$ is the sum of all submodules of the form
3187: 		\[ \bigwedge^{\lambda_1}E \otimes \cdots \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_{a-1}}E \otimes R_{a, a+1}E \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_{a+2}}E \otimes \cdots \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_s}E \]
3188: 		for $1 \leq a \leq s-1$. Here $R_{a,a+1}E$ is the submodule spanned by the images of the maps $\theta(\lambda, a, u, v, E)$
3189: 		\[ \xymatrix{ \bigwedge^uE \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_a-u+\lambda_{a+1}-v}E \otimes \bigwedge^vE \ar[d]^{1 \otimes \Delta \otimes 1} \\
3190: 			\bigwedge^uE \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_a-u}E \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_{a+1}-v}E \bigwedge^vE \ar[d]^{m_{12} \otimes m_{34}} \\
3191: 			\bigwedge^{\lambda_a}E \otimes \bigwedge^{\lambda_{a+1}}E}.\]
3192: 		Here, $\Delta$ is the diagonal embedding (or comultiplication), and $m: \bigwedge^r E \otimes \bigwedge^s E \to \bigwedge^{r+s}E$ is a component of the multiplication map on the exterior algebra $\bigwedge^{\bullet}E$. Combinatorially, we may use the theory of Young tableaux to describe the modules $L_{\lambda}E$. It is well known the standard 
3193: 		tableaux form a basis of $L_{\lambda}E$. Further, the relations $R(\lambda, E)$ may be visualized as follows. Let $\lambda \vdash 
3194: 		m$ be a partition with at most $n$ parts. To the map $\theta(\lambda, a, u, v, E)$ we associate a \textbf{Young scheme}. The Young 
3195: 		scheme is defined as being a diagram of shape $\lambda$ with empty boxes everywhere except the $a^{th}$ and 
3196: 		$a+1^{st}$ rows. In row $a$ there are $u$ empty boxes followed by $\lambda_a-u$ filled boxes. In row $a+1$ there are 
3197: 		$\lambda_{a+1}-v$ filled boxes followed by $v$ empty boxes. We restrict to $u+v<\lambda_{a+1}$ as in the definition of 
3198: 		$L_{\lambda}E$. Let $U_j = e_{t(j,1)} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{t(j, \lambda_j)}$, $V_1 = e_{x_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{x_u}$, 
3199: 		$V_2 = e_{y_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{y_{\lambda_a-u+\lambda_v-v}}$, and $V_3 = e_{z_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{z_v}$. 
3200: 		Then the image of a typical element 
3201: 		\[ U_1 \otimes \cdots \otimes U_{a-1} \otimes V_1 \otimes V_2 \otimes V_3 \otimes U_{a+2} \otimes \cdots \otimes U_s \]
3202: 		is a sum of tableaux where we put $x_1, ..., x_u$ in the empty $u$ boxes in row $a$, and $z_1, ..., z_v$ in the empty $v$ boxes of row $a+1$, and we shuffle the elements $y_1, ..., y_{\lambda_a-u+\lambda_{a+1}-v}$ between the filled boxes in row $a$ and $a+1$. The coefficients of the tableaux in the summation are $\pm 1$ depending on the sign of the permutations coming from the exterior diagonals.\sidenote{From now on, we will let $L_{\lambda}F$ denote the Schur functor corresponding to the diagram $\lambda$, and we will let $S_{(\lambda-k)}F = L_{\lambda}F \otimes (\bigwedge^{\dim F}F^*)^{\otimes k}$, where $\lambda-k$ is obtained by subtracting the integer $k$ from every entry of the vector $\lambda$.}
3203: 		
3204: 		
3205: 		\section{Standard Monomials,The Straightening Law, and Determinantal Varieties}\label{Straightening Law 1}
3206: 		
3207: 		\subsection{Standard Monomials and Straightening Laws}\label{monomial orders}
3208: 		Let us now introduce the notion of a standard monomial theory. 
3209: 		
3210: 		\begin{defn}
3211: 			Let $R$ be a ring and let $A$ be a commutative $R$-algebra, and let $S:=\{s_1, ..., s_N\} \subset R$ have a partial order $\leq$. 
3212: 			\begin{enumerate}
3213: 				\item An ordered product $s_{i_1} \cdots s_{i_k}$ of elements from $S$ is said to be a \textbf{standard monomial} if the elements appear in nondecreasing order, with respect to the partial order $\leq$. 
3214: 				\item We say $A$ has a \textbf{standard monomial theory for $S$} if the standard monomials for a basis of $A$ over $R$.
3215: 				\item If $s, s' \in S$ are incomparable in the partial order $\leq$, we have a unique expression called a \textbf{straightening law}:
3216: 				\[ s \cdot s' = \sum_i c_iM_i, \quad c_i \in R, M_i \text{ standard}. \]
3217: 				If a monomial $s_{i_1} \cdots s_{i_k}$ is nonstandard we may replace it with a standard one as follows. If there is a product $s \cdot s'$ in $s_{i_1} \cdots s_{i_k}$ with $s > s'$ we replace if with $s' \cdot s$. If $s$ and $s'$ are incomparable, we replace $s \cdot s'$ with $s \cdot \left(\sum_i c_iM_i\right)$. 
3218: 				\item We say the $R$-algebra $A$ has a \textbf{straightening law} if the previous replacement algorithm always terminates after finitely many steps. 
3219: 			\end{enumerate}
3220: 		\end{defn}
3221: 		
3222: 		We define a \textbf{standard bi-tableau}\sidenote{An archetypal example of bi-tableaux and an algebra with straightening law and standard monomial theory is the algebra $\Sym(V \otimes W^*)$ for two vector spaces $V$ and $W$, or more generally, let $X = (x_{ij})$ be a matrix of indeterminates so that $X \in \Hom_K(E, F) = E^* \otimes F$, where $E$ and $F$ are two free $\ZZ$-modules. Define the ring $R = \ZZ[X] = \ZZ[x_{ij}]$, to be the polynomial ring in the indeterminates $(x_{ij})$, and let $\{e_1, ..., e_m\}$ and $\{f_1, ..., f_n\}$ be ordered bases of $E$ and $F$ respectively. There is a natural action of the group $G = \GL(E) \times \GL(F)$ on $R$ induced by the action on $X$ define by $(A, B) \cdot X = A^{-1}XB$. If $K$ is a field, the orbits in $\Hom_K(E,F)$ are classified by ranks, and the orbit closures of maps of rank $k$ say, are all maps of rank $\leq k$. The action of $G$ on $R$ is more complicated. If $E$ and $F$ are free $K$-modules for a field $K$, and $V_k$ is the orbit closure of rank $k$ maps, then the defining ideal of $V_k$ is $I_{k+1} = R(\bigwedge^{k+1} E^* \otimes \bigwedge^{k+1}F)$ generated by minors of size $k+1$, and the coordinate ring $R/I_{k+1}$ is normal and Cohen Macaulay. A classical characteristic free approach using the Schur functors allows one to define a basis of $\KK[E^* \otimes F]$ in terms of certain \emph{standard monomials} given by standard bi-tableaux.}, denoted $(s|t)$ as a pair of standard tableaux $s$ and $t$ of the same shape $\lambda$, where by convention we write $s$ in reverse order. To a bitableau we associate a product of minors of $X$, where the entries of row $a$ of $s$ give the columns and the entries of row $a$ of $t$ give the rows of the minor of $X$. Then each pair of rows in $s$ and $t$ defines a minor of size $\lambda_a$, and the bi-tableau is associated to the product of these minors. It is well known that the standard bi-tableaux with at most $\min\{\dim E, \dim F\}$ columns forms a $K$-free basis of $K[E^* \otimes F]$, and that the standard bi-tableaux with at most $k$ columns form a $K$-free basis of $R/I_{k+1}$. We call the associated minors to a standard bi-tableau a \emph{standard monomial} in $R$ or $R/I_{k+1}$. It can be shown that any nonstandard monomial can be written as a sum of standard monomials each of which are earlier in the partial order on monomials. In particular if $M$ is nonstandard then we can write $M = \sum_i n_iM_i + \sum_jn_jM_j$, where $M_i$ are standard and of the same shape as the tableau corresponding to $M$, and the $M_j$ are standard but of a smaller shape in the order on tableau. \\
3223: 		Let $\mathbf{Gr}(n,m+n)$ denote the Grassmannian variety of $n$-dimensional subspaces of an $(m+n)$-dimensional vector space $V$. Let $\{w_1, ..., w_n\}$ be a basis for an $n$-dimensional subspace $W \subset V$. Then the vector $w_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge w_n$ determines a point $[W]$ in the projective space $\mathbb{P}(\bigwedge^nV)$, and the map $W \mapsto [W]$ is a one-to-one correspondence between $n$-dimensional subspaces of $V$ and points in $\mathbb{P}(\bigwedge^n V)$ (which are lines in $\bigwedge^nV$). For the standard basis $\{e_i\}_{i=1}^{m+n}$ of $V$, we have the standard basis $\{e_{i_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{i_n}: i_1< \cdots < i_n\}$ of $\bigwedge^n V$. If to any $v_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge v_n \in \bigwedge^n V$ we associate the matrix 
3224: 		\[X = \begin{pmatrix}
3225: 			v_1 \\
3226: 			\vdots \\
3227: 			v_n
3228: 		\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}
3229: 			v_{1,1} & v_{1,2} & \cdots & v_{1,m+n} \\
3230: 			v_{2,1} & v_{2,2} & \cdots & v_{2,m+n} \\
3231: 			\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
3232: 			v_{n,1} & v_{n,2} \& \cdots & v_{n,m+n}
3233: 		\end{pmatrix},\]
3234: 		we have that in the basis $\{e_{i_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{i_n}:i_1 < \cdots < i_n\}$ the coordinates of $v_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge v_n$ are given by the maximal minors of $X$. Let $[i_1, i_2, ..., i_n]$ denote the minor given by columns $i_1, i_2, ..., i_n$ of $X$. So,
3235: 		\[ v_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge v_n = \sum_{1 \leq i_1 \leq \cdots \leq i_n \leq m+n} [i_1, ..., i_n]e_{i_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{i_n}.\]
3236: 		The open set $S_{n,m+1} = \{(v_1, ..., v_n) \in V^n: v_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge v_n \neq 0\} \subset \Hom(K^{m+n}, K^n)$ of maximal rank $n \times (m+n)$-matrices is called the \textbf{Stiefel manifold}. Now, $\mathbf{Gr}(n,m+n)$ can be identified with the orbits in $S_{n,m+n} \subset \Hom(K^{m+n}, K^n)$ under the action of $\GL(n,K)$ by left multiplication. Further, given a homomorphism $\pi: K^r \to K^{r+s}$ of two affine spaces, of the form
3237: 		\[ \pi(x_1, ..., x_r) = (x_1, ..., x_r, p_1, ..., p_s) \]
3238: 		where $p_i$ are polynomials in the $x_j$, the image of $\pi$ is a closed subvariety of $K^{r+s}$, and $\pi$ is an isomorphism of $K^r$ onto its image (it is the \emph{graph} of a polynomial map). The defining ideal is $I = (x_{r+i}-p_i)$, and the inverse map is
3239: 		\[ (x_1, ..., x_r, ..., x_{r+s}) \mapsto (x_1, ..., x_r).\]
3240: 		Now, consider the open set $U$ of $\mathbf{Gr}(n,m+n)$ where the Pl\"{u}cker coordinate given by the minor coming from the last $n$ columns is nonzero. $U$ can be identified with the space of $n \times m$ matrices. The association is given by associating any $X$ with the row span of the matrix
3241: 		\[ (X| \tilde{\id_n}) = \begin{pmatrix}
3242: 			x_{11} & \cdots & x_{1m} & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 1 \\
3243: 			x_{21} & \cdots & x_{2m} & 0 & \cdots& 1 & 0 \\
3244: 			\vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots &   & \vdots & \vdots \\
3245: 			x_{n1} & \cdots & x_{nm} & 1 & \cdots & 0 & 0
3246: 		\end{pmatrix}.\]
3247: 		One may now identify Pl\"{u}cker coordinates with \textbf{bi-tableaux}. 
3248: 		\begin{ex}
3249: 			Let $n=3$, $m=5$, and write
3250: 			\[ X = \begin{pmatrix}
3251: 				x_{31} & x_{32} & x_{33} & x_{34} & x_{35} & 1 & 0 & 0 \\
3252: 				x_{21} & x_{22} & x_{23} & x_{24} & x_{25} & 0 & 1 & 0 \\
3253: 				x_{11} & x_{12} & x_{13} & x_{14} & x_{15} & 0 & 0 & 1 \\
3254: 			\end{pmatrix}.\]
3255: 			Now let the Pl\"{u}cker coordinate $[1,2,3][1,2,5][1,2,7][2,3,6][5,6,7][5,6,8]$ be represented by the tableau
3256: 			\[ \young(123,125,127,238,567,568). \quad (*) \]
3257: 			To this we may associate the bi-tableau
3258: 			\[ \begin{pmatrix}
3259: 				\young(123,123,13,12,1,2) & &  \young(123,125,12,23,5,5)
3260: 			\end{pmatrix} \quad \quad (**) \]
3261: 			Neither the tableau $(*)$, nor the double tableau $(**)$ are standard, and thus we may use the quadratic relations on Pl\"{u}cker coordinates to "straighten" the tableau $(*)$, which induces a straightening of the double tableau $(**)$ to a standard bi-tableau. These quadratic relations can be seen as the shuffling relations coming from the definition of the Schur functors from the previous sections. 
3262: 		\end{ex}
3263: 		
3264: 		\subsection{Standard Pl\"{u}cker Coordinates}\label{Plucker monomial orders}
3265: 		We consider the Pl\"{u}cker coordinate standard if and only if the associated tableau is, i.e. the tableau is strickly increasing along rows, and nondecreasing along columns. In this example, the coordinate is not standard. We have a \textbf{straightening law} on the coordinates which is given by the definition of the Schur-functors and the relations among tableaux induced by the multilinear map defining them, i.e. the \textbf{shuffling relations}. One may also view the straightening law on standard bi-tableaux as a consequence of the quadratic relations on Pl\"{u}cker coordinates in the Grassmannian. If we take the perspective of double tableaux, we let the left tableau be the "row tableau", with indices $j \in [1,n]$ and the right tableau as the "column tableau" with indices $i \in [1,m+n]$. Each pair of rows gives a minor of the matrix $X$. We think of the space of one line tableau of size $k$ as a vector space $M_k$, with basis $(j_k, ..., j_i|i_1, ..., i_k)$, so that if two indices on the right or left are equal, then the symbol is zero, and the symbols are alternating separately on the left and right. For any partition $\lambda:= m_1 \geq m_2 \geq \cdots \geq m_r$, the tableaux of shape $\lambda$ can be thought of as a tensor product $M_{m_1} \otimes M_{m_2} \otimes \cdots \otimes M_{m_r}$. Evaluating a formal tabeleau as a product of minors gives a nontrivial kernel, which is the space spanned by the shuffling relations, or equivalently the straightening law. The action of $\GL(E) \times \GL(F)$ on $K[X]$ induces an action by the two groups of diagonal matrices, and the content of a bi-tableau is a weight vector for the product of the two algebraic tori. In particular If $(A,B) = (a_i) \times (b_j) \in T(E) \times T(F)$ is an element of the product of the tori $T(E) \subset \GL(E)$ and $T(F) \subset \GL(F)$, then the weight is $(\prod_{i+1}^n a_i^{-k(i)}; \prod_{j=1}^{m+n} b_j^{h(j)})$. At this point we should comment that there is a well-known partial order on multi-tableaux. 
3266: 		
3267: 		% \section{Partial Order on Multitableux}
