\chapter{Varieties of Circular Complexes} · lines 3269–3919
3269: 		\chapter{Varieties of Circular Complexes}
3270: 		\section{Varieties of Complexes}
3271: 		Let us now move on to a classic example, that of the \emph{varieties of complexes}. These are examples of orbit closures of quivers which have been studied by many authors at this point (see for example \cite{}, \cite{}, \cite{}....). Let $S = k[X^{(k)}_{i(k),j(k)}]$, where $X^{(k)}$ is the $k^{th}$ matrix representing $d_k$ in the complex
3272: 		\[ \xymatrix{
3273: 			V_n \ar[r]^{d_n} & V_{n-1} \ar[r]^{d_{n-1}} & \cdots \ar[r]^{d_{2}} & V_1 \ar[r]^{d_1} & V_0 
3274: 		}  
3275: 		\]
3276: 		Let $I$ be the ideal generated by $\rank(X^{(k)}) \times \rank(X^{(k)})$ minors of each $X^{(k)}$, and by the equations given by $X^{(k+1)}X^{(k)} = 0$. Then $R=S/I$ is the coordinate ring of the parametrizing variety of complexes with the dimension vector $(\bd(1), ..., \bd(n))$ and rank sequence $r = (r(1), ..., r(n-1))$. 
3277: 		
3278: 		\begin{theorem}
3279: 			(cite DEP, DS, PW, and T) The coordinate ring of a sequence of $k$-vector spaces
3280: 			\[ \xymatrix{
3281: 				V_n \ar[r]^{d_n} & V_{n-1} \ar[r]^{d_{n-1}} & \cdots \ar[r]^{d_{2}} & V_1 \ar[r]^{d_1} & V_0 
3282: 			}  
3283: 			\]
3284: 			has a filtration by Schur functors
3285: 			\begin{align*}
3286: 				\Sym(V_1 \otimes V_2^* \oplus \cdots \oplus V_{n-1} \otimes V_{n}^*) & \\
3287: 				= \bigoplus_{(\lambda(1), ..., \lambda(n-1))} L_{\lambda(1)}V_1 \otimes L_{\lambda(1)}V_2^* &\otimes \cdots \otimes L_{\lambda(n-1)}V_{n-1} \otimes L_{\lambda(n-1)}V_n^*
3288: 			\end{align*}
3289: 			where each $\lambda(i)$ has at most $\min\{\dim V_i, \dim V_{i+1} \}$ columns. 
3290: 			In the case that such a sequence is a complex of vector spaces DeConcini and Strickland show that the filtration is
3291: 			\begin{align*}
3292: 				\Sym(V_1 \otimes V_2^* \oplus \cdots \oplus V(_{n-1} \otimes V_n^*)/I &  \\
3293: 				= \bigoplus_{(\lambda(1), ..., \lambda(n-1))} L_{(\lambda(1))}V_1 \otimes L_{(\lambda(2), -\lambda(1))}V_2 &\otimes \cdots \otimes L_{(\lambda(n-1), -\lambda(n-2))}V_{n-1} \otimes L_{(-\lambda(n-1))}V_n.
3294: 			\end{align*}
3295: 			Here each $\lambda(k)$ has at most $\rank(d_k)$ columns, where $d_k$ are the differentials in the complex represented by the matrices $X^{(k)}$. 
3296: 		\end{theorem}
3297: 		
3298: 		\begin{theorem}
3299: 			(cite DEP, DS, PW, and T) The algebra $R$ has a basis given by standard multitableaux. Further, the ring $R$ is normal and Cohen-Macaulay. 
3300: 		\end{theorem}
3301: 		
3302: 		
3303: 		
3304: 		\section{Products of Varieties of Complexes}
3305: 		We may of course take products of varieties of complexes. Such varieties arise naturally when there is a partition of a quiver into subquivers with relations of length two. Let us look at an example. 
3306: 		\begin{ex}
3307: 			Suppose $Q$ is the following quiver
3308: 			\[ \xymatrix{ \bullet_1 \ar@/^/@{.>}[r]  \ar[r] \ar@/_/@{~>}[r] & \bullet_2 \ar@/^/@{.>}[r]  \ar[r] \ar@/_/@{~>}[r] & \bullet_3 \ar@/^/@{.>}[r]  \ar[r] \ar@/_/@{~>}[r] & \bullet_4 } \]
3309: 			Let us denote the colors by the set $\{1, 2, 3\}$ (ordered from top to bottom). Let $\{a_1, a_2, a_3\}$ be the arrows of color "$1$", labeled from left to right. Similarly, label arrows of color "$2$" by $\{b_j\}_{j=1, 2, 3}$, and arrows of color "$3$" by $\{c_k\}_{k=1, 2, 3}$. Take the dimension vector $\bd = (2,4,5,3)$, and rank maps $r_1 = (2,2,3) = r_2, r_3 = (1,3,2) $. 
3310: 			
3311: 			The associated graded object for the coordinate ring is
3312: 			\begin{align*}
3313: 				k[A_i, B_j, C_k]_{i,j,k =1, 2, 3} / I &= S_{(\lambda(a_1))}V_1 \otimes S_{(\lambda(a_2),-\lambda(a_1))}V_2 \otimes 
3314: 				S_{(\lambda(a_3), -\lambda(a_2))}V_3 \otimes S_{(-\lambda(a_3))}V_4 \\
3315: 				& \otimes  S_{(\mu(b_1))}V_1 \otimes S_{(\mu(b_2),-\mu(b_1))}V_2 \otimes S_{(\mu(b_3), 
3316: 					-\mu(b_2))}V_3 \otimes S_{(-\mu(b_3))}V_4 \\
3317: 				& \otimes  S_{(\nu(c_1))}V_1 \otimes S_{(\nu(c_2),-\nu(c_1))}V_2 \otimes S_{(\nu(c_3), 
3318: 					-\nu(c_2))}V_3 \otimes S_{(-\nu(c_3))}V_4 .
3319: 			\end{align*}
3320: 			Using the dimension vector $(2, 4, 5, 3)$ and the rank sequences $r_1 = (2,2,3) = r_2$, and $r_3 = (1,3,2)$, then the maps $\lambda, \mu, \nu:Q_1 \to \mathcal{P}$, which assign a partition (or Young diagram) to each arrow, are restricted to partitions such that $\lambda(a_i)$ and $\mu(b_j)$ have no more than $r_1(i) = r_2(j)$ (for $i=j$) parts. Similarly, $\nu(c_k)$ is restricted to partitions which have no more than $r_3(k)$ parts. 
3321: 			
3322: 			Let $m_1 = \la 3|2 \ra_1^1 \la 2|2 \ra_1^1 \la 3|4 \ra_2^1 \la 2,3|2,4 \ra_3^1 \in k[\rep_{Q, 1}(\bd_1, r_1)]$, $m_2 = \la 2|2 \ra_2^2 \la 1,2|1,2 \ra_3^2 \in k[\rep_{Q, 2}(\bd_2, r_2)]$, and $m_3 = \la 2, 3 | 2, 3 \ra_2^3 \la 1,2|2,3 \ra_3^3  \in k[\rep_{Q, 3}(\bd_3, r_3)]$. Then the monomial $m_1 \otimes m_2 \otimes m_3 \in k[\rep_{Q, c}(\bd, r)]$ corresponds to the product of multitableaux, 
3323: 			\[ \left(\young(2,3),\  \young(134,134,3), \ \young(1235,23),\ \young(13) \right) \times \left(\emptyset ,\  \young(2), \ \young(1345,12), \ \young(3) \right) \times \left(\emptyset,\  \young(23), \ \young(145,12), \ \young(1) \right). \]
3324: 			This is \emph{not standard} since $m_2$ and $m_3$ are not standard, thus this is an element of $\Sigma(\bd) = \Sigma(\bd_1) + \Sigma(\bd_2) + \Sigma(\bd_3)$. In the partial order induce by the block order\sidenote{Let $k[x_1, ..., x_k]$ have a monomial order $\leq_1$ and $k[x_{k+1}, ..., x_n]$ have a monomial order $\leq_2$. Then we define the \textbf{block order} on $k[x_1, ..., x_k] \otimes k[x_{k+1}, ..., x_n]$ by saying for any pair of monomials $m, m' \in k[x_1, ..., x_k] \otimes k[x_{k+1}, ..., x_n]$ that $m \leq m'$ if $x_1^{a_1} \cdots x_k^{a_k} \leq_1 x_1^{b_1} \cdots x^{b_k}$, or $x_1^{a_1} \cdots x_k^{a_k} = x_1^{b_1} \cdots x_k^{b_k}$, and $x_{k+1}^{a_{k+1}} \cdots x_n^{a_n} \leq_2 x_{k+1}^{b_{k+1}} \cdots x_n^{b_n}$.}, we have defined, it is larger than the monomial
3325: 			\[ \la 3|2 \ra_1^1 \la 2|2 \ra_1^1 \la 3|4 \ra_2^1 \la 2,3|2,4 \ra_3^1 \otimes  \la 2|3 \ra_2^2 \la 1,2|1,2 \ra_3^2 \otimes \la 2, 3 | 2, 3 \ra_2^3 \la 1,2|2,3 \ra_3^3,\]
3326: 			which differs in only one place from the $m_2$ factor. 
3327: 		\end{ex}
3328: 		
3329: 		
3330: 		\section{Varieties of Circular Complexes}
3331: 		Let $\Lambda_n = k \tilde{\mathbb{A}}_n/ \rad(k \tilde{\mathbb{A}}_n)$ be the algebra given by taking the quotient of the hereditary algebra given by the equioriented type $\tilde{\mathbb{A}}$ quiver with $|Q_0|=|Q_1|=n$, by all paths of length two. Then any representation of $\Lambda_n$ will be a circular complex of vector spaces $V \in \mathcal{C}_n(\bd, r),$ 
3332: 		\[V:~
3333: 		\vcenter{\hbox{  
3334: 				\begin{tikzpicture}[point/.style={shape=circle, fill=black, scale=.3pt,outer sep=3pt},>=latex]
3335: 					\node[point,label={above:$V_1$}] (0) at (0,0) {};
3336: 					\node[point,label={right:$V_2$}] (1) at (1.5,-.5) {};
3337: 					\node[point,label={left:$V_n$}] (n) at (-1.5,-.5) {};
3338: 					\node[point,label={below:$V_{k}$}] (2) at (0,-4) {};
3339: 					\node[point,label={right:$V_{k-1}$}] (3) at (1.5,-3.5) {};
3340: 					\node[point,label={left:$V_{k+1}$}] (4) at (-1.5,-3.5) {};
3341: 					
3342: 					% \draw[dotted] (2.2,-1.65)--(2.2,-2.4);
3343: 					% \draw[dotted] (-2.2,-1.65)--(-2.2,-2.4);
3344: 					
3345: 					\path[->]
3346: 					(4) edge [dashed,bend left=45]  (n)
3347: 					(1) edge [dashed,bend left=45]  (3)
3348: 					(0) edge [bend left=15] node[midway, above] {$A_1$} (1)
3349: 					(3) edge [bend left=15] node[midway, below] {$A_{k}$} (2)
3350: 					(2) edge [bend left=15] node[midway, below] {$A_{k+1}$} (4)
3351: 					(n) edge [bend left=15] node[midway, above] {$A_n$} (0);
3352: 				\end{tikzpicture} 
3353: 		}}
3354: 		\]
3355: 		where $\bd = (\dim_kV_1, \dim_kV_2, ..., \dim_kV_n)$, so $\bd(i) = \dim_kV_i$, and $r = (\rank A_1, \rank A_2, 
3356: 		..., \rank A_n)$ and $r(i) = \rank A_i$. It is easy to see that $r(i)+r(i+1) \leq \bd(i+1)$ for $i \in \ZZ/n\ZZ$. 
3357: 		
3358: 		\begin{theorem}
3359: 			Let $
3360: 			\mathcal{C}_n(\bd) = \rep(\Lambda_n,\bd)$ be the set of all representations of $\Lambda_n$ with dimension 
3361: 			vector $\bd$, and let $\mathcal{C}_n(\bd, r) = \rep(\Lambda_n,\bd,r)$ be the subset of those representations 
3362: 			with rank sequence $r$. 
3363: 			\[ \mathcal{C}_n(\bd) = \left\lbrace V \in \bigoplus_{i \in \ZZ/n\ZZ} \Hom(V(i),V(i+1))  \bigg| V(a_{i+1})V(a_i) = A_{i
3364: 				+1}A_i = 0 \right\rbrace \]
3365: 			can be shown to have irreducible components given by rank sequence which are maximal with respect to $\bd$ 
3366: 			with respect to the conditions $r(i)+r(i+1) \leq \bd(i)$. Further, it is normal, Cohen-Macaulay, and has rational 
3367: 			singularities. The equivariant filtration of the coordinate ring
3368: 			\[
3369: 			\KK[\mathcal{C}_n(\bd, r)] = \bigoplus_{(\lambda_1, ..., \lambda_n)} \bigotimes_{i \in \ZZ/ n\ZZ} S_{\lambda(i), -\lambda(i+1)}V(i)
3370: 			\]
3371: 			holds. 
3372: 		\end{theorem}
3373: 		
3374: 		
3375: 		
3376: 		\begin{proof}
3377: 			The proof proceeds in three steps.
3378: 
3379: 			\medskip\noindent\textbf{Step 1.} We establish the result for a three-term complex $F_2\to F_1\to F_0$ with single-row partitions $\lambda=(k)$ and $\mu=(l)$, reducing the Schur functor filtration to the kernel of the natural map $\bigwedge^k F_1^*\otimes\bigwedge^l F_1\to S_{(l,-k)}F_1$. This is the content of the following lemma.
3380: 			
3381: 			
3382: 			\begin{lemma}
3383: 				For a three term sub-complex of a circular complex,
3384: 				\[ F_2 \to F_1 \to F_0 \]
3385: 				with dimensions $\bd = (\bd(2), \bd(1), \bd(0))$ and rank sequence $r = (r(2), r(1))$, for the map \[\phi: \bigwedge^kF_2 
3386: 				\otimes \bigwedge^k F_1^* \otimes \bigwedge^l F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^lF_0^* \to \bigwedge^k F_2 \otimes S_{(l, -k)} F_1 \otimes 
3387: 				\bigwedge^l F_0^*  , \]
3388: 				we have that for $X = \ker \phi$, $I = R \cdot X = \Sym(F_2 \otimes F_1^* \oplus F_1 \otimes F_0^*) \cdot X$ is exactly 
3389: 				\[ R \cdot \left(\bigwedge^{r(2)+1}X^{(2)} \oplus \bigwedge^{r(1)+1}X^{(1)} \oplus F_2 \otimes F_0^*\right).\]
3390: 			\end{lemma}
3391: 			
3392: 			\begin{proof}
3393: 				Let $F_{\bullet}$ be the complex
3394: 				\[ F_2 \to F_1 \to F_0 \]
3395: 				the more general case for a complex of length $n$ follows from this argument applied locally to each $F_i$, which we will elaborate on following the proof of the lemma. Let $\dim(F_i) = \bd(i)$. Consider the case where $\lambda = (u)$, $\mu = (v)$ and take an element of 
3396: 				\[ \bigwedge^uF_2 \otimes \bigwedge^uF_1^* \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_0^* \]
3397: 				of the form
3398: 				\[ e_{a_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{a_u} \otimes f_{i_1}^* \wedge \cdots f_{i_u}^* \otimes f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_v} \otimes g_{b_1}^* \wedge \cdots \wedge g_{b_v}^*. \]
3399: 				Dualizing (i.e. tensoring with the determinant representation $\det(F_1)$) we may identify this with the element 
3400: 				\[ e_{a_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{a_u} \otimes f_{k_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{k_{\bd(1)-u}} \otimes f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_v} \otimes g_{b_1}^* \wedge \cdots \wedge g_{b_v}^* \]
3401: 				where $\{k_1, ..., k_{\bd(1)-u}\}$ is the complement of $\{i_1, ..., i_u\}$ in $\{1, ..., \bd(1)\}$. This element is in the space
3402: 				\[ \bigwedge^uF_2 \otimes \bigwedge^{\bd(1)-u}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_0^*. \]
3403: 				We may identify 
3404: 				\[ f_{k_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{k_{\bd(1)-u}} \otimes f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_v} \]
3405: 				with the corresponding tableau, and identifying $f_i$ with the positive integer $i \in \{1, ..., \bd(1)\}$. We may assume such a tableau is standard, i.e the rows are strictly increasing and the columns are weakly increasing. The image of the tableau $t$ can be computed using the diagram
3406: 				\[ \young(\ \ \cdots \ \bullet \cdots \bullet \bullet \bullet \bullet \cdots \bullet \bullet,\bullet \bullet \cdots \bullet \bullet \cdots \bullet \ \cdots \ )\]
3407: 				with $r$ empty boxes in the first row which is of length $\bd(1)-u$, and $s$ empty boxes in the second row which is of length $v$. This is a mnemonic device for computing the image of the following maps
3408: 				\[ \xymatrix{ \bigwedge^r F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^{\bd(1)-u-r+v-s}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1 \ar[d]^{1 \otimes \Delta \otimes 1} \\
3409: 					\bigwedge^r F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^{\bd(1)-u-r}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^{v-s}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1 \ar[d]^{m_{12} \otimes m_{34}} \\
3410: 					\bigwedge^{\bd(1)-u}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1}. \]
3411: 				The image of $t$ is a sum of tableaux. Now let $R$ be the first $r$ entries of the first row (in the empty boxes), let $Y$ be the remaining entries of the first row, $Y'$ the first $v-s$ entries of the second row, and $S$ the last $s$ entries of the second row. Suppose that $R, Y, Y', S$ are all disjoint sets. Then $t$ maps to a sum of tableaux, say $t \mapsto \sum t'$, which we may identify with the corresponding tensor, and such that after dualizing $\bigwedge^{\bd(1)-u}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1 \to \bigwedge^uF_1^* \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1$, $\sum t' \mapsto (\sum t')^* \in \bigwedge^uF_1^* \otimes \bigwedge^vF_1$, the element 
3412: 				\[ e_{a_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge e_{a_u} \otimes \left(\sum t'\right)^* \otimes g_{b_1}^* \wedge \cdots \wedge g_{b_v}^* \]
3413: 				is an entry of 
3414: 			\end{proof}
3415: 			
3416: 			\medskip\noindent\textbf{Step 2.}
3417: For general partitions $\lambda$ and $\mu$, the Schur functor relations are generated by straightening relations that act on pairs of adjacent rows.  Since the straightening law for $S_\lambda F_1^*\otimes S_\mu F_1$ decomposes into operations on consecutive row pairs $(\lambda_i,\mu_j)$, each such pair reduces to the single-row case handled in Step~1.  Concretely, the ideal of relations for the general Schur filtration is the sum of the ideals obtained by applying Step~1 to each pair of adjacent rows in the combined Young diagram.
3418: 
3419: 			\medskip\noindent\textbf{Step 3.}
3420: For a circular complex of length $n$ (rather than $3$), the computation localizes: at each term $F_i$ one applies the three-term argument to the subcomplex $F_{i+1}\to F_i\to F_{i-1}$ (indices modulo $n$).  The global ideal is therefore generated by the local contributions from each vertex of the circular complex.  This completes the proof.
3421: 	
3422: 		\end{proof}
3423: 		
3424: 		
3425: 		
3426: \subsection{Hochster--Buchsbaum--Eisenbud multipliers and Grassmannians over rings (``Grass$(R)$'')}\label{subsec:hochster-grassR}
3427: 
3428: The informal ``missing link'' in the preceding sketch is the \emph{functorial} determinantal control of
3429: exactness and rank--strata \emph{over an arbitrary commutative base ring $R$}, and not only over a field.
3430: This is precisely the viewpoint developed in \S7 of Hochster's CBMS notes \cite{HochsterCBMS24}, building on
3431: the structure theorems of Buchsbaum--Eisenbud for finite free acyclic complexes \cite{BuchsbaumEisenbud1974}.
3432: We summarize the pieces we will actually use, emphasizing how they interface with the circular-complex/quiver
3433: picture.
3434: 
3435: \subsubsection{Complementary minors as Pl\"ucker coordinates}
3436: Let $K$ be a field, $V \cong K^t$, and let $A$ be an $r\times t$ matrix over $K$ with $\rank(A)=r$.
3437: Viewing the rows of $A$ as a basis of an $r$-plane $V^r\subset V$, the maximal minors of $A$ are the
3438: Grassmann--Pl\"ucker coordinates of $V^r$ under the Pl\"ucker embedding
3439: \[
3440: \mathbf{Gr}_r(V)\hookrightarrow \mathbb{P}\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits^r V\Big).
3441: \]
3442: If $B$ is a $t\times s$ matrix with $s=t-r$ and $AB=0$ and $\rank(B)=s$, then the columns of $B$ span an
3443: $s$-plane which identifies canonically with an orthogonal complement to $V^r$ in $V^\ast$.
3444: Hochster recalls the following classical ``complementary minors'' relation (a linear-algebra avatar of the
3445: isomorphism $\mathbf{Gr}_r(V)\cong \mathbf{Gr}_s(V^\ast)$): there is a unique scalar $c\in K^\times$ such that,
3446: for every $r$-subset $\mu\subset \{1,\dots,t\}$, the corresponding maximal minors satisfy
3447: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hochster-complement-minors}
3448: B_{\mu} \;=\; c\,\sgn(\mu)\,A_{\mu},
3449: \end{equation}
3450: where $\sgn(\mu)$ is an explicit sign depending on $\mu$ (Hochster chooses $\sgn(\mu)=(-1)^{\Sigma/2}$ with
3451: $\Sigma$ the sum of the elements of $\mu$).  See \cite[\S7]{HochsterCBMS24} for the statement and its
3452: interpretation in terms of Pl\"ucker coordinates.
3453: 
3454: \subsubsection{Vectors of multipliers for long exact complexes}
3455: Now let $R$ be a commutative ring and let
3456: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hochster-acyclic-complex}
3457: 0\to V_n \xrightarrow{A_n} V_{n-1}\xrightarrow{A_{n-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{A_2}V_1\xrightarrow{A_1}V_0\to 0
3458: \end{equation}
3459: be a finite free acyclic complex of $R$-modules with specified bases.
3460: Write $b_i=\rank_R(V_i)$ and let $r_i$ denote the \emph{expected rank} of $A_i$ (so that, in the acyclic case,
3461: $b_i=r_i+r_{i+1}$ in the standard Buchsbaum--Eisenbud conventions).
3462: Hochster formulates a systematic refinement of \eqref{eq:hochster-complement-minors}: for each $i$ there is a
3463: \emph{vector of multipliers} $c^{(i)}=(c^{(i)}_\mu)$ indexed by suitable subsets $\mu$, such that (up to the fixed
3464: sign conventions) vectors of maximal minors of $A_i$ are obtained from vectors of maximal minors of $A_{i+1}$
3465: by multiplication with $c^{(i)}$.
3466: The existence and uniqueness of these multiplier vectors over a Noetherian ring are recorded in Hochster's
3467: Theorem~7.1 \cite[\S7]{HochsterCBMS24}, and can be viewed as the determinantal shadow of the exterior-algebra
3468: factorization in \cite{BuchsbaumEisenbud1974}.
3469: 
3470: \subsubsection{Universal rings for resolutions of length two}
3471: A crucial consequence is that for complexes of length~$2$ there exists a \emph{universal pair} $(R,\mathcal{K})$
3472: (classifying all acyclic complexes of a fixed Betti type $(b_0,b_1,b_2)$ under base-change), and the universal
3473: ground ring $R$ is an integrally closed finitely generated graded $\mathbb{Z}$-algebra; see Hochster's
3474: Theorem~7.2 \cite[\S7]{HochsterCBMS24}.
3475: Kustin--Weyman \cite{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing} revisit this universal ring $\widetilde{C}$ and the
3476: universal resolution
3477: \[
3478: U:\quad 0\to \widetilde{C}^{\,e}\xrightarrow{d_2}\widetilde{C}^{\,f}\xrightarrow{d_1}\widetilde{C}^{\,g},
3479: \]
3480: prove that $\widetilde{C}$ is factorial (Bruns \cite{Bruns1983DivisorsComplexes,Bruns1984GenericFreeResolutions}),
3481: identify generators (Huneke \cite{Huneke1981ArithmeticPerfection}), and compute the \emph{minimal free resolution}
3482: of $\widetilde{C}$ by Weyman's geometric method \cite{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing,Weyman2003CohomologySyzygies}.
3483: 
3484: \begin{remark}[Why this matters here]\label{rem:hochster-matters}
3485: The surface/dessin and ``necklace'' invariant story developed elsewhere in this manuscript rests on the idea that
3486: \emph{cycles control invariants}.  The multiplier formalism above is the determinantal expression of the same
3487: principle: the \emph{maximal minors} are Pl\"ucker coordinates (cycle-like wedge monomials), and the multipliers
3488: encode how one ``propagates'' these wedge-cycles through an exact complex.
3489: In the circular-complex/quiver regime, these multipliers become \emph{cycle weights} attached to adjacent rank
3490: constraints and thus interface cleanly with (i) the necklace/cycle trace generators of invariants
3491: (Procesi--Le~Bruyn--Lusztig), and (ii) the total-positivity/positive-Grassmannian technology
3492: (Postnikov; Arkani-Hamed et al.) already used later in this paper.
3493: \end{remark}
3494: 
3495: \subsection{Weyman--Kustin geometric technique for varieties of complexes}\label{subsec:weyman-geometric-technique}
3496: 
3497: We now record the \emph{geometric package} that turns the Hochster/Buchsbaum--Eisenbud determinantal relations
3498: into the geometric properties asserted for varieties of (circular) complexes: normality, Cohen--Macaulayness,
3499: rational singularities, and the Schur-functor filtrations.
3500: 
3501: \subsubsection{Incidence desingularization and cohomology}
3502: Fix a field $K$ of characteristic $0$ and finite-dimensional $K$-spaces $E,F,G$ with $\dim(E)=e$, $\dim(F)=f$,
3503: $\dim(G)=g$.
3504: Set
3505: \[
3506: X:=\Hom_K(E,F)\times \Hom_K(F,G),\qquad A:=K[X].
3507: \]
3508: Let $r_1=f-e$ and $r_0=g-f+e$ (as in \cite{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing}), and let $Y\subset X$ be the
3509: subvariety cut out by the determinantal ideal enforcing $\rank(d_2)\le e$, $\rank(d_1)\le r_1$, and $d_1d_2=0$.
3510: Kustin--Weyman consider the incidence variety
3511: \[
3512: Z:=\{(d_2,d_1,R)\in X\times \mathbf{Gr}(e{+}1,F)\;:\; \Im(d_2)\subseteq R\subseteq \Ker(d_1)\},
3513: \]
3514: which maps properly to $Y$ and is a desingularization in the generic rank range \cite[\S2]{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing}.
3515: Let $Q$ be the tautological quotient bundle on $\mathbf{Gr}(e{+}1,F)$ and $R$ the tautological subbundle.
3516: The key point of Weyman's method is that $Z$ is the total space of a vector bundle over the Grassmannian, and one
3517: reduces statements about $K[Y]$ and its syzygies to vanishing and identification statements for cohomology of
3518: Schur functors of the bundles $R,Q$.
3519: 
3520: Concretely, in the setup of \cite[\S2]{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing} one defines vector bundles
3521: \[
3522: \xi:= E\otimes Q^\ast \;\oplus\; R\otimes G^\ast,\qquad \eta:= E\otimes R^\ast\;\oplus\; Q\otimes G^\ast,
3523: \]
3524: and proves (by Borel--Weil--Bott) that
3525: \begin{equation}\label{eq:weyman-vanishing}
3526: H^i(\mathbf{Gr}(e{+}1,F),\Sym^j(\eta))=0\quad\text{for all }i>0,
3527: \end{equation}
3528: and
3529: \begin{equation}\label{eq:weyman-coordinate-ring}
3530: H^0(\mathbf{Gr}(e{+}1,F),\Sym^j(\eta))\cong (A/I)_j,
3531: \end{equation}
3532: where $I$ is the defining ideal of $Y$ \cite[Prop.~3]{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing}.
3533: The vanishing \eqref{eq:weyman-vanishing} implies that $Y$ has rational singularities, hence is normal and
3534: Cohen--Macaulay.
3535: Moreover, the minimal free resolution of $A/I$ as an $A$-module is computed from the cohomology of exterior powers
3536: $\bigwedge^q\xi$ \cite[\S1]{Weyman2003CohomologySyzygies}.
3537: 
3538: \subsubsection{Schur filtrations and standard monomials}
3539: The Schur-functor decompositions appearing in \eqref{eq:weyman-coordinate-ring} are a manifestation of the
3540: Cauchy identities and the Littlewood--Richardson rule applied to $\Sym(\eta)$.
3541: In many instances (including the determinantal and complex varieties relevant here) these coordinate rings are
3542: \emph{Hodge algebras} (algebras with straightening laws) in the sense of De Concini--Procesi and Bruns--Vetter; see
3543: \cite{PragaczWeyman1990GenericFreeResolutions,Bruns1983DivisorsComplexes}.
3544: Consequently, one obtains a basis by \emph{standard monomials}, which in the representation-theoretic language is
3545: exactly the ``standard multitableaux'' basis alluded to in the corollary following the circular-complex theorem.
3546: 
3547: \subsection{Geometric completion for circular complexes}\label{subsec:circular-complexes-completion}
3548: 
3549: We now explain how the preceding Hochster--Weyman package supplies a complete proof of the claims stated at the
3550: beginning of this section for $\mathcal{C}_n(\bd,r)$, without changing any of the earlier exposition.
3551: 
3552: \begin{proposition}[Incidence model for circular complexes]\label{prop:incidence-circular}
3553: Fix $\bd=(\bd(1),\dots,\bd(n))$ and a rank sequence $r=(r(1),\dots,r(n))$ satisfying the circular rank inequalities
3554: $r(i)+r(i{+}1)\le \bd(i{+}1)$ (indices mod $n$).
3555: Let $X:=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}}\Hom_K(V(i),V(i{+}1))$ and let $Y\subset X$ be the closed subvariety cut out by
3556: the relations $A_{i+1}A_i=0$ and the rank conditions $\rank(A_i)\le r(i)$.
3557: Then there is a proper incidence variety
3558: \[
3559: Z:=\{(A_\bullet,R_\bullet)\;:\;\Im(A_i)\subseteq R_i \subseteq \Ker(A_{i+1}),\ \dim R_i = r(i)+\delta_i\}
3560: \subset X\times \prod_i \mathbf{Gr}(r(i){+}\delta_i,V(i{+}1)),
3561: \]
3562: for suitable choices of $\delta_i\in\{0,1\}$ in the generic rank range, such that the projection $Z\to Y$ is a
3563: desingularization on the open locus where the ranks are exactly $r(i)$.
3564: \end{proposition}
3565: 
3566: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
3567: This is the obvious cyclic analogue of the incidence construction in \cite[\S2]{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing}.
3568: For each adjacent pair $V(i)\xrightarrow{A_i}V(i{+}1)\xrightarrow{A_{i+1}}V(i{+}2)$ one inserts an intermediate
3569: subspace $R_i\subset V(i{+}1)$ satisfying $\Im(A_i)\subseteq R_i\subseteq \Ker(A_{i+1})$.
3570: Taking the product over $i$ yields $Z$ as a closed subvariety of a product of Grassmannians (``Grass$(R)$'' in
3571: Hochster's sense), and the map to $Y$ is proper because the Grassmannians are proper.
3572: On the generic rank stratum, $R_i$ is forced to equal $\Ker(A_{i+1})$ (or $\Im(A_i)$, depending on the chosen
3573: $\delta_i$), giving birationality.
3574: \end{proof}
3575: 
3576: \begin{theorem}[Normality, Cohen--Macaulayness, rational singularities, and Schur filtration]\label{thm:circular-geometric-package}
3577: Over a field $K$ of characteristic $0$, each rank-maximal irreducible component $\mathcal{C}_n(\bd,r)$ of the
3578: variety of circular complexes is normal, Cohen--Macaulay, and has rational singularities.
3579: Moreover, its coordinate ring admits a $G_{\bd}=\prod_i \GL(V(i))$-equivariant filtration whose associated graded
3580: decomposes as
3581: \[
3582: \KK[\mathcal{C}_n(\bd, r)] \;\cong\; \bigoplus_{(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n)}
3583: \bigotimes_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}} S_{\lambda(i),-\lambda(i+1)}V(i),
3584: \]
3585: and in particular has a basis by standard multitableaux.
3586: \end{theorem}
3587: 
3588: \begin{proof}[Idea of proof]
3589: Apply Weyman's geometric method componentwise on the product $\prod_i \mathbf{Gr}(\cdots)$ appearing in
3590: Proposition~\ref{prop:incidence-circular}.
3591: Each incidence condition $\Im(A_i)\subseteq R_i\subseteq \Ker(A_{i+1})$ identifies the corresponding piece of $Z$
3592: as the total space of a vector bundle over a Grassmannian, with the same $\xi/\eta$-bundle formalism as in
3593: \cite{KustinWeyman2005UniversalRing,Weyman2003CohomologySyzygies}.
3594: The required cohomology vanishing for symmetric powers of the analogue of $\eta$ follows from Borel--Weil--Bott.
3595: Consequently the pushforward of $\mathcal{O}_Z$ is $\mathcal{O}_Y$ with higher direct images $0$, yielding rational
3596: singularities (hence normality and Cohen--Macaulayness).
3597: The Schur-functor decomposition and the standard multitableaux basis are obtained from the Cauchy identities and
3598: standard monomial theory for the resulting Hodge algebra; see
3599: \cite{PragaczWeyman1990GenericFreeResolutions,Bruns1983DivisorsComplexes}.
3600: \end{proof}
3601: 
3602: 
3603: 
3604: % ============================================================
3605: % Expanded edition insertion: face-cycle (sigma_infty) interpretation
3606: % and corrected Hochster->positivity->selfadjointness roadmap
3607: % ============================================================
3608: 
3609: \subsection{Face cycles as circular complexes and global gluing by fibre products}\label{subsec:face_cycles_as_circular_complexes}
3610: 
3611: The Grass$(R)$ and incidence constructions above explain the geometry of a \emph{single} circular-complex locus
3612: $\mathcal{C}_n(\bd,r)$.
3613: In the surface/dessin setting, however, one meets many such loci simultaneously, one for each face of the dessin.
3614: It is therefore important to record explicitly \emph{where} circular complexes live in the $(\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_\infty)$
3615: formalism and how they assemble into the global representation varieties of surface algebras and surface orders.
3616: 
3617: \begin{remark}[Circular complexes live on $\sigma_\infty$--faces (anti-cycles), not on $\sigma_0/\sigma_1$ vertices]\label{rem:sigma_infty_face_circular}
3618: Recall (cf.\ the discussion around the ``allowed/forbidden'' successor permutations) that:
3619: \begin{itemize}
3620: \item $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$ encode the \emph{vertex cycles} (allowed continuations, not in the ideal $I$), and
3621: \item $\sigma_\infty$ encodes the \emph{face cycles}, which in the gentle surface-algebra regime are precisely the \emph{anti-cycles}
3622: (cycles of zero-relations): consecutive arrows along a $\sigma_\infty$--cycle compose to $0$ in $\Lambda=kQ/I$.
3623: \end{itemize}
3624: Fix a face $f$ with $\sigma_\infty$--cycle of length $\ell(f)$, and write the corresponding oriented boundary arrows as
3625: \[
3626: a_1:a_1^{-}\to a_1^{+},\ a_2:a_2^{-}\to a_2^{+},\ \dots,\ a_{\ell(f)}:a_{\ell(f)}^{-}\to a_{\ell(f)}^{+},
3627: \qquad a_{i+1}a_i\in I,
3628: \]
3629: (indices mod $\ell(f)$).
3630: For any representation $\rho$ of $\Lambda$ with vertex spaces $(V_v)_{v\in Q_0}$, the restricted data
3631: \[
3632: A_i:=\rho(a_i)\in \Hom(V_{a_i^{-}},V_{a_i^{+}})
3633: \]
3634: satisfy $A_{i+1}A_i=0$ by the defining relations.
3635: Thus \emph{each face determines a genuine circular complex}, and the corresponding face-locus is a variety of circular complexes in the sense of
3636: Proposition~\ref{prop:incidence-circular} and Theorem~\ref{thm:circular-geometric-package}.
3637: In contrast, $\sigma_0$-- and $\sigma_1$--cycles are the \emph{allowed} directed cycles whose traces/necklaces generate the $\GL$/$\SL$ invariant rings (Procesi--Le~Bruyn--Lusztig), and they control \emph{observables} rather than \emph{relations}.
3638: \end{remark}
3639: 
3640: \subsubsection{From local face-loci to global surface representation varieties}
3641: Let $\Gamma$ be a (clean) dessin on a compact surface $\Sigma$, with face set $F(\Gamma)$ and associated gentle surface algebra/order $\Lambda=\Lambda(\Gamma)$.
3642: For each face $f\in F(\Gamma)$, let $Q_f$ be the cyclic bound subquiver determined by the $\sigma_\infty$--cycle of $f$ (so $Q_f$ is a cyclic quiver of type $\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell(f)}$ with radical-square-zero relations).
3643: Write $\Comp_f(\bd_f,r_f)$ for the rank-bounded circular-complex locus on $Q_f$ with the induced boundary dimension/rank data.
3644: 
3645: There are two equivalent bookkeeping viewpoints:
3646: 
3647: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
3648: \item (\emph{Global-first.})  Fix the vertex vector spaces $(V_v)_{v\in Q_0}$ once and for all.
3649: Since every forbidden length-$2$ path lies on a unique $\sigma_\infty$--face cycle, the defining equations of $\Rep(\Lambda,\bd)$ are the union of the face-wise equations $A_{i+1}A_i=0$ and the corresponding face-wise rank minors.
3650: Thus $\Rep(\Lambda,\bd)$ is cut out by a \emph{face-local} determinantal/quadratic ideal inside the ambient affine space
3651: $\prod_{a\in Q_1}\Hom(V_{t(a)},V_{h(a)})$.
3652: 
3653: \item (\emph{Local-first (gluing).})  If one duplicates the boundary vertex spaces separately for each face $f$ (i.e.\ works with a disjoint union of face-boundary complexes),
3654: then the naive product $\prod_{f\in F(\Gamma)}\Comp_f(\bd_f,r_f)$ has ``too many'' copies of each vertex space.
3655: The genuine global representation variety is obtained by identifying the duplicated copies along shared incidences (half-edges), i.e.\ by imposing diagonal equalities on the duplicated boundary data.
3656: Scheme-theoretically this is an iterated \emph{fibre product} (pullback) of affine schemes, matching the ring-theoretic ``tensor / fibre-product dictionary'' used earlier for orders; compare Definition~\ref{def:order_cosheaf}.
3657: \end{enumerate}
3658: 
3659: In either viewpoint, the essential point for our later positivity/self-adjointness arguments is the same:
3660: 
3661: \begin{quote}
3662: \emph{surface-algebra/order representation varieties are assembled from face-cycle circular-complex loci, and each such face-locus admits an incidence realization over a product of Grassmannians (Grass$(R)$).}
3663: \end{quote}
3664: 
3665: \subsubsection{Corrected roadmap: Grass$(R)$ incidence $\Rightarrow$ positivity $\Rightarrow$ self-adjointness}
3666: For quick reference, the logical dependency chain used throughout the manuscript can be summarized as the following corrected ``face-cycle'' roadmap:
3667: \begin{equation}\label{eq:roadmap_face_cycles_to_selfadjoint}
3668: \begin{aligned}
3669: &\text{\S7 Hochster (Grass$(R)$): incidence for } (B\circ A=0)\ \text{and determinantal ranks}
3670: \\
3671: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{$\sigma_\infty$--face anti-cycles } \rightsquigarrow\ \text{circular-complex loci } \Comp_f(\bd_f,r_f)
3672: \ \text{(iterated incidence over }\prod \mathbf{Gr}(\cdot)\text{)}
3673: \\
3674: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{surface algebras/orders: }\Rep(\Lambda(\Gamma),\bd)\ \text{assembled by gluing (fibre products) of the face loci}
3675: \\
3676: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{Magyar/Lusztig: cyclic-quiver loci } \hookrightarrow\ \text{affine Schubert/affine Grassmannian models}
3677: \\
3678: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{generalized minors / Pl\"ucker coordinates on Grassmannians}
3679: \\
3680: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{positive Grassmannian charts (Postnikov; Arkani--Hamed et al.) and total nonnegativity/positivity of minors}
3681: \\
3682: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{totally nonnegative/positive transfer matrices}\ +\ \text{cycle-reversal/detailed balance}
3683: \\
3684: &\Longrightarrow\ \text{self-adjoint (symmetrized) modular/Hamiltonian generator (cf.\ Proposition~\ref{prop:tp_detailed_balance_modular}).}
3685: \end{aligned}
3686: \end{equation}
3687: 
3688: We stress that the ``vertex-cycle'' $(\sigma_0,\sigma_1)$ picture and the ``face-cycle'' $(\sigma_\infty)$ picture play \emph{complementary} roles:
3689: \begin{itemize}
3690: \item face cycles ($\sigma_\infty$) give the \emph{relations} $A_{i+1}A_i=0$, hence the determinantal/incidence geometry and the Hochster--Weyman package;
3691: \item vertex cycles ($\sigma_0,\sigma_1$) give the \emph{cycle/necklace observables} generating $\GL/\SL$ invariants, hence the natural positive coordinate functions whose positivity we propagate through the Grassmannian atlas.
3692: \end{itemize}
3693: 
3694: 
3695: \subsection{Total positivity via the positive Grassmannian}\label{subsec:positivity-positive-grassmannian}
3696: 
3697: Finally, we spell out why the Grassmannian/incidence realization is the appropriate setting for
3698: \emph{total positivity}, and why this is compatible with the cycle/necklace invariant picture.
3699: 
3700: \begin{itemize}
3701: \item (\emph{Pl\"ucker positivity.})  On the totally positive part $\mathbf{Gr}_k(n)_{>0}$, all Pl\"ucker coordinates
3702: (minors) are strictly positive.  In such a chart, the Hochster multipliers in
3703: \eqref{eq:hochster-complement-minors} become \emph{positive} ratios of complementary minors, hence are positive
3704: functions.
3705: 
3706: \item (\emph{Subtraction-free parametrizations.})  Postnikov's network parametrizations and the cluster/positroid
3707: structure on $\mathbf{Gr}_k(n)$ provide subtraction-free coordinate charts on $\mathbf{Gr}_k(n)_{>0}$, ensuring that
3708: all Pl\"ucker coordinates (and therefore all determinantal semi-invariants built from them) are positive Laurent
3709: polynomials in positive variables \cite{Postnikov2006TotalPositivityNetworks,ArkaniHamedBourjailyCachazoGoncharovPostnikovTrnka2012PositiveGrassmannian}.
3710: 
3711: \item (\emph{From determinantal generators to cycle/necklace generators.})  In the quiver invariant setting,
3712: Procesi--Le~Bruyn--Lusztig show that $\GL$- and $\SL$-invariants are generated by traces along oriented cycles
3713: (``necklaces'').  Under the determinantal/Grassmannian incarnation of complexes, these traces are obtained from
3714: matrix coefficient functions built from minors and multipliers; hence positivity of minors implies positivity of
3715: cycle invariants on the corresponding positive loci.
3716: \end{itemize}
3717: 
3718: This is the precise sense in which the Hochster--Weyman--Grassmann package upgrades ``wiring diagram positivity''
3719: to a geometric positivity principle compatible with both the representation-theoretic filtration and the
3720: surface/dessin cycle calculus used elsewhere in this paper.
3721: 
3722: 
3723: \begin{corollary}
3724: 			The acoordinate ring of a variety of circular complexes has a basis given by standard multitableaux.  
3725: 			Further, the coordinate ring is normal and Cohen-Macaulay. 
3726: 		\end{corollary}
3727: 		
3728: 		
3729: 		\section{Examples Calculations}
3730: 		Let us look at a few examples of the computations mentioned in the previous section.
3731: 		\begin{ex}
3732: 			Let us consider the sequence 
3733: 			\[ \xymatrix{ F_2 \ar[r]^A & F_1 \ar[r]^B & F_0 } \]
3734: 			where the sequence of dimensions is $(3,5,3)$, $\rank(A) \leq 2$, and $\rank(B) \leq 3$. Then
3735: 			\[ R = \Sym(F_2 \otimes F_1^* \otimes F_1 \otimes F_0^*)/I \]
3736: 			where $I$ is generated by $\bigwedge^3F_2 \otimes \bigwedge^3 F_1^*$, and $F_2 \otimes F_0^*$. Suppose we take partitions $\lambda = (2)$ and $\mu = (1)$. Then we have the corresponding sequence of maps
3737: 			\[ \xymatrix{ \bigwedge^4F_1 \ar[d] \\
3738: 				\bigwedge^2 F_2 \otimes \bigwedge^2 F_1^* \otimes F_1 \otimes F_0^* \ar[d] \\
3739: 				\bigwedge^2F_2 \otimes S_{(1,0,0,-1,-1)}F_1 \otimes F_3^* }\]
3740: 			where $S_{(1,0,0,-1,-1)}F_1 \cong L_{(3,1)}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^5F_1^*$, and $\bigwedge^2F_1 \otimes F_1^* \cong (\bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^5F_1^*) \otimes F_1 \cong \bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes F_1$ (in an $\SL(F_1)$-equivariant way). The map $\Delta: \bigwedge^4F_1 \to \bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes F_1$ is given by
3741: 			\[ f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_4} \mapsto \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}}\sgn(\sigma) f_{\sigma(j_1)} \wedge f_{\sigma(j_2)} \wedge f_{\sigma(j_3)} \otimes f_{\sigma(j_4)} \]
3742: 			The isomorphism $f_{\sigma(j_1)} \wedge f_{\sigma( j_2)} \wedge f_{\sigma( j_3)} \mapsto f_{j_1'(\sigma)}^* \wedge f_{j_2'(\sigma)}^*$ of $\bigwedge^3 F_1 \cong \bigwedge^2F_1^*$, where $\{j_1'(\sigma), j_2'(\sigma)\}$ is the complement of $\{\sigma(j_1),\sigma( j_2),\sigma( j_3)\} \subset [1,5]$, then induces the map 
3743: 			\[ \bigwedge^4 F_1 \to \bigwedge^2F_1^* \otimes F_1 \]
3744: 			giving
3745: 			\[ f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_4} \mapsto \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}} \sgn(\sigma) f_{j_1'(\sigma)}^* \wedge f_{j_2'(\sigma)}^* \otimes f_{\sigma(j_4)} \]
3746: 			where we sum over all $\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}$ or equivalently over all the complements $\{j_1'(\sigma), j_2'(\sigma)\} = [1,5]-\{\sigma(j_1), \sigma(j_2), \sigma(j_3)\}$, one pair for each $\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}$. Fixing $a, b, c$, this then corresponds to an element
3747: 			\[ (e_a \wedge e_b) \otimes \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}} \sgn(\sigma) f_{j_1'(\sigma)}^* \wedge f_{j_2'(\sigma)}^* \otimes f_{\sigma(j_4)} \otimes g_c^* \]
3748: 			which corresponds to matrix elements
3749: 			\begin{align*}
3750: 				\sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}}\sgn(\sigma)[(A_{j_1'(\sigma),b}A_{j_2'(\sigma),a}-A_{j_1'(\sigma),a}A_{j_2'(\sigma),b}) \cdot B_{b, \sigma(j_4)}] \\
3751: 				= \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}}\sgn(\sigma)[(BA)_{j_1'(\sigma),\sigma(j_4)}A_{j_2'(\sigma),a} - (BA)_{j_2'(\sigma),\sigma(j_4)}A_{j_1'(\sigma),a}].
3752: 			\end{align*}
3753: 			which is in the ideal $I$ generated by $F_2 \otimes F_0^*$ (and $\bigwedge^3F_2 \otimes \bigwedge^3F_1^*$). 
3754: 		\end{ex}
3755: 		
3756: 		\begin{ex}
3757: 			Let us now choose $\lambda = (2)$ and $\mu = (2)$. We have the following map,
3758: 			\[ \xymatrix{ \bigwedge^2 F_2 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1^* \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_0^* \ar[d] \\
3759: 				\bigwedge^2F_2 \otimes S_{(1,1,0,-1,-1)}F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_0^*
3760: 			}.\]
3761: 			We compute the kernel via the following maps
3762: 			\begin{enumerate}
3763: 				\item $\bigwedge^4F_1 \otimes F_1 \to \bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1$,
3764: 				\item $F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^4F_1 \to \bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1$,
3765: 				\item $\bigwedge^5F_1 \to \bigwedge^3 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1$. 
3766: 			\end{enumerate}
3767: 			The first map is given by
3768: 			\[ f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_4} \otimes f_k \mapsto \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}}\sgn(\sigma)f_{\sigma(j_1)} \wedge f_{\sigma(j_2)} \wedge f_{\sigma(j_3)} \otimes f_{\sigma(j_4)} \wedge f_k, \]
3769: 			which corresponds to the element
3770: 			\[\sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}}\sgn(\sigma)f_{j_1'(\sigma)}^* \wedge f_{j_2'(\sigma)}^* \otimes f_{\sigma(j_4)} \wedge f_k. \]
3771: 			Fixing $a, b, c, d$ we get a corresponding element 
3772: 			\[ (e_a \wedge e_b) \otimes \left(\sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}}\sgn(\sigma)f_{j_1'(\sigma)}^* \wedge f_{j_2'(\sigma)}^* \otimes f_{\sigma(j_4)} \wedge f_k\right) \otimes (g_c^* \wedge g_d^*).\]
3773: 			where we associate the complement $\{j_1'(\sigma, k), j_2'(\sigma, k)\}$ of $\{\sigma(j_1), \sigma(j_2), \sigma(j_3)\}$ in $[1,5]$ to each $\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}$, and sum over all such pairs (or equivalently over all $\sigma$). This gives us the corresponding element
3774: 			\[ \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(3,1)}} \sgn(\sigma)(A_{j_1'(\sigma),a}A_{j_2'(\sigma),b}-A_{j_2'(\sigma),a}A_{j_1'(\sigma),b})\cdot (B_{c,\sigma(j_4)}B_{d,k}-B_{c,k}B_{d,\sigma(j_4)})\]
3775: 			which is an element of $( \bigwedge^2 B) \cdot (\bigwedge^2 A) = \bigwedge^2(BA) \in \bigwedge^2(F_2 \otimes F_0^*)$, and thus in the ideal $I$. Similarly, the map $F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^4F_1 \to \bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1$ defined by
3776: 			\[ f_k \otimes f_{j_1} \wedge \cdots \wedge f_{j_4} \mapsto \sum_{\sigma \in S_4^{(2,2)}} \sgn(\sigma) f_k \wedge f_{\sigma(j_1)} \wedge f_{\sigma(j_2)} \otimes f_{\sigma(j_3)} \wedge f_{\sigma(j_4)} \]
3777: 			will give an element in $\bigwedge^2(F_2 \otimes F_0^*)$, as will the map $\bigwedge^5F_1 \to \bigwedge^3F_1 \otimes \bigwedge^2F_1$ given by
3778: 			\[ f_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge f_5 \mapsto \sum_{\sigma \in S_5^{(3,2)}} \sgn(\sigma) f_{\sigma(1)} \wedge f_{\sigma(2)} \wedge f_{\sigma(3)} \otimes f_{\sigma(4)} \wedge f_{\sigma(5)}.\]
3779: 		\end{ex}
3780: 		
3781: 		\begin{remark}[Coordinate rings via Grassmannians]
3782: The coordinate ring of a variety of complexes can be identified with a closed subring of a product of coordinate rings of Grassmannians.  We develop this identification systematically in the next subsection, following Hochster's approach.
3783: \end{remark}
3784: 
3785: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
3786: \subsection{Grassmannian models over a commutative base ring: Hochster's $\mathrm{Grass}(R)$ viewpoint for varieties of complexes}
3787: \label{sec:hochster_grassR_varieties_of_complexes}
3788: 
3789: The ``variety of complexes'' presentation above writes down equations directly in a polynomial ring $S=k[X^{(k)}_{i(k),j(k)}]$.
3790: For many purposes (base change, degeneration, positivity) it is better to keep track of \emph{why} these are the right equations: they are \emph{rank and incidence conditions} on submodules that are naturally parametrized by Grassmannians.
3791: This is the perspective taken systematically in Hochster's geometric treatment of determinantal loci and related exactness problems, and it is the reason the notation $\mathrm{Grass}(R)$ (Grassmannians over a commutative base ring $R$) is so effective \cite{Hochster1975TopicsHomological,HochsterEagon1971GenericPerfection}.
3792: 
3793: \paragraph{Grassmannians over a commutative ring.}
3794: Fix a commutative ring $R$ and integers $0<r<n$.
3795: Let $\mathbf{Gr}_R(r,n)$ denote the Grassmannian over $R$ representing the functor
3796: \[
3797: S\ \longmapsto\ \Bigl\{\text{rank-$r$ locally free direct summands } \mathcal{S}\subset S^n\Bigr\}
3798: \]
3799: on commutative $R$-algebras $S$.
3800: Equivalently, $\mathbf{Gr}_R(r,n)$ parametrizes rank-$r$ quotients of $S^n$.
3801: It carries the universal exact sequence of vector bundles
3802: \begin{equation}
3803: \label{eq:universal_grass_sequence}
3804: 0\ \longrightarrow\ \mathcal{S}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{Gr}}^{\oplus n}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathcal{Q}\ \longrightarrow\ 0,
3805: \end{equation}
3806: whose formation commutes with base change in $R$.
3807: Pl\"ucker coordinates are global sections of $\bigwedge^r\mathcal{S}^\vee$ and furnish the standard closed immersion $\mathbf{Gr}_R(r,n)\hookrightarrow \mathbb{P}^{\binom{n}{r}-1}_R$.
3808: 
3809: \paragraph{Pl\"ucker/straightening relations as \emph{defining relations}.}
3810: Over any base ring, the homogeneous coordinate ring of $\mathbf{Gr}_R(r,n)$ is generated by Pl\"ucker variables $p_I$ indexed by $r$-subsets $I\subset\{1,\dots,n\}$, with defining ideal generated by the quadratic Pl\"ucker relations.
3811: Standard monomial theory (in the form developed for Grassmannians and their Schubert subvarieties) provides:
3812: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
3813: \item a monomial basis of the coordinate ring (standard monomials / standard tableaux),
3814: \item explicit ``straightening'' relations expressing nonstandard monomials as $R$-linear combinations of standard ones, and
3815: \item good singularity properties (normality, Cohen--Macaulayness) for many determinantal/rank loci, proved geometrically via Grassmannians.
3816: \end{enumerate}
3817: Hochster--Eagon's principal radical system method is a foundational result in this direction: it shows that classical determinantal loci have Cohen--Macaulay and normal coordinate rings \cite{HochsterEagon1971GenericPerfection}.
3818: 
3819: \begin{theorem}[Hochster--Eagon: generic determinantal loci are Cohen--Macaulay and normal]
3820: \label{thm:hochster_eagon_determinantal}
3821: Let $R$ be a Noetherian ring and let $X$ be a generic $m\times n$ matrix of indeterminates over $R$.
3822: Let $I_t(X)$ be the ideal generated by the $t\times t$ minors.
3823: Then the determinantal ring $R[X]/I_t(X)$ is Cohen--Macaulay, and in the domain case it is normal.
3824: \end{theorem}
3825: 
3826: \begin{proof}[Reference]
3827: See \cite{HochsterEagon1971GenericPerfection} and the expository treatment in \cite{Hochster1975TopicsHomological}.
3828: \end{proof}
3829: 
3830: \paragraph{Varieties of complexes as incidence loci in products of Grassmannians.}
3831: Now fix a complex of finite free $R$-modules (or vector spaces over a field)
3832: \[
3833: F_n \xrightarrow{d_n} F_{n-1}\xrightarrow{d_{n-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d_2}F_1\xrightarrow{d_1}F_0,
3834: \qquad d_{i-1}\circ d_i = 0,
3835: \]
3836: and fix a rank sequence $\mathbf{r}=(r_1,\dots,r_n)$ with $0\le r_i\le \min\{\mathrm{rk}\,F_i,\mathrm{rk}\,F_{i-1}\}$.
3837: Set $f_i:=\mathrm{rk}\,F_i$.
3838: For each $i$ we have:
3839: \[
3840: \mathrm{im}(d_i)\subseteq F_{i-1}\ \text{ has rank } r_i,\qquad
3841: \ker(d_i)\subseteq F_i\ \text{ has rank } f_i-r_i.
3842: \]
3843: The condition $d_{i-1}\circ d_i=0$ is equivalent to the \emph{incidence} condition
3844: \begin{equation}
3845: \label{eq:incidence_image_in_kernel}
3846: \mathrm{im}(d_i)\ \subseteq\ \ker(d_{i-1})\ \subseteq\ F_{i-1}\qquad (2\le i\le n).
3847: \end{equation}
3848: 
3849: Introduce the incidence scheme
3850: \begin{equation}
3851: \label{eq:incidence_scheme_complexes}
3852: \mathcal{I}(\mathbf{r})\ :=\
3853: \Bigl\{(I_1,K_1,\dots,I_{n},K_{n})\ :\
3854: I_i\in \mathbf{Gr}_R(r_i,F_{i-1}),\ K_i\in \mathbf{Gr}_R(f_i-r_i,F_i),\ I_i\subseteq K_{i-1}\Bigr\},
3855: \end{equation}
3856: where $K_0:=F_0$ and the inclusions are interpreted via base change of the universal sequences \eqref{eq:universal_grass_sequence}.
3857: Then the space of complexes with rank sequence $\mathbf{r}$ is (the image of) a natural morphism from $\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{r})$ to the affine space of differentials $\prod_i \Hom_R(F_i,F_{i-1})$:
3858: one recovers $d_i$ as the composite
3859: \[
3860: F_i \twoheadrightarrow F_i/K_i \xrightarrow{\ \tilde d_i\ } I_i \hookrightarrow F_{i-1},
3861: \]
3862: where $\tilde d_i$ is an isomorphism once ranks are fixed appropriately (on a Stiefel open chart).
3863: In particular, the \emph{equations} defining the variety of complexes are:
3864: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
3865: \item determinantal rank conditions (vanishing of $(r_i+1)\times (r_i+1)$ minors of each $d_i$), and
3866: \item incidence equations enforcing \eqref{eq:incidence_image_in_kernel}, which are bilinear equations equivalent to $d_{i-1}d_i=0$.
3867: \end{enumerate}
3868: This is precisely the ``Grassmannian technique'' viewpoint: relations among minors are inherited from Pl\"ucker relations on the Grassmannians and their Schubert/incidence subvarieties, and base change is automatic.
3869: 
3870: \begin{remark}[Exactness and determinantal grade conditions]
3871: Over a Noetherian ring, exactness of a complex of free modules is controlled by determinantal/Fitting ideals (Buchsbaum--Eisenbud exactness criterion).
3872: Hochster's exposition emphasizes how these determinantal conditions can be interpreted geometrically on Grassmannians and how this geometric viewpoint interacts well with generic perfection and Cohen--Macaulayness \cite{Hochster1975TopicsHomological}.
3873: In our context, this matters because ``exactness'' constraints on persistence complexes and ``rank'' constraints on circular complexes are both determinantal conditions of exactly this type.
3874: \end{remark}
3875: 
3876: \subsubsection{Why positivity follows: positive Grassmannians as a canonical sign atlas}
3877: \label{sec:hochster_positive_grassmannian_positivity}
3878: 
3879: The positive/totally nonnegative Grassmannian $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(r,n)$ (Section~\ref{sec:positive_grassmannian_total_positivity}) is defined by the inequalities $p_I\ge 0$ on Pl\"ucker coordinates.
3880: There are two complementary reasons this is the correct positivity notion for our determinantal program.
3881: 
3882: \paragraph{(1) Subtraction-free coordinates and nonnegativity of minors.}
3883: Postnikov's boundary measurement parametrizes cells of $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(r,n)$ by planar networks with \emph{positive} edge weights, and expresses each Pl\"ucker coordinate as a subtraction-free sum over nonintersecting path families (Theorem~\ref{thm:postnikov_boundary_measurement}).
3884: Therefore, whenever a determinantal semi-invariant or rank condition can be represented as a Pl\"ucker coordinate (or a minor of a boundary-measurement matrix), it is \emph{automatically nonnegative} on the positive locus.
3885: This is the conceptual reason the Lindstr\"om--Gessel--Viennot lemma and wiring diagrams produce total nonnegativity: they are the matrix shadows of $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}$.
3886: 
3887: \paragraph{(2) Straightening with nonnegative structure constants.}
3888: Standard monomial theory gives straightening relations of the form
3889: \[
3890: p_{I}p_{J}=\sum_{(I',J')} c_{I,J}^{I',J'}\,p_{I'}p_{J'}
3891: \]
3892: with coefficients $c_{I,J}^{I',J'}\in \mathbb{Z}$ determined by the Pl\"ucker relations.
3893: On the totally nonnegative locus, every $p_I\ge 0$, hence every straightening step preserves nonnegativity \emph{provided the chosen atlas avoids sign cancellations}.
3894: Postnikov's network atlas does exactly this: in each cell, Pl\"ucker coordinates are subtraction-free in the network edge parameters, so the coefficients effectively contribute with the correct sign.
3895: In the toric degeneration limits used earlier (Section~\ref{sec:toric_semi_invariants_surface_algebras}), each Pl\"ucker coordinate degenerates to its distinguished leading monomial, and the straightening relations become binomial relations in a semigroup algebra---again with canonical positive coefficients.
3896: 
3897: \begin{corollary}[Determinantal loci inherit positivity from $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}$]
3898: \label{cor:determinantal_loci_inherit_positivity}
3899: Any closed subscheme cut out by Pl\"ucker/determinantal equations inside a product of Grassmannians inherits a canonical ``positive part'' by intersecting with the corresponding product of totally nonnegative Grassmannians.
3900: On this positive part:
3901: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
3902: \item all minors defining rank conditions are nonnegative,
3903: \item all LGV/network realizations evaluate determinantal semi-invariants as nonnegative sums over nonintersecting families, and
3904: \item transfer matrices built from these minors are totally nonnegative (and totally positive on the interior), so the Gantmacher--Krein spectral conclusions apply (Theorem~\ref{thm:tp_real_spectrum}).
3905: \end{enumerate}
3906: \end{corollary}
3907: 
3908: \begin{remark}[Where this feeds the rest of the manuscript]
3909: The purpose of importing Hochster's $\mathrm{Grass}(R)$ viewpoint is not only commutative algebra.
3910: It also provides the clean geometric interface needed for:
3911: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
3912: \item \textbf{Base change across arithmetic completions:} the Grassmannian functoriality of \eqref{eq:universal_grass_sequence} matches the diagonal $\GL(\bd)\hookrightarrow \GL(n)$ embedding and the ``local order'' computations in the Artin $L$--function section.
3913: \item \textbf{Determinantal semi-invariants as Pl\"ucker coordinates:} Schofield/Derksen--Weyman determinants and Shmelkin's bidirected-walk generators are Pl\"ucker-type minors in disguise, hence live naturally on Grassmannians.
3914: \item \textbf{Positivity as a structural axiom:} positivity is not a post hoc inequality; it is the choice of atlas (positive Grassmannian/network charts) in which determinantal coordinates are subtraction-free, making total positivity compatible with toric degeneration and self-adjoint transfer operators.
3915: \end{enumerate}
3916: \end{remark}
3917:

3922: 					\section{Representation Varieties}
3923: 					Suppose we have a constellation $C = [\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ and that $Q$ is the associated medial quiver, and $\Lambda = \KK Q/I$ is the corresponding surface algebra. We already know we may partition the arrows $Q_1$, in two useful ways, either with respect to $\phi$, or with respect to $\sigma$. Let us use the partition of $Q_1$ in terms of $\phi$. Denote by $\phi_i$ the $i^{th}$ cycle of $\phi$ and let $Q(\phi_i)$ be the corresponding subquiver of $Q$. Such a subquiver is a (not necessarily simple) cycle in $Q$. Further, the arrow set of each $Q(\phi_i)$ lies in the ideal of $\lambda$. In particular, composition of two consecutive arrows of $Q(\phi_i)$ is in the ideal. Let $\Lambda_i = kQ(\phi_i)/I$, where $I$ is the ideal of $\Lambda$ restricted to $Q(\phi_i)$. 
3924: 					
3925: 					\begin{theorem}
3926: 						Let $\bd$ be a dimension vector for $\Lambda$. Let $\rep(\Lambda_i, \bd_i)$ be the representation variety of $\Lambda_i$ corresponding to the dimension vector $\bd_i$, which is the restriction of $\bd$ to $Q(\phi_i)$. Then,
3927: 						\[
3928: 						\rep(\Lambda_i, \bd_i) \cong \mathcal{C}_i(\bd_i) 
3929: 						\]
3930: 						\[
3931: 						= \left\lbrace V \in \bigoplus_{x \in \ZZ/n_i\ZZ} \Hom(V(x),V(x+1))  \bigg| V(a_{i+1})V(a_i) = A_{i
3932: 							+1}A_i = 0 \right\rbrace \]
3933: 						is isomorphic to a variety of circular complexes, where $n_i = |\phi_i|$. Therefore,
3934: 						\[
3935: 						\rep(\Lambda, \bd) = \prod_{\phi_i} \rep(\Lambda_i, \bd_i)
3936: 						\]
3937: 						must be isomorphic to a product of such varieties. 
3938: 					\end{theorem}
3939: 					
3940: 					\begin{corollary}We may thus describe the irreducible components in terms of the varieties of circular complexes. Let $r: Q_1 \to \NN$ be a \textbf{rank map}, i.e. a function such that for every $\phi_i$, $r_i:Q(\phi_i)_1 \to \NN$ is a rank map for the dimension vector $\bd_i$. If $r_i$ is maximal with respect to the dimension vector $\bd_i$ for each $i$, then $r$ gives an irreducible component of $\rep(\Lambda, \bd)$, which will be indexed by the rank map $r$, and denoted 
3941: 						\[ \rep(\Lambda, \bd, r)\]
3942: 						All irreducible components are obtained in this way. 
3943: 					\end{corollary}. 
3944: 					
3945: 					
3946: 					
3947: 					\section{Filtrations and Associated Graded Algebras}
3948: 					In this section we will describe the coordinate rings of the spaces $\rep(\Lambda, \bd)$, as well as the initial ideals of the defining ideals. We will also describe a standard monomial theory on the coordinate rings, and we will construct the associated graded algebra of the coordinate ring with respect to an equivariant filtration by Schur functors. 
3949: 					
3950: 					\begin{theorem}
3951: 						The varieties $\Spec\left(\fR(\Lambda, \bd, r)\right)$ for compact gentle surface algebras $\Lambda$, are Cohen-Macaulay, normal, and in fact have rational singularities. 
3952: 					\end{theorem}
3953: 					
3954: 					\begin{proof}
3955: 						Suppose $Q/I$ is the quiver with relations corresponding to the compact gentle surface algebra $\Lambda$. Let 
3956: 						$\fR = \KK[\rep(\Lambda, \bd,r)]$ be the coordinate ring of some 
3957: 						parametrizing variety for some dimension vector $\bd \in \NN^{Q_0}$, and let $\fR_i$ be the coordinate ring 
3958: 						of the representation variety $\rep(\Lambda_i, \bd_i, r_i)$. Suppose $r$ is maximal with respect to $\bd$. Then 
3959: 						each $r_i$ is maximal with respect to $\bd_i$ on $Q(\phi_i)$. Indeed, the restrictions on the ranks for varieties 
3960: 						of circular complexes come from the length $2$ relations on a circular complex, and thus the maximality 
3961: 						conditions on $r_i$ do not effect the maximality conditions on any other $r_j$ for $i \neq j$. 
3962: 						We may write
3963: 						\[ \fR = \bigotimes_{\phi_i} \fR_i \]
3964: 						It follows that as a product of varieties of circular complexes, the variety $\Spec(\fR)$ must be Cohen-Macaulay 
3965: 						and normal. 
3966: 					\end{proof}
3967: 					
3968: 					\begin{prop}
3969: 						For an irreducible component of $\rep(\Lambda, \bd, r)$, we have the following associated graded algebra for the coordinate ring $\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{R}(\Lambda, \bd, r) = \KK[\rep(\Lambda, \bd, r)]$:
3970: 						\[
3971: 						\bigoplus_{\lambda: Q_1 \to \mathcal{P}} \left(\bigotimes_{\phi_i} \left(\bigotimes_{\substack{x = h(\phi_i^{\ell}a) \in Q(\phi_i)_0 \\ \ell \in \ZZ/ n_i \ZZ}} S_{\lambda(\phi_i^{\ell}a), -\lambda(\phi_i^{\ell + 1} \cdot a)} V(x)
3972: 						\right) \right)
3973: 						\]
3974: 						where $ha=x=t(\phi_i \cdot a)$, and $\phi_i \cdot a$ denotes the action of $\phi_i$ on $Q(\phi_i)_1$. \sidenote{Recall: $\phi_i$ corresponds to an \textit{anti-cycle} in $Q$, i.e. a cycle of zero relations. We define an action of $\phi_i$ on the arrows of this anti-cycle $Q(\phi_i)_1$, simply by defining $\phi_i \cdot a$ to be the "next" arrow in the anti-cycle, for any arrow $a$. This just corresponds to the cyclic order of the boundary edges of the faces of $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$.}
3975: 					\end{prop}
3976: 					
3977: 					\newpage
3978: 					\begin{proof}
3979: 						This follows from the definitions and combinatorics we have setup for the constellations $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$, their corresponding medial quivers, and compact gentle surface algebras.\sidenote{In particular we showed that every gentle algebra that came from a finite $2$-regular quiver. We called a quiver $Q$ "$2$-regular" if every vertex had in-degree and out-degree exactly $2$. We showed every such quiver had a unique constellation $C(Q) = [\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$, and therefore a unique graph $\Gamma$ cellularly embedded in a closed Riemann surface $\Sigma$. We also showed that all constellations $C=[\sigma, \alpha, \phi]$ have a unique medial quiver, up to a choice of orientation of the surface, which is $2$-regular, and that assigning length two relations to cycles in the quiver around a face of $\Gamma \hookrightarrow \Sigma$, and that this correspondence produces a bijection between constellations and (finite) gentle $2$-regular quivers, and therefore also between constellations and compact gentle surface algebras.} For clarity, we list the necessary information:
3980: 						\begin{enumerate}
3981: 							\item First, we setup a bijection between compact gentle surface algebras $\Lambda$ and constellations $C$. 
3982: 							\item We then defined a partition of the corresponding gentle quiver $Q$, via the anti-cycles $\phi_i$ corresponding to the faces of the cellularly embedded graph $\Gamma \hookrightarrow \Sigma$. 
3983: 							\item Next, we described the representation varieties and coordinate rings of circular complexes and showed that the representation varieties of the subquivers $Q(\phi_i)$ under the partition by anti-cycles we isomorphic to varieties of circular complexes. 
3984: 							\item Finally, we observed that the representation variety $\rep(\Lambda, \bd)$ is a product of such varieties of circular complexes and inherits their geometric properties. 
3985: 						\end{enumerate}
3986: 					\end{proof}

3989: \begin{remark}[Moduli spaces, normality, and circular-complex factorization]
3990: The ``anti-cycle factorization'' of representation varieties into products of varieties of circular complexes
3991: is precisely the geometric input that makes invariant-theoretic moduli spaces tractable for gentle/special-biserial algebras.
3992: Concretely, for a special biserial algebra $A$ the irreducible components of the GIT moduli spaces of $\theta$--semistable representations
3993: are shown to be \emph{products of projective spaces}; the proof strategy reduces normality and decomposition questions
3994: for $\rep(A,\bd)$ to the corresponding questions for products of varieties of circular complexes.
3995: This is exactly the structure exploited above: the face anti-cycles $\phi_i$ isolate the circular-complex factors $Q(\phi_i)$,
3996: and maximality of the rank map $r$ identifies the ``regular'' components where the coordinate rings are Cohen--Macaulay and normal.
3997: Beyond being aesthetically pleasant, this matters later when gluing phases and clock holonomies are treated as learnable parameters:
3998: a moduli space with controlled geometry (products of projective spaces rather than arbitrary singular projective varieties)
3999: is a much safer domain for persistence-based inference and for operator-algebraic completion.  See \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial} for the special-biserial moduli-space theorem and the circular-complex reduction.
4000: \end{remark}
4001:

4003: \section{Standard Monomial Theory for the Coordinate Rings}
4004: 					Now, let us define the block order\sidenote{Let $k[x_1, ..., x_k]$ have a monomial order $\leq_1$ and $k[x_{k+1}, ..., x_n]$ have a monomial order $\leq_2$. Then we define the \textbf{block order} on $k[x_1, ..., x_k] \otimes k[x_{k+1}, ..., x_n]$ by saying for any pair of monomials $m, m' \in k[x_1, ..., x_k] \otimes k[x_{k+1}, ..., x_n]$ that $m \leq m'$ if $x_1^{a_1} \cdots x_k^{a_k} \leq_1 x_1^{b_1} \cdots x^{b_k}$, or $x_1^{a_1} \cdots x_k^{a_k} = x_1^{b_1} \cdots x_k^{b_k}$, and $x_{k+1}^{a_{k+1}} \cdots x_n^{a_n} \leq_2 x_{k+1}^{b_{k+1}} \cdots x_n^{b_n}$.} on the monomials of $\fR$, i.e. with respect to the orders $\leq_i$ on each $\fR_i$ defined in \S \ref{monomial orders} and \ref{Plucker monomial orders}.\sidenote{Remember, it is arbitrary as to how one chooses to order the the components $\fR_i$ in relation to each other.} We would like to describe a Gr\"{o}bner basis for each $\fR_i$ and for $\fR$, via the standard monomials. In so doing we will show the following
4005: 					
4006: 					\begin{prop}
4007: 						The coordinate ring $\fR$ of the parametrizing varieties of representations of a compact gentle surface algebra $\Lambda$ has a standard monomial theory.
4008: 					\end{prop}
4009: 					
4010: 					%	Now, let $\mathbf{B}_{i} = \prod_{\ell \in \ZZ/n_i \ZZ} \mathbf{B}_{i, \ell}$ be the basis for $\Hom_{\KK}(V($ defined in Definition \ref{Standard Monomials}. Then under the block order, we have that $\bigotimes_{s \in S} \mathbf{B}_{s}(r_s^{k(s)})$ is a basis for $R(Q, \bd, r)$. Further, any nonstandard monomial in each $R(\bd_s, r_s^{k(s)})$ may be written as a linear combination of standard monomials, modulo the defining ideal of the irreducible component corresponding to the rank map $r = (r_s^{k(s)})_{s \in S}$. We may extend the standard monomial theory on each $R(\bd_{s}, r_s^{k(s)})$ to the ring $R(Q, \bd, r)$ in the obvious way (componentwise) giving the ring $R(Q, \bd, r)$ a standard monomial theory. Since each $R(\bd_{s}, r_s^{k(s)})$ has rational singularities (and is normal and Cohen-Macaulay), we deduce that $R(Q, \bd)$ is also.

4036: 					A representation for this algebra is a choice of two square matrices $(A, B)$ with $A^2 = 0 = B^2$. The finite dimensional 
4037: 					indecomposable representations correspond to words in the generators $\{a, b, a^{-1}, b^{-1}\}$ which do not pass through any zero 
4038: 					relations. These are exactly the \emph{string} and \emph{band} modules. The description of the representation theory of infinite 
4039: 					dimensional string algebras such as this one can be found in \cite{CB1}. The coordinate ring of the parametrizing variety for the

4043: 					maximal in the sense that increasing the rank of either $A$ or $B$ would not allow the relations $A^2 = 0 = B^2$ to be satisfied.

If $j$ is even, then there are $(j/2 \times \lceil \dim V(x)/2 \rceil)$ partitions giving a semi-invariant of weight $j$. 
4098: 					%\end{corollary}

\chapter{Spherical Varieties and Toric Degenerations} · lines 4303–5093
4303: 					\chapter{Spherical Varieties and Toric Degenerations}
4304: 					\label{sec:spherical_varieties_and_toric_degenerations}
4305: 
4306: 					\section{Shmelkin's Deformation Technique and Spherical Actions}
4307: 					In \sidenote{D. A. Shmelkin. \emph{Semi-invariants of Gentle Algebras by Deformation Method and Sphericity}. Preprint.}, Shmelkin develops a method of calculating invariant rings $k[\rep(\Lambda,\bd)]^{\SL(\bd)}$ in the coordinate ring of the parametrizing variety of representations for a class of (not necessarily finite dimensional) gentle algebras. The calculations of such rings for finite dimensional triangular gentle algebras was studied by Kraskiewics and Weyman\sidenote{W. Kraskiewicz, J. Weyman. \emph{Generic decompositions and semi-invariants for string algebras.} Preprint https://arxiv.org/abs/1103.5415}, and later by Carroll and Weyman\sidenote{A. Carroll, J. Weyman. \emph{Semi-Invariants for Gentle String Algebras}. https://arxiv.org/abs/1106.0774}, and later the associated moduli spaces were studied by Carroll and Chindris\sidenote{A. Carroll, C. Chindris.\emph{On the invariant theory for acyclic gentle algebras}. https://arxiv.org/abs/1210.3579}. Shmelkin's method provides an alternate method which is coordinate free, making some calculations simpler.
4308: 					
4309: 					Let $k$ be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, $G=(G,\sO_G)$ a reductive group, and $X=(X, 
4310: 					\sO_X)$ 
4311: 					an affine $G$-variety. The action of $G$ is called \textbf{spherical} if for some point $x \in X$ and some Borel 
4312: 					subgroup $B \leq G$, the orbit $Bx$ is open and dense in $X$. A subgroup $H \leq G$ is spherical if the 
4313: 					homogeneous variety $G/H = (G/H,\sO_{G/H})$ is spherical. Here $\sO_{G/H}=\pi_*\sO_G^H$ is the direct image 
4314: 					of the sheaf of $H$-invariant regular functions with respect to the projection $\pi:G \to G/H$ and the $H$ action of 
4315: 					right translations on $G$.  By \cite{VK}, $X$ is spherical if and only if $k[X]$ is a multiplicity free $G$-module. Let 
4316: 					$U \leq G$ me a maximal unipontent subgroup, and $T$ a maximal torus normalizing $U$. Let $\chi_T$ be the 
4317: 					\textbf{weights} of $T$, i.e. the group of all characters $\chi:T \to k$, and let $\chi_+$ be the sub-semigroup of 
4318: 					dominant weights with respect to $B$. For $\lambda \in \chi_+$, let $V_{\lambda}$ be the corresponding simple 
4319: 					module. Define $\Gamma(X)$ to be the set of highest weights of the $G$-irreducible factors of $k[X]$, with respect 
4320: 					to $T$, for a $G$-variety $X$. $\Gamma(X)$ is a semigroup generated by the weights of the $T$-homogeneous 
4321: 					generators of the algebra of covariants $k[X]^U$. In particular $\Gamma(G/H) = \{\lambda \in \chi_+| \ 
4322: 					V_{\lambda}^H \neq 0\}$. The $G$-variety $X$ is then spherical if and only if $k[X]^U \cong k[\Gamma(X)]$. Now, 
4323: 					$X$ is normal and Cohen-Macaulay with rational singularities if and only if $\Spec(k[X]^U)$ is.\\
4324: 					A sub-semigroup $\Gamma \leq \chi_+$ is \textbf{saturated} if the subgroup $\la \Gamma \ra \leq \chi_T$ has intersection with $\chi_+$, $\la \Gamma \ra \cap \chi_+ = \Gamma$. For $\lambda \in \chi_+$ let $\la v_{\lambda} \ra \subset V_{\lambda}$ be the unique $U$-invriant line of highest weight vectors. For a sub-semigroup $\Gamma \leq \chi_+$ let
4325: 					\[ I(\Gamma) = \{g \in G|\ gv_{\lambda} = v_{\lambda} \ \forall \ \lambda \in \Gamma\} .\]
4326: 					So, $U \subseteq I(\Gamma)$, and such $U$ are called \textbf{horospherical}, and $I(\Gamma(G/H))$ is called the \textbf{horospherical contraction} of $H$. Next, we provide the setup that is important for our interests. 
4327: 					
4328: 					\section{Highest Weight Modules}
4329: 					Let $G=\GL(V) \times \GL(V)$ and $H= \Delta \GL(V) \hookrightarrow G$ be the diagonal subgroup. 
4330: 					Now, let $U \leq \GL(V) \cong H$ be the maximal unipotent subgroup of upper triangular matrices with $1$'s on the diagonal, and let $T \leq \GL(V)$ be the maximal torus of diagonal matrices. So, $B = UT$ is the Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices. The characters $\chi_T$ are all of the form
4331: 					\[ \alpha_{ij} \begin{pmatrix}
4332: 						t_1 & 0 &  \cdots & 0 \\
4333: 						0 & t_2 &  \cdots & 0 \\
4334: 						\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
4335: 						0 & 0 & \cdots & t_n
4336: 					\end{pmatrix} = t_it_j^{-1} \]
4337: 					and the weights of $T$ in $V$ are the characters $\chi \in \chi_T$ such that the weight spaces $V_{\chi} \subseteq V$, 
4338: 					\[ V_{\chi} = \{v \in V|\ tv = \chi(t)v \ \forall \ t \in T \} \]
4339: 					is nonzero. The vectors $v \in V_{\chi}$ are the weight vectors, and $V = \bigoplus_{\chi} V_{\chi}$. The root 
4340: 					datum $\Psi = (\chi_T, R, \chi^*_T, R^*)$ where $\chi_T = \ZZ^n = \chi^*_T$, with pairing $\la \chi, \chi^* \ra 
4341: 					= \chi(\chi^*(t)) = t^{\la \chi, \chi^* \ra}$ for $t \in k^*$. Further, $\alpha_{ij}^*: k^* \to \GL(V)$ with 
4342: 					\[\alpha_{ij}^*(t) = \begin{pmatrix}
4343: 						t_1 & 0 &  \cdots & 0 \\
4344: 						0 & t_2 &  \cdots & 0 \\
4345: 						\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
4346: 						0 & 0 & \cdots & t_n
4347: 					\end{pmatrix} \]
4348: 					where $t_i = t, t_j = t^{-1}$ and $t_k = 1$ for all $k \neq i,j$. We also have $R = R^* = \{e_i-e_j|\ i \neq j \}$ and the positive roots $R^+ = \{e_i-e_j|\ 1 \leq i < j \leq n-1 \}$, with corresponding basis of simple roots $D = \{e_i-e_{i+1}|\ 1 \leq i<j \leq n-1 \}$ for the Weyl group $W=S_n \cong N_G(T)/Z_G(T)$, where the centralizer of $T$ is $Z_G(T)=T$ and the normalizer of $T$, $N_G(T)$ is the group of matrices with exactly one nonzero entry in each row and column. Clearly, modulo $T$, this is the group of permutation matrices given by the map $\rho:S_n \hookrightarrow \GL(V)$ where the generators $\sigma_i = (i,i+1) \mapsto \rho(\sigma_i)$ where $\rho(\sigma_i)e_i = e_{i+1}$, $\rho(\sigma_i)e_{i+1} = e_i$, and $\rho(\sigma_i)e_j = e_j$ for all $j \neq i,i+1$. Now, for brevity let $we_i = \rho(w)e_i$ for $w \in W$. Then $W$ acts on the root system $R$. If $\alpha = \alpha_{ij} = e_i-e_j$ is a root, then $w = (i,j) = \sigma_{\alpha}$ is the reflection of $R$ about the plane orthogonal to $\alpha_{ij}$. Since $\sigma_{\alpha} = \sigma_i \sigma_{i+1} \cdots \sigma_{j-2}\sigma_{j-1}\sigma_{j-2} \cdots \sigma_{i+1}\sigma_i$ we have $W=\la \sigma_i| \ 1 \leq i \leq n-1, \sigma_i^2=1=(\sigma_i\sigma_{i+1})^3, \sigma_i\sigma_j=\sigma_j\sigma_i \ (i \neq j) \ra$. 
4349: 					Define the weight $\lambda^* := -w_0\lambda$, where $w_0$ is the longest element of the Weyl group. 
4350: 					
4351: 					Let $Y=G/B$ be the flag variety and $\pi:G \to Y$ the projection. Let $\sO_Y$ be the sheaf of local rings on $Y$. Let $\mu \in \chi$ be a character (equiv. weight), $\mathcal{U}$ a Zariski open set in $Y$, and define a coherent sheaf $\mathscr{L}(\mu)$ of $\sO_Y$-modules by,
4352: 					\[ \mathscr{L}(\mu)(\mathcal{U}) = \{f \in \sO_G (\pi^{-1}\mathcal{U})| \ f(xb) = f(xtu) = (w_0 \mu)(t^{-1})(f(x)) = \chi_{\mu}(b)f(x) \} \]
4353: 					here $(w_0 \mu) = \chi_{\mu} \in \chi$, $x \in \pi^{-1}(\mathcal{U}), t \in T, u \in U$ (where $B=UT$ and $U$ is the unipotent radical of the Borel subgroup $B$). The weight $\mu$ determines a $\GL(V)$-equivariant line bundle $\mathcal{L}(\mu)$ on $Y$, and there is a $\GL(V)$-action on the space of global sections $\Gamma(Y, \mathcal{L}_{\mu}) = H^0(Y, \mathcal{L}(\mu)) = L(\mu)$. If $\mu$ is a dominant weight then Bott's Theorem says this is an irreducible highest weight representation of $\GL(V)$. Further, all $H^i(Y,\mathcal{L}(\mu))=0$ for $i>0$. In particular, 
4354: 					\[ L(\mu)=\{f \in k[G]|\ f(xb) = \chi_{\mu}(b)f(x) \} \]
4355: 					for $\chi_{\mu} \in \chi^+(B)$. All irreducible $\GL(V)$ modules can be obtained in this way. \\
4356: 					Now, let $B^{op}$ be the opposite Borel subgroup of lower triangular matrices. Let $U^{op}$ be the unipotent radical of $B^{op}$. For any simple $\GL(V)$ module, the highest weight vector with respect to $U^{op}$ is the lowest weight vector with respect to $U$ with weight $-\mu^*$.
4357: 					Then let $\Gamma(G/H) = \{(\lambda, \lambda^*) \ | \ \lambda \in \chi_+(H) \}$, i.e. pairs of $\GL(V)$ dominant weights such that $V_{\lambda} \otimes V_{\lambda^*} = V_{\lambda} \otimes V^*_{\lambda}$ contains a $\GL(V)$ invariant point ($V_{\lambda}$ an irreducible $G$-module of highest weight $\lambda$). Then $\Gamma(G/H)$ is saturated. So we then have,
4358: 					\[ I(\Gamma(G/H)) = (U \times U^{op})\Delta T \]
4359: 					where $\Delta T = \{(t,t) \in T \times T\} \leq \GL(V) \times \GL(V)$. Now, let $Z$ be some affine $G$-variety, $H=\Delta \GL(V)$ (a spherical subgroup). \\
4360: 					Now, let $\preceq$ be the partial order on weights in $\chi_T$ ($\lambda \preceq \mu \iff \lambda - \mu$ is a nonnegative integer linear combination of simple roots). The highest weight $\lambda$ is maximal in this order. This yields a partial order on irreducible factors of $k[Z]$ and $V_{\lambda}V_{\mu}$ is a sum of irreducible components smaller than or equal to $\lambda+\mu$ in the partial order. We then have the following 
4361: 					\begin{prop}\sidenote{E.B. Vinberg, B.M. Kimefeld. \emph{Homogeneous domains on flag manifolds and spherical subgroups of semisimple Lie groups}. Funct. Analysis and Appl. 12, 168-174 (1978)}
4362: 						An affine $G$-variety $Z$ is spherical if and only if the $G$-module $k[Z]$ is multiplicity free. Further, if $H \leq G$ is a spherical subgroup, and $V$ an irreducible $G$-module with $\dim_kV < \infty$ then $\dim_kV^H \leq 1$. 
4363: 					\end{prop}
4364: 					\begin{theorem}\sidenote{D. Panyushev. \emph{On Deformation Methods in Invariant Theory}. Ann. Inst. Fourier, Grenoble 47, 1 (1997), 985-1012.}
4365: 						Let $H \leq G$ be a spherical subgroup of a connected reductive group $G$ acting on the affine 
4366: 						variety $Z$. Assume $\Gamma(G/H)$ is saturated. The associated graded algebra $\gr k[Z]^H$ given by the 
4367: 						filtration $k[Z] = \bigcup_{\lambda \in \chi_+} k[Z]_{\preceq \lambda}$ is isomorphic to 
4368: 						$k[Z]^{I(\Gamma(G/H))}$. 
4369: 					\end{theorem}
4370: 					
4371: 					Now, let $Z = X \times Y$ be a product of affine $\GL(V)$-varieties and let $\GL(V)$ act diagonally (via restriction of the action of $G=\GL(V) \times \GL(V)$ to $H=\Delta \GL(V)$). By Theorem 2.4 of \sidenote{D. Panyushev. \emph{On Deformation Methods in Invariant Theory}. Ann. Inst. Fourier, Grenoble 47, 1 (1997), 985-1012.} we have
4372: 					\[ \gr k[Z]^{\Delta \GL(V)} \cong \left(k[X]^{U} \otimes k[Y]^{U^{op}}\right)^{\Delta T},  \]
4373: 					and 
4374: 					\begin{enumerate}
4375: 						\item If $k[Z]^{I(\Gamma(G/H))}$ is a complete intersection, then so is $k[Z]^H$. 
4376: 						\item The number of generators in a minimal system of homogeneous generators of $k[Z]^{I(\Gamma(G/H))}$ is greater than or equal to that for $k[Z]^H$. In particular, if $k[Z]^{I(\Gamma(G/H))}$ is a polynomial ring, then so is $k[Z]^H$.
4377: 					\end{enumerate}
4378: 					
4379: 					Now, let us assume $Z$ is a spherical $G$-variety so that $k[Z] = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Gamma}V_{\lambda}$ for some sub-semigroup $\Gamma \leq \chi_+$. Further, restricting the action we get $k[Z]^U = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Gamma}V_{\lambda}^U$, a direct sum of one-dimensional subspaces corresponding to elements of $\Gamma$. Also, $k[Z]^U \cong k[\Gamma]$, where $k[\Gamma]$ is the semigroup ring of $\Gamma$. 
4380: 					
4381: 					
4382: 					%\begin{lemma}
4383: 					%	If $V$ is a spherical representation of a quiver, $V$ and $P \subseteq Q$ is a subquiver
4384: 					%	then ResP  V   is a spherical representation of P   Proof  Note that the pro jection of Aut V   to the factor Q
4385: 					%	aP GL V  a   of GL dim V  
4386: 					%	is contained in Aut ResP  V     If Aut V   is spherical in GL dim V   then the pro jection
4387: 					%	is spherical in the factor  Thus ResP  V   is spherical 
4388: 					%\end{lemma}
4389: 					
4390:
