4392: 					
4393: 					
4394: \section{Determinantal presentations and toric degenerations: making the bridge explicit}
4395: \label{sec:determinantal_toric_bridge}
4396: 
4397: The abstract spherical-geometry discussion above (Vinberg--Kimelfeld multiplicity-freeness and Panyushev's deformation theorem) becomes computationally useful once one identifies \emph{explicit} $U$--covariants and their relations.
4398: In essentially all representation-theoretic situations that occur in this manuscript, those covariants are given by \emph{determinants}---minors of matrices encoding linear maps along paths in quivers, or Pl\"ucker-type minors on Grassmannians and flag varieties.
4399: This is precisely the input that makes toric degenerations accessible: straightening relations among determinants (Laplace and Cauchy--Binet identities) degenerate to binomial relations in a semigroup algebra.
4400: 
4401: The goal of this section is therefore twofold:
4402: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
4403: \item to record, in a self-contained determinantal form, the standard relations that govern these covariants (Pl\"ucker/straightening identities), and
4404: \item to explain why the corresponding associated graded rings are toric (semigroup) rings, matching the semigroup picture $k[Z]^U\cong k[\Gamma(Z)]$ in the spherical setting.
4405: \end{enumerate}
4406: 
4407: We begin with the classical test case of Grassmannians and Schubert varieties, because it is the cleanest place where determinantal identities, standard monomial theory, and toric degenerations can all be written down explicitly.
4408: We then connect to the determinantal geometry of \emph{circular complexes} used in the special-biserial moduli-space theorem of Carroll--Chindris--Kinser--Weyman \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial} and to Schreiber's affine-Grassmannian viewpoint for surface orders \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasII}.
4409: 
4410: \subsection{Grassmannians: Pl\"ucker coordinates and determinantal relations}
4411: Fix a field $K$ of characteristic $0$ and an $n$--dimensional $K$--vector space $V$.
4412: For $1\le k\le n$, the Grassmannian $\mathbf{Gr}(k,V)$ is a projective $K$--variety with a natural action of $G=\GL(V)$, and it is a basic example of a spherical $G$--variety.
4413: 
4414: Choose a basis of $V$ and identify a point of $\mathbf{Gr}(k,V)$ with the row span of a full-rank $k\times n$ matrix $X$.
4415: For each $k$--subset $I=\{i_1<\cdots<i_k\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}$, denote by
4416: \[
4417: p_I(X):=\det(X_I)
4418: \]
4419: the maximal minor of $X$ in columns $I$.
4420: The Pl\"ucker embedding
4421: \[
4422: \mathbf{Gr}(k,V)\hookrightarrow \PP\!\bigl(\bigwedge\nolimits^k V\bigr)
4423: \]
4424: has homogeneous coordinates $\{p_I\}$, and the homogeneous coordinate ring of $\mathbf{Gr}(k,V)$ is the subring of $K[p_I]$ cut out by the Pl\"ucker relations.
4425: 
4426: \begin{theorem}[Pl\"ucker relations as a determinantal identity]
4427: \label{thm:plucker_determinantal}
4428: Fix a $(k\!-\!1)$--subset $A\subset\{1,\dots,n\}$ and a $(k\!+\!1)$--subset $B=\{b_1<\cdots<b_{k+1}\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}$.
4429: Then the Pl\"ucker coordinates satisfy the quadratic identity
4430: \begin{equation}
4431: \label{eq:plucker_relation}
4432: \sum_{r=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{r-1}\,p_{A\cup\{b_r\}}\;p_{B\setminus\{b_r\}} \;=\; 0.
4433: \end{equation}
4434: Equivalently, the $2\times 2$ minors of the matrix of Pl\"ucker coordinates obtained by arranging $\{p_{A\cup\{b_r\}}\}$ and $\{p_{B\setminus\{b_r\}}\}$ in columns vanish.
4435: \end{theorem}
4436: 
4437: \begin{proof}
4438: Let $W\in\mathbf{Gr}(k,V)$ and choose vectors $w_1,\dots,w_{k+1}\in W$.
4439: Since $\dim W=k$, we have $w_1\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{k+1}=0$ in $\bigwedge^{k+1}V$.
4440: Expand each $w_j$ in the fixed basis of $V$ and apply multilinearity of the wedge product.
4441: The coefficient of any basis wedge $e_{i_0}\wedge e_{i_1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{i_k}$ is a $(k\!+\!1)\times(k\!+\!1)$ determinant built from the coordinate matrix of the $w_j$'s.
4442: Grouping terms by Laplace expansion along the column indexed by $b_r$ produces exactly \eqref{eq:plucker_relation}, with the signs coming from the alternating nature of the wedge.
4443: \end{proof}
4444: 
4445: \begin{remark}[Straightening law viewpoint]
4446: The quadratic relations \eqref{eq:plucker_relation} are the prototypical \emph{straightening relations} for minors.
4447: They are the degree-$2$ manifestation of the general straightening law discussed in Section~\ref{Straightening Law 1}, and they imply that products of incomparable Pl\"ucker coordinates can be rewritten as $K$--linear combinations of \emph{standard} products (chains in the dominance/poset order on $k$--subsets).
4448: \end{remark}
4449: 
4450: \subsection{Schubert varieties: rank conditions and determinantal ideals}
4451: Fix a complete flag $0\subset F_1\subset\cdots\subset F_n=V$.
4452: Schubert subvarieties of $\mathbf{Gr}(k,V)$ are cut out by \emph{rank conditions}
4453: \[
4454: \dim(W\cap F_j)\ge a_j,\qquad j=1,\dots,n,
4455: \]
4456: and in coordinates these conditions are expressed by vanishing of appropriate minors of the matrix $X$ representing $W$.
4457: Thus Schubert varieties are determinantal varieties inside $\mathbf{Gr}(k,V)$ (and hence inside projective space via Pl\"ucker).
4458: 
4459: For the purposes of this manuscript the key point is structural:
4460: \begin{quote}
4461: \emph{the coordinate rings of Schubert varieties admit standard monomial theory, so their defining relations are governed by determinantal straightening.}
4462: \end{quote}
4463: This is the mechanism that makes toric degenerations and multiplicity-free filtrations compatible with the spherical picture.
4464: 
4465: \subsection{Why this produces toric degenerations: initial algebras of determinantal straightening laws}
4466: A toric degeneration is, at heart, a statement that a non-toric coordinate ring has a filtration whose associated graded ring is a semigroup (toric) ring.
4467: In the spherical setting, Panyushev's theorem gives a canonical filtration: the $\preceq$--filtration by highest weights.
4468: In the determinantal setting, one often uses a monomial/term-order filtration compatible with straightening.
4469: 
4470: The two viewpoints are consistent because the generators (minors) are highest-weight vectors, and the straightening partial order is itself a shadow of the dominant weight order.
4471: Concretely, one obtains a \emph{SAGBI} degeneration in which:
4472: \begin{itemize}
4473: \item the generators are minors (Pl\"ucker coordinates, generalized minors, or Schofield determinants),
4474: \item each straightening relation has a distinguished ``leading'' monomial (the nonstandard product),
4475: \item the associated graded algebra is generated by the same symbols subject only to binomial relations, hence is a semigroup ring.
4476: \end{itemize}
4477: 
4478: \begin{proposition}[Toric associated graded from a straightening law]
4479: \label{prop:straightening_to_toric}
4480: Let $A$ be a finitely generated $K$--algebra generated by a finite partially ordered set $S=\{s_1,\dots,s_N\}$.
4481: Assume $A$ admits a straightening law in the sense of Section~\ref{Straightening Law 1} such that for every incomparable pair $s,s'\in S$ the straightening relation has the form
4482: \[
4483: s\,s' \;=\; \sum_i c_i\,M_i,
4484: \]
4485: where each $M_i$ is a \emph{standard} monomial that is strictly smaller than $ss'$ in a fixed term order.
4486: Then the associated graded algebra $\gr(A)$ for the induced filtration is a semigroup ring (hence the coordinate ring of an affine toric variety).
4487: \end{proposition}
4488: 
4489: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
4490: By hypothesis, the standard monomials form a $K$--basis and every nonstandard product reduces to a $K$--linear combination of smaller standard monomials.
4491: Passing to the associated graded kills all lower-order correction terms, so the straightening relations degenerate to \emph{monomial/binomial} relations among the generators.
4492: The resulting algebra is thus the quotient of a polynomial ring by an ideal generated by binomials (and possibly monomials), i.e.\ a semigroup algebra.
4493: \end{proof}
4494: 
4495: \begin{remark}[Spherical interpretation]
4496: When $A=k[Z]^U$ for a spherical $G$--variety $Z$, the semigroup ring in Proposition~\ref{prop:straightening_to_toric} is precisely $k[\Gamma(Z)]$.
4497: In this sense, determinantal straightening is an \emph{explicit} presentation of the abstract multiplicity-free semigroup phenomenon.
4498: \end{remark}
4499: 
4500: \subsection{Affine Grassmannians, minors, and the surface-order viewpoint (after Schreiber)}
4501: Schreiber emphasizes that loop-group/affine-Grassmannian charts are naturally coordinatized by minors that play the role of Pl\"ucker coordinates in infinite rank \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasII}.
4502: On the ``big cell'' of an affine Grassmannian (or of an affine flag variety) one may write elements as semi-infinite block matrices, and the relevant Pl\"ucker-type coordinates are again determinants of finite minors.
4503: These minors restrict to polynomial functions on the big cell, giving a determinantal coordinate algebra that is compatible with Schubert stratifications (and hence with toric degenerations arising from affine Schubert theory).
4504: This is exactly the bridge between the order-theoretic gluing picture and the representation-theoretic Schubert picture.
4505: 
4506: \begin{remark}[Determinantal invariants and toric semigroup rings in the surface-algebra setting (Schreiber~I)]
4507: In the dessin/surface-algebra setting, Schreiber repeatedly emphasizes that natural (rational) invariants are often \emph{determinantal}---built from determinants of local path matrices or from minors encoding how local cyclic modules glue \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI}.
4508: One motivation given there is that these determinantal invariants frequently generate semigroup (toric) coordinate rings.
4509: From the point of view of this chapter, this is not a coincidence but an instance of the general mechanism:
4510: \[
4511: \text{(determinants/minors)}\quad+\quad \text{(straightening)}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \text{(semigroup / toric degeneration)}.
4512: \]
4513: This is exactly the form needed later when we interpret renormalization/coarse-graining as passing to associated graded (toric) models.
4514: \end{remark}
4515: 
4516: 
4517: 
4518: \subsection{Circular complexes: determinantal rank conditions and an explicit degeneration argument}
4519: \label{sec:circular_complexes_determinantal}
4520: We now recall the determinantal geometry of \emph{circular complexes} used in \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial}.
4521: 
4522: Fix an integer $\ell\ge 1$ and an $\ell$--tuple of positive integers $n=(n_i)_{i\in\Z/\ell\Z}$.
4523: Consider the cyclic quiver with vertices $\Z/\ell\Z$ and arrows $a_i:i\to i+1$ (indices mod $\ell$), together with relations
4524: \[
4525: a_{i+1}a_i=0\qquad \text{for all }i\in\Z/\ell\Z.
4526: \]
4527: A representation of this bound quiver is a tuple of matrices
4528: \[
4529: A_i\in \mathrm{Mat}_{n_{i+1}\times n_i}(K)
4530: \quad \text{with}\quad A_{i+1}A_i=0.
4531: \]
4532: The corresponding representation variety is the \emph{variety of circular complexes}
4533: \[
4534: \mathrm{Comp}(n):=\Bigl\{(A_i)_i\in \prod_{i\in\Z/\ell\Z}\mathrm{Mat}_{n_{i+1}\times n_i}(K)\;:\;A_{i+1}A_i=0\ \forall i\Bigr\}.
4535: \]
4536: Now fix a \emph{rank sequence} $r=(r_i)_{i\in\Z/\ell\Z}$ satisfying the obvious inequalities $r_{i-1}+r_i\le n_i$.
4537: Define the rank-bounded subvariety
4538: \[
4539: \mathrm{Comp}(n,r):=\Bigl\{(A_i)\in \mathrm{Comp}(n): \rank(A_i)\le r_i\ \forall i\Bigr\}.
4540: \]
4541: Each constraint $\rank(A_i)\le r_i$ is determinantal: it is equivalent to vanishing of all $(r_i+1)\times (r_i+1)$ minors of $A_i$.
4542: Thus $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ is cut out by a mix of quadratic equations (the composition relations) and determinantal equations (minors).
4543: 
4544: The key geometric fact (proved in \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial}, following Lusztig) is that the ``expected'' rank stratum is dense:
4545: 
4546: \begin{lemma}[Rank-stratum closure equals the determinantal variety]
4547: \label{lem:comp_rank_stratum_closure}
4548: Let $r$ be a rank sequence for $n$.
4549: Let
4550: \[
4551: \mathrm{Comp}^0(n,r):=\Bigl\{(A_i)\in \mathrm{Comp}(n): \rank(A_i)= r_i\ \forall i\Bigr\}.
4552: \]
4553: Then $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ is the Zariski closure of $\mathrm{Comp}^0(n,r)$, hence is irreducible.
4554: \end{lemma}
4555: 
4556: \begin{proof}[Proof idea (degeneration)]
4557: The inclusion $\overline{\mathrm{Comp}^0(n,r)}\subseteq \mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ follows from upper semicontinuity of rank.
4558: For the reverse inclusion, start from an arbitrary point $(A_i)\in\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ and write $r'_i:=\rank(A_i)\le r_i$.
4559: One constructs an explicit $1$--parameter family of representations over $K[\lambda]$ that has generic fibre of rank sequence $r$ and special fibre of rank sequence $r'$ (and hence degenerates to the chosen point after acting by $\GL(n)$).
4560: Concretely, one adds, along each arrow $a_i$, a direct-summand representation supported only on the two endpoints and scaled by $\lambda^{\varepsilon_i}$ with $\varepsilon_i=r_i-r'_i$.
4561: For $\lambda\neq 0$ this summand upgrades the rank to $r_i$, while at $\lambda=0$ the summand vanishes.
4562: This produces a degeneration from a rank-$r$ point to the rank-$r'$ point, showing every point of $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ lies in the closure of $\mathrm{Comp}^0(n,r)$.
4563: \end{proof}
4564: 
4565: \begin{proposition}[Normality via affine Schubert charts]
4566: \label{prop:comp_normal}
4567: For any rank sequence $r$ for $n$, the variety $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ is normal.
4568: \end{proposition}
4569: 
4570: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
4571: Orbit closures in $\mathrm{Comp}(n)$ are orbit closures of nilpotent representations of cyclic quivers of type $\widetilde{A}$.
4572: Lusztig's local model theorem identifies these orbit closures with affine Schubert varieties in an appropriate (type $A$) affine flag variety.
4573: Affine Schubert varieties are normal (e.g.\ by the normality theorem of Faltings for algebraic loop groups).
4574: Therefore each $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ is normal.
4575: See \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial} for this reduction in the circular-complex setting, and the cited local-model inputs \cite{Lusztig1990CanonicalBases,Faltings2003LoopGroups}.
4576: \end{proof}
4577: 
4578: \begin{remark}[Why this is a determinantal proof in spirit]
4579: Both inputs---rank constraints and Schubert local models---are determinantal.
4580: Rank constraints are literally vanishing of minors.
4581: Affine Schubert varieties in type $A$ can be presented by rank conditions on semi-infinite matrices (generalized determinantal equations), and their standard monomial theory is again governed by determinantal straightening.
4582: Thus the normality statement is not a black box: it is a manifestation of determinantal geometry at infinite rank.
4583: \end{remark}
4584: 
4585: \begin{remark}[Back to spherical/toric degenerations]
4586: The determinantal presentation of $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ means its coordinate ring is built from minors and their straightening relations.
4587: Whenever one chooses a compatible term order (or highest-weight filtration), the associated graded ring is a semigroup ring, hence $\mathrm{Comp}(n,r)$ admits toric degenerations.
4588: In the surface-order setting, these degenerations are the algebraic shadow of passing from a glued, curved geometry to a combinatorial skeleton, exactly as in Panyushev's horospherical contraction.
4589: \end{remark}
4590: 
4591: 
4592: 
4593: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
4594: \section{Determinantal invariants for gentle/surface algebras and their toric degenerations}
4595: \label{sec:determinantal_invariants_gentle_toric}
4596: 
4597: The preceding section (\S\ref{sec:spherical_varieties_and_toric_degenerations}) sketched the \emph{geometric} slogan
4598: \[
4599: \text{``spherical''}\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{multiplicity free}\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{toric degeneration},
4600: \]
4601: and then explained how Zelevinsky-style wiring diagrams provide a \emph{combinatorial} parametrization of totally positive pieces of groups and flag varieties.
4602: For the applications in later chapters (notably the determinantal Hilbert--Pólya closure argument),
4603: we need a second slogan that is less poetic and more computational:
4604: \begin{quote}
4605: \emph{The semi-invariants and many derived invariants of gentle/surface algebras are \textbf{determinants} of explicit maps, and under toric/horospherical degeneration these determinants become \textbf{monomials}.}
4606: \end{quote}
4607: This section records the determinantal input with enough detail to be used as a black box later, while keeping the exposition compatible with both the \emph{state-vector} and \emph{density-operator} narratives used throughout the manuscript.
4608: 
4609: \subsection{Schofield determinants and determinantal semi-invariants (quivers with relations)}
4610: \label{sec:schofield_determinants}
4611: 
4612: Let $k$ be an algebraically closed field of characteristic $0$.
4613: Let $Q$ be a finite quiver, $I\subset kQ$ an admissible ideal of relations, and $A=kQ/I$ the bound quiver algebra.
4614: Fix a dimension vector $\mathbf{d}=(d_i)_{i\in Q_0}$ and denote the representation variety by
4615: \[
4616: \Rep(A,\mathbf{d}) \;\subseteq\; \prod_{a:i\to j\in Q_1}\Mat_{d_j\times d_i}(k),
4617: \]
4618: cut out by the polynomial equations corresponding to the relations in $I$.
4619: The base-change group
4620: \[
4621: G_\mathbf{d}:=\prod_{i\in Q_0}\GL_{d_i}(k)
4622: \]
4623: acts on $\Rep(A,\mathbf{d})$ by simultaneous change of bases at vertices.
4624: Write $\SL_\mathbf{d}:=\prod_i \SL_{d_i}(k)$.
4625: 
4626: \begin{definition}[Semi-invariants]
4627: The \emph{ring of semi-invariants} is
4628: \[
4629: \SI(A,\mathbf{d}) \;:=\; k[\Rep(A,\mathbf{d})]^{\SL_\mathbf{d}}.
4630: \]
4631: Equivalently, $\SI(A,\mathbf{d})$ is the direct sum of weight spaces for the $G_\mathbf{d}$-action:
4632: \[
4633: \SI(A,\mathbf{d})\;=\;\bigoplus_{\chi\in X^\ast(G_\mathbf{d})}\SI(A,\mathbf{d})_\chi,
4634: \qquad
4635: g\cdot f = \chi(g)\,f\ \ \text{for }f\in \SI(A,\mathbf{d})_\chi .
4636: \]
4637: \end{definition}
4638: 
4639: The fundamental point is that the \emph{basic semi-invariants are determinantal}.
4640: One convenient formulation uses projective presentations.
4641: 
4642: \begin{definition}[Determinantal semi-invariant from a projective presentation]
4643: \label{def:det_semi_inv_projective}
4644: Let $P_1\xrightarrow{\ \phi\ } P_0\to M\to 0$ be a finite projective presentation of a (finite-dimensional) $A$-module $M$.
4645: For $N\in\Rep(A,\mathbf{d})$, applying $\Hom_A(-,N)$ yields a linear map
4646: \[
4647: \Hom_A(P_0,N)\xrightarrow{\ \Hom(\phi,N)\ }\Hom_A(P_1,N).
4648: \]
4649: When $\dim_k\Hom_A(P_0,N)=\dim_k\Hom_A(P_1,N)$ (a weight/dimension-match condition depending on $M$ and $\mathbf{d}$),
4650: define the \emph{determinantal semi-invariant}
4651: \[
4652: c^M(N)\ :=\ \det\!\big(\Hom_A(\phi,N)\big).
4653: \]
4654: \end{definition}
4655: 
4656: \begin{remark}[Why this is the right determinant]
4657: Definition~\ref{def:det_semi_inv_projective} recovers Schofield's semi-invariants in the acyclic case and their standard generalizations to quivers with relations.
4658: The key facts are:
4659: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
4660: \item $c^M$ is a semi-invariant of a specific weight determined by $M$;
4661: \item varying $M$ (or equivalently varying the presentation) produces enough determinants to generate $\SI(A,\mathbf{d})$;
4662: \item the determinantal nature is robust under base change, which is exactly why these objects are appropriate for arithmetic applications (surface orders, local-to-global gluing).
4663: \end{enumerate}
4664: We use these determinants as the algebraic ``atoms'' that will later be glued, filtered, and completed.
4665: \end{remark}
4666: 
4667: \begin{theorem}[Generation by determinantal semi-invariants (informal statement)]
4668: \label{thm:determinantal_generators_SI}
4669: For a wide class of bound quiver algebras (including the gentle/special-biserial families relevant to surface algebras),
4670: the semi-invariant ring $\SI(A,\mathbf{d})$ is generated by determinantal semi-invariants $c^M$ arising from suitable projective presentations of modules $M$.
4671: \end{theorem}
4672: 
4673: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch and references]
4674: In the acyclic case, Schofield constructed the semi-invariants $c^M$ and showed that they generate the semi-invariant ring; Derksen--Weyman and Domokos--Zubkov recast this in a uniform determinantal framework.
4675: For quivers with relations, one replaces $M$ by a presentation adapted to $I$ and uses the same determinant-of-Hom-map mechanism.
4676: A detailed exposition is standard in invariant theory for quivers and is also the technical underpinning of the surface-algebra program in \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI,CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial}.
4677: \end{proof}
4678: 
4679: \subsection{Weighted Cartan determinants for (locally) gentle quivers: an explicit cycle product}
4680: \label{sec:weighted_cartan_determinant}
4681: 
4682: A second determinantal invariant that is extremely well-adapted to surface/gentle combinatorics is the \emph{Cartan determinant}.
4683: For finite-dimensional algebras it is classically a derived invariant (up to unimodular equivalence).
4684: For (possibly infinite-dimensional) \emph{locally} gentle algebras, Bessenrodt--Holm compute a \emph{weighted} Cartan determinant as a rational function whose factors are controlled by oriented cycles.
4685: 
4686: \begin{definition}[Weighted locally gentle quiver and weighted Cartan matrix (informal)]
4687: A \emph{weighted locally gentle quiver} is a locally gentle bound quiver $(Q,I)$ together with a weight function $w:Q_1\to k^\times$ (or with weights in a formal parameter ring), inducing a grading/weight on paths by multiplicativity.
4688: The \emph{weighted Cartan matrix} $C_Q^w(x)$ is the matrix whose $(i,j)$ entry is the weighted generating series of nonzero paths from $j$ to $i$ (the precise definition is given in \cite{BessenrodtHolm2005WeightedLocallyGentle}).
4689: \end{definition}
4690: 
4691: To state the determinant formula, denote by:
4692: \begin{itemize}
4693: \item $\mathrm{ZC}(Q)$ the set of minimal oriented cycles with \emph{full relations} (every consecutive length-two subpath is in $I$),
4694: \item $\mathrm{IC}(Q)$ the set of minimal oriented cycles with \emph{no relations} (no consecutive length-two subpath lies in $I$),
4695: \item for an oriented cycle $C=\alpha_1\cdots \alpha_{\ell(C)}$, its weight $w(C):=\prod_{r=1}^{\ell(C)} w(\alpha_r)$ and length $\ell(C)$.
4696: \end{itemize}
4697: 
4698: \begin{theorem}[Bessenrodt--Holm: determinant of the weighted Cartan matrix]
4699: \label{thm:BH_weighted_cartan_det}
4700: Let $(Q,I,w)$ be a weighted locally gentle quiver and $C_Q^w(x)$ its weighted Cartan matrix.
4701: Then $\det C_Q^w(x)$ is a rational function determined entirely by the oriented cycle combinatorics:
4702: \begin{equation}
4703: \label{eq:BH_cartan_det}
4704: \det C_Q^w(x)
4705: =
4706: \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{C\in \mathrm{ZC}(Q)} \Bigl(1-(-1)^{\ell(C)}\,w(C)\Bigr)}
4707: {\displaystyle\prod_{C\in \mathrm{IC}(Q)} \Bigl(1-w(C)\Bigr)}.
4708: \end{equation}
4709: \end{theorem}
4710: 
4711: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch and why we care]
4712: The proof in \cite{BessenrodtHolm2005WeightedLocallyGentle} proceeds by a reduction algorithm that removes cycles while tracking unimodular transformations of $C_Q^w(x)$, together with a duality argument (Koszul duality in the unweighted case).
4713: For this manuscript the key output is the \emph{shape} of \eqref{eq:BH_cartan_det}:
4714: a determinant becomes an explicit product of simple factors indexed by oriented cycles.
4715: 
4716: In other words, the Cartan determinant is a controlled \emph{cycle zeta function} of the quiver.
4717: This is exactly the kind of object one can later match to:
4718: (i) Euler products (primes and prime powers), and
4719: (ii) trace/log-determinant expansions $\log\det(I-T)=-\sum_{n\ge 1}\frac{1}{n}\Tr(T^n)$ where $\Tr(T^n)$ is a closed-cycle count.
4720: \end{proof}
4721: 
4722: \begin{remark}[Surface-order interpretation]
4723: For surface algebras coming from dessins or embedded ribbon graphs, the relevant oriented cycles are precisely those encoded by the permutation data $(\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_\infty)$:
4724: black/white vertex cycles (in $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$) and face cycles (in $\sigma_\infty$).
4725: The ``full relations'' vs ``no relations'' dichotomy matches the surface-algebra distinction between cycles killed by the ideal and cycles that remain admissible in the completed order.
4726: Thus \eqref{eq:BH_cartan_det} is a determinantal certificate that the algebra ``remembers'' its surface cycle structure in a rigid, multiplicative way.
4727: \end{remark}
4728: 
4729: \subsection{Regular components, circular complexes, and toric (semi-)invariant geometry}
4730: \label{sec:regular_components_toric}
4731: 
4732: The determinantal facts above become geometric once combined with the special-biserial/gentle structure theory.
4733: Recall from \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial} (reviewed earlier) that:
4734: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
4735: \item irreducible components of representation varieties of special biserial algebras reduce to products of varieties of circular complexes,
4736: \item these varieties are normal and have rational singularities,
4737: \item and the resulting GIT moduli components are products of projective spaces.
4738: \end{enumerate}
4739: A product of projective spaces is the simplest kind of spherical variety and admits many toric degenerations (indeed it is already toric).
4740: This is the geometric backbone behind the ``semi-invariants are toric'' statements used in the Artin $L$-function discussion.
4741: 
4742: \subsection{Semi-invariant rings as semigroup rings: a toric theorem for surface algebras}
4743: \label{sec:toric_semi_invariants_surface_algebras}
4744: 
4745: We now record the toric/monomial conclusion in the form that is most useful later.
4746: 
4747: \begin{theorem}[Toricity of semi-invariants for surface/gentle families (after Schreiber and CCKW)]
4748: \label{thm:surface_toric_semi_invariants}
4749: Let $A$ be a surface algebra (or a gentle/special biserial algebra arising from a clean or bipartite dessin), and fix a dimension vector $\mathbf{d}$.
4750: Then:
4751: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
4752: \item the semi-invariant ring $\SI(A,\mathbf{d})$ is a finitely generated \emph{semigroup ring} (hence the coordinate ring of an affine toric variety);
4753: \item the relevant representation varieties are normal, Cohen--Macaulay, and have rational singularities in the regular components;
4754: \item moduli spaces of $\theta$-semistable regular modules have irreducible components isomorphic to products of projective spaces (in particular, many components are projective lines in the band-module regime).
4755: \end{enumerate}
4756: \end{theorem}
4757: 
4758: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch: determinantal $\Rightarrow$ toric via degeneration]
4759: Combine three ingredients:
4760: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
4761: \item \textbf{Determinantal generators.} By Theorem~\ref{thm:determinantal_generators_SI}, $\SI(A,\mathbf{d})$ is generated by determinants $c^M$.
4762: \item \textbf{Spherical/horospherical contraction.} The representation varieties (and their covariant rings) carry reductive group actions; the Vinberg--Kimelfeld/Panyushev deformation machinery yields a flat degeneration to a multiplicity-free (spherical) associated graded object (see \S\ref{sec:spherical_varieties_and_toric_degenerations}).
4763: \item \textbf{Initial-term monomiality in the gentle/special-biserial regime.} In the gentle/special-biserial case, the determinantal semi-invariants admit combinatorial interpretations in terms of nonintersecting path families (LGV) on the underlying surface/ribbon graph.
4764: Under the degeneration, each determinant has a distinguished leading term corresponding to a unique extremal path family, hence becomes a monomial.
4765: Therefore the associated graded semi-invariant ring is a semigroup ring; flatness transports finite generation and normality properties back to the original ring.
4766: Finally, the moduli-space conclusions follow from the normality results and the decomposition theorem for moduli spaces in \cite{CarrollChindrisKinserWeyman2018ModuliSpecialBiserial}.
4767: \end{enumerate}
4768: \end{proof}
4769: 
4770: \subsection{Artin $L$-functions and surface orders: determinants, monomials, and cyclic subgroups}
4771: \label{sec:artin_L_determinants_surface_orders}
4772: 
4773: The surface-order technology in \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI} is explicitly designed so that local matrix orders compute local characteristic polynomials.
4774: For Artin $L$-functions this is the natural language because the Euler factors are already determinants of Frobenius matrices.
4775: 
4776: We isolate (and slightly rephrase) the statements used later as an ``assisting theorem''.
4777: 
4778: \begin{theorem}[Artin-theoretic determinantal/toric package (after \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI})]
4779: \label{thm:artin_determinantal_toric_package}
4780: Let $K/\Q$ be a finite extension (Galois or not) and let $\rho:G(K/\Q)\to \GL(V)$ be a finite-dimensional complex representation.
4781: Then:
4782: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
4783: \item the local Euler factors can be written as determinants
4784: \[
4785: L_p(\rho,s)=\det\!\Bigl(I-\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p)\,N(p)^{-s}\ \big|\ V^{I_p}\Bigr)^{-1},
4786: \]
4787: with the usual inertia correction at ramified primes;
4788: \item by embedding the base-change action diagonally into a larger $\GL(n)$ (summing over local matrix sizes), one may compute these local determinants inside the hereditary orders appearing in the surface-order pullback construction;
4789: \item the rings of polynomial semi-invariants for these surface-order representation varieties are semigroup rings (affine toric), and the associated semistable regular moduli components are products of projective spaces;
4790: \item consequently, Artin $L$-functions admit factorizations into \emph{monomials} corresponding to cyclic subgroup characters (integral, not merely rational, after choosing the appropriate surface-order framework).
4791: \end{enumerate}
4792: \end{theorem}
4793: 
4794: \begin{remark}[Why this matters for GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya]
4795: Items (1)--(2) explain why determinants are the correct local arithmetic language.
4796: Items (3)--(4) explain why toric/monomial structures are not aesthetic decoration:
4797: they are what makes it plausible that one can build \emph{positive} transfer operators and control limits (weak-$\ast$ and trace-class) across a twist family.
4798: This is the bridge to the determinantal Hurwitz closure argument in the final chapter.
4799: \end{remark}
4800: 
4801: 
4802: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
4803: \subsection{Artin holomorphy via cyclic (Brauer) induction and toric semigroup geometry}
4804: \label{sec:artin_holomorphy_semigroup_toric}
4805: 
4806: Theorem~\ref{thm:artin_determinantal_toric_package} distilled the surface-order input from \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI}.
4807: Because later chapters treat Artin twists and GRH-strength families, we now expand two points that were only implicit above:
4808: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
4809: \item why \emph{every} Artin $L$--function is a \emph{determinant} and, via Brauer induction, a \emph{monomial} in cyclic (hence Hecke/Dirichlet) $L$--functions; and
4810: \item how holomorphy questions can be expressed as \emph{semigroup-algebra} questions that are naturally controlled by toric geometry.
4811: \end{enumerate}
4812: The resulting package is exactly what is used later when we treat Hilbert--Pólya approximants as determinants and then close the argument by Hurwitz.
4813: 
4814: \subsubsection{Brauer--Artin monomialization: from cyclic subgroups to monomials}
4815: Let $G$ be a finite group.
4816: Write $R(G)$ for the Grothendieck group of complex representations (virtual characters), and similarly $R(H)$ for subgroups $H\le G$.
4817: 
4818: \begin{theorem}[Brauer induction / Artin's theorem on induced cyclic characters]
4819: \label{thm:brauer_induction_cyclic}
4820: Every virtual character $\chi\in R(G)$ is an \emph{integer} linear combination of characters induced from \emph{one-dimensional} characters of \emph{cyclic} subgroups:
4821: \[
4822: \chi\ =\ \sum_{r=1}^m a_r\,\Ind_{H_r}^G(\psi_r),
4823: \qquad
4824: a_r\in\mathbb{Z},
4825: \quad
4826: H_r\le G\text{ cyclic},
4827: \quad
4828: \psi_r\in R(H_r)\text{ one-dimensional}.
4829: \]
4830: \end{theorem}
4831: 
4832: \begin{remark}[Why we include this]
4833: This is the exact representation-theoretic statement behind the slogan in \cite[\S14]{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI} that ``Artin $L$--functions become monomials attached to cyclic local orders.''
4834: The surface-order formalism does \emph{not} change Brauer induction; it changes the \emph{geometry} in which one computes the corresponding determinants and invariants, and it provides an invariant-theoretic (toric) way to organize cancellation/holomorphy constraints.
4835: \end{remark}
4836: 
4837: \begin{corollary}[Artin $L$--functions as monomials in cyclic (Hecke) $L$--functions]
4838: \label{cor:artin_L_monomialization}
4839: Let $K/\mathbb{Q}$ be a finite extension and let $\rho:G(K/\mathbb{Q})\to \GL(V)$ be a complex representation with character $\chi_\rho$.
4840: Then there exist cyclic subgroups $H_r\le G(K/\mathbb{Q})$, one-dimensional characters $\psi_r$ of $H_r$, and integers $a_r$ such that
4841: \begin{equation}
4842: \label{eq:artin_L_monomial}
4843: L(s,\rho)\ =\ \prod_{r=1}^m L\!\bigl(s,\Ind_{H_r}^G(\psi_r)\bigr)^{a_r}
4844: \ =\ \prod_{r=1}^m L(s,\psi_r)^{a_r},
4845: \end{equation}
4846: where $L(s,\psi_r)$ denotes the Artin $L$--function for the one-dimensional character $\psi_r$ (equivalently a Hecke $L$--function for the corresponding abelian extension).
4847: \end{corollary}
4848: 
4849: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
4850: Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:brauer_induction_cyclic} to $\chi_\rho$.
4851: Artin $L$--functions are multiplicative in virtual characters and compatible with induction:
4852: $L(s,\Ind_{H}^G\psi)=L(s,\psi)$ for the induced representation (after identifying Euler factors), and
4853: $L(s,\chi_1+\chi_2)=L(s,\chi_1)L(s,\chi_2)$, $L(s,n\chi)=L(s,\chi)^n$.
4854: This yields \eqref{eq:artin_L_monomial}.
4855: \end{proof}
4856: 
4857: \begin{remark}[Meromorphy is easy; holomorphy is the subtle part]
4858: Equation~\eqref{eq:artin_L_monomial} expresses any Artin $L$--function as a \emph{monomial} in degree-one (abelian) $L$--functions, but with possibly negative exponents.
4859: Since degree-one $L$--functions are meromorphic with controlled poles, this immediately implies meromorphy of $L(s,\rho)$.
4860: The problem of showing holomorphy in the critical strip for nontrivial irreducible $\rho$ is exactly the cancellation problem created by negative exponents (Artin's holomorphy conjecture).
4861: \end{remark}
4862: 
4863: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
4864: \subsubsection{Gabriel--Riedtmann ``group representations without groups'': Brauer trees, special biserial algebras, and the surface-order philosophy}
4865: \label{sec:gabriel_riedtmann_without_groups}
4866: 
4867: A recurring methodological claim in this manuscript is that one can often replace explicit \emph{groups} (Galois groups, monodromy groups, or local decomposition groups) by \emph{quivers with relations} whose representation theory retains precisely the data needed to compute determinantal invariants.
4868: This philosophy is not new: a classical and influential prototype is the paper of Gabriel and Riedtmann on \emph{group representations without groups} \cite{GabrielRiedtmann1979WithoutGroups}.
4869: 
4870: \paragraph{What ``without groups'' means.}
4871: The slogan refers to the phenomenon that, in large regions of modular representation theory, the stable/derived representation category of a finite group algebra is governed by a \emph{combinatorial quiver model} (Auslander--Reiten translation quivers, mesh categories, and explicit quivers with relations) rather than by the group itself.
4872: In particular, for blocks with \emph{cyclic defect groups}, the basic algebras are (Morita equivalent to) \emph{Brauer tree algebras}: symmetric special-biserial algebras whose quivers and relations are determined by a decorated tree with a cyclic ordering of edges at each vertex.
4873: Gabriel--Riedtmann's analysis packages this as: a block is controlled by a translation quiver/mesh category that can be written down \emph{intrinsically}, without naming the ambient group \cite{GabrielRiedtmann1979WithoutGroups}.
4874: 
4875: \paragraph{Why this aligns with surface orders.}
4876: Surface/dessin algebras and surface orders are likewise \emph{special-biserial/gentle} in broad families and hence sit in the same geometric landscape as Brauer tree algebras.
4877: The surface-order pullback construction in \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI} replaces local arithmetic stabilizers
4878: (decomposition groups, inertia, Frobenius)
4879: by explicit \emph{hereditary orders} inside matrix algebras over local rings.
4880: From the representation-theoretic perspective, this is again ``without groups'': the local group action is encoded by a quiver-with-relations (or order) whose determinantal invariants are stable under base change and can be computed in a purely algebraic/geometric manner.
4881: 
4882: \paragraph{Connection to cyclic subgroups and Brauer induction.}
4883: Brauer induction (Theorem~\ref{thm:brauer_induction_cyclic}) reduces arbitrary virtual characters to integer combinations induced from \emph{cyclic} subgroups.
4884: On the modular side, cyclic defect is exactly the condition under which blocks admit Brauer-tree models.
4885: Thus, the same ``cyclicity'' that makes monomialization of Artin $L$--functions possible is also what makes quiver models canonical in the representation category.
4886: This is one reason the surface-order framework is well matched to Artin data: it is engineered to remember cyclic local pieces functorially.
4887: 
4888: \paragraph{Connection to necklaces and trace invariants.}
4889: Once a representation problem is expressed in quiver form, the correct polynomial invariants are generated by \emph{cycle words}.
4890: Concretely, Le Bruyn--Procesi identify $\GL(\bd)$-invariants on $\Rep(Q,\bd)$ with the trace algebra generated by necklace traces \cite{LeBruynProcesi1990SemisimpleQuivers}; Craw--Yamagishi/Lusztig extend this to quivers with relations and frozen vertices (Section~\ref{sec:necklaces_LBP_Lusztig}) \cite{CrawYamagishi2023LBPFollowingLusztig}.
4891: On the arithmetic side, the Euler factors are determinants and hence polynomials in traces of powers by Newton identities (Lemma~\ref{lem:newton_necklace}).
4892: Therefore the combined message is:
4893: 
4894: \begin{quote}
4895: \emph{once arithmetic representation data is recoded as quiver/order data, the ``local Euler determinants'' live in the same cycle/necklace trace algebra as the geometric invariants controlling moduli.}
4896: \end{quote}
4897: 
4898: \begin{remark}[Practical implication for holomorphy arguments]
4899: In the determinantal holomorphy program, the difficult analytic step is to control cancellations of poles created by negative exponents in the Brauer--Artin monomialization (Remark after Corollary~\ref{cor:artin_L_monomialization}).
4900: The geometric input provided by ``without groups'' (Gabriel--Riedtmann) and ``cycles generate invariants'' (Le Bruyn--Procesi/Lusztig) is that
4901: \emph{all relevant denominators and potential pole loci can be expressed in determinantal/cycle coordinates on explicit moduli spaces}.
4902: This is the point at which one can plausibly bring toric degenerations and positivity (Sections~\ref{sec:toric_semi_invariants_surface_algebras} and \ref{sec:positive_grassmannian_total_positivity}) to bear on the cancellation problem.
4903: \end{remark}
4904: 
4905: 
4906: 
4907: 
4908: 
4909: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
4910: \subsubsection{Carroll--Chindris: $\SL$ semi-invariants, $\GL$ rational invariants, and rational moduli geometry}
4911: \label{sec:cc_SL_GL_rational_invariants}
4912: 
4913: A recurring theme in this manuscript is that \emph{determinants} are the natural interface between:
4914: (i) representation varieties (and their invariant theory), and
4915: (ii) Euler factors of arithmetic $L$--functions.
4916: The work of Carroll--Chindris on acyclic gentle algebras makes this link completely explicit at the level of \emph{rational invariant functions}.
4917: 
4918: \paragraph{Setup: $\GL(\bd)$--action and its derived subgroup.}
4919: Fix a bound quiver algebra $A=kQ/I$ over an algebraically closed field $k$ of characteristic $0$, and a dimension vector $\bd\in\NN^{Q_0}$.
4920: The base-change group
4921: \[
4922: G:=\GL(\bd)\ :=\ \prod_{i\in Q_0}\GL(\bd(i))
4923: \]
4924: acts on the representation variety $\rep(A,\bd)$ by change of bases.
4925: Its derived subgroup is
4926: \[
4927: G':=\SL(\bd)\ :=\ \prod_{i\in Q_0}\SL(\bd(i)).
4928: \]
4929: 
4930: \paragraph{Semi-invariants as $\SL(\bd)$--invariants.}
4931: The character lattice of $G$ is naturally identified with $\ZZ^{Q_0}$: a weight $\theta=(\theta_i)_{i\in Q_0}$ defines the character
4932: \[
4933: \chi_\theta(g)\ :=\ \prod_{i\in Q_0}\det(g_i)^{\theta_i}\qquad (g=(g_i)_i\in G).
4934: \]
4935: A polynomial function $f\in k[\rep(A,\bd)]$ is a \emph{semi-invariant of weight $\theta$} if
4936: \[
4937: g\cdot f \ =\ \chi_\theta(g)\,f\qquad \forall\,g\in G.
4938: \]
4939: Write $\SI(A,\bd)_\theta$ for the weight space of such semi-invariants.
4940: Because $\chi_\theta|_{G'}\equiv 1$, every semi-invariant is $G'$--invariant and one has the canonical weight decomposition
4941: \[
4942: \SI(A,\bd)\ :=\ k[\rep(A,\bd)]^{G'}
4943: \ =\ \bigoplus_{\theta\in\ZZ^{Q_0}}\SI(A,\bd)_\theta.
4944: \]
4945: For an irreducible component $C\subseteq\rep(A,\bd)$, write $\SI(C)_\theta$ for the restriction of $\SI(A,\bd)_\theta$ to $C$.
4946: 
4947: \paragraph{Rational invariants as ratios of semi-invariants.}
4948: Let $k(C)$ be the function field of an irreducible component $C$.
4949: Whenever $\SI(C)_\theta\neq 0$, any ratio $f/g$ with $f,g\in \SI(C)_\theta$ defines a \emph{$G$--invariant rational function} on $C$, because the $\chi_\theta$--factors cancel:
4950: \[
4951: g\cdot \frac{f}{g}\ =\ \frac{\chi_\theta(g)f}{\chi_\theta(g)g}\ =\ \frac{f}{g}.
4952: \]
4953: Thus $k(C)^{G}$ is naturally identified with the degree-$0$ fraction field of the graded ring $\bigoplus_{n\ge 0}\SI(C)_{n\theta}$ when $\theta$ is chosen so that the stable locus is nonempty.
4954: 
4955: \begin{lemma}[Function field of the moduli equals the field of rational invariants]
4956: \label{lem:cc_function_field_equals_invariants}
4957: Let $C\subseteq\rep(A,\bd)$ be an irreducible component and let $\theta\in\ZZ^{Q_0}$ be a weight such that the $\theta$--stable locus $C^s_\theta$ is nonempty.
4958: Then the moduli space
4959: \[
4960: M(C)^{ss}_\theta \ :=\ \operatorname{Proj}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{n\ge 0}\SI(C)_{n\theta}\Bigr)
4961: \]
4962: contains an open subset $M(C)^{s}_\theta$ which is a geometric quotient of $C^s_\theta$ by $\PGL(\bd)$, and one has an identification of function fields
4963: \[
4964: k(C)^{\GL(\bd)}\ \cong\ k\!\bigl(M(C)^{ss}_\theta\bigr).
4965: \]
4966: \end{lemma}
4967: 
4968: \begin{proof}[Proof (recorded in \cite{CarrollChindris2012InvariantGentle})]
4969: This is precisely the content of King-style GIT for quiver moduli, and is stated as Remark~3 in \cite{CarrollChindris2012InvariantGentle}:
4970: $M(C)^{s}_\theta$ is the rational quotient of $C$ by $\GL(\bd)$, hence the corresponding function fields agree.
4971: \end{proof}
4972: 
4973: \paragraph{Carroll--Chindris geometric traits for acyclic gentle algebras.}
4974: For triangular (acyclic) gentle algebras $A=kQ/I$, Carroll--Chindris prove two structural results that are especially relevant for our later ``determinant $\Rightarrow$ holomorphy'' logic:
4975: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
4976: \item \textbf{$\GL$--rational invariants are rational.}
4977: For every irreducible component $C\subseteq\rep(A,\bd)$, the invariant field $k(C)^{\GL(\bd)}$ is a purely transcendental extension of $k$, with transcendence degree determined by the \emph{generic decomposition} of $C$ into indecomposable regular components \cite[Theorem~1(1)]{CarrollChindris2012InvariantGentle}.
4978: \item \textbf{Regular moduli are products of projective spaces.}
4979: If $C$ is an irreducible regular component and $\theta$ is chosen so that $C^{ss}_\theta\neq\emptyset$ and the $\theta$--stable summands are regular, then $M(C)^{ss}_\theta$ is a product of projective spaces \cite[Theorem~1(2)]{CarrollChindris2012InvariantGentle}.
4980: \end{enumerate}
4981: 
4982: \paragraph{Determinantal coordinates.}
4983: Even more concretely, Carroll--Chindris exhibit a transcendence basis for $k(C)^{\GL(\bd)}$ in terms of \emph{ratios of generalized Schofield determinantal semi-invariants} \cite[Theorem~2]{CarrollChindris2012InvariantGentle}.
4984: This is exactly the algebraic structure we want for the analytic package later:
4985: \[
4986: \text{``$\GL$--invariant rational function''} \quad=\quad
4987: \text{``ratio of determinants''}.
4988: \]
4989: 
4990: \begin{remark}[Why this matters for holomorphy in the Hilbert--P\'olya program]
4991: The relevance for holomorphy is twofold.
4992: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
4993: \item \textbf{Determinants are holomorphic in analytic parameters.}
4994: Schofield semi-invariants are determinants of explicit linear maps built functorially from $\rep(A,\bd)$ (cf.\ Theorem~\ref{thm:determinantal_generators_SI}).
4995: Whenever the underlying matrix entries depend holomorphically on a spectral parameter $s$ (e.g.\ through weights like $u_p=p^{-s}$ or more general ``clock'' phases), the numerator and denominator determinants are holomorphic in $s$.
4996: \item \textbf{Pole loci are geometric boundary divisors.}
4997: On a product of projective spaces, the possible poles/zeros of a ratio of homogeneous coordinates occur only along explicit boundary divisors.
4998: Under toric degeneration, these divisors become coordinate hyperplanes, so ``no poles'' becomes a statement about \emph{nonnegativity of exponent vectors} in an affine semigroup.
4999: This is the geometric origin of the semigroup-algebra holomorphy criterion in Proposition~\ref{prop:toric_holomorphy_criterion}.
5000: \end{enumerate}
5001: \end{remark}
5002: 
5003: \paragraph{Hilbert--P\'olya / Hilbert--Polya bridge.}
5004: The relevance of the Carroll--Chindris picture for the \emph{Hilbert--P\'olya (Hilbert--Polya)} program is that it provides, on the \emph{representation-theoretic side}, a \emph{canonical determinantal coordinate system}:
5005: the generic $\GL(\bd)$--quotient $C/\!/\GL(\bd)$ is controlled by ratios of Schofield-type determinants.
5006: In the analytic part of the manuscript we package these ratios into \emph{spectral determinants} of self-adjoint (or symmetrizable) transfer operators; the point is that the \emph{same objects} now serve two roles:
5007: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
5008: \item as algebraic coordinates on moduli (hence rational/toric control of poles and zeros as divisors), and
5009: \item as Euler-factor building blocks of operator determinants (hence holomorphy and zero-location by operator theory).
5010: \end{enumerate}
5011: This is the explicit place where invariant theory feeds the Hilbert--P\'olya operator construction rather than appearing as a parallel narrative.
5012: 
5013: 
5014: 
5015: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
5016: \subsubsection{Semigroup algebras of holomorphic $L$--functions and a toric holomorphy criterion}
5017: Fix a point $s_0\in\mathbb{C}$ (typically $s_0\neq 0,1$).
5018: Consider the multiplicative semigroup of Artin $L$--functions that are holomorphic at $s_0$.
5019: 
5020: \begin{definition}[Holomorphy semigroup at $s_0$]
5021: \label{def:holomorphy_semigroup}
5022: Let $\mathcal{L}$ be the set of Artin $L$--functions for a fixed extension $K/F$ (or a fixed global Galois group $G$).
5023: Define
5024: \[
5025: H(s_0)\ :=\ \{\,L(s,\chi)\in \mathcal{L}\ :\ L(s,\chi)\ \text{is holomorphic at }s=s_0\,\},
5026: \]
5027: with multiplication induced by multiplication of meromorphic functions.
5028: \end{definition}
5029: 
5030: \begin{definition}[Semigroup algebra of holomorphy]
5031: Given a field $k\subset \mathbb{C}$, define the semigroup algebra
5032: \[
5033: k[H(s_0)]\ :=\ \Bigl\{\sum_{\ell} c_\ell\,h_\ell\ :\ c_\ell\in k,\ h_\ell\in H(s_0)\Bigr\},
5034: \]
5035: with multiplication induced by $H(s_0)$.
5036: \end{definition}
5037: 
5038: The point is that $k[H(s_0)]$ is naturally an \emph{affine semigroup ring} once one fixes a finite generating set of cyclic $L$--functions (coming from Brauer induction).
5039: Affine semigroup rings are precisely coordinate rings of affine toric varieties.
5040: 
5041: \begin{proposition}[Toric holomorphy criterion (algebraic form)]
5042: \label{prop:toric_holomorphy_criterion}
5043: Assume that:
5044: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
5045: \item the cyclic/degree-one $L$--functions appearing in \eqref{eq:artin_L_monomial} generate $H(s_0)$ as a semigroup, and
5046: \item the semigroup algebra $k[H(s_0)]$ is (graded) isomorphic to a polynomial ring $k[x_1,\dots,x_r]$.
5047: \end{enumerate}
5048: Then every Artin $L$--function $L(s,\rho)$ that is \emph{meromorphic} at $s_0$ is in fact \emph{holomorphic} at $s_0$ unless it contains the trivial character as a summand (the known pole case).
5049: In particular, Artin holomorphy at $s_0$ reduces to checking that $k[H(s_0)]$ is polynomial.
5050: \end{proposition}
5051: 
5052: \begin{proof}[Idea]
5053: If $k[H(s_0)]\cong k[x_1,\dots,x_r]$, then the semigroup $H(s_0)$ is free on $r$ generators and has unique factorization in the multiplicative sense.
5054: A monomial expression with integer exponents in the generators is holomorphic at $s_0$ if and only if all exponents are $\ge 0$ (negative exponents would introduce poles because generators are holomorphic and nonvanishing at $s_0$).
5055: Therefore any cancellation mechanism that would allow a negative exponent while remaining holomorphic is forbidden by freeness/unique factorization.
5056: This pins down the only possible poles to the known trivial-character factors.
5057: \end{proof}
5058: 
5059: \begin{remark}[Where surface algebras enter]
5060: Theorem~14.3 of \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI} asserts that the relevant (semi)invariant rings for surface-order representation varieties are affine toric (semigroup) rings and that regular moduli components are products of projective spaces (often $\mathbb{P}^1$ in the band-module regime).
5061: In the cleanest cases, these toric coordinate rings \emph{are} polynomial rings, and Proposition~\ref{prop:toric_holomorphy_criterion} becomes a concrete holomorphy certificate.
5062: Even when the ring is not polynomial, toric normality/Cohen--Macaulayness gives strong control over factorization and valuation semigroups, which is the correct algebraic substrate for the pole-cancellation problem.
5063: \end{remark}
5064: 
5065: \subsubsection{How this feeds the Hilbert--Pólya/Hurwitz closure}
5066: From the perspective of Chapter~\ref{ch:hilbertpolya_density}, the only analytic facts needed about $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ are:
5067: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
5068: \item holomorphy in the open strip $0<\Re(s)<1$, and
5069: \item a convergent logarithmic derivative expansion \eqref{eq:log_derivative_generic} in $\Re(s)>1$.
5070: \end{enumerate}
5071: The semigroup/toric perspective is precisely what makes (i) plausible from the surface-order model:
5072: it turns holomorphy into a \emph{finitary} statement about exponent vectors in a semigroup.
5073: Once holomorphy is granted, the Hurwitz closure theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:hurwitz_hp_implies_grh}) becomes the \emph{final analytic step}:
5074: all remaining work is pushed into constructing self-adjoint determinantal approximants and proving their convergence (Theorem~\ref{thm:determinantal_HP_GRH}).
5075: 
5076: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
5077: \subsection{Back to spherical varieties: why toric degeneration is the correct organizing principle}
5078: \label{sec:back_to_spherical_toric_det}
5079: 
5080: The spherical/toric framework is not limited to quivers.
5081: A general theorem of Alexeev--Brion \cite{AlexeevBrion2004ToricDegenerations} asserts that any affine (resp.\ polarized projective) spherical variety admits a flat degeneration to an affine (resp.\ polarized projective) toric variety.
5082: In practice this is implemented by filtrations coming from highest-weight combinatorics (string polytopes, Newton--Okounkov bodies, etc.).
5083: We will not reprove those deep results here; for us they serve as an organizing principle:
5084: \begin{quote}
5085: \emph{once one has determinantal generators, one should search for a filtration/degeneration where those determinants become monomials.}
5086: \end{quote}
5087: In the gentle/surface setting, the LGV interpretation of minors and the Bessenrodt--Holm cycle determinant formula give precisely the kind of explicit control that makes the degeneration \emph{computable} rather than merely existential.
5088: 
5089: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
5090: 
5091: 
5092: \part{Geometric Methods: Coverings and Hom-Controlled Functors}
5093:
\chapter{Hom-controlled Functors and Covering Theory}\label{ch:homcontrolled} · lines 5094–5450
5094: \chapter{Hom-controlled Functors and Covering Theory}\label{ch:homcontrolled}
5095: 
5096: This chapter upgrades the earlier, mostly combinatorial use of coverings (Chapter~2.5--2.6) into a
5097: representation-theoretic \emph{control principle}: many hard questions about the module category of a
5098: (surface/gentle) algebra $\Lambda$ can be reduced to easier questions on a (typically infinite) covering
5099: $\widetilde{\Lambda}$, provided one works with functors that are \emph{Hom-controlled} in the sense of
5100: Zwara.
5101: 
5102: \section{Hom-controlled functors: the control axiom}
5103: Let $\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}$ be $k$-linear additive categories (for us $\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{mod}\text{-}\widetilde{\Lambda}$
5104: and $\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{mod}\text{-}\Lambda$), and let $F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}$ be an additive functor.
5105: 
5106: \begin{definition}[Hom-controlled functor (Zwara-style)]
5107: We say that $F$ is \emph{Hom-controlled} if there exists a (typically finite) indexing set $\Omega=\Omega(X,Y)$
5108: and a canonical decomposition
5109: \[
5110: \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(F X, F Y)\;\cong\;\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X, \omega\cdot Y)
5111: \]
5112: functorial in $(X,Y)$, where $\omega\cdot Y$ denotes a (deck-group) action on objects in $\mathcal{A}$,
5113: and such that composition in $\mathcal{B}$ corresponds to the obvious convolution on the right-hand side.
5114: \end{definition}
5115: 
5116: \begin{remark}[Why this is the right notion here]
5117: In Galois covering situations, $\Omega$ is (a set of representatives of) the covering group $G$ and
5118: $\omega\cdot Y$ is the translate $gY$. The point is not just a vector-space isomorphism: \emph{functoriality}
5119: and \emph{composition-compatibility} mean that vanishing/nonvanishing of morphisms, dimensions of Hom,
5120: and irreducibility properties can be read off from the cover.
5121: \end{remark}
5122: 
5123: \subsection{Zwara's Hom-order and degeneration heuristics}
5124: A robust way to organize orbit closures in representation varieties is via \emph{Hom-dimension inequalities}.
5125: Zwara's key observation (in various forms, depending on finiteness hypotheses) is that degenerations force
5126: monotonicity of Hom-dimensions:
5127: \[
5128: M \leadsto N \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \dim_k\mathrm{Hom}_\Lambda(X,M)\;\le\;\dim_k\mathrm{Hom}_\Lambda(X,N)
5129: \quad\text{for all }X,
5130: \]
5131: and similarly for $\mathrm{Hom}(M,X)$. In the gentle/surface regime we use this \emph{not} as an all-purpose
5132: classification theorem, but as a \emph{control heuristic}:
5133: \begin{itemize}
5134: \item lift the problem to $\widetilde{\Lambda}$ (where indecomposables are strings/bands and Hom is combinatorial),
5135: \item compute Hom-dimensions upstairs,
5136: \item push the inequalities (and their sharpness) back downstairs using Hom-controlledness.
5137: \end{itemize}
5138: This is particularly effective when $\Lambda$ decomposes face-wise into circular-complex factors
5139: (Chapter~21--22): the cover replaces cyclic constraints by line constraints.
5140: 
5141: \section{(1) Cyclic quivers and circular-complex algebras}
5142: \subsection{The covering picture}
5143: Let $Q=\widetilde{A}_n$ be the oriented $n$-cycle with radical-square-zero relations (so representations are
5144: \emph{circular complexes}, Chapter~21). Its universal cover is the bi-infinite line quiver $\widetilde{Q}\simeq A_\infty$
5145: with the lifted relations, and the deck group is $\mathbb{Z}$ acting by translation.
5146: 
5147: Write $\Lambda=\;kQ/I$ and $\widetilde{\Lambda}=k\widetilde{Q}/\widetilde{I}$. The push-down functor
5148: \[
5149: F:\mathrm{mod}\text{-}\widetilde{\Lambda}\longrightarrow \mathrm{mod}\text{-}\Lambda
5150: \]
5151: is induced by identifying vertices modulo $n$ and summing the translated structure maps.
5152: 
5153: \subsection{Hom-control for the push-down}
5154: In this setting, $F$ is Hom-controlled with $\Omega\simeq\mathbb{Z}$ (or a finite window when supports are
5155: finite), and one has a canonical decomposition
5156: \[
5157: \mathrm{Hom}_\Lambda(FX,FY)\;\cong\;\bigoplus_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(X, \tau^t Y),
5158: \]
5159: where $\tau^t$ is the translation action on the cover (deck action).
5160: Consequences:
5161: \begin{itemize}
5162: \item \textbf{Hom computations reduce to string calculus upstairs.} For $\widetilde{\Lambda}$, indecomposables are
5163: strings (and, in periodic situations, bands), so $\mathrm{Hom}$ is computed by overlaps of words.
5164: \item \textbf{AR-components downstairs are orbit-quotients upstairs.} Components in the Auslander--Reiten quiver of
5165: $\Lambda$ arise as $\mathbb{Z}$-orbit quotients of components for $\widetilde{\Lambda}$; this is the clean mechanism by
5166: which tubes appear in cyclic settings.
5167: \item \textbf{Degeneration control.} Zwara-type Hom-inequalities can be tested upstairs and then transported
5168: downstairs using the decomposition formula.
5169: \end{itemize}
5170: 
5171: \section{(2) Self-injective algebras and AR-translation via coverings}
5172: Many surface-adjacent self-injective algebras (notably symmetric special-biserial algebras such as Brauer-graph
5173: algebras, Chapter~34) admit presentations as \emph{orbit algebras} of repetitive constructions or as quotients of
5174: gentle covers. In such cases, the stable module category $\underline{\mathrm{mod}}\text{-}\Lambda$ carries a triangulated
5175: structure and the AR-translation satisfies $\tau \simeq \Omega^2\nu$ (with $\nu$ the Nakayama functor; for symmetric
5176: algebras, $\nu\simeq\mathrm{id}$, hence $\tau\simeq\Omega^2$).
5177: 
5178: \subsection{Hom-controlled lift of almost split sequences}
5179: Let $\widetilde{\Lambda}$ be a (locally gentle) cover of a self-injective $\Lambda$ with deck group $G$.
5180: Under mild finiteness assumptions on supports, the push-down $F$ is Hom-controlled and preserves
5181: irreducibility of morphisms up to $G$-orbits. In practice this means:
5182: \begin{itemize}
5183: \item almost split sequences in $\mathrm{mod}\text{-}\Lambda$ can be lifted to $G$-equivariant families of almost split
5184: sequences upstairs, and
5185: \item the AR-quiver of $\Lambda$ is obtained from the AR-quiver of $\widetilde{\Lambda}$ by identifying vertices and
5186: arrows along $G$-orbits (a stable translation-quiver quotient).
5187: \end{itemize}
5188: 
5189: \subsection{Connection to Paquette et al.\ theorems and the surface/AR dictionary}
5190: The manuscript already flags a ``Liu--Paquette--'' theorem as the entry point for describing Auslander--Reiten
5191: quivers of gentle surface algebras (Chapter~9 in the roadmap). The Hom-controlled viewpoint supplies the
5192: \emph{mechanism} behind such theorems in the two settings mentioned thus far:
5193: \begin{enumerate}
5194: \item \textbf{Cyclic quivers / circular complexes:} the AR-components predicted by the surface combinatorics
5195: (face cycles, anti-cycles, and their lengths) arise as orbit-quotients of the universal cover, hence can be
5196: recovered from string/band combinatorics plus the deck action.
5197: \item \textbf{Self-injective surface-adjacent algebras:} the stable AR-quiver is governed by the repetitive (or
5198: covering) construction; Paquette-type surface/arc models for $\mathrm{mod}\text{-}\Lambda$ (and for the derived/stable
5199: categories) can be interpreted as giving a geometric fundamental domain for the covering action on the AR
5200: quiver, with $\tau$ identified with the surface ``rotation''/shift on arcs.
5201: \end{enumerate}
5202: 
5203: \begin{remark}[What to check in computations]
5204: For a concrete algebra in this manuscript (e.g.\ a double simple cycle order or a Brauer graph algebra),
5205: to \emph{use} this chapter one typically:
5206: (i) writes an explicit covering quiver $\widetilde{Q}$ (often the universal cover),
5207: (ii) identifies the deck group action $G$,
5208: (iii) computes strings/bands and Hom-spaces upstairs,
5209: (iv) quotients by $G$ to read off AR-components and mesh relations downstairs,
5210: and (v) uses Zwara's Hom-inequalities as a sanity check on proposed degenerations or closure relations in the
5211: representation varieties.
5212: \end{remark}
5213:

5216: 					%
5217: 					%
5218: 					%
5219: 					%
5220: 					%
5221:

5223: \section{Hom-controlled push-down along Galois coverings (Zwara--Riedtmann)}
5224: \label{sec:homcontrolled_pushdown_proofs}
5225: 
5226: \subsection{Setup: coverings of bound quivers and the push-down functor}
5227: Let $k$ be algebraically closed. Let $(\widetilde Q,\widetilde I)\to(Q,I)$ be a (connected) \emph{Galois covering}
5228: of bound quivers with deck group $G$ (in the sense of Riedtmann's covering theory for locally bounded categories).
5229: Write $\widetilde\Lambda := k\widetilde Q/\widetilde I$ and $\Lambda := kQ/I$.
5230: Assume $\widetilde\Lambda$ is \emph{locally bounded} and \emph{locally finite} so that the categories of finitely
5231: presented representations $\rep^+(\widetilde\Lambda)$ and $\rep(\Lambda)$ are Krull--Schmidt and Hom-finite.
5232: 
5233: There is a canonical \emph{push-down} (a.k.a.\ covering) functor
5234: \[
5235: F_\lambda:\rep^+(\widetilde\Lambda)\longrightarrow \rep(\Lambda),
5236: \]
5237: defined on objects by \emph{orbit-summing along fibers}:
5238: for a vertex $x\in Q_0$, choose a lift $\tilde x\in\widetilde Q_0$ and set
5239: \[
5240: (F_\lambda M)(x)\ :=\ \bigoplus_{g\in G} M(g\tilde x),
5241: \qquad
5242: (F_\lambda M)(a)\ :=\ \bigoplus_{g\in G} M(g\tilde a),
5243: \]
5244: where $\tilde a$ is a chosen lift of $a$. One checks independence of choices by the $G$-equivariance of the covering.
5245: 
5246: \subsection{Zwara's ``Hom-control'' identity for coverings}
5247: The key mechanism is that $F_\lambda$ is exact and controls Homs by a \emph{direct-sum decomposition} over the deck group.
5248: 
5249: \begin{theorem}[Hom-controlled decomposition for a Galois push-down]
5250: \label{thm:homcontrolled_galois}
5251: Let $F_\lambda:\rep^+(\widetilde\Lambda)\to\rep(\Lambda)$ be the push-down of a Galois covering with deck group $G$.
5252: For any $M,N\in\rep^+(\widetilde\Lambda)$ there is a natural isomorphism
5253: \[
5254: \Hom_\Lambda(F_\lambda M,\;F_\lambda N)\ \cong\ \bigoplus_{g\in G}\Hom_{\widetilde\Lambda}(M,\;{}^{g}N),
5255: \]
5256: where ${}^{g}N$ denotes the $G$-translate (pullback along the deck transformation $g$).
5257: In particular,
5258: \[
5259: \dim_k\Hom_\Lambda(F_\lambda M,F_\lambda N)\ =\ \sum_{g\in G}\dim_k\Hom_{\widetilde\Lambda}(M,{}^{g}N),
5260: \]
5261: so $F_\lambda$ is Hom-controlled in the strong sense: the Hom-dimension is determined by finitely many
5262: translated Hom-dimensions on the cover.
5263: \end{theorem}
5264: 
5265: \begin{proof}
5266: Write $x\in Q_0$ and fix lifts $\tilde x\in\widetilde Q_0$.
5267: By definition $(F_\lambda M)(x)=\bigoplus_{g\in G}M(g\tilde x)$ and similarly for $N$.
5268: A $\Lambda$-morphism $\varphi:F_\lambda M\to F_\lambda N$ is a collection of linear maps
5269: $\varphi_x:(F_\lambda M)(x)\to(F_\lambda N)(x)$ commuting with all arrow maps.
5270: Writing $\varphi_x$ in block form with respect to the direct-sum decompositions along $G$,
5271: we can project to the $(1,g)$-block
5272: \[
5273: \varphi_{x}^{(g)}:\ M(\tilde x)\longrightarrow N(g\tilde x).
5274: \]
5275: Naturality with respect to arrows forces these blocks to form a morphism of $\widetilde\Lambda$-representations
5276: $M\to {}^{g}N$. Conversely, given a family $(f_g:M\to {}^{g}N)_{g\in G}$, define $\varphi_x$ by putting the
5277: $(h,hg)$-block equal to $({}^{h}f_g)_{x}:M(h\tilde x)\to N(hg\tilde x)$; the $G$-equivariance of the covering
5278: ensures this is well-defined and yields a $\Lambda$-morphism.
5279: These constructions are inverse to each other and are compatible with composition, giving the stated isomorphism.
5280: \end{proof}
5281: 
5282: \subsection{Geometric consequence: smoothness on module schemes}
5283: Let $\rep(\Lambda,\mathbf d)$ denote the representation variety of dimension vector $\mathbf d$ (and similarly on the cover).
5284: Zwara's theory shows that Hom-control implies smoothness of the induced morphisms on module schemes and rank schemes.
5285: 
5286: \begin{corollary}[Smoothness along covering images]
5287: \label{cor:smooth_covering}
5288: Fix dimension data so that $F_\lambda$ maps $\rep^+(\widetilde\Lambda,\tilde{\mathbf d})$ into $\rep(\Lambda,\mathbf d)$.
5289: Then the induced morphism of schemes (on the corresponding strata)
5290: \[
5291: f_{\mathbf d}:\rep^+(\widetilde\Lambda,\tilde{\mathbf d})\longrightarrow \rep(\Lambda,\mathbf d)
5292: \]
5293: is smooth. In particular, for any $M$ on the cover, the étale-local singularity type of the orbit closure
5294: $\overline{\mathcal O_{F_\lambda M}}$ in $\rep(\Lambda,\mathbf d)$ agrees with that of
5295: $\overline{\mathcal O_M}$ in $\rep(\widetilde\Lambda,\tilde{\mathbf d})$ up to a smooth factor.
5296: \end{corollary}
5297: 
5298: \begin{proof}
5299: The tangent space at a representation can be identified with derivations modulo inner derivations,
5300: and the obstruction space is governed by $\Ext^1$. The decomposition of Theorem~\ref{thm:homcontrolled_galois}
5301: shows that the linear maps controlling first-order deformations split into a direct sum over $G$,
5302: hence the differential of $f_{\mathbf d}$ is surjective (formally smooth). Finite-type hypotheses then imply smoothness.
5303: This is precisely the covering-specialized instance of Zwara's smoothness theorem for Hom-controlled functors.
5304: \end{proof}
5305: 
5306: \section{Auslander--Reiten components and TikZ illustrations}
5307: \label{sec:ar_tikz}
5308: 
5309: This section provides \emph{explicit} translation-quiver pictures for the AR component types that occur in
5310: gentle/surface settings: (i) string components with local meshes (diamonds), (ii) homogeneous tubes for bands,
5311: and (iii) components containing projectives/injectives (preprojective/preinjective).
5312: 
5313: \subsection{String components: a 9-vertex mesh (hook/cohook diamond)}
5314: In a gentle algebra, irreducible maps between string modules are generated by the elementary operations
5315: of adding/removing a hook or a cohook at either end of the defining string.
5316: When both ends allow both moves, the local AR mesh consists of 9 indecomposables.
5317:

5319: 	\centering

5421: \section{Self-injective algebras: stable AR translation and the Nakayama functor}
5422: \label{sec:selfinjective_tau}
5423: 
5424: Let $\Lambda$ be a finite-dimensional self-injective algebra. Write $\nu$ for the Nakayama functor
5425: $\nu := D\Hom_\Lambda(-,\Lambda)$ and $\Omega$ for the syzygy functor on the stable category $\underline{\mathrm{mod}}\,\Lambda$.
5426: 
5427: \begin{theorem}[Stable AR translation for self-injective algebras]
5428: \label{thm:tau_selfinjective}
5429: In the stable category $\underline{\mathrm{mod}}\,\Lambda$ of a self-injective algebra, the Auslander--Reiten translation satisfies
5430: \[
5431: \tau \ \cong\ \Omega^2\circ \nu.
5432: \]
5433: If $\Lambda$ is moreover symmetric (so $\nu\cong \mathrm{Id}$), then $\tau \cong \Omega^2$.
5434: \end{theorem}
5435: 
5436: \begin{proof}
5437: For any finite-dimensional algebra, $\tau \cong D\Tr$ on nonprojective modules, where $\Tr$ is the transpose.
5438: For self-injective $\Lambda$, the stable category is Frobenius and $\Omega$ is an autoequivalence.
5439: One identifies $D\Tr$ with $\Omega^2\nu$ by comparing the standard projective presentation
5440: $P_1\to P_0\to M\to 0$ and its dual, using that injectives coincide with projectives and that $\nu$ accounts for the
5441: twist between left and right projectives in the duality. Concretely, the AR sequence ending in $M$ is obtained from a
5442: minimal projective resolution of $M$ by splicing, and the middle term identifies with $\Omega^{-1}\nu^{-1}\Omega M$,
5443: yielding $\tau\simeq \Omega^2\nu$. Symmetry implies $\nu\simeq \mathrm{Id}$, giving $\tau\simeq\Omega^2$.
5444: \end{proof}
5445: 
5446: % === END AR TIKZ + PROOFS INSERT ===
