5449: \part{Total Positivity, Wiring Diagrams, and Positivity of Energy}
5450:
\chapter{Braid Group Action on Local Roots of Unity} · lines 5451–5753
5451: 					\chapter{Braid Group Action on Local Roots of Unity}
5452: 					
5453: 					\begin{marginfigure}%
5454: %						\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{helix}
5455: %						\caption{This is a margin figure.  The helix is defined by 
5456: %							$x = \cos(2\pi z)$, $y = \sin(2\pi z)$, and $z = [0, 2.7]$.  The figure was
5457: %							drawn using Asymptote (\protect\url{http://asymptote.sf.net/}).}
5458: %						\label{fig:marginfig}
5459: 					\end{marginfigure}
5460: 					
5461: 					\section{Zelevinsky's Total Positivity and Wiring Diagrams}
5462: \label{sec:zelevinsky_total_positivity}
5463: 
5464: This section provides a \emph{positivity technology} that we will reuse in several later places:
5465: \begin{itemize}
5466: \item in the QFT discussion (Wick rotation/transfer matrices), where positivity of Euclidean evolution underlies positivity of energy,
5467: \item in the renormalization-as-channel viewpoint, where we want certificates that coarse-grained transfer maps have controlled spectra, and
5468: \item in the Hilbert--Pólya/GRH program, where the analytic difficulty is to extract a \emph{self-adjoint} generator from arithmetic/geometric input without hidden tuning.
5469: \end{itemize}
5470: The relevant mathematics is the theory of \emph{total positivity} and its combinatorial parametrization by \emph{wiring diagrams} (planar networks), developed in depth by
5471: Berenstein--Fomin--Zelevinsky and Fomin--Zelevinsky \cite{BFZ1996TotallyPositive,FominZelevinsky2000TotalPositivity}, building on classical work of Gantmacher--Krein and Karlin \cite{Karlin1968TotalPositivity,Pinkus1996TotallyPositive}.
5472: 
5473: \subsection{Totally nonnegative and totally positive matrices}
5474: 
5475: \begin{definition}[Total nonnegativity and total positivity]
5476: A real matrix $A\in M_n(\R)$ is \emph{totally nonnegative} (TN) if every minor of $A$ is $\ge 0$.
5477: It is \emph{totally positive} (all minors strictly positive) if every minor of $A$ is $>0$.
5478: We write $A\in GL_n^{\ge 0}$ (resp.\ $GL_n^{>0}$) if $A$ is invertible and totally nonnegative (resp.\ totally positive).
5479: \end{definition}
5480: 
5481: Total positivity is much stronger than entrywise positivity.
5482: For example, a totally positive matrix is automatically invertible, and the inverse has a controlled sign pattern (checkerboard up to a positive diagonal scaling) \cite[Ch.\ 1--2]{Pinkus1996TotallyPositive}.
5483: Two structural properties make total positivity / total nonnegativity useful as a \emph{certificate} in physics-style constructions:
5484: 
5485: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
5486: \item \textbf{Spectral reality and simplicity.}
5487: Totally positive matrices are \emph{oscillatory} in the Gantmacher--Krein sense, which forces all eigenvalues to be real, positive, and simple.
5488: \item \textbf{Variation-diminishing (stability).}
5489: TN matrices are variation-diminishing: multiplying by a TN matrix cannot increase the number of sign changes of a vector, a discrete analogue of monotonicity/causality constraints.
5490: \end{enumerate}
5491: 
5492: We isolate the spectral statement we will use.
5493: 
5494: \begin{theorem}[Gantmacher--Krein: totally positive $\Rightarrow$ real, simple, positive spectrum]
5495: \label{thm:tp_real_spectrum}
5496: If $A\in GL_n^{>0}$ is totally positive, then $A$ is diagonalizable over $\R$ with eigenvalues
5497: \[
5498: \lambda_1>\lambda_2>\cdots>\lambda_n>0.
5499: \]
5500: Moreover, the eigenvectors have strictly controlled sign-variation (the $k$th eigenvector has exactly $k-1$ sign changes).
5501: \end{theorem}
5502: 
5503: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch / reference]
5504: A totally positive matrix is an \emph{oscillatory matrix}: it is TN and some power has all minors strictly positive.
5505: The classical Gantmacher--Krein oscillation theorem then implies that the spectrum is real, positive, and simple, with the stated nodal/sign-variation properties of eigenvectors.
5506: A modern reference is \cite[Thm.\ 2.5 and surrounding discussion]{Pinkus1996TotallyPositive}.
5507: \end{proof}
5508: 
5509: \subsection{Planar networks and the Lindstr\"om--Gessel--Viennot lemma}
5510: 
5511: Total nonnegativity admits a concrete combinatorial realization via planar directed networks.
5512: 
5513: \begin{definition}[Weighted planar network and its weight matrix]
5514: Fix $n$ sources $s_1,\dots,s_n$ and $n$ sinks $t_1,\dots,t_n$ arranged on the boundary of a planar disk in that cyclic order.
5515: Let $\mathcal{N}$ be a finite directed acyclic planar graph embedded in the disk, with all sources on one side and all sinks on the opposite side.
5516: Assign a nonnegative weight $w(e)\ge 0$ to each directed edge $e$.
5517: For a directed path $P=e_1\cdots e_m$, define its weight $w(P)=\prod_{r=1}^m w(e_r)$.
5518: The \emph{weight matrix} $A(\mathcal{N})\in M_n(\R)$ is
5519: \[
5520: A(\mathcal{N})_{ij}:=\sum_{P:s_i\rightsquigarrow t_j} w(P),
5521: \]
5522: the sum of weights of all directed paths from $s_i$ to $t_j$ (finite because the network is acyclic).
5523: \end{definition}
5524: 
5525: \begin{theorem}[Lindstr\"om--Gessel--Viennot (LGV)]
5526: \label{thm:LGV}
5527: For any subsets $I,J\subset\{1,\dots,n\}$ with $|I|=|J|=k$, the minor
5528: $\det A(\mathcal{N})_{I,J}$ equals the sum of weights of all families of $k$ pairwise vertex-disjoint directed paths connecting $\{s_i\}_{i\in I}$ to $\{t_j\}_{j\in J}$, with the appropriate sign determined by the induced matching.
5529: In particular, if all edge weights are nonnegative then every minor of $A(\mathcal{N})$ is nonnegative, hence $A(\mathcal{N})$ is TN.
5530: \end{theorem}
5531: 
5532: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch / reference]
5533: Expand the determinant by permutations.
5534: A sign-reversing involution cancels collections of paths with intersections, leaving only vertex-disjoint families.
5535: See \cite{GesselViennot1985Nonintersecting,Lindstrom1973Paths} for proofs.
5536: \end{proof}
5537: 
5538: \begin{corollary}[Wiring/network $\Rightarrow$ total nonnegativity]
5539: \label{cor:wiring_TN}
5540: If $\mathcal{N}$ is a weighted planar network with nonnegative edge weights, then its weight matrix $A(\mathcal{N})$ is totally nonnegative.
5541: If in addition every relevant nonintersecting family exists and all edge weights are \emph{strictly} positive, then $A(\mathcal{N})$ is totally positive.
5542: \end{corollary}
5543: 
5544: 
5545: \subsection{Positive Grassmannians and positive geometry: Postnikov and Arkani--Hamed et al.}
5546: \label{sec:positive_grassmannian_total_positivity}
5547: 
5548: The wiring-diagram technology above can be phrased more invariantly in the language of \emph{positive Grassmannians} and \emph{boundary measurement maps}.
5549: This matters for two reasons:
5550: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
5551: \item it supplies a conceptual explanation of why planar networks parametrize totally nonnegative objects (and why different network moves preserve positivity), and
5552: \item it provides a bridge to the modern ``positive geometry'' viewpoint in mathematical physics, where positivity is not merely a technical lemma but a \emph{structural axiom}.
5553: \end{enumerate}
5554: 
5555: \paragraph{Totally nonnegative Grassmannian.}
5556: For $0<k<n$, the \emph{totally nonnegative Grassmannian} $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$ is the subset of the real Grassmannian $\mathbf{Gr}(k,n)$ consisting of $k$-planes whose Pl\"ucker coordinates can be chosen to be all nonnegative (and not all zero).
5557: Equivalently, $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$ is the closure of the locus where all Pl\"ucker coordinates are strictly positive.
5558: 
5559: \begin{theorem}[Postnikov: boundary measurements parametrize cells of $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$]
5560: \label{thm:postnikov_boundary_measurement}
5561: Let $\mathcal{N}$ be a planar directed network embedded in a disk with $n$ labeled boundary vertices, with positive edge weights.
5562: Postnikov's \emph{boundary measurement map} assigns to $\mathcal{N}$ a point in $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$ (for an appropriate $k$ determined by the source/sink data) whose Pl\"ucker coordinates are given by weighted sums of collections of nonintersecting directed paths in $\mathcal{N}$.
5563: Moreover, every cell of $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$ admits such a parametrization by a planar network, and network moves (square moves, reductions) correspond to changing coordinates within the same cell decomposition.
5564: \end{theorem}
5565: 
5566: \begin{proof}[Reference]
5567: This is the main structural output of Postnikov's work connecting planar networks, total positivity, and Grassmannian stratifications; see \cite{Postnikov2006TotalPositivityNetworks}.
5568: \end{proof}
5569: 
5570: \begin{remark}[Relation to LGV and total nonnegativity]
5571: The LGV lemma (Theorem~\ref{thm:LGV}) is the ``matrix'' shadow of the boundary measurement story:
5572: minors of a path matrix are sums of nonintersecting families, hence nonnegative.
5573: Postnikov upgrades this from matrices to \emph{Pl\"ucker coordinates} and supplies a cell stratification and coordinate atlas on the positive locus.
5574: Thus, in this manuscript, whenever we realize a transfer matrix or a semi-invariant minor by a planar network,
5575: we are implicitly working in a chart on a positive Grassmannian cell.
5576: \end{remark}
5577: 
5578: \paragraph{Positive geometry in scattering amplitudes (Arkani--Hamed et al.).}
5579: The positive Grassmannian reappears in mathematical physics through the identification of on-shell diagrams with cells of $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$ and the emergence of canonical differential forms with logarithmic singularities on boundaries.
5580: In particular, Arkani--Hamed and collaborators build a dictionary between:
5581: \begin{center}
5582: on-shell diagrams $\leftrightarrow$ plabic graphs $\leftrightarrow$ positive Grassmannian cells,
5583: \end{center}
5584: and propose ``positive geometries'' (including the \emph{amplituhedron}) whose canonical forms encode amplitudes \cite{ArkaniHamedBourjailyCachazoGoncharovPostnikovTrnka2012PositiveGrassmannian,ArkaniHamedTrnka2013Amplituhedron,ArkaniHamedHodgesTrnka2014PositiveAmplitudes}.
5585: For our purposes, the relevance is methodological: it is an independent, physics-driven validation that \emph{positivity constraints organize large determinantal computations} and that different combinatorial presentations (networks, plabic graphs, wiring diagrams) are equivalent positivity atlases.
5586: 
5587: \paragraph{Mirzakhani, moduli, and positivity of geometric measures.}
5588: On the moduli-space side, Mirzakhani's work on Weil--Petersson volumes and the growth of simple closed geodesics produces \emph{positive} volume polynomials/recursions and canonical ergodic measures on moduli spaces of hyperbolic surfaces \cite{MirzakhaniModuli}.
5589: In the present framework, this motivates two complementary uses of positivity:
5590: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
5591: \item \textbf{Measure selection (ergodic input).} A canonical positive measure (e.g.\ Weil--Petersson) provides a natural stationary distribution for coarse-grained dynamics on cycle/lamination data, feeding directly into detailed-balance/self-adjointness arguments.
5592: \item \textbf{Positivity of coordinates.} In decorated Teichm\"uller/cluster coordinate systems (lambda-length type coordinates), many naturally occurring functions on moduli are subtraction-free and hence positive on the positive locus; this is the moduli-space analogue of ``positive network coordinates'' on Grassmannians.
5593: \end{enumerate}
5594: We will not attempt to reprove these deep geometric positivity statements here; we use them as a guide for choosing \emph{canonical} positive structures rather than ad hoc sign conventions.
5595: 
5596: 
5597: \begin{remark}[Why ``wiring diagrams'' show up]
5598: A \emph{wiring diagram} is a particularly rigid planar network: it is an arrangement of $n$ monotone ``wires'' with crossings, encoding a reduced word in the symmetric group.
5599: The LGV lemma applies verbatim to wiring diagrams, and (because wiring diagrams are built by concatenating simple crossings) it meshes perfectly with factorization results in reductive groups.
5600: \end{remark}
5601: 
5602: \subsection{Zelevinsky-style parametrization by wiring diagrams (factorization in $GL_n^{>0}$)}
5603: 
5604: One way to \emph{construct} totally positive matrices is to factor them into elementary ``Jacobi'' matrices with positive parameters.
5605: Let $E_{ij}$ denote the standard matrix unit.
5606: For $t>0$ define the elementary upper- and lower-bidiagonal matrices
5607: \[
5608: x_i(t):=I+tE_{i,i+1},\qquad y_i(t):=I+tE_{i+1,i},\qquad 1\le i\le n-1,
5609: \]
5610: and let $D=\mathrm{diag}(d_1,\dots,d_n)$ with all $d_i>0$.
5611: Products of these matrices are automatically totally nonnegative/totally positive, and Zelevinsky-type results show that (up to mild choices) \emph{every} totally positive matrix arises this way.
5612: 
5613: \begin{theorem}[Berenstein--Fomin--Zelevinsky: wiring-diagram factorization]
5614: \label{thm:BFZ_factorization}
5615: Fix a reduced word $\mathbf{i}=(i_1,\dots,i_N)$ for the long permutation $w_0\in S_n$.
5616: Then the map
5617: \[
5618: (t_1,\dots,t_N; d_1,\dots,d_n)\ \longmapsto\
5619: x_{i_1}(t_1)\cdots x_{i_N}(t_N)\,D\, y_{i_N}(t_N)\cdots y_{i_1}(t_1)
5620: \]
5621: parametrizes an open dense subset of $GL_n^{>0}$, and the reduced word $\mathbf{i}$ can be drawn as a wiring diagram whose crossings carry the parameters $t_r$.
5622: \end{theorem}
5623: 
5624: \begin{proof}[Reference]
5625: This is one of the standard parametrizations of the totally positive part of a reductive group (here $GL_n$), expressed in terms of reduced words and generalized minors.
5626: See \cite{BFZ1996TotallyPositive,FominZelevinsky2000TotalPositivity}.
5627: \end{proof}
5628: 
5629: \begin{remark}[Network interpretation]
5630: Each elementary factor $x_i(t)$ or $y_i(t)$ is the weight matrix of a tiny planar ``crossing gadget'' with a single tunable edge of weight $t$.
5631: Concatenating gadgets corresponds to matrix multiplication, hence the entire product in Theorem~\ref{thm:BFZ_factorization} is the weight matrix of an explicit wiring diagram network.
5632: Thus, by Corollary~\ref{cor:wiring_TN}, total positivity of these products is a direct LGV consequence.
5633: \end{remark}
5634: 
5635: \subsection{Total positivity as a positive-energy / self-adjointness certificate (Wick-rotated transfer matrices)}
5636: \label{sec:tp_positive_energy_certificate}
5637: 
5638: In Euclidean (Wick-rotated) lattice QFT, one often works with a \emph{transfer matrix} $T$ rather than a unitary.
5639: The formal relation is $T=\exp(-aH)$ for lattice spacing $a>0$.
5640: To conclude that $H$ is self-adjoint and bounded below, one typically imposes Osterwalder--Schrader reflection positivity.
5641: Total positivity gives a more combinatorial (and, in discrete settings, sometimes easier to verify) sufficient condition.
5642: 
5643: \begin{theorem}[Symmetric totally positive transfer matrix $\Rightarrow$ self-adjoint Hamiltonian with positive energy]
5644: \label{thm:tp_positive_energy}
5645: Let $T\in M_n(\R)$ be \emph{symmetric} and totally positive.
5646: Then:
5647: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
5648: \item $T$ has real, positive, simple eigenvalues $\lambda_1>\cdots>\lambda_n>0$;
5649: \item for any $a>0$, the matrix
5650: \[
5651: H:=-\frac{1}{a}\log T
5652: \]
5653: is real symmetric (hence self-adjoint) with eigenvalues $E_k=-(1/a)\log\lambda_k\in\R$;
5654: \item after rescaling $T$ by its top eigenvalue (equivalently shifting $H$ by a scalar), one may assume $\lambda_1=1$, in which case $E_k\ge 0$ for all $k$ (positivity of energy).
5655: \end{enumerate}
5656: \end{theorem}
5657: 
5658: \begin{proof}
5659: (a) By Theorem~\ref{thm:tp_real_spectrum}, $T$ has real, positive, simple eigenvalues.
5660: (b) Because $T$ is symmetric, there exists an orthogonal matrix $U$ with $T=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n)\,U^\top$.
5661: Define $\log T:=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\log\lambda_1,\dots,\log\lambda_n)\,U^\top$; this is real symmetric.
5662: Then $H=-(1/a)\log T$ is real symmetric with eigenvalues $E_k$ as stated.
5663: (c) Replace $T$ by $\widetilde T:=\lambda_1^{-1}T$.
5664: Then $\widetilde T=\exp(-a\widetilde H)$ where $\widetilde H=H-(1/a)\log\lambda_1\,I$ is a scalar shift of $H$.
5665: Now $0<\widetilde\lambda_k\le 1$, hence $-\log\widetilde\lambda_k\ge 0$ and $\widetilde H\ge 0$.
5666: \end{proof}
5667: 
5668: \paragraph{Worked example: a $2\times 2$ totally positive transfer matrix.}
5669: 
5670: To make the theorem concrete, consider the simplest case: a symmetric $2\times 2$ totally positive matrix
5671: \[
5672: T = \begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ b & c \end{pmatrix},
5673: \quad a,b,c > 0, \quad \det(T)=ac-b^2>0.
5674: \]
5675: For total positivity, we need all minors positive: $a>0$, $c>0$, $\det(T)>0$, which hold by assumption.
5676: 
5677: The eigenvalues are
5678: \[
5679: \lambda_\pm = \frac{(a+c) \pm \sqrt{(a+c)^2-4(ac-b^2)}}{2} = \frac{(a+c) \pm \sqrt{(a-c)^2+4b^2}}{2}.
5680: \]
5681: Both are real and positive (since $a,c,b>0$), and $\lambda_1>\lambda_2>0$ by the spectral ordering.
5682: 
5683: Now define $H=-\frac{1}{a}\log T$ (using Euclidean time step $a>0$).
5684: Diagonalizing $T=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_1,\lambda_2)U^\top$ with an orthogonal $U$, we get
5685: \[
5686: H = U\,\mathrm{diag}\left(-\frac{1}{a}\log\lambda_1, -\frac{1}{a}\log\lambda_2\right)U^\top.
5687: \]
5688: This is manifestly real symmetric. Its eigenvalues are
5689: \[
5690: E_1=-\frac{1}{a}\log\lambda_1, \quad E_2=-\frac{1}{a}\log\lambda_2.
5691: \]
5692: If we rescale $T \to \widetilde T:=\lambda_1^{-1}T$ so that $\widetilde\lambda_1=1$, then $\widetilde E_1=0$ and $\widetilde E_2=-\frac{1}{a}\log(\lambda_2/\lambda_1)\ge 0$ (since $0<\lambda_2\le\lambda_1$).
5693: Thus the shifted Hamiltonian has nonnegative spectrum—positive energy, as desired.
5694: 
5695: This example shows explicitly how total positivity of $T$ (enforced via planar-network construction) leads to the required self-adjointness and spectral positivity without any appeal to Osterwalder--Schrader positivity or other limiting procedures.
5696: 
5697: \subsection{Density-operator phrasing: totally positive transfer matrices as states and modular Hamiltonians}
5698: 
5699: If $T$ is symmetric and positive definite, then after normalization it is literally a density operator.
5700: Define
5701: \[
5702: \rho:=\frac{T}{\Tr(T)}.
5703: \]
5704: Then $\rho\succeq 0$, $\rho=\rho^\top$ and $\Tr(\rho)=1$, so $\rho$ is a finite-dimensional (real) density matrix.
5705: Its modular Hamiltonian is
5706: \[
5707: K:=-\log\rho = -\log T + \log\Tr(T)\,I.
5708: \]
5709: Thus Theorem~\ref{thm:tp_positive_energy} can be reread as:
5710: \begin{quote}
5711: \emph{if a one-step Euclidean evolution operator is symmetric totally positive, then its normalized version is a bona fide density operator whose modular Hamiltonian is self-adjoint and nonnegative (after a scalar shift).}
5712: \end{quote}
5713: 
5714: \begin{remark}[How this is used later]
5715: In the Hilbert--Pólya/GRH chapter we extract candidate generators from \emph{channels} (CPTP maps) rather than from a single matrix.
5716: A common finite-dimensional reduction is to look at the channel's action on a commutative subalgebra (a coarse-grained classical shadow) or to restrict to a basis in which the channel becomes reversible.
5717: In such reductions, wiring-diagram/total-positivity conditions give a concrete, checkable route to self-adjoint modular generators.
5718: \end{remark}
5719: 
5720: \paragraph{Capstone: The architecture of total positivity in the Hilbert--Pólya program.}
5721: 
5722: We now summarize the complete picture that this chapter has built.
5723: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
5724: \item \textbf{Combinatorial layer (Zelevinsky).}
5725: Wiring diagrams and planar networks provide a concrete, parameter-counting realization of totally positive matrices.
5726: By the Berenstein--Fomin--Zelevinsky factorization theorem, every totally positive $n\times n$ matrix factors as a product of elementary bidiagonal matrices with nonnegative parameters, and this factorization structure is encoded in a wiring diagram.
5727: \item \textbf{Algebraic layer (Postnikov; positive Grassmannians).}
5728: The positive Grassmannian and its boundary measurement maps supply an invariant geometric language for positivity: wiring diagrams are coordinate charts on cells of $\mathbf{Gr}_{\ge 0}(k,n)$, and different network moves correspond to different coordinate patches on the same cell.
5729: This ties positivity to the underlying moduli geometry.
5730: \item \textbf{Spectral layer (Gantmacher--Krein).}
5731: Totally positive matrices have \emph{real, positive, and simple eigenvalues}.
5732: This is the crucial certificate: in any transfer-matrix or channel-based construction, total positivity automatically ensures the spectrum is real and bounded.
5733: \item \textbf{Self-adjointness and energy (Wick rotation).}
5734: When a symmetric totally positive transfer matrix $T$ is Wick-rotated via $H=-\frac{1}{a}\log T$, the resulting Hamiltonian is self-adjoint and, after a scalar shift, has nonnegative eigenvalues (positive energy).
5735: This is exactly what the Hilbert--Pólya construction requires: a self-adjoint generator whose spectrum is real and controlled.
5736: \end{enumerate}
5737: 
5738: The synthesis is this:
5739: \begin{quote}
5740: \emph{A clock/shift operator built by gluing totally positive transfer matrices (via wiring-diagram concatenation) automatically yields a self-adjoint Hamiltonian with real spectrum and positive energy.
5741: The wiring diagram is the certificate of positivity; the Gantmacher--Krein theorem is the certificate of reality; the logarithm is the bridge to self-adjointness.
5742: This mechanism is the core of the Hilbert--Pólya proposal for GRH developed in the final chapters:
5743: we will construct candidate generators of time evolution from surface orders and arithmetic data, demand that they are (or factor through) totally positive transfer matrices, and thereby obtain self-adjoint operators whose spectrum encodes the Riemann zeros.}
5744: \end{quote}
5745: 
5746: This is the ``icing in the middle of the cake''—a complete, self-contained layer that makes rigorous and checkable the claim that discrete/combinatorial/arithmetic input can yield a self-adjoint spectral problem with controlled reality and positivity properties.
5747:

5751: 					% \section{Braid Group Actions on Local Roots of Unity}
