5753:
\chapter{Wick Rotation and the Positivity of Energy in QFT} · lines 5754–5826
5754: \chapter{Wick Rotation and the Positivity of Energy in QFT}
5755: \label{ch:wick_rotation_positive_energy}
5756: 
5757: \section*{Bridge from total positivity}
5758: 
5759: The total positivity machinery developed in the preceding chapter provides the \emph{algebraic certificate} that certain transfer matrices have controlled spectra and self-adjoint logarithms.
5760: We now translate that machinery into the \emph{physical language} of Wick rotation and positive energy—the form needed for the Hilbert--Pólya operator construction.
5761: The key insight is simple: a symmetric totally positive transfer matrix $T$, when analytically continued to imaginary Euclidean time via $H:=i\log T$ (or equivalently $H=-\frac{1}{a}\log T$), automatically yields a self-adjoint Hamiltonian whose spectrum is real and, after normalization, nonnegative.
5762: This chapter makes the connection explicit and develops the quantum-field-theoretic context in which these transfer matrices arise naturally.
5763: 
5764: \section*{}
5765: 
5766: The basic analytic tension in QFT (and in any proposal that tries to build QFT-like dynamics from discrete/combinatorial input) is:
5767: 
5768: \begin{quote}
5769: \emph{Lorentzian time evolution wants a self-adjoint Hamiltonian $H$ (so that $U(t)=e^{-itH}$ is unitary), while Euclidean constructions and coarse-grained dynamics naturally produce \emph{positive} transfer operators $T$ (or channels) instead.}
5770: \end{quote}
5771: 
5772: A standard bridge between the two is \textbf{Wick rotation}:
5773: \[
5774: t\mapsto -i\tau,\qquad U(t)=e^{-itH}\ \leadsto\ e^{-\tau H}.
5775: \]
5776: On a lattice, $\tau$ is discretized and one works with a one-step transfer matrix $T$ that formally satisfies $T=e^{-aH}$ for time step $a>0$.
5777: The question is then: \emph{when is $H:=-(1/a)\log T$ actually self-adjoint and bounded below?}
5778: 
5779: \section{Euclidean transfer matrices and positivity}
5780: 
5781: In finite volume, a Euclidean path integral with real action $S_E$ produces weights $e^{-S_E}\ge 0$ and hence a transfer matrix with nonnegative matrix elements in a preferred basis.
5782: In infinite volume, the right condition is \emph{reflection positivity} (Osterwalder--Schrader positivity), which guarantees reconstruction of a Hilbert space and a self-adjoint Hamiltonian with spectrum bounded below.
5783: 
5784: For the purposes of this manuscript (which is explicitly constructive and often finite-dimensional at intermediate stages), it is useful to keep a clean, checkable sufficient condition:
5785: 
5786: \begin{quote}
5787: \emph{If $T$ is symmetric positive definite, then $H=-\frac{1}{a}\log T$ is self-adjoint, and energy positivity reduces to a normalization of the top eigenvalue.}
5788: \end{quote}
5789: 
5790: \section{A wiring-diagram sufficient condition}
5791: 
5792: Total positivity upgrades ``$T$ has nonnegative entries'' to ``every minor of $T$ is positive'', and this stronger property has two practical consequences:
5793: (i) it is stable under local concatenations, and
5794: (ii) it forces the spectrum to be real, positive, and simple.
5795: 
5796: The preceding chapter's total-positivity results immediately yield a Wick-rotation positivity statement.
5797: 
5798: \begin{corollary}[Reflection-symmetric wiring diagram $\Rightarrow$ positive-energy Hamiltonian]
5799: \label{cor:wiring_positive_energy}
5800: Let $\mathcal{N}$ be a positive-weight wiring-diagram network whose weight matrix $T=A(\mathcal{N})$ is \emph{symmetric} (e.g.\ the network is invariant under planar reflection exchanging sources and sinks, which is a discrete detailed-balance condition).
5801: Assume $\mathcal{N}$ is such that $T$ is totally positive (Corollary~\ref{cor:wiring_TN}).
5802: Then $H:=-(1/a)\log T$ is self-adjoint and (after a scalar shift/normalization) satisfies $H\succeq 0$.
5803: \end{corollary}
5804: 
5805: \begin{proof}
5806: By Corollary~\ref{cor:wiring_TN}, $T$ is totally positive.
5807: By hypothesis $T$ is symmetric, so Theorem~\ref{thm:tp_positive_energy} applies.
5808: \end{proof}
5809: 
5810: \section{Density-operator viewpoint: Euclidean evolution as a Gibbs state}
5811: 
5812: Given a positive definite transfer matrix $T$, one may form a finite-volume Gibbs state at inverse temperature $\beta$ by
5813: \[
5814: \rho_\beta := \frac{T^\beta}{\Tr(T^\beta)} = \frac{e^{-\beta a H}}{\Tr(e^{-\beta a H})}.
5815: \]
5816: The modular Hamiltonian of $\rho_\beta$ is
5817: \[
5818: K_\beta:=-\log\rho_\beta=\beta a H+\log Z(\beta)\,I,\qquad Z(\beta)=\Tr(e^{-\beta a H}).
5819: \]
5820: Thus, in Euclidean settings the modular Hamiltonian is essentially the physical Hamiltonian (up to an affine transformation), and the ``positivity of energy'' is precisely the statement $K_\beta\succeq 0$ after subtracting its scalar part.
5821: Total positivity provides one constructive route to guarantee that this modular generator is self-adjoint, with good spectral control, \emph{before} taking any thermodynamic limit.
5822: 
5823: 					
5824: 					
5825: \part{Time, Holonomy, Hyperbolic Geometry, and Emergent Dynamics}
5826:
\chapter{Time as a Shift Operator: finite and infinite qudits} · lines 5827–6267
5827: \chapter{Time as a Shift Operator: finite and infinite qudits}
5828: \label{ch:time_shift}
5829: 
5830: The practical study of physical clocks—whether digital, mechanical, or quantum—reveals that temporal evolution is fundamentally encoded in shift dynamics: a clock's state advances from one value to the next via an operator that increments or cycles the internal register. This chapter formalizes this intuition by studying how shift operators generate the simplest non-trivial models of time, from finite-dimensional cyclic qudits to infinite-dimensional systems. Understanding the spectral and holonomic properties of these shifts forms the foundation for later chapters, where cyclic time emerges as torsion in the holonomy of glued local orders.
5831: 
5832: \section{Finite-dimensional clocks: the cyclic shift and its spectrum}
5833: Fix a local Hilbert space $\cH\cong\C^d$ with computational basis $\{|j\rangle\}_{j\in\Z/d\Z}$.
5834: Define the \emph{shift} (``successor'') operator
5835: \[
5836: X_d|j\rangle = |j+1\rangle,
5837: \]
5838: and the \emph{phase} operator
5839: \[
5840: Z_d|j\rangle = \omega^j |j\rangle,\qquad \omega=e^{2\pi i/d}.
5841: \]
5842: Then $X_d^d=Z_d^d=I$ and the Weyl commutation relation holds:
5843: \[
5844: Z_d X_d = \omega\, X_d Z_d.
5845: \]
5846: The discrete Fourier transform $F_d$ diagonalizes $X_d$:
5847: \[
5848: F_d\, X_d\, F_d^{-1} = Z_d,\qquad
5849: (F_d)_{jk}=d^{-1/2}\,\omega^{jk}.
5850: \]
5851: Thus the eigenvalues of $X_d$ are $\{\omega^k\}_{k=0}^{d-1}$, and the ``clock phases'' advance uniformly.
5852: 
5853: 
5854: \section{Irrational rotation algebras and the noncommutative torus: from finite qudits to infinite clocks}
5855: \label{sec:irrational_rotation_algebra}
5856: 
5857: The finite qudit Weyl relation
5858: \[
5859: Z_d X_d = \omega\, X_d Z_d,\qquad \omega=e^{2\pi i/d},
5860: \]
5861: is the ``root of unity'' case of a much more rigid operator-algebraic object: the \emph{irrational rotation algebra}
5862: (also called the \emph{noncommutative $2$--torus}).
5863: 
5864: \begin{definition}[Irrational rotation algebra / noncommutative torus]
5865: Fix $\theta\in\R$.
5866: The \emph{rotation algebra} $A_\theta$ is the universal unital $C^*$--algebra generated by two unitaries $U,V$ such that
5867: \begin{equation}
5868: \label{eq:rotation_relation}
5869: VU = e^{2\pi i \theta}\, UV .
5870: \end{equation}
5871: \end{definition}
5872: 
5873: \begin{remark}[Rational versus irrational phases]
5874: If $\theta=p/q\in\Q$ (in lowest terms), then \eqref{eq:rotation_relation} is the projective commutation relation of a finite $q$--level clock:
5875: one may take $U=X_q$ and $V=Z_q^{\,p}$ so that $VU=e^{2\pi i p/q}UV$.
5876: If $\theta\notin\Q$, then $A_\theta$ has \emph{no} nontrivial finite-dimensional representations (Proposition~\ref{prop:finite_rep_implies_rational}), and one is forced into the infinite-clock regime.
5877: \end{remark}
5878: 
5879: \begin{proposition}[Finite-dimensional representations force $\theta$ to be rational]
5880: \label{prop:finite_rep_implies_rational}
5881: Suppose there exist unitaries $U,V\in M_n(\C)$ satisfying \eqref{eq:rotation_relation}.
5882: Then $e^{2\pi i n\theta}=1$, hence $\theta\in \frac1n\Z\subset\Q$.
5883: \end{proposition}
5884: 
5885: \begin{proof}
5886: Taking determinants on both sides of $VU=e^{2\pi i\theta}UV$ gives
5887: \[
5888: \det(V)\det(U)=e^{2\pi i n\theta}\det(U)\det(V).
5889: \]
5890: Since $\det(U),\det(V)\neq 0$, cancel to obtain $e^{2\pi i n\theta}=1$.
5891: \end{proof}
5892: 
5893: \subsection{Crossed-product realization and dynamical meaning}
5894: A key structural fact is that $A_\theta$ is a crossed product by an irrational circle rotation.
5895: 
5896: Let $\mathbb{T}=S^1$ and let $\alpha_\theta:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}$ be the rotation $\alpha_\theta(z)=e^{2\pi i\theta}z$.
5897: Let $\alpha$ denote the induced automorphism of $C(\mathbb{T})$,
5898: \[
5899: (\alpha(f))(z):=f(\alpha_\theta^{-1}z)=f(e^{-2\pi i\theta}z).
5900: \]
5901: Then the crossed product $C(\mathbb{T})\rtimes_\alpha \Z$ is the universal $C^*$--algebra generated by
5902: \begin{itemize}
5903: \item a copy of $C(\mathbb{T})$, and
5904: \item a unitary $U$ implementing the $\Z$--action: $U f U^*=\alpha(f)$,
5905: \end{itemize}
5906: and is canonically isomorphic to $A_\theta$ by sending $V$ to the coordinate function $V(z)=z$ and $U$ to the implementing unitary.
5907: This is the standard ``dynamics $\Rightarrow$ noncommutative observable algebra'' mechanism \cite{PimsnerVoiculescu1980ExactSequence,Rieffel1981IrrationalRotations}.
5908: 
5909: \subsection{Ergodic theory input: unique ergodicity and the canonical trace}
5910: For $\theta\notin\Q$, the rotation $\alpha_\theta$ on the circle is \emph{minimal} and \emph{uniquely ergodic}:
5911: Lebesgue measure $m$ is the unique $\alpha_\theta$--invariant Borel probability measure, and Birkhoff averages converge uniformly
5912: \[
5913: \frac1N\sum_{k=0}^{N-1} f(\alpha_\theta^k z)\ \xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\ \int_{\mathbb{T}} f\,dm
5914: \quad\text{for all continuous } f \text{ and all } z\in\mathbb{T}
5915: \]
5916: (see e.g.\ \cite{Walters1982ErgodicTheory}).
5917: 
5918: This uniquely ergodic fact has a concrete operator-algebraic consequence:
5919: 
5920: \begin{proposition}[Canonical trace on $A_\theta$]
5921: \label{prop:trace_rotation_algebra}
5922: If $\theta\notin\Q$, then $A_\theta$ admits a unique tracial state $\tau$ characterized on Fourier monomials by
5923: \[
5924: \tau(U^m V^n)=
5925: \begin{cases}
5926: 1,& (m,n)=(0,0),\\
5927: 0,& \text{otherwise.}
5928: \end{cases}
5929: \]
5930: Equivalently, in the crossed-product picture $A_\theta\cong C(\mathbb{T})\rtimes_\alpha\Z$,
5931: \[
5932: \tau\Bigl(\sum_{k} f_k U^k\Bigr)=\int_{\mathbb{T}} f_0\, dm .
5933: \]
5934: \end{proposition}
5935: 
5936: \begin{remark}[Why ergodicity is the right ``physics'' hypothesis]
5937: The heuristic ``long-time averages equal equilibrium expectations'' is \emph{exactly} Birkhoff's theorem in the uniquely ergodic case.
5938: In our setting, $\tau$ is the canonical ``equilibrium'' state induced by the orbit geometry.
5939: When we use traces in later Hilbert--Pólya/explicit-formula discussions, this is the clean place where one justifies passing from orbit sums (time averages) to traces (state expectations).
5940: \end{remark}
5941: 
5942: 
5943: \subsection{Continued fractions, renormalization, and finite-clock approximants}
5944: \label{sec:rotation_continued_fraction_rg}
5945: 
5946: A practical reason the rotation algebra belongs in a computational manuscript is that irrational parameters are naturally approximated by rational ones, and the corresponding algebras are realized by finite clocks.
5947: 
5948: Write the (infinite) continued fraction expansion
5949: \[
5950: \theta = [a_0;a_1,a_2,a_3,\dots],
5951: \]
5952: and let $p_n/q_n$ be the convergents.
5953: Classical Diophantine approximation gives the uniform estimate
5954: \[
5955: \Bigl|\theta-\frac{p_n}{q_n}\Bigr| < \frac{1}{q_n^2}.
5956: \]
5957: Each rational approximant $\theta_n=p_n/q_n$ yields a finite-dimensional ``magnetic translation'' model:
5958: \[
5959: A_{\theta_n}\ \text{admits a faithful $q_n$--dimensional projective representation generated by } (X_{q_n},Z_{q_n}^{\,p_n}).
5960: \]
5961: Thus, \emph{finite qudit clocks} are exactly the natural rational approximations to an irrational-clock observable algebra.
5962: 
5963: \begin{remark}[Renormalization viewpoint and an ergodic map on parameters]
5964: The continued-fraction shift $\theta\mapsto [a_1;a_2,\dots]$ is the Gauss map $G(\theta)=\{1/\theta\}$ on $(0,1)$,
5965: which is a paradigmatic ergodic dynamical system \cite{Walters1982ErgodicTheory}.
5966: Interpreting ``coarse-graining'' as passing from $\theta$ to a better rational approximant $p_n/q_n$ is therefore literally a renormalization flow on the space of rotation numbers.
5967: In the operator-algebraic classification of $A_\theta$, this continued-fraction structure controls inductive-limit approximations by matrix algebras (the Elliott--Evans picture \cite{ElliottEvans1993Rotation}),
5968: which is precisely what one wants for numerical and hardware-friendly truncations.
5969: \end{remark}
5970: 
5971: In short: irrational clocks can be simulated at scale by a multiscale ladder of finite qudits dictated by the continued fraction of $\theta$,
5972: and ergodic theory governs the typical complexity of that ladder (digit statistics, mixing of the Gauss map), which in turn controls what a learned renormalization scheme must discover.
5973: 
5974: \subsection{Density operators and modular Hamiltonians for rotation-algebra observables}
5975: A $C^*$--algebraic \emph{state} is a positive linear functional $\omega:A_\theta\to\C$ with $\omega(1)=1$.
5976: Once a concrete representation $\pi:A_\theta\to\mathcal{B}(\cH)$ is chosen, \emph{normal} states are implemented by density operators:
5977: \[
5978: \omega(a)=\Tr(\rho\,\pi(a)),\qquad \rho\succeq 0,\ \Tr(\rho)=1.
5979: \]
5980: In particular, the tracial state $\tau$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:trace_rotation_algebra} is the infinite-temperature limit in which the modular Hamiltonian is trivial (the modular flow is identity).
5981: Nontrivial modular Hamiltonians appear once one specifies a one-parameter dynamics $\{\sigma_t\}$ on $A_\theta$ (e.g.\ a one-parameter subgroup of the natural $\mathbb{T}^2$--action)
5982: and chooses a corresponding KMS state; then Tomita--Takesaki theory produces a modular generator compatible with the ergodic geometry.
5983: 
5984: \subsection{Why this belongs in the ``clock'' chapter}
5985: Two points link $A_\theta$ directly to our holonomy-as-time story:
5986: 
5987: \begin{enumerate}
5988: \item \textbf{Finite clocks are rational noncommutative tori.}
5989: The pair $(X_d,Z_d)$ generates a finite-dimensional projective representation of $\Z^2$ with a $U(1)$ $2$--cocycle (the phase $\omega$).
5990: The irrational rotation algebra is the $C^*$--completion when the cocycle phase is irrational and cannot close in finite dimension.
5991: 
5992: \item \textbf{Quasi-periodic time is ergodic time.}
5993: If ``time'' is generated by a shift $U$ whose action on a commutative subalgebra is an irrational rotation,
5994: then repeated ticking explores the entire phase circle uniformly.
5995: This makes the trace state computable by orbit averaging and provides the ergodic underpinning for replacing long products of local clock moves by trace-class expressions later.
5996: \end{enumerate}
5997: 
5998: 
5999: 
6000: \subsection{Procyclic root towers and ``infinite-period'' cyclic qudits}
6001: \label{sec:procyclic_root_towers}
6002: 
6003: The rotation algebra $A_\theta$ is the analytic completion of a \emph{single} irrational rotation on a circle.
6004: There is a closely related (and, for surface orders, even more canonical) way to generate ``infinite-period'' clocks:
6005: build a \emph{tower of finite cyclic clocks} and pass to a limit.
6006: This picture appears naturally in the local-field/monodromy description of surface orders \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasII,Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI}.
6007: 
6008: \paragraph{Root towers at punctures and profinite cyclicity.}
6009: Let $x_j$ be a puncture (or ramification point) on a curve, with local field $F_j=\C((x_j))$.
6010: For each $n\ge 1$, adjoin an $n$th root of the local parameter to obtain an extension $F_j((x_j^{1/n}))$.
6011: There is a canonical cyclic subgroup of automorphisms generated by the rotation of the root,
6012: \[
6013: x_j^{1/n}\ \longmapsto\ e^{2\pi i k/n}\,x_j^{1/n},
6014: \qquad k\in\Z/n\Z,
6015: \]
6016: so $\mathrm{Gal}(F_j((x_j^{1/n}))/F_j)\cong \Z/n\Z$ in this elementary setting.
6017: Passing to the directed system $n\mid m$ produces an inductive limit containing all roots of unity,
6018: and dually produces the \emph{procyclic completion}
6019: \[
6020: \widehat{\Z}\;\cong\;\varprojlim_n \Z/n\Z,
6021: \]
6022: which is a canonical home for ``clock phases'' of arbitrarily large period.
6023: 
6024: \paragraph{Operator-algebraic packaging: odometers and solenoids.}
6025: A standard ergodic model for $\widehat{\Z}$--structured time is the \emph{adding machine} (odometer):
6026: a minimal uniquely ergodic action of $\Z$ on a Cantor space $X_{\mathbf{n}}$ built from the inverse limit of residue rings
6027: \[
6028: X_{\mathbf{n}} := \varprojlim_k \Z/n_k\Z,
6029: \qquad n_k\mid n_{k+1}.
6030: \]
6031: The crossed product $C(X_{\mathbf{n}})\rtimes \Z$ is then an ``inductive-limit clock algebra'':
6032: it is an analytic completion of the observable algebra generated by \emph{all} the finite clocks of periods $n_k$ at once.
6033: For our purposes the key fact is the same as in Proposition~\ref{prop:trace_rotation_algebra}:
6034: unique ergodicity provides a canonical invariant probability measure (Haar measure on the profinite group), hence a canonical trace state.
6035: 
6036: \paragraph{How irrational rotations arise from the tower.}
6037: The procyclic tower is not the same object as $A_\theta$ (the rotation algebra is generated by a \emph{single} irrational phase),
6038: but it is the correct device for implementing ``irrational period'' in hardware:
6039: choose convergents $p_k/q_k$ of $\theta$ and set $n_k:=q_k$.
6040: Then each level $k$ supports a faithful $q_k$--dimensional projective representation of the rational noncommutative torus,
6041: generated by $(X_{q_k},Z_{q_k}^{\,p_k})$, and the tower $q_k\mid q_{k+1}$ gives a coherent refinement ladder.
6042: In the limit (strong operator topology / inductive-limit sense), the spectrum of the refined clock becomes dense on $S^1$,
6043: and orbit averages converge to Haar/Lebesgue expectations by unique ergodicity.
6044: 
6045: \subsection{Quantum-circuit implementation: nested cyclic qudits as irrational-rotation approximants}
6046: \label{sec:irrational_rotation_nested_qudits}
6047: 
6048: The tower picture can be made circuit-explicit while staying in the state-vector model.
6049: 
6050: \paragraph{Level-$k$ clock register.}
6051: Fix $q_k$ and work on $\cH_k\cong\C^{q_k}$ with basis $\{\ket{j}\}_{j\in\Z/q_k\Z}$.
6052: Implement
6053: \[
6054: U_k := X_{q_k},\qquad V_k := Z_{q_k}^{\,p_k},
6055: \qquad\text{so that}\qquad V_kU_k=e^{2\pi i p_k/q_k}\,U_kV_k.
6056: \]
6057: Both gates are native on qudit hardware; on qubits, $U_k$ is modular addition by $1$ and $V_k$ is a diagonal phase gate.
6058: 
6059: \paragraph{Coherent refinement (``infinite period'' limit register).}
6060: Choose an embedding isometry $J_k:\cH_k\hookrightarrow \cH_{k+1}$ compatible with the residue map $\Z/q_{k+1}\Z\to\Z/q_k\Z$,
6061: so that $J_k U_k = U_{k+1} J_k$ and likewise for $V_k$ up to a controlled phase that vanishes as $k\to\infty$ for good approximants.
6062: Then the direct limit Hilbert space $\cH_\infty=\varinjlim (\cH_k,J_k)$ carries limiting unitaries $U_\infty,V_\infty$
6063: whose commutator phase is the target irrational $e^{2\pi i\theta}$ in the limit sense.
6064: Operationally: you never build $\cH_\infty$ in hardware; you pick the level $k$ needed for a given accuracy $\epsilon$,
6065: using the Diophantine bound $|\theta-p_k/q_k|<1/q_k^2$.
6066: 
6067: \paragraph{Validation checks (ergodic and spectral).}
6068: The two simplest empirical diagnostics are:
6069: \begin{enumerate}
6070: \item \textbf{Equidistribution / orbit tests:} for a fixed basis state, repeatedly apply $U_k$ and measure in the $V_k$--eigenbasis;
6071: as $k$ grows, the empirical distribution of phases should approach uniformity for ``generic'' initial states.
6072: \item \textbf{Commutator phase calibration:} estimate $\langle\psi|V_kU_kU_k^\dagger V_k^\dagger|\psi\rangle$ for random $\ket{\psi}$;
6073: it should concentrate near $e^{2\pi i p_k/q_k}$ and track the continued-fraction ladder as $k$ is increased.
6074: \end{enumerate}
6075: 
6076: These are the circuit-level counterparts of the operator-algebraic statements above:
6077: unique ergodicity justifies replacing long-time orbit averages by trace-state expectations,
6078: which is exactly what later trace-formula / explicit-formula comparisons require.
6079: 
6080: 
6081: \section{Twisted shifts from orders (the valuation-aware successor)}
6082: In the order-theoretic layer (Chapter~\ref{sec:twisted_shift}), the successor can acquire a uniformizer factor on wrap-around:
6083: \[
6084: S_d(\pi)=
6085: \begin{pmatrix}
6086: 0&0&\cdots&0&\pi\\
6087: 1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
6088: 0&1&\ddots&0&0\\
6089: \vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
6090: 0&0&\cdots&1&0
6091: \end{pmatrix},
6092: \qquad S_d(\pi)^d=\pi I_d.
6093: \]
6094: Over a field extension adjoining a $d$th root $\pi^{1/d}$, the spectrum is $\pi^{1/d}\omega^k$.
6095: Projectively, the ``clock'' still has period $d$ (up to central scaling), which is exactly the holonomy mechanism exploited later.
6096: 
6097: \section{Infinite-dimensional clocks: the bilateral shift and continuous spectrum}
6098: Let $\cH=\ell^2(\Z)$ with basis $\{|n\rangle\}_{n\in\Z}$ and define the bilateral shift
6099: \[
6100: (S|n\rangle)=|n+1\rangle.
6101: \]
6102: Then $S$ is unitary and has \emph{continuous} spectrum equal to the unit circle.
6103: Under the Fourier transform $\ell^2(\Z)\cong L^2(S^1)$, $S$ becomes multiplication by $e^{i\theta}$.
6104: A (non-unique) self-adjoint generator $H$ can be chosen by functional calculus so that $S=e^{-iH}$ (Section~\ref{sec:hamiltonian_log}).
6105: 
6106: \section{From shifts to Hamiltonians: branch choices and physical units}
6107: If $T$ is unitary, one may write $T=e^{-iH\Delta t/\hbar}$ for a self-adjoint (generally unbounded) $H$, but $H$ is only determined up to adding integer multiples of $2\pi\hbar/\Delta t$ on spectral components.
6108: This \emph{branch ambiguity} becomes a structural input when we glue local clocks: global consistency of branch choices is an additional holonomy constraint rather than a nuisance.
6109: 
6110: \section{Implementation notes (quantum circuits)}
6111: For $d=2^n$ (an $n$-qubit register), $X_d$ is modular addition by $1$ and can be implemented with a ripple-carry or QFT-based adder.
6112: For general $d$, one may either (i) embed $\C^d$ into a qubit register with unused states and post-select, or (ii) use native qudit hardware where the shift is a cyclic permutation gate.
6113: In all cases, hardware noise most strongly perturbs phase coherence; the persistence diagnostics in Chapter~\ref{ch:persistence} are designed to be stable to such perturbations.
6114: 
6115: 
6116: 
6117: 
6118: \subsection{Beyond Clifford: learned controlled-$U$ (CU) layers and universal gate sets}
6119: \label{sec:learned_CU_layers}
6120: 
6121: The circuit sketches in this manuscript sometimes mention Clifford layers because they are convenient for (i) randomized measurement and (ii) baseline stabilizer diagnostics.
6122: This should not be misread as a restriction of the dynamics to Clifford circuits.
6123: The renormalization layers are intended to be \emph{learned} and therefore must include non-Clifford operations.
6124: 
6125: \paragraph{Controlled-$U$ gates.}
6126: A controlled-$U$ (CU) gate on a control qubit and a $d$-level target is the unitary
6127: \[
6128: \mathrm{CU}(U)=|0\rangle\!\langle0|\otimes I_d + |1\rangle\!\langle1|\otimes U,
6129: \]
6130: and more generally on a qudit control one may condition on multiple control values.
6131: On an embedded graph/quiver, CU gates are canonical \emph{edge-local entanglers}: place the control on one endpoint and the target on the adjacent endpoint.
6132: 
6133: \paragraph{Learnable edge gates and attention weights.}
6134: In the QGCN/QGAT viewpoint (Sections~\ref{sec:qgcn_renorm}--\ref{sec:qgat_renorm}), one may treat each edge $e=(u,v)$ as carrying a trainable two-site unitary
6135: \[
6136: U_e(\theta_e)\in \mathrm{SU}(d_ud_v),
6137: \]
6138: compiled into hardware primitives (often CU-like entanglers plus single-site rotations).
6139: In a graph-attention layer, the classical attention coefficient $a_{uv}$ can modulate either (i) the angle of a controlled phase/rotation or (ii) the probability of applying a gate, yielding a stochastic quantum channel.
6140: 
6141: \paragraph{Why Clifford-only learning is a dead end.}
6142: Clifford circuits are efficiently classically simulable and cannot densely explore $\mathrm{SU}(2^n)$.
6143: They are excellent for error mitigation and tomography primitives, but an emergent-geometry model requires genuinely non-Clifford entanglement generation.
6144: Pragmatically, one therefore uses:
6145: \begin{itemize}
6146: \item Clifford or approximate unitary $2$-design layers for \emph{measurement/tomography}, and
6147: \item learned CU/non-Clifford layers for \emph{state preparation and dynamics}.
6148: \end{itemize}
6149: 
6150: \paragraph{Density-operator phrasing.}
6151: A learned unitary layer corresponds to the channel $\rho\mapsto U(\theta)\rho U(\theta)^\dagger$, optionally composed with noise.
6152: This is the language used in Section~\ref{sec:circuit_density_channels} when reduced density operators are mapped to modular Hamiltonians.
6153: 
6154: 
6155: \section{Circuit dynamics as quantum channels and density operators}
6156: \label{sec:circuit_density_channels}
6157: 
6158: The discussion above used state vectors $\ket{\psi}$ for clarity, but most of the program---especially the parts that are meant to survive hardware noise, mid-circuit measurements, and coarse-graining---is more naturally phrased in terms of \emph{density operators}.
6159: We record the translation explicitly so later chapters (modular Hamiltonians, renormalization channels, and the Hilbert--Pólya trace identities) can reuse it without ambiguity.
6160: 
6161: \subsection{Pure states, mixed states, and expectations}
6162: A (finite-dimensional) \emph{density operator} on a Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}$ is an operator $\rho\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})$ such that $\rho\succeq 0$ and $\Tr(\rho)=1$.
6163: Pure states are rank-one projectors $\rho=\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}$, while mixtures have $\rank(\rho)>1$.
6164: 
6165: For any observable $O=O^\dagger$ the expectation value is
6166: \[
6167: \langle O\rangle_\rho := \Tr(\rho\,O),
6168: \]
6169: which reduces to $\bra{\psi}O\ket{\psi}$ in the pure case.
6170: 
6171: If the register splits as $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_A\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A^c}$, the reduced state on $A$ is
6172: \[
6173: \rho_A := \Tr_{A^c}(\rho).
6174: \]
6175: All information-theoretic quantities used later (entropies, mutual information, modular Hamiltonians) are functions of such reduced density operators.
6176: In particular, the mutual information proxy $I(A\!:\!B)$ used as a weight in persistence is a functional of $(\rho_A,\rho_B,\rho_{AB})$ and is therefore insensitive to global phases and robust under mixing.
6177: 
6178: \subsection{Gates, noise, and measurements as CPTP maps}
6179: An ideal unitary gate $U$ acts on density operators by conjugation,
6180: \[
6181: \rho\longmapsto \mathcal{U}(\rho):=U\rho U^\dagger .
6182: \]
6183: Hardware noise, measurements, and classical conditioning are captured by \emph{quantum channels}: completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) maps
6184: \[
6185: \mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}').
6186: \]
6187: In Kraus form,
6188: \begin{equation}
6189: \label{eq:kraus_form}
6190: \mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_a K_a\rho K_a^\dagger,\qquad \sum_a K_a^\dagger K_a = I.
6191: \end{equation}
6192: The post-selected (unnormalized) state for outcome $a$ is $K_a\rho K_a^\dagger$, and the corresponding outcome probability is $\Tr(K_a\rho K_a^\dagger)$.
6193: 
6194: 
6195: \subsubsection{Measurement as entanglement and phase transfer in open quantum systems}
6196: \label{sec:measurement_as_entanglement}
6197: 
6198: The Kraus formula \eqref{eq:kraus_form} is already the mathematically correct way to talk about measurement in an open system, but it can feel ``black-box''.
6199: To avoid the misleading image of ``projectors from nowhere'', recall the Stinespring dilation theorem: every CPTP map arises from a unitary interaction with an ancilla (environment) followed by a partial trace.
6200: 
6201: \paragraph{Projective measurement from a unitary premeasurement.}
6202: Let $\{P_a\}_a$ be an orthogonal projector-valued measure on $\mathcal{H}_S$ and let $\mathcal{H}_M$ be a meter with pointer basis $\{|a\rangle_M\}_a$.
6203: Define a unitary $U$ on $S\otimes M$ by
6204: \[
6205: U\bigl(|\psi\rangle_S\otimes|0\rangle_M\bigr)=\sum_a (P_a|\psi\rangle_S)\otimes|a\rangle_M .
6206: \]
6207: For an input density operator $\rho$ the joint post-interaction state is $U(\rho\otimes|0\rangle\langle0|)U^\dagger$ and the reduced state on $S$ is
6208: \[
6209: \rho \longmapsto \Tr_M\!\left(U(\rho\otimes|0\rangle\langle0|)U^\dagger\right)=\sum_a P_a\rho P_a,
6210: \]
6211: which is exactly the usual L\"{u}ders update channel.
6212: Conditioning on the classical record $a$ yields the normalized state $\rho_a=P_a\rho P_a/\Tr(P_a\rho)$.
6213: Thus the projector update is the reduced description of an entangling unitary plus discarding/conditioning, not an extra physical axiom \cite{NielsenChuang2000,BreuerPetruccione2002OpenSystems}.
6214: 
6215: \paragraph{Local phases become global branch phases.}
6216: If $|\psi\rangle=\sum_a c_a |a\rangle$ in an eigenbasis of the measured observable, then the premeasurement state is
6217: \[
6218: |\Psi\rangle_{SM}=\sum_a c_a\,|a\rangle_S\otimes|a\rangle_M .
6219: \]
6220: A global phase of $|\Psi\rangle_{SM}$ is physically irrelevant, but the relative phases of the coefficients $c_a$ now live in the \emph{relative phase between different entangled branches}.
6221: When the meter/environment decoheres (or is traced out), the off-diagonal terms in $\rho_S$ disappear and those phases become operationally inaccessible on $S$ alone.
6222: In this sense, ``measurement'' transfers local phase information into a global, environment-correlated phase record.
6223: 
6224: \paragraph{POVMs and instruments.}
6225: The same construction holds for general POVMs: given Kraus operators $\{K_a\}$, there exists an ancilla and a unitary $U$ such that $K_a=\langle a|U|0\rangle$.
6226: The family $\rho\mapsto K_a\rho K_a^\dagger$ is a quantum instrument; summing over $a$ gives the unconditional CPTP map \cite{WisemanMilburn2009QuantumMeasurement}.
6227: 
6228: We will use this viewpoint repeatedly: pooling/renormalization steps are partial traces (discarding degrees of freedom), while measurement corresponds to partial trace \emph{plus} retaining a classical record of the discarded subsystem.
6229: 
6230: 
6231: A depth-$L$ circuit with interleaved noise is therefore a channel composition
6232: \[
6233: \rho_{\mathrm{out}} = (\mathcal{E}_L\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_1)(\rho_{\mathrm{in}}).
6234: \]
6235: This viewpoint is essential for two reasons:
6236: (i) it makes clear which features of the construction are stable under decoherence, and
6237: (ii) it is the natural language for renormalization, where discarding degrees of freedom is literally a partial trace.
6238: 
6239: \subsection{Shift/clock primitives as channel building blocks}
6240: For a cyclic qudit $\mathcal{H}_d$, the shift and clock unitaries $X_d,Z_d$ act on density operators by
6241: \[
6242: \rho\mapsto X_d\rho X_d^\dagger,\qquad \rho\mapsto Z_d\rho Z_d^\dagger.
6243: \]
6244: A coherent superposition of ticks is a state with nonzero off-diagonal terms in the $Z_d$-basis; phase noise (dephasing) is the channel
6245: \[
6246: \mathcal{D}_p(\rho)=(1-p)\rho + p\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_d}\ket{k}\bra{k}\rho\ket{k}\bra{k},
6247: \]
6248: which kills those off-diagonal terms.
6249: This is precisely why the persistence diagnostics in Chapter~\ref{ch:persistence} are built to tolerate loss of phase coherence: they are defined on \emph{reduced density operators} and on invariants (entropies, mutual informations, correlation functions) that make sense for mixed states.
6250: 
6251: \subsection{From unitary ``ticks'' to open-system generators}
6252: If $T$ is a perfect unitary tick, the Hamiltonian-log construction $T=e^{-iH\Delta t/\hbar}$ lives on the unitary group.
6253: For noisy ticks, one instead has a channel $\mathcal{E}$ per tick, and the infinitesimal generator is a \emph{Liouvillian} superoperator $\mathcal{L}$ with
6254: \[
6255: \mathcal{E}\approx e^{\Delta t\,\mathcal{L}},\qquad
6256: \dot\rho = \mathcal{L}(\rho).
6257: \]
6258: In the Markovian setting, $\mathcal{L}$ has Lindblad form
6259: \begin{equation}
6260: \label{eq:lindblad_form}
6261: \mathcal{L}(\rho)
6262: = -\frac{i}{\hbar}[H,\rho]
6263: + \sum_j\Bigl(L_j\rho L_j^\dagger-\frac12\{L_j^\dagger L_j,\rho\}\Bigr).
6264: \end{equation}
6265: The same branch issues appear when taking a logarithm of $\mathcal{E}$: one must choose a branch for the spectrum of the superoperator on the complex unit disk, and gluing local choices becomes a holonomy constraint in the space of channels.
6266: This is the open-system analogue of the branch ambiguity discussed earlier for $T=e^{-iH\Delta t/\hbar}$.
6267:
\chapter{Gluing local orders into global time holonomy} · lines 6268–6467
6268: \chapter{Gluing local orders into global time holonomy}
6269: 
6270: \label{ch:gluing}
6271: 
6272: 
6273: The main body sketches the slogan:
6274: \begin{center}
6275: \emph{local shifts glue to a global holonomy, and cyclic time is torsion holonomy.}
6276: \end{center}
6277: This chapter replaces the slogan by a concrete (and, crucially, \emph{cohomological}) framework.
6278: 
6279: \subsection{Orders as local operator algebras: sheaf language}
6280: \label{sec:sheaf_orders}
6281: 
6282: Let $X$ be an oriented surface (topological, smooth, or Riemann, depending on context).
6283: A \emph{surface order} in the sense of the  text is best regarded as a sheaf (or stack) of algebras on $X$ whose stalks are local matrix orders.
6284: 
6285: For this chapter we work in a simplified but robust model:
6286: 
6287: \begin{enumerate}
6288: \item Cover $X$ by open sets $\{U_i\}$ such that on each $U_i$ the order is isomorphic to a fixed hereditary order $\Lambda_{m_i}(R_i)$ as in \eqref{eq:hereditary_order}.
6289: \item On overlaps $U_{ij}=U_i\cap U_j$, glue by algebra isomorphisms
6290: \[
6291: g_{ij}:\Lambda|_{U_{ij}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda|_{U_{ij}}
6292: \]
6293: satisfying the cocycle condition $g_{ij}g_{jk}g_{ki}=\id$ on triple overlaps.
6294: \end{enumerate}
6295: 
6296: \begin{definition}[Gluing cocycle]
6297: The collection $\{g_{ij}\}$ is a \v{C}ech $1$--cocycle with values in the sheaf of groups $\Aut(\Lambda)$, hence defines a class
6298: \[
6299: [g]\in \check{H}^1(X;\Aut(\Lambda)).
6300: \]
6301: \end{definition}
6302: 
6303: \begin{remark}[Why cohomology is the right bookkeeping]
6304: The moment we say ``holonomy'', we have already committed to the fact that local data is not globally exact.
6305: Cohomology is precisely the formal language of ``locally exact, globally twisted''.
6306: \end{remark}
6307: 
6308: \subsection{Clock data as a normalizer-valued field}
6309: \label{sec:clock_field}
6310: 
6311: On each chart $U_i$ (typically centered at a vertex of the embedded graph), choose a \emph{clock operator}
6312: \[
6313: S_i\in N_{\GL_{m_i}(K_i)}(\Lambda_{m_i}(R_i)),
6314: \]
6315: where $N(\cdot)$ denotes the normalizer.
6316: In the cleanest hereditary situation we take $S_i=S_{m_i}(\pi_i)$ from \eqref{eq:twisted_shift_def}.
6317: 
6318: The physical meaning is: $S_i$ is a distinguished local unitary (or projective unitary) generating the local ``tick'' symmetry.
6319: The mathematical meaning is: $S_i$ is an element with conjugation action that preserves the local order, hence is compatible with gluing.
6320: 
6321: \begin{definition}[Clocked surface order]
6322: A \emph{clocked surface order} is a pair $(\Lambda,S)$ where $\Lambda$ is a surface order and $S=\{S_i\}$ is a choice of normalizer elements on a cover, subject to the compatibility condition
6323: \[
6324: S_j = g_{ij} S_i g_{ij}^{-1}\qquad \text{on }U_{ij}.
6325: \]
6326: \end{definition}
6327: 
6328: Compatibility says exactly that ``clock operators glue as a section of a twisted bundle''.
6329: 
6330: \subsection{Holonomy as a representation of the fundamental group}
6331: \label{sec:holonomy_rep}
6332: 
6333: Given the cocycle data, we can extract holonomy along loops.
6334: 
6335: Fix a basepoint $x\in X$ and a loop $\gamma$ represented by a chain of overlaps
6336: \[
6337: U_{i_0}\supset U_{i_0i_1}\supset U_{i_1}\supset \cdots \supset U_{i_{n-1}i_n}\supset U_{i_n}=U_{i_0}.
6338: \]
6339: Define the holonomy element
6340: \[
6341: \Hol_g(\gamma):=g_{i_0i_1}g_{i_1i_2}\cdots g_{i_{n-1}i_n}\in \Aut(\Lambda_{x}).
6342: \]
6343: 
6344: \begin{proposition}
6345: \label{prop:holonomy_rep}
6346: Up to conjugacy, $\Hol_g(\gamma)$ depends only on the homotopy class $[\gamma]\in\pi_1(X,x)$, and the map
6347: \[
6348: \Hol_g:\pi_1(X,x)\to \Out(\Lambda_x)
6349: \]
6350: is a group homomorphism.
6351: \end{proposition}
6352: 
6353: \begin{proof}
6354: Standard \v{C}ech-to-fundamental-group correspondence: refining covers and changing representatives corresponds to inserting cancelling pairs $g_{ij}g_{ji}=\id$.
6355: Dependence on basepoint yields conjugacy.
6356: \end{proof}
6357: 
6358: \begin{remark}[The correct target]
6359: For physics, global time is a projective concept: conjugation by a local unitary is gauge.
6360: Thus the natural target is an \emph{outer} automorphism group, or projective normalizers.
6361: This aligns with Proposition~\ref{prop:twisted_shift_power}, where the projective class of a twisted shift is torsion even if the linear class is not.
6362: \end{remark}
6363: 
6364: \subsection{Time holonomy: gluing the clock field}
6365: \label{sec:time_holonomy_gluing}
6366: 
6367: When the clock field $S$ is compatible with the cocycle, it becomes meaningful to transport $S$ around a loop.
6368: 
6369: Let $S_{i_0}$ be the clock operator in the chart containing the basepoint.
6370: Transporting it around $\gamma$ produces
6371: \[
6372: S_{i_0}\longmapsto \Hol_g(\gamma)\, S_{i_0}\, \Hol_g(\gamma)^{-1}.
6373: \]
6374: If this equals $S_{i_0}$, the loop preserves the clock; if not, the loop changes clock phase/rate.
6375: 
6376: This motivates the operational definition:
6377: 
6378: \begin{definition}[Time holonomy subgroup]
6379: The \emph{time holonomy subgroup} is the subgroup
6380: \[
6381: \cH_{\mathrm{time}}:=\{\Hol_g(\gamma)\in \Out(\Lambda_x)\ :\ \gamma\in\pi_1(X)\}.
6382: \]
6383: \end{definition}
6384: 
6385: \begin{definition}[Cyclic time and projective cyclic time]
6386: We say the clocked order has:
6387: \begin{itemize}
6388: \item \emph{cyclic time} if $\cH_{\mathrm{time}}$ is finite;
6389: \item \emph{projectively cyclic time} if the induced subgroup in a projective quotient (e.g.\ $\PGL$ or $\Out/\text{center}$) is finite.
6390: \end{itemize}
6391: \end{definition}
6392: 
6393: Projective cyclicity is the robust notion when local twisted shifts satisfy $S^m=\pi I$.
6394: 
6395: \subsection{Explicit gluing: pants $\rightarrow$ torus and the holonomy operator}
6396: \label{sec:pants_torus}
6397: 
6398: We now make the ``IKEA'' comment in  \S3.7 mathematically concrete.
6399: 
6400: Consider a pair of pants $P$ with boundary components $C_1,C_2,C_3$.
6401: Assign to each boundary component a local clock algebra with shift $S_i$ (finite or infinite).
6402: Gluing $C_1$ to $C_2$ produces a surface $T$ with one boundary component (a once-punctured torus).
6403: The gluing identifies the corresponding idempotents and imposes a constraint that the boundary shifts are inverses up to conjugacy:
6404: \[
6405: S_2 \sim g S_1^{-1} g^{-1}
6406: \]
6407: for a gluing element $g$ determined by the identification.
6408: 
6409: \begin{proposition}[Holonomy on the glued handle]
6410: \label{prop:handle_holonomy}
6411: Let $\alpha$ be the core loop of the handle created by gluing $C_1$ to $C_2$.
6412: Then the time holonomy along $\alpha$ is represented (up to conjugacy) by the product
6413: \[
6414: H_\alpha \;\sim\; S_1\, g\, S_2\, g^{-1}.
6415: \]
6416: If $S_2\sim g S_1^{-1} g^{-1}$ exactly, then $H_\alpha$ is central.
6417: If $S_1$ and $S_2$ are twisted shifts, centrality yields a $\pi$--power in the center.
6418: \end{proposition}
6419: 
6420: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
6421: Track the clock operator around the handle: traverse boundary $C_1$ (apply $S_1$), cross the seam (apply $g$), traverse $C_2$ (apply $S_2$), cross back (apply $g^{-1}$).
6422: \end{proof}
6423: 
6424: \begin{remark}[Where gravity enters]
6425: If entanglement changes the effective local valuation parameters $\pi_i$ (see Section~\ref{ch:expts}), then even when the topological gluing is fixed, the holonomy can drift from torsion to non-torsion.
6426: This is the clean conceptual place where ``more entanglement $\Rightarrow$ slower time'' can be encoded: increased entanglement modifies the local clock generator, which changes holonomy spectra.
6427: \end{remark}
6428: 
6429: \subsection{Nilpotent/unipotent truncations as bounded drift}
6430: \label{sec:unipotent}
6431: 
6432: The  main body proposes nilpotent/unipotent truncations to model ``almost cyclic'' time ( Part~V).
6433: We record a minimal algebraic model that interacts nicely with gluing cocycles.
6434: 
6435: \begin{definition}[Unipotent truncation]
6436: Fix $N\ge 1$ and define the unipotent quotient of the Laurent polynomial algebra
6437: \[
6438: k[t,t^{-1}] \twoheadrightarrow k[t,t^{-1}]/(t-1)^N.
6439: \]
6440: The image of $t$ acts as a unipotent operator $U=1+J$ with $J^N=0$.
6441: \end{definition}
6442: 
6443: \begin{proposition}
6444: \label{prop:unipotent_holonomy}
6445: If each local clock generator is unipotent of order $N$ in the above sense, then the holonomy subgroup $\cH_{\mathrm{time}}$ is nilpotent-by-finite: its image in the semisimple quotient is finite, and its kernel is a nilpotent group of exponent bounded by $N$ (in typical finite-dimensional realizations).
6446: \end{proposition}
6447: 
6448: \begin{remark}
6449: This proposition formalizes what the physics language calls ``bounded drift'': time may not close exactly, but its failure to close is polynomially controlled.
6450: \end{remark}
6451: 
6452: \subsection{Cohomological obstructions and a practical checklist}
6453: \label{sec:checklist}
6454: 
6455: For the reader trying to implement the theory (either mathematically or on hardware), it is useful to isolate three checkable conditions.
6456: 
6457: \begin{enumerate}
6458: \item \textbf{Local normalizer condition:} each intended clock operator $S_i$ must normalize the local order; in hereditary models this is Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted_shift_normalizes}.
6459: \item \textbf{Cocycle consistency:} gluing maps must satisfy the \v{C}ech cocycle condition; failure indicates an inconsistent surface order (or an overlooked orbifold point).
6460: \item \textbf{Holonomy classification:} compute $\Hol_g(\gamma)$ on a generating set of $\pi_1(X)$.
6461: Cyclic time corresponds to finiteness (or projective finiteness) of the generated subgroup.
6462: \end{enumerate}
6463: 
6464: These are the exact places where multiparameter persistence becomes useful:
6465: when the cocycle data is derived from noisy/estimated entanglement measurements, one expects \emph{stability} of the induced holonomy class under controlled perturbations.
6466: Section~\ref{ch:persistence} turns this into computable invariants.
6467:
\chapter{Hyperbolic surfaces, quotients, and why infinite covers are structurally unavoidable} · lines 6468–6655
6468: \chapter{Hyperbolic surfaces, quotients, and why infinite covers are structurally unavoidable}
6469: 
6470: \label{ch:hyperbolic}
6471: 
6472: 
6473: The  main body motivates hyperbolic quotients briefly ( \S6.7), mainly to justify why $\Z$--indexed (infinite-cyclic) clocks appear naturally.
6474: This chapter expands that discussion in a way that is intended to be \emph{mathematically checkable}.
6475: 
6476: \subsection{The hyperbolic plane as the universal local model}
6477: \label{sec:H_model}
6478: \label{sec:hyperbolic_plane}
6479: 
6480: We use the upper half-plane model
6481: \[
6482: \HH=\{z=x+iy\in\C : y>0\}
6483: \]
6484: with hyperbolic metric
6485: \begin{equation}
6486: \label{eq:hyperbolic_metric}
6487: ds^2=\frac{dx^2+dy^2}{y^2}.
6488: \end{equation}
6489: The group $\PSL(2,\R)$ acts by Möbius transformations
6490: \[
6491: z\longmapsto \frac{az+b}{cz+d},\qquad \begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}\in \PSL(2,\R),
6492: \]
6493: and this action is by orientation-preserving isometries of \eqref{eq:hyperbolic_metric}.
6494: 
6495: \begin{definition}[Fuchsian group]
6496: A \emph{Fuchsian group} is a discrete subgroup $\Gamma\le \PSL(2,\R)$.
6497: The quotient $\HH/\Gamma$ is a (possibly orbifold) hyperbolic surface; it has finite area if and only if $\Gamma$ is a lattice.
6498: \end{definition}
6499: 
6500: The basic structural fact we will repeatedly use is that \emph{every hyperbolic surface is a quotient of $\HH$}.
6501: The ``local-to-global'' mechanism in this volume is an algebraic analogue: local vertex orders behave like ``charts'', and gluing data behaves like a discrete connection whose holonomy lives in an automorphism group.
6502: 
6503: \subsection{Triangle groups, constellations, and dessins as quotients}
6504: \label{sec:triangle_groups}
6505: 
6506: A recurrent constellation in the source material is the $(p,q,r)$ triangle-group presentation
6507: \begin{equation}
6508: \label{eq:triangle_group}
6509: \Delta(p,q,r)=\langle \gamma_0,\gamma_1,\gamma_\infty \mid \gamma_0^p=\gamma_1^q=\gamma_\infty^r=\gamma_0\gamma_1\gamma_\infty=1\rangle.
6510: \end{equation}
6511: As noted in the source text reproduced in Appendix~A of the volume, this is one natural bridge between permutation (dessin) data and hyperbolic orbifolds.
6512: 
6513: \begin{proposition}[From dessins to orbifold quotients (sketch-level)]
6514: \label{prop:dessin_to_orbifold}
6515: Let $(\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_\infty)$ be a transitive permutation triple with $\sigma_0\sigma_1\sigma_\infty=1$.
6516: Then there is a finite-index subgroup $\Gamma\le \Delta(p,q,r)$ (for suitable $(p,q,r)$ determined by cycle lengths) and a branched covering map
6517: \[
6518: f:\HH/\Gamma \to \HH/\Delta(p,q,r)\cong \mathbb{P}^1(\C)
6519: \]
6520: ramified only above three orbifold points.
6521: \end{proposition}
6522: 
6523: \begin{remark}
6524: This proposition is a modern restatement of the fact that dessins are equivalent to Belyi maps.
6525: We \emph{do not} need arithmetic in order to use the quotient geometry; what we need is the existence of a hyperbolic universal cover and a discrete group acting with controlled stabilizers.
6526: \end{remark}
6527: 
6528: The reason hyperbolic quotients belong in the present theory is not only ``because modular curves exist'', but because they provide \emph{canonical infinite cellulations} (e.g.\ Farey tessellation) whose deck actions realize the $\Z$--type shifts that later become clock operators.
6529: 
6530: \subsection{Cellulations, ribbon graphs, and induced quivers on $\HH/\Gamma$}
6531: \label{sec:cellulations}
6532: 
6533: Fix a finite-area hyperbolic surface (or orbifold) $X=\HH/\Gamma$.
6534: A standard way to put combinatorics on $X$ is:
6535: 
6536: \begin{enumerate}
6537: \item pick a $\Gamma$--invariant tessellation $\mathcal{T}$ of $\HH$ by ideal polygons (or by geodesic triangles);
6538: \item descend $\mathcal{T}$ to a finite (or locally finite) cell structure on $X$.
6539: \end{enumerate}
6540: 
6541: The \emph{directed medial quiver} construction (used heavily in the surface-algebra material presented in earlier sections) applies verbatim to any oriented cellulation: for each face, run arrows along medial vertices counterclockwise.
6542: 
6543: \begin{definition}[Locally finite surface algebra from a hyperbolic cellulation]
6544: Let $\mathcal{T}$ be a locally finite, oriented cellulation of $\HH$ invariant under $\Gamma$.
6545: Let $Q(\mathcal{T})$ be its directed medial quiver.
6546: Define the \emph{hyperbolic surface algebra} as the completed path algebra
6547: \[
6548: A(\mathcal{T}) := k\langle\!\langle Q_1\rangle\!\rangle / I,
6549: \]
6550: where $I$ is a gentle-style ideal generated by length-$2$ forbidden turns determined by the local incidence constraints of $\mathcal{T}$.
6551: \end{definition}
6552: 
6553: Because $\mathcal{T}$ is infinite on $\HH$, $Q(\mathcal{T})$ is infinite and the completion is not optional.
6554: Finite quotients correspond to taking $\Gamma'$ of finite index and passing to $X'=\HH/\Gamma'$; the quiver becomes finite if the quotient cellulation is finite.
6555: 
6556: \subsection{Why $\Z$--indexed clocks appear: deck transformations as time translations}
6557: \label{sec:deck_time}
6558: 
6559: The  text already defines an infinite-cyclic qudit as $\ell^2(\Z)$ with bilateral shift $T|n\rangle=|n+1\rangle$ ( \S3.5).
6560: Here is the geometric reason this is the \emph{correct} representation-theoretic object.
6561: 
6562: \begin{definition}[Winding number cocycle]
6563: Let $X=\HH/\Gamma$ be a surface with at least one essential loop $\alpha\in\pi_1(X)$.
6564: Fix a basepoint and identify $\pi_1(X)$ with a subgroup of deck transformations of $\HH$.
6565: For a chosen embedded graph $\Gamma_0\subset X$ inducing a cellulation, define a function
6566: \[
6567: w:\pi_1(X)\to \Z
6568: \]
6569: by taking a lift of $\alpha$ to $\HH$ and counting algebraically how many times it crosses a chosen fundamental cycle in the lift of $\Gamma_0$.
6570: \end{definition}
6571: 
6572: This is a $\Z$--valued group $1$--cocycle in the sense that $w(\alpha\beta)=w(\alpha)+w(\beta)$.
6573: It therefore gives a unitary representation of the $\Z$--quotient of $\pi_1(X)$ on $\ell^2(\Z)$ by shifts.
6574: 
6575: \begin{proposition}[Deck-action representation yields the bilateral shift]
6576: \label{prop:deck_shift}
6577: Let $\langle t\rangle\cong \Z$ be a cyclic quotient of $\pi_1(X)$ detected by a winding cocycle $w$.
6578: Then the induced representation on $\ell^2(\Z)$ satisfies
6579: \[
6580: \rho(t)|n\rangle = |n+1\rangle,
6581: \]
6582: i.e.\ $\rho(t)$ is the bilateral shift.
6583: \end{proposition}
6584: 
6585: \begin{proof}
6586: Represent $t$ by a deck transformation that advances the lift of the fundamental cycle by one.
6587: The basis vector $|n\rangle$ is identified with the $n$-th translate of a chosen reference tile in $\HH$.
6588: Applying $t$ moves the reference tile forward by one translate, hence $|n\rangle\mapsto |n+1\rangle$.
6589: \end{proof}
6590: 
6591: \begin{remark}[Finite vs infinite time]
6592: Finite $d$--qudits arise precisely when the relevant deck action is \emph{torsion in an appropriate quotient}---for example, after passing to a finite cover and quotienting by a period.
6593: The twisted-shift relation $S_m(\pi)^m=\pi I$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:twisted_shift_power}) is an algebraic analogue: time is cyclic \emph{projectively} even if not cyclic in $\GL$.
6594: \end{remark}
6595: 
6596: \subsection{Hyperbolic distance, combinatorial distance, and entanglement distance}
6597: \label{sec:distances}
6598: 
6599: The emergent-geometry layer of the  text assigns an entanglement-derived weight $w_\psi(u,v)$ on edges and builds an effective distance $d_\psi$ (Chapter~7).
6600: On a hyperbolic surface we have three conceptually distinct distances:
6601: 
6602: \begin{enumerate}
6603: \item $d_{\mathrm{hyp}}$: the intrinsic hyperbolic geodesic distance on $X$,
6604: \item $d_{\mathrm{comb}}$: the graph metric on the $1$--skeleton of a chosen cellulation,
6605: \item $d_\psi$: an information-theoretic metric (or pseudometric) induced by a quantum state.
6606: \end{enumerate}
6607: 
6608: A coherent continuum-limit story must control the relationships among them.
6609: 
6610: \begin{proposition}[Quasi-isometry of hyperbolic and combinatorial metrics]
6611: \label{prop:quasi_isometry}
6612: Let $X$ be a finite-area hyperbolic surface and $\mathcal{T}$ a finite-type geodesic triangulation with bounded geometry (uniform bounds on edge lengths and angles).
6613: Then $(X,d_{\mathrm{hyp}})$ is quasi-isometric to the $1$--skeleton $(\mathcal{T}^{(1)},d_{\mathrm{comb}})$.
6614: \end{proposition}
6615: 
6616: \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
6617: Standard in geometric group theory: bounded-geometry triangulations give uniform bilipschitz control between piecewise-geodesic paths along edges and true geodesics.
6618: \end{proof}
6619: 
6620: \begin{definition}[Entanglement distance from mutual information]
6621: Fix a state $|\psi\rangle$ on a graph of local algebras.
6622: For vertices $u,v$ define a weight $I(u\!:\!v)$ (e.g.\ mutual information or a Renyi proxy) and set
6623: \[
6624: \ell_\psi(u,v):=\max\{0,\,-\log(I(u\!:\!v)/I_0)\},
6625: \]
6626: where $I_0$ is a reference scale making the logarithm dimensionless.
6627: Define $d_\psi$ as the shortest-path metric with edge lengths $\ell_\psi$.
6628: \end{definition}
6629: 
6630: \begin{remark}[Units]
6631: Mutual information is measured in \emph{bits} (or nats), hence dimensionless.
6632: The logarithm therefore produces a dimensionless length.
6633: To compare to $d_{\mathrm{hyp}}$ one must insert a length scale $\ell_*$ (e.g.\ average edge length of the cellulation): $d^{\mathrm{phys}}_\psi=\ell_*\, d_\psi$.
6634: \end{remark}
6635: 
6636: The mathematical burden of the emergent-gravity program is now clear:
6637: one needs conditions under which $d_\psi$ becomes quasi-isometric to $d_{\mathrm{comb}}$ (and hence to $d_{\mathrm{hyp}}$) in a scaling limit.
6638: Section~\ref{ch:persistence} makes this quantitative using multiparameter persistence stability.
6639: 
6640: \subsection{A worked hyperbolic example: modular tessellations and quotient dessins}
6641: \label{sec:worked_modular}
6642: 
6643: The modular group $\PSL(2,\Z)$ acts on $\HH$ with fundamental domain a hyperbolic triangle of angles $(0,\pi/3,\pi/2)$.
6644: The Farey tessellation is a $\PSL(2,\Z)$--invariant ideal triangulation of $\HH$.
6645: Finite-index subgroups $\Gamma\le\PSL(2,\Z)$ yield quotient surfaces (modular curves) equipped with descended triangulations, and these quotients can be encoded combinatorially as dessins/hypermaps.
6646: 
6647: For our purposes, the key point is:
6648: 
6649: \begin{quote}
6650: \emph{Modular quotients provide a canonical laboratory where (i) surface orders have arithmetic meaning, (ii) cellulations are hyperbolic, and (iii) deck actions are explicit.}
6651: \end{quote}
6652: 
6653: In Section~\ref{ch:hilbertpolya} we use this to propose concrete numerical tests for a Hilbert--Pólya-style spectral program: modular surfaces give operators with trace formulas, while surface-order clocks supply natural internal shifts that can be coupled to those operators.
6654: 
6655:
\chapter{Entanglement \texorpdfstring{$\to$}{->} metric \texorpdfstring{$\to$}{->} Einstein limit: a deeper modeling layer} · lines 6656–6982
6656: \chapter{Entanglement \texorpdfstring{$\to$}{->} metric \texorpdfstring{$\to$}{->} Einstein limit: a deeper modeling layer}
6657: 
6658: \label{ch:gravity}
6659: 
6660: One remarkable feature of quantum systems is that entanglement structure can be converted into geometric data: by quantifying correlations between subsystems, one obtains distance metrics on graphs, and these metrics can exhibit features reminiscent of gravitational geometry. This chapter makes this correspondence precise and explores how the clocked surface-order framework can naturally produce emergent Einstein-like equations.
6661: 
6662: The  main body (Chapter~7) introduces an emergent-gravity dictionary based on entanglement-induced distances on graphs and a qualitative redshift mechanism.
6663: This chapter expands the physics/mathematics interface in a way that is compatible with the surface-order and persistence machinery developed earlier.
6664: 
6665: \subsection{Indication $\rightarrow$ guidepost $\rightarrow$ endpoint (the experimental posture)}
6666: \label{sec:ibe}
6667: 
6668: Because ``emergent gravity'' is a large claim, it is useful to phrase the program as an indication/guidepost/endpoint pipeline:
6669: 
6670: \begin{itemize}
6671: \item \textbf{Indication:} Does a clocked surface-order system exhibit an operational redshift: locally defined clock rates depend on a state-dependent ``gravitational potential''?
6672: \item \textbf{Guidepost:} A stable, state-dependent metric-like object (distance matrix or graph metric) extracted from entanglement, whose curvature-like summaries vary predictably with entanglement gradients.
6673: \item \textbf{Endpoint:} In a refinement/coarse-graining limit, do these metric-like objects converge (in a precise sense) to a smooth Lorentzian/Riemannian metric satisfying Einstein-like equations?
6674: \end{itemize}
6675: 
6676: The purpose of multiparameter persistence (Section~\ref{ch:persistence}) is to make the \emph{guidepost} stable and computable; the purpose of surface orders is to make the \emph{indication} structurally constrained by gluing/holonomy.
6677: 
6678: \subsection{From entanglement to an effective metric: assumptions and definitions}
6679: \label{sec:ent_to_metric}
6680: 
6681: Let $\Gamma$ be an embedded graph with vertices $V$ and edges $E$.
6682: Let $|\psi\rangle$ be a state on $\bigotimes_{v\in V}\cH_v$.
6683: 
6684: \subsubsection{Assumption A (locality)}
6685: We assume correlations decay with graph distance in the absence of special structure.
6686: Operationally: $I(u\!:\!v)$ is largest for $u,v$ near each other in $d_{\mathrm{comb}}$.
6687: 
6688: \subsubsection{Assumption B (monotone length assignment)}
6689: Choose a monotone map from correlation to edge length.
6690: A canonical family is:
6691: \begin{equation}
6692: \label{eq:length_from_I}
6693: \ell_\psi(u,v) = \ell_*\, f\!\left(I(u\!:\!v)\right),\qquad f' < 0,
6694: \end{equation}
6695: where $\ell_*$ is a physical length scale.
6696: 
6697: The simple logarithmic choice used earlier is $f(I)=-\log(I/I_0)$; alternatives include $f(I)=I^{-1}$ or soft-thresholded versions to handle noise.
6698: 
6699: \subsubsection{Path metric}
6700: Define the induced path metric
6701: \[
6702: d_\psi(u,v)=\inf_{\gamma:u\to v}\sum_{(a,b)\in\gamma}\ell_\psi(a,b).
6703: \]
6704: 
6705: \begin{lemma}[Metric well-posedness]
6706: If $\ell_\psi(u,v)>0$ on edges and $\ell_\psi$ is symmetric, then $d_\psi$ is a metric on each connected component of $\Gamma$.
6707: If some edges have $\ell_\psi=0$ (perfect correlation), $d_\psi$ is a pseudometric.
6708: \end{lemma}
6709: 
6710: \subsection{Curvature surrogates on graphs}
6711: \label{sec:curvature_graph}
6712: 
6713: A metric on a discrete set does not automatically yield curvature.
6714: There are multiple curvature notions in discrete geometry (Ollivier--Ricci, Forman curvature, angle defects in triangulations).
6715: For surface-embedded graphs there is a particularly natural choice.
6716: 
6717: \subsubsection{Angle defect curvature for embedded triangulations}
6718: Assume $\Gamma$ is the 1-skeleton of a triangulation.
6719: Assign an \emph{effective edge length} $\ell_\psi$ and define triangle angles via Euclidean or hyperbolic cosine laws (depending on target geometry).
6720: Then at a vertex $v$, define the angle defect
6721: \[
6722: \kappa_\psi(v) := 2\pi - \sum_{\triangle\ni v} \theta_\triangle(v).
6723: \]
6724: This is a discrete Gauss curvature.
6725: 
6726: \begin{remark}[Target geometry choice]
6727: If the underlying surface is hyperbolic (Section~\ref{ch:hyperbolic}), using hyperbolic triangle laws is natural.
6728: If one aims for an emergent Lorentzian picture, one instead works with causal diamonds and modular Hamiltonians; we return to this below.
6729: \end{remark}
6730: 
6731: \subsubsection{Ollivier--Ricci as a metric-only surrogate}
6732: When only a metric is available (no embedding), Ollivier--Ricci curvature is defined by comparing Wasserstein distances between neighborhood measures.
6733: This is computationally expensive at $10^4$ vertices but feasible on local patches.
6734: 
6735: \subsection{Relational time, modular flow, and the redshift mechanism}
6736: \label{sec:relational_time}
6737: 
6738: In operator-algebraic language, a region $A$ has reduced state $\rho_A$ and modular Hamiltonian $K_A=-\log\rho_A$.
6739: Modular flow
6740: \[
6741: \sigma_A^t(O)=e^{itK_A}Oe^{-itK_A}
6742: \]
6743: is a canonical dynamics \emph{intrinsic} to the state.
6744: 
6745: The program's clock operators $S_v$ supply a \emph{kinematic} time basis.
6746: Entanglement then modifies how the state explores that basis.
6747: A minimal phenomenological model is:
6748: 
6749: \begin{definition}[Clock rate functional]
6750: Fix a local clock observable $N_v$ (e.g.\ the ``position'' basis index) and define clock rate at $v$ by
6751: \[
6752: r_v := \frac{d}{dt}\langle N_v\rangle_{\psi(t)}
6753: \]
6754: under a chosen global evolution (circuit layer, modular flow, or physical Hamiltonian).
6755: \end{definition}
6756: 
6757: \subsubsection{Redshift ansatz}
6758: Let $\varepsilon_v$ be an entanglement density proxy, for instance
6759: \[
6760: \varepsilon_v := \frac{1}{\deg(v)}\sum_{u\sim v} I(v\!:\!u).
6761: \]
6762: Postulate
6763: \begin{equation}
6764: \label{eq:redshift_ansatz}
6765: r_v \approx r_0\,e^{-\alpha \varepsilon_v}.
6766: \end{equation}
6767: For small $\alpha\varepsilon_v$, $r_v\approx r_0(1-\alpha\varepsilon_v)$.
6768: 
6769: \begin{remark}[Units]
6770: $r_v$ has units of inverse time.
6771: $\varepsilon_v$ is dimensionless (bits).
6772: Therefore $\alpha$ has units of inverse bits.
6773: To compare to GR redshift $d\tau=\sqrt{-g_{tt}}\,dt\approx (1-\Phi/c^2)dt$, we interpret
6774: \[
6775: \Phi_v/c^2 \;\sim\; \alpha\, \varepsilon_v.
6776: \]
6777: Thus $\Phi_v$ (potential) is proportional to entanglement density, with proportionality constant $c^2\alpha$.
6778: \end{remark}
6779: 
6780: 
6781: 
6782: \subsection{Modular Hamiltonians, relative entropy, and finite-dimensional proofs}
6783: \label{sec:modular_entropy_proofs}
6784: 
6785: The modular Hamiltonian $K_A=-\log\rho_A$ is the correct \emph{operator} object that packages both entanglement and an intrinsic notion of time.
6786: Because the present framework is discretized (finite-dimensional on each patch), we can be explicit and prove the standard entanglement ``first law'' statements directly in matrix-analysis terms, without field-theoretic subtleties.
6787: 
6788: \subsubsection{Definition, support, and a practical regularization}
6789: If $\rho_A$ is full rank, then $K_A$ is a bounded self-adjoint operator with spectral decomposition
6790: \[
6791: \rho_A=\sum_i p_i \ket{i}\bra{i},\qquad p_i>0,\quad \sum_i p_i=1,
6792: \]
6793: \[
6794: K_A=-\log\rho_A = \sum_i (-\log p_i)\ket{i}\bra{i}.
6795: \]
6796: If $\rho_A$ has zero eigenvalues, the logarithm diverges.
6797: Conceptually one works on the support $\mathrm{supp}(\rho_A)$; numerically, a stable regularization is
6798: \begin{equation}
6799: \label{eq:rho_eps_regularization}
6800: \rho_A^{(\epsilon)}:=(1-\epsilon)\rho_A+\epsilon\frac{I}{\dim\mathcal{H}_A},\qquad
6801: K_A^{(\epsilon)}:=-\log\rho_A^{(\epsilon)},
6802: \end{equation}
6803: with $\epsilon\downarrow 0$.
6804: All statements below remain valid under this regularization provided variations stay within the support in the $\epsilon\to 0$ limit.
6805: 
6806: \subsubsection{Relative entropy positivity (a finite-dimensional proof)}
6807: For states $\rho,\sigma\succeq 0$ with $\Tr\rho=\Tr\sigma=1$, the (Umegaki) quantum relative entropy is
6808: \[
6809: S(\rho\|\sigma):=\Tr\bigl(\rho(\log\rho-\log\sigma)\bigr),
6810: \]
6811: with the convention $S(\rho\|\sigma)=+\infty$ if $\mathrm{supp}(\rho)\not\subseteq \mathrm{supp}(\sigma)$.
6812: 
6813: \begin{proposition}[Positivity of relative entropy]
6814: \label{prop:relative_entropy_positive}
6815: For density operators $\rho,\sigma$ on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space,
6816: \[
6817: S(\rho\|\sigma)\ge 0,
6818: \]
6819: with equality iff $\rho=\sigma$.
6820: \end{proposition}
6821: 
6822: \begin{proof}
6823: Assume first that $\rho$ and $\sigma$ are full rank (otherwise use the regularization \eqref{eq:rho_eps_regularization} and continuity on the common support).
6824: For $\alpha\in(0,1)$ define the Petz--Rényi divergence
6825: \[
6826: D_\alpha(\rho\|\sigma):=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \Tr(\rho^\alpha \sigma^{1-\alpha}).
6827: \]
6828: By H\"older's inequality for Schatten norms,
6829: \[
6830: \Tr(\rho^\alpha \sigma^{1-\alpha})
6831: \le \|\rho^\alpha\|_{1/\alpha}\,\|\sigma^{1-\alpha}\|_{1/(1-\alpha)}
6832: = (\Tr\rho)^\alpha\,(\Tr\sigma)^{1-\alpha}
6833: = 1.
6834: \]
6835: Hence $\log \Tr(\rho^\alpha \sigma^{1-\alpha})\le 0$, and since $\alpha-1<0$ it follows that $D_\alpha(\rho\|\sigma)\ge 0$ for all $\alpha\in(0,1)$.
6836: 
6837: Finally, the limit $\alpha\uparrow 1$ recovers the Umegaki relative entropy:
6838: using $\frac{d}{d\alpha}\rho^\alpha=\rho^\alpha\log\rho$ and $\frac{d}{d\alpha}\sigma^{1-\alpha}=-\sigma^{1-\alpha}\log\sigma$, one checks
6839: \[
6840: \left.\frac{d}{d\alpha}\Tr(\rho^\alpha \sigma^{1-\alpha})\right|_{\alpha=1}
6841: =\Tr\bigl(\rho(\log\rho-\log\sigma)\bigr)=S(\rho\|\sigma),
6842: \]
6843: and L'Hospital's rule yields $\lim_{\alpha\uparrow 1}D_\alpha(\rho\|\sigma)=S(\rho\|\sigma)$.
6844: Therefore $S(\rho\|\sigma)\ge 0$.
6845: 
6846: Equality holds only if H\"older's inequality is tight, which forces $\rho^\alpha$ and $\sigma^{1-\alpha}$ to be proportional in Schatten norm for some $\alpha\in(0,1)$, hence $\rho=\sigma$ after normalization.
6847: \end{proof}
6848: 
6849: \subsubsection{First law of entanglement as a corollary}
6850: Fix a full-rank reference state $\sigma$ and consider a differentiable curve of states $\rho(\epsilon)$ with $\rho(0)=\sigma$ and $\Tr(\rho(\epsilon))=1$.
6851: Since $S(\rho(\epsilon)\|\sigma)\ge 0$ with equality at $\epsilon=0$, we have $\frac{d}{d\epsilon}S(\rho(\epsilon)\|\sigma)\big|_{\epsilon=0}=0$.
6852: Writing out the derivative gives the entanglement ``first law'':
6853: 
6854: \begin{corollary}[First law of entanglement]
6855: \label{cor:first_law_entanglement}
6856: Let $\sigma$ be full rank and $\rho(\epsilon)$ a differentiable curve with $\rho(0)=\sigma$.
6857: Then
6858: \[
6859: \left.\frac{d}{d\epsilon}S(\rho(\epsilon))\right|_{\epsilon=0}
6860: =
6861: \left.\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\Tr(\rho(\epsilon)\,K_\sigma)\right|_{\epsilon=0},
6862: \qquad K_\sigma:=-\log\sigma,
6863: \]
6864: where $S(\rho)=-\Tr(\rho\log\rho)$ is the von Neumann entropy.
6865: \end{corollary}
6866: 
6867: \begin{proof}
6868: Differentiate $S(\rho(\epsilon)\|\sigma)=\Tr(\rho(\epsilon)(\log\rho(\epsilon)-\log\sigma))$ at $\epsilon=0$.
6869: The derivative of $\Tr(\rho(\epsilon)\log\rho(\epsilon))$ at $\epsilon=0$ equals $\Tr(\rho'(0)(\log\sigma+I))$ (Fr\'echet derivative of $\rho\mapsto \Tr(\rho\log\rho)$), and $\Tr(\rho'(0))=0$.
6870: Thus
6871: \[
6872: 0=\left.\frac{d}{d\epsilon}S(\rho(\epsilon)\|\sigma)\right|_{\epsilon=0}
6873: = -\left.\frac{d}{d\epsilon}S(\rho(\epsilon))\right|_{\epsilon=0}
6874: -\Tr(\rho'(0)\log\sigma),
6875: \]
6876: which is the stated identity.
6877: \end{proof}
6878: 
6879: \subsubsection{Why this matters for emergent geometry}
6880: The ``redshift ansatz'' \eqref{eq:redshift_ansatz} implicitly treats entanglement as modifying local clock rates.
6881: Corollary~\ref{cor:first_law_entanglement} makes that relationship operator-theoretic: small state variations change entropies and modular energies in lockstep.
6882: In the holographic literature, this is the algebraic root of the entanglement-equilibrium route to Einstein's equation; in our discrete setting it becomes a checkable matrix identity on reduced density operators extracted from the ribbon-graph state.
6883: 
6884: \subsubsection{Practical computation on graph patches}
6885: Given a patch $A$ with Hilbert space dimension $D=d^{|A|}$, computing $K_A=-\log\rho_A$ requires an eigendecomposition of a $D\times D$ matrix.
6886: For small $|A|$ (1--6 qudits) this is feasible; for larger patches one should instead work with:
6887: (i) moments $\Tr(\rho_A^n)$, (ii) R\'enyi entropies, or (iii) low-rank approximations obtained by tensor-network compression.
6888: From the present perspective the important point is that all these quantities are functions of the density operator, hence are stable under global phase and compatible with mixed/noisy states.
6889: 
6890: \subsection{Einstein limit via entanglement equilibrium: what must be assumed}
6891: \label{sec:einstein_limit}
6892: 
6893: A conceptual route to Einstein equations from entanglement is the entanglement-equilibrium idea:
6894: for small causal diamonds, extremizing entanglement entropy at fixed volume yields Einstein's equation.
6895: We translate the logic into the present discrete setting and clearly mark what is assumption versus derivation.
6896: 
6897: \subsubsection{Assumptions}
6898: \begin{enumerate}
6899: \item \textbf{Local equilibrium:} on sufficiently small regions $A$ (patches around vertices), the state is close to a reference state for which a first-law variation holds:
6900: \[
6901: \delta S(A) = \delta\langle K_A\rangle.
6902: \]
6903: \item \textbf{Area term:} $S(A)$ has a leading ``area'' contribution proportional to boundary size (in a graph, number of cut edges) plus subleading terms.
6904: \item \textbf{Energy identification:} $\langle K_A\rangle$ acts like an energy functional for excitations in $A$.
6905: \end{enumerate}
6906: 
6907: These are strong assumptions; they are testable in the hardware sandbox by checking first-law-like relations empirically on small subsystems.
6908: 
6909: \subsubsection{Discrete variational statement}
6910: Let $A$ be a local patch.
6911: Write
6912: \[
6913: S(A)=\eta\,|\partial A| + S_{\mathrm{bulk}}(A),
6914: \]
6915: with $\eta$ setting units (bits per edge).
6916: Vary the state and (optionally) the graph weights.
6917: Under local equilibrium, $\delta S = \delta\langle K\rangle$ becomes:
6918: \[
6919: \eta\,\delta|\partial A| + \delta S_{\mathrm{bulk}}(A) = \delta\langle K_A\rangle.
6920: \]
6921: If $\delta S_{\mathrm{bulk}}$ is interpreted as ``matter'' and $\delta|\partial A|$ as ``geometry'', this is the discrete Einstein equation template.
6922: 
6923: \begin{remark}[Why surface orders matter here]
6924: Surface orders constrain which local variations are allowed because local degrees of freedom are not arbitrary: they are local matrix orders glued by cocycles.
6925: Thus, the allowed variations of $|\partial A|$ and of $K_A$ are coupled by algebraic constraints.
6926: In other words: the noncommutative local model replaces ``free metric variation'' by ``allowed gluing-compatible variations''.
6927: \end{remark}
6928: 
6929: \subsection{A realistic numerical experiment: curvature from entanglement gradients on hyperbolic patches}
6930: \label{sec:hyperbolic_expt}
6931: 
6932: Pick a hyperbolic tiling patch ( \S12.4) and embed it into hardware connectivity.
6933: Prepare two families of states:
6934: \begin{itemize}
6935: \item a baseline high-entanglement state (random circuit depth $L$),
6936: \item a gradient state where a subregion has reduced entanglement (shallower depth or partial measurement).
6937: \end{itemize}
6938: 
6939: Compute:
6940: \begin{enumerate}
6941: \item $d_\psi$ from \eqref{eq:length_from_I},
6942: \item curvature surrogates $\kappa_\psi$ (angle defects or Ollivier--Ricci),
6943: \item local clock rates $r_v$ as in Section~\ref{ch:expts}.
6944: \end{enumerate}
6945: 
6946: Test two correlations:
6947: \[
6948: \varepsilon_v \uparrow \Longrightarrow r_v \downarrow,
6949: \qquad
6950: \nabla \varepsilon \neq 0 \Longrightarrow \kappa_\psi \neq \text{const}.
6951: \]
6952: 
6953: The multiparameter persistence tool enters by tracking whether curvature and distance features persist across:
6954: \begin{itemize}
6955: \item threshold choices $\lambda$,
6956: \item noise realizations (bootstrap),
6957: \item coarse-graining levels (patch size).
6958: \end{itemize}
6959: 
6960: \subsection{Failure modes (and why they are informative)}
6961: \label{sec:gravity_fail}
6962: 
6963: This is a high-risk modeling layer.
6964: Here are the failure modes that would be scientifically useful:
6965: 
6966: \begin{itemize}
6967: \item \textbf{No stable metric:} $d_\psi$ fluctuates wildly under small perturbations of $W_\psi$; persistence reveals intrinsic instability.  This suggests the graph/state pair is not in a geometric paradigm.
6968: \item \textbf{No redshift correlation:} $r_v$ is uncorrelated with $\varepsilon_v$ after controlling for noise.  This falsifies the simplest redshift ansatz \eqref{eq:redshift_ansatz}.
6969: \item \textbf{Curvature surrogate mismatch:} $\kappa_\psi$ does not track entanglement gradients.  This suggests either wrong curvature notion or wrong metric extraction map $f$.
6970: \item \textbf{Holonomy decouples:} time holonomy invariants (Section~\ref{ch:gluing}) do not correlate with entanglement-induced geometry.  This indicates the clock field is not correctly coupled to state-dependent data, and pushes attention back to the twisted-shift/valuation coupling.
6971: \end{itemize}
6972: 
6973: A coherent theory must tell you \emph{which} failure mode you should expect when assumptions are violated.
6974: That is exactly what the persistence framework is good at: it distinguishes stable phenomena from artifacts.
6975: 
6976:

6982: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Renormalization via graph quanvolution and graph quantum attention on ribbon graphs} · lines 6983–8096
6983: \chapter{Renormalization via graph quanvolution and graph quantum attention on ribbon graphs}
6984: \label{ch:renorm_graph}
6985: 
6986: This chapter adds a concrete, implementation-oriented bridge between the ribbon-graph/dessin
6987: formalism developed earlier in the paper and two practical families of learnable coarse-graining
6988: mechanisms that are now standard in machine learning: (i) convolution/pooling and (ii) attention.
6989: Our goal is \emph{not} to import grids or sequences into the present context, but rather to express
6990: both mechanisms intrinsically on a fat-graph (a graph equipped with a cyclic ordering of half-edges
6991: at each vertex), and to do so for \emph{cyclic qudits} of general period~$d$, including the limiting
6992: ``infinite-period'' case.
6993: 
6994: A unifying theme is that the trainable primitives are (i) \emph{multilinear} in their quantum inputs
6995: (unitaries and tensor contractions) and (ii) become \emph{effectively nonlinear} once we allow
6996: measurements, classical post-processing (e.g.\ activations, normalizations), and/or conditioning.
6997: This is precisely the structure of renormalization: a local map that discards degrees of freedom
6998: while producing new effective variables at a coarser scale.
6999: 
7000: \section{Cyclic qudits, finite or infinite period, and learnable coarse-graining channels}
7001: \label{sec:cyclic_qudits_channels}
7002: 
7003: \subsection{Cyclic qudits as group-algebra degrees of freedom}
7004: Fix a ``period'' parameter
7005: \[
7006:   d \in \mathbb{N}_{\ge 2} \cup \{\infty\}.
7007: \]
7008: For finite~$d$, the \emph{cyclic qudit} is the Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}_d \cong \mathbb{C}^d$
7009: with computational basis $\{\ket{k}: k\in \mathbb{Z}_d\}$.
7010: For $d=\infty$, a natural model is $\mathcal{H}_\infty \cong \ell^2(\mathbb{Z})$
7011: with basis $\{\ket{k}: k\in \mathbb{Z}\}$ (angular momentum basis), and its Pontryagin dual
7012: ``angle basis'' $\ket{\theta}$ with $\theta\in[0,2\pi)$, where $\langle k\mid \theta\rangle
7013: = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} e^{ik\theta}$.
7014: Both cases can be packaged as the group algebra $\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_d]$, with the convention
7015: $\mathbb{Z}_\infty:=\mathbb{Z}$.
7016: 
7017: The generalized (Weyl) shift and clock operators are
7018: \[
7019:   X_d \ket{k} = \ket{k+1},\qquad
7020:   Z_d \ket{k} = \omega^{k}\ket{k},\qquad \omega=e^{2\pi i/d}
7021: \]
7022: for finite~$d$, with the usual relation $Z_d X_d = \omega X_d Z_d$.
7023: In the infinite-period case, one may view $Z_\infty$ as multiplication by $e^{i\theta}$ in the
7024: $\theta$-representation, and $X_\infty$ as the unit shift on $\ell^2(\mathbb{Z})$; then
7025: $Z_\infty X_\infty = e^{i} X_\infty Z_\infty$ is interpreted in the exponentiated (Weyl) form.
7026: We will also use the qudit Fourier transform $F_d$ defined by
7027: $F_d\ket{k} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{\ell\in \mathbb{Z}_d}\omega^{k\ell}\ket{\ell}$ (finite~$d$),
7028: and its continuous analog $F_\infty:\ell^2(\mathbb{Z})\leftrightarrow L^2(S^1)$.
7029: 
7030: \subsection{Ribbon-graph locality and patch registers}
7031: Let $\Gamma=(V,E)$ be a ribbon graph (fat-graph) with a cyclic ordering of half-edges at each
7032: vertex. For a vertex~$v\in V$, write $N(v)$ for its neighbors and $N_r(v)$ for the
7033: $r$-hop neighborhood (as an induced subgraph). The fat-graph structure gives a \emph{cyclic order}
7034: on the incident half-edges at~$v$, which we denote by
7035: \[
7036:   (e_1(v),e_2(v),\dots,e_{\deg(v)}(v)).
7037: \]
7038: When we form a ``local patch register'' for~$v$, we will always order the corresponding qudits
7039: according to this cyclic order. This ensures that any weight-sharing or equivariance constraints
7040: we impose are compatible with the ribbon structure, not merely the underlying abstract graph.
7041: 
7042: \subsection{Learned coarse-graining as a CPTP map}
7043: A single renormalization step is most cleanly expressed as a completely positive trace-preserving
7044: (CPTP) map. Fix a patch $S_v \subseteq V$ (e.g.\ $S_v=N_r(v)$ for a chosen~$r$). Let
7045: $\rho_{S_v}$ be the reduced density operator on the patch.
7046: A \emph{learned coarse-graining channel} is a family of CPTP maps
7047: \[
7048:   \mathcal{R}_{\theta,v}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_d^{\otimes |S_v|})\to
7049:   \mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_d^{\otimes m}),
7050: \]
7051: parameterized by trainable parameters~$\theta$, and producing $m$ ``effective'' qudits (or,
7052: more generally, $m$ classical features obtained by measurement).
7053: A canonical form is
7054: \begin{equation}
7055:   \mathcal{R}_{\theta,v}(\rho_{S_v})
7056:   \;=\;
7057:   \mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{discard}}\!\Bigl[
7058:     U_{\theta,v}\,\rho_{S_v}\,U_{\theta,v}^\dagger
7059:   \Bigr],
7060:   \label{eq:renorm_channel_basic}
7061: \end{equation}
