7062: where $U_{\theta,v}$ is a unitary acting on the patch, and $\mathrm{discard}$ denotes a chosen
7063: subset of degrees of freedom that are traced out (pooling). If we additionally measure part of the
7064: patch and feed the outcomes forward, \eqref{eq:renorm_channel_basic} becomes a general quantum
7065: instrument; \emph{nonlinearity} then arises at the level of the overall learning system via
7066: conditioning and classical post-processing.
7067: 
7068: \paragraph{Multilinearity versus effective nonlinearity.}
7069: Unitary evolution is linear in $\rho$, but observables of the form
7070: \[
7071:   f(\rho;\theta)=\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(O\,U_\theta \rho U_\theta^\dagger\right)
7072: \]
7073: are \emph{multilinear} in the tensor factors of $\rho$ when $\rho$ is a product state across a
7074: patch, and are polynomial in the matrix elements of $U_\theta$.
7075: Nonlinearities in practical architectures arise from:
7076: (i) pooling/partial trace (discarding factors), (ii) conditioning on measurement outcomes, and
7077: (iii) explicit classical nonlinearities applied to measured features (e.g.\ ReLU, sigmoid, softmax).
7078: This is the precise sense in which ``learned multilinear maps with nonlinearities'' naturally
7079: implement renormalization.
7080: 
7081: \subsection{Finite-period truncations and quotients of the infinite-period model}
7082: The infinite-period model $\mathcal{H}_\infty \cong \ell^2(\mathbb{Z})$ is useful conceptually,
7083: but hardware and simulations require finite resources.
7084: Two standard reductions are:
7085: \begin{enumerate}
7086: \item \textbf{Truncation (energy cutoff).} Fix $N\in\mathbb{N}$ and restrict to
7087: $\mathrm{span}\{\ket{k}: |k|\le N\}$, giving a $(2N+1)$-dimensional approximation.
7088: Truncation is appropriate when dynamics and states remain approximately band-limited in the
7089: $\ket{k}$ basis. A failure mode is ``wrap-around'' or leakage when gates create significant weight
7090: near the cutoff.
7091: \item \textbf{Quotient (periodization).} Impose the relation $k\sim k+d$, i.e.\ replace
7092: $\mathbb{Z}$ by $\mathbb{Z}_d$. Formally, this is passing from the group algebra
7093: $\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}]$ to $\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_d]$. Quotients are appropriate when the model
7094: has an intrinsic periodicity (or when we deliberately enforce one as an inductive bias).
7095: A failure mode is aliasing: physically distinct modes become identified by the quotient.
7096: \end{enumerate}
7097: In later examples we will keep the notation~$d$ and interpret $d=\infty$ as the conceptual limit,
7098: while implementations use one of the two finite reductions.
7099: 
7100: \section{Quanvolutional renormalization on ribbon graphs (graph convolution)}
7101: \label{sec:graph_quanv}
7102: 
7103: Quanvolutional layers were introduced as a hybrid quantum--classical analog of convolution \cite{Henderson2019Quanv}:
7104: a small quantum circuit is applied repeatedly to local patches, and expectation values are used as
7105: features for a subsequent classical (or quantum) network. In the image setting, a patch is an
7106: $n\times n$ window; here the patch is an induced subgraph $S_v$ around a vertex~$v$.
7107: 
7108: A graph-native instantiation of the same philosophy---explicitly combining edge-local quantum ``convolution'' with measurement-based pooling---is the QGCN model of Zheng--Gao--L\"u \cite{Zheng2021QGCN}, which we adapt to ribbon graphs and cyclic qudits in Section~\ref{sec:qgcn_renorm}.
7109: 
7110: \subsection{Random versus trainable patch circuits (and why this looks like RG)}
7111: \label{subsec:quanv_random_trainable}
7112: In the original quanvolution proposal \cite{Henderson2019Quanv}, the patch circuit is often taken to be
7113: \emph{random} rather than trained: one samples a shallow circuit from a fixed gate set, applies it to each
7114: patch, and uses a small collection of expectation values as features. The classical network downstream then
7115: learns how to combine these features. Two points make this relevant to the present renormalization framing:
7116: \begin{enumerate}
7117: \item \textbf{Random circuits as learned feature maps.} Even without training, a family of random local
7118: unitaries induces a rich, nonlinear feature map after measurement. In geometric terms, one is learning in the
7119: space of \emph{pushforwards} of local patch measures through a family of multilinear maps.
7120: \item \textbf{Trainable circuits as adaptive coarse-graining.} When the patch circuit is trained, it becomes a
7121: bona fide \emph{learned RG move}: it selects a local basis (via $U_\theta$), compresses by discarding degrees of
7122: freedom, and exposes the effective observables (the readout) that are helpful for the task.
7123: \end{enumerate}
7124: For ribbon graphs, both variants are natural. A random patch circuit provides a strong inductive bias while
7125: keeping training costs low; a trainable circuit offers greater expressivity at the expense of more quantum
7126: resources (shots) for gradient estimation.
7127: 
7128: \subsection{From grid patches to ribbon-graph patches}
7129: Fix a patch rule $v\mapsto S_v$ (for example $S_v=N_r(v)$), and fix an ordering of the vertices
7130: in $S_v$ consistent with the cyclic order of half-edges in the ribbon structure.
7131: For each patch we specify:
7132: \begin{enumerate}
7133: \item an \textbf{encoding map} $x\mapsto \rho_{S_v}(x)$ from classical features (node/edge labels)
7134: into a product (or weakly entangled) state on the patch register;
7135: \item a \textbf{patch circuit} $U_{\theta}$, shared across all~$v$ (weight sharing);
7136: \item a \textbf{readout} given by a family of observables $\{O_a\}_{a=1}^p$ on a subset of the
7137: patch qudits.
7138: \end{enumerate}
7139: The quanvolutional feature at~$v$ is then
7140: \begin{equation}
7141:   \phi_a(v)
7142:   \;=\;
7143:   \mathrm{Tr}\!\left(
7144:     O_a\,
7145:     U_{\theta}\,\rho_{S_v}(x)\,U_{\theta}^\dagger
7146:   \right),
7147:   \qquad a=1,\dots,p.
7148:   \label{eq:quanv_feature}
7149: \end{equation}
7150: In practice the trace is estimated by sampling. Equation~\eqref{eq:quanv_feature} is exactly a
7151: local renormalization step: a patch is mapped to a smaller feature vector (or to a smaller quantum
7152: register if we keep the post-unitary reduced state on a subset of qudits).
7153: 
7154: \subsection{A ribbon-compatible graph convolution ansatz}
7155: Let $v$ have degree $\deg(v)=q$ and incident half-edges ordered cyclically.
7156: A simple and ribbon-compatible patch circuit on the star $S_v=\{v\}\cup N(v)$ is
7157: \begin{equation}
7158:   U_{\theta}^{(v)}
7159:   \;=\;
7160:   \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{q} \mathrm{CZ}_d^{(v,u_j)}\Bigr)\,
7161:   \Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{q} R_d^{(u_j)}(\alpha_j)\Bigr)\,
7162:   R_d^{(v)}(\beta)\,F_d^{(v)},
7163:   \label{eq:star_patch_unitary}
7164: \end{equation}
7165: where $(u_1,\dots,u_q)$ are the neighbors of~$v$ in cyclic order, $R_d(\cdot)$ denotes a chosen
7166: one-qudit rotation family generated by $X_d$ and $Z_d$, and
7167: \[
7168:   \mathrm{CZ}_d \;:=\; \sum_{a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_d}\omega^{ab}\,\ket{a,b}\!\bra{a,b}
7169: \]
7170: is the generalized controlled-phase.
7171: For $d=\infty$, replace $\mathrm{CZ}_d$ by the corresponding Weyl-type entangling gate
7172: $e^{i\,\hat n\otimes \hat n}$ on the momentum basis (or its truncated/periodized version).
7173: 
7174: The readout can be taken as generalized Pauli expectations on the central site, e.g.\
7175: \[
7176:   \phi_1(v)=\langle Z_d^{(v)}\rangle,\qquad
7177:   \phi_2(v)=\langle X_d^{(v)}\rangle,
7178: \]
7179: or, more invariantly, a small set of Fourier modes $\langle (Z_d^{(v)})^m\rangle$.
7180: 
7181: \subsection{Pooling as edge contraction: a QCNN/MERA-style renormalization step}
7182: To build a \emph{hierarchical} renormalization (rather than a single feature extraction layer),
7183: we need pooling that produces a coarser ribbon graph.
7184: One convenient pooling rule on a graph is:
7185: choose a maximal matching $M\subseteq E$ (disjoint edges), and for each matched edge $(u,v)\in M$,
7186: apply a two-vertex channel that produces a single effective vertex, i.e.\ contract $(u,v)$.
7187: At the level of Hilbert spaces, a pooling isometry is a map
7188: \[
7189:   W:\mathcal{H}_d^{\otimes 2}\to \mathcal{H}_d,
7190: \]
7191: implemented as a unitary on $\mathcal{H}_d^{\otimes 2}$ followed by discarding one factor.
7192: On a matched edge we may take, for example,
7193: \[
7194:   W(\rho_{uv}) = \mathrm{Tr}_{v}\!\left[ U_{\theta}^{(uv)}\,\rho_{uv}\,(U_{\theta}^{(uv)})^\dagger\right],
7195: \]
7196: where $U_{\theta}^{(uv)}$ is built from generalized SUM and phase gates,
7197: \[
7198:   \mathrm{SUM}_d:\ket{a,b}\mapsto \ket{a,a+b\;(\mathrm{mod}\;d)}.
7199: \]
7200: When $\Gamma$ is a ribbon graph, contracting an edge has a canonical effect on the cyclic order
7201: data at the endpoints; thus the pooled graph remains a ribbon graph.
7202: Iterating this matching-and-contraction procedure yields a bona fide renormalization flow
7203: $\Gamma\to \Gamma_1\to \Gamma_2\to \cdots$.
7204: 
7205: \subsection{A worked micro-example: trivalent dessin patch}
7206: Consider a trivalent ribbon graph (typical for dessins) and take the star patch $S_v=\{v\}\cup N(v)$.
7207: Encode real node features $x_u\in\mathbb{R}$ into one-qudit phase states by
7208: $\ket{\psi(x_u)} = Z_d^{\lfloor \kappa x_u\rfloor}\ket{0}$ for a scale~$\kappa$ (finite~$d$),
7209: or $\ket{\psi(x_u)} = e^{i x_u \hat n}\ket{0}$ in the $d=\infty$ model (followed by truncation).
7210: Apply the star circuit~\eqref{eq:star_patch_unitary} and read out $\phi_1(v)=\langle Z_d^{(v)}\rangle$.
7211: Even this minimal construction produces a nonlinear function of the neighborhood features once
7212: we post-process $\phi_1(v)$ (e.g.\ apply a classical nonlinearity and/or stack layers), while keeping
7213: the inductive bias that the computation is ribbon-local.
7214: 
7215: 
7216: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
7217: \section{Renormalization via quantum graph convolutional neural networks (QGCN) on ribbon graphs}
7218: \label{sec:qgcn_renorm}
7219: 
7220: The quanvolutional discussion above deliberately started from the most ``physics'' viewpoint:
7221: a local quantum circuit on a patch, followed by readout and (optionally) pooling, is already a
7222: renormalization step.  The \emph{Quantum Graph Convolutional Neural Network} (QGCN) architecture
7223: of Zheng--Gao--L\"u \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} is a clean reference point because it makes explicit how
7224: graph convolution, pooling, and measurement can be implemented by parameterized quantum circuits,
7225: while keeping the overall layout close to the classical GCN paradigm.
7226: 
7227: Our aim in this section is threefold:
7228: \begin{enumerate}
7229: \item to extract the \emph{renormalization-theoretic core} of QGCN, namely ``local aggregation + dimensional reduction'',
7230: \item to recast the construction \emph{intrinsically on a ribbon graph} (no grids, no sequences, no loss of cyclic-order data),
7231: \item to generalize from \emph{qubits} to \emph{cyclic qudits of period $d\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}$}, and to make the
7232: finite-period truncation/quotient issues completely explicit.
7233: \end{enumerate}
7234: 
7235: \subsection{Learned multilinear patch maps and where nonlinearity really enters}
7236: \label{subsec:qgcn_multilinear}
7237: 
7238: A recurring point in this chapter is that ``quantum layers are multilinear'' and yet ``the overall architecture is nonlinear''.
7239: Here is the clean statement in the present graph setting.
7240: 
7241: Fix a patch $S_v$ and an encoding map $x\mapsto \rho_{S_v}(x)$.
7242: For a family of observables $\{O_a\}_{a=1}^p$ and a patch unitary $U_\theta$, the measurable features are
7243: \begin{equation}
7244:   \phi_a(v;x,\theta)
7245:   \;:=\;
7246:   \mathrm{Tr}\!\left(O_a\,U_\theta\,\rho_{S_v}(x)\,U_\theta^\dagger\right).
7247:   \label{eq:qgcn_feature_general}
7248: \end{equation}
7249: If $\rho_{S_v}(x)$ is a tensor product of \emph{single-site amplitude encodings},
7250: $\rho_{S_v}(x)=\bigotimes_{u\in S_v}\ket{\psi(x_u)}\!\bra{\psi(x_u)}$ with
7251: $\ket{\psi(x_u)}=\sum_{i} x_{u,i}\ket{i}$, then each $\phi_a$ is a \emph{multilinear polynomial}
7252: in the amplitudes and their conjugates:
7253: \begin{equation}
7254:   \phi_a(v;x,\theta)
7255:   \;=\;
7256:   \sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
7257:   C^{(a)}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}(\theta)
7258:   \prod_{u\in S_v} x_{u,i_u}\,\overline{x_{u,j_u}},
7259:   \label{eq:qgcn_multilinear_expansion}
7260: \end{equation}
7261: where $\mathbf{i}=(i_u)_{u\in S_v}$ and $\mathbf{j}=(j_u)_{u\in S_v}$ index basis labels on the patch.
7262: Equivalently, for a single register $| \psi(x)\rangle$ of dimension $D$,
7263: $\phi_a(x,\theta)=x^\ast M_a(\theta) x$ is a quadratic form with $M_a(\theta)=U_\theta^\dagger O_a U_\theta$.
7264: 
7265: Thus, \emph{the quantum part is algebraically tame}: it is a learned multilinear (tensor) map.
7266: The true nonlinearities arise from operations that are \emph{essentially renormalization}:
7267: \begin{itemize}
7268: \item \textbf{Pooling / partial trace:} discarding qudits is not invertible and changes the effective degrees of freedom.
7269: \item \textbf{Measurement and conditioning:} measurement outcomes select branches of an instrument (nonlinear at the level of
7270: the induced update rule on classical features).
7271: \item \textbf{Classical nonlinearities:} activations, normalizations, and softmax applied to measured features.
7272: \end{itemize}
7273: This division of labor is exactly what one expects from RG: linear/multilinear local physics together with
7274: nonlinear state reduction and rescaling steps.
7275: 
7276: \subsection{The QGCN architecture of Zheng--Gao--L\"u as a ribbon-local RG move}
7277: \label{subsec:qgcn_architecture}
7278: 
7279: Zheng--Gao--L\"u \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} propose a four-block architecture:
7280: \emph{(i) quantum state preparation}, \emph{(ii) quantum graph convolutional layers},
7281: \emph{(iii) quantum pooling layers}, and \emph{(iv) quantum measurement}.
7282: We recall each block in some detail before adapting the whole to ribbon graphs with cyclic qudits.
7283: 
7284: \paragraph{Block (i): quantum state preparation (amplitude encoding).}
7285: Given a graph $G=(V,E)$ with $|V|=N$ vertices and a feature matrix $X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$,
7286: QGCN encodes graph data into a quantum state via \emph{amplitude encoding}:
7287: \[
7288:   \ket{\psi_X}
7289:   \;=\;
7290:   \frac{1}{\|X\|_F}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sum_{j=0}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}-1} X_{ij}\,\ket{i}\ket{j},
7291: \]
7292: using $\lceil\log_2 N\rceil+\lceil\log_2 d_{\mathrm{in}}\rceil$ qubits.
7293: The adjacency structure is encoded separately in ``topology qubits'' that control whether a
7294: two-qubit gate is applied on a given pair~\cite{Zheng2021QGCN}.
7295: 
7296: \paragraph{Block (ii): quantum graph convolution layers.}
7297: A convolution layer applies a universal two-qubit gate $U^{(2)}\in\mathrm{SU}(4)$ on each pair of connected
7298: vertices.  In~\cite{Zheng2021QGCN}, $U^{(2)}$ is decomposed into 3~CNOT gates and 15~basic single-qubit
7299: rotation gates (the Khaneja--Glaser decomposition), yielding a constant-depth parameterized circuit per edge.
7300: The ``graph convolution'' interpretation is that each application of $U^{(2)}$ on edge $(u,v)$ mixes the
7301: quantum features of $u$ and~$v$---the quantum analog of message passing.
7302: Stacking $L$ such layers propagates information $L$~hops.
7303: 
7304: \paragraph{Block (iii): quantum pooling.}
7305: After convolution, a subset of qubits is measured in the computational basis, producing classical bits.
7306: Conditioned on these measurement outcomes, a unitary correction is applied to the remaining (unmeasured) qubits.
7307: This implements a quantum instrument whose classical branch determines the nonlinear update rule for the coarse
7308: features---the renormalization step.
7309: 
7310: \paragraph{Block (iv): quantum measurement (readout).}
7311: The final block measures all remaining qubits to produce a classical feature vector used for downstream
7312: classification or regression.  In~\cite{Zheng2021QGCN}, the measurement is in the Pauli-$Z$ basis, and the
7313: resulting expectation values are the features.
7314: 
7315: \medskip
7316: For our purposes, the key conceptual step is to \emph{externalize the adjacency}:
7317: \begin{quote}
7318: \emph{on a physical graph device, adjacency is not an input register but the native connectivity graph on which two-site gates exist.}
7319: \end{quote}
7320: Thus the ``controlled-by-topology'' mechanism becomes simply ``apply a fixed two-site gate on each physical edge''.
7321: This is exactly the graph-centric viewpoint compatible with dessins/fat-graphs.
7322: 
7323: Concretely, let $\Gamma=(V,E)$ be the ribbon graph of the computation.
7324: A QGCN-style \emph{convolution layer} is specified by a two-site unitary $U_{\theta}^{(2)}$ (shared across edges) and a schedule
7325: (e.g.\ edge coloring) so that gates on disjoint edges can be executed in parallel.
7326: One layer applies $U_{\theta}^{(2)}$ to every edge:
7327: \begin{equation}
7328:   \rho \;\longmapsto\; \Bigl(\prod_{(u,v)\in E}^{\mathrm{schedule}} U_{\theta}^{(2)}(u,v)\Bigr)\,\rho\,
7329:   \Bigl(\prod_{(u,v)\in E}^{\mathrm{schedule}} U_{\theta}^{(2)}(u,v)\Bigr)^\dagger.
7330:   \label{eq:qgcn_edge_layer}
7331: \end{equation}
7332: As in \cite{Zheng2021QGCN}, stacking $L$ such layers implements higher-order neighborhood aggregation
7333: (because information propagates $L$ hops in $L$ rounds of edge-local mixing).
7334: 
7335: A QGCN-style \emph{pooling layer} then reduces the active degrees of freedom.
7336: The pooling circuit in \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} measures a subset of qubits and conditionally applies a unitary on remaining qubits
7337: based on the measurement outcomes.  In renormalization language, this is a local quantum instrument whose classical outcomes
7338: drive a nonlinear update rule for coarse features.
7339: 
7340: On a ribbon graph, the most geometrically transparent pooling rule is still \emph{edge contraction along a matching}
7341: (Section~\ref{sec:graph_quanv}): choose a maximal matching $M\subseteq E$, and on each $(u,v)\in M$ apply an isometry
7342: $W:\mathcal{H}_d^{\otimes 2}\to\mathcal{H}_d$ implemented by ``unitary + discard'' (and optionally measurement-conditioned
7343: corrections).
7344: Because contraction is canonical in the ribbon category, the cyclic order data is preserved and the pooled graph remains a ribbon graph.
7345: 
7346: \subsection{Generalization from qubits to cyclic qudits of period \texorpdfstring{$d$}{d}}
7347: \label{subsec:qgcn_qudit_generalization}
7348: 
7349: The original QGCN paper \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} is written in the qubit language (amplitude encoding into $2^n$-dimensional states,
7350: two-qubit unitaries in $\mathrm{SU}(4)$, and Pauli-$Z$ measurements).
7351: In our setting each site carries a cyclic qudit $\mathcal{H}_d$ with $d\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}$
7352: (Section~\ref{sec:cyclic_qudits_channels}).  The replacements are systematic:
7353: 
7354: \begin{enumerate}
7355: \item \textbf{Local feature encoding.}
7356: Amplitude encoding becomes the map
7357: \[
7358:   x\in\mathbb{C}^{d^n},\ \|x\|_2=1
7359:   \quad\longmapsto\quad
7360:   \ket{\psi_d(x)}=\sum_{i=0}^{d^n-1} x_i\ket{i}\ \in (\mathcal{H}_d)^{\otimes n}.
7361: \]
7362: For $d=\infty$, one uses a truncation to a finite-dimensional subspace or a quotient to $\mathbb{Z}_d$
7363: (Section~\ref{sec:cyclic_qudits_channels}).
7364: 
7365: \item \textbf{Two-site ``convolution'' gates.}
7366: Two-qubit universality statements used in \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} (e.g.\ decompositions into CNOTs and one-qubit rotations)
7367: generalize to two-qudit universality: a generic two-qudit unitary in $\mathrm{SU}(d^2)$ can be synthesized from
7368: one-qudit $\mathrm{SU}(d)$ rotations together with an entangling gate such as $\mathrm{SUM}_d$ or $\mathrm{CZ}_d$.
7369: For cyclic qudits, $\mathrm{SUM}_d$ is the natural analog of CNOT,
7370: \[
7371:   \mathrm{SUM}_d:\ket{a,b}\mapsto \ket{a,a+b\;(\mathrm{mod}\;d)}.
7372: \]
7373: In the $d=\infty$ (rotor) picture, replace modular addition by addition on $\mathbb{Z}$, and implement it approximately
7374: on a truncated subspace.
7375: 
7376: \item \textbf{Readout.}
7377: Pauli-$Z$ expectation values become Weyl-mode expectations such as $\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle$ for small $m$,
7378: or, in the rotor model, $\langle e^{im\theta}\rangle$.
7379: This is particularly aligned with the ``clock'' interpretation of cyclic qudits:
7380: we are literally reading out low Fourier modes of an angular distribution.
7381: \end{enumerate}
7382: 
7383: The practical message is that QGCN is not ``a qubit model'' but a \emph{group-algebra model}:
7384: once the cyclic group $\mathbb{Z}_d$ is the organizing principle, the qubit case is simply $d=2$.
7385: 
7386: \subsection{Pooling as a measurement-conditioned contraction channel}
7387: \label{subsec:qgcn_pooling_channel}
7388: 
7389: To emphasize the RG reading, it is helpful to write pooling as a channel.
7390: Fix an edge $(u,v)\in M$ in a matching.
7391: A pooling layer specifies a channel
7392: \begin{equation}
7393:   \mathcal{P}_{\theta}^{(uv)}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_d^{\otimes 2})\to \mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_d),
7394:   \qquad
7395:   \mathcal{P}_{\theta}^{(uv)}(\rho_{uv})
7396:   \;=\;
7397:   \sum_{m} \mathrm{Tr}_{v}\!\Bigl[
7398:     V_{\theta,m}\,U_{\theta}\,\rho_{uv}\,U_{\theta}^\dagger\,V_{\theta,m}^\dagger
7399:   \Bigr],
7400:   \label{eq:qgcn_pool_channel}
7401: \end{equation}
7402: where $U_\theta$ is a two-site unitary, $\{V_{\theta,m}\}$ are (possibly trivial) conditional corrections depending on a
7403: measurement outcome $m$ obtained from measuring $v$, and the trace discards the measured/pooled site.
7404: Equation~\eqref{eq:qgcn_pool_channel} is the natural graph generalization of the pooling block in \cite{Zheng2021QGCN}.
7405: 
7406: Iterating ``edge-layer + pooling'' defines a discrete RG flow on ribbon graphs:
7407: \[
7408: (\Gamma,\rho)\ \longmapsto\ (\Gamma_1,\rho_1)\ \longmapsto\ (\Gamma_2,\rho_2)\ \longmapsto\ \cdots,
7409: \]
7410: where $\Gamma_{k+1}$ is obtained from $\Gamma_k$ by contracting a matching and inheriting the ribbon structure.
7411: In this form it becomes completely transparent how to attach persistence-based diagnostics (Section~\ref{ch:persistence}):
7412: one can track which topological/geometric features of the state-induced filtrations persist across RG scale.
7413: 
7414: \subsection{A worked micro-example: a 3-vertex path and one QGCN-RG step}
7415: \label{subsec:qgcn_micro_example}
7416: 
7417: Consider the path graph $u$--$v$--$w$ with the induced ribbon structure (trivial cyclic order at each degree-$2$ vertex).
7418: Let each site carry a cyclic qudit $\mathcal{H}_d$.
7419: Encode scalar features $x_u,x_v,x_w\in\mathbb{R}$ into phase states
7420: $\ket{\psi(x)}:=Z_d^{\lfloor \kappa x\rfloor}\ket{0}$ (finite $d$) or $\ket{\psi(x)}:=e^{ix\hat n}\ket{0}$ (rotor model),
7421: and begin with the product state
7422: $\rho_0=\ket{\psi(x_u)}\!\bra{\psi(x_u)}\otimes \ket{\psi(x_v)}\!\bra{\psi(x_v)}\otimes \ket{\psi(x_w)}\!\bra{\psi(x_w)}$.
7423: 
7424: \paragraph{Convolution step.}
7425: Apply a shared two-site entangler $U_\theta^{(2)}$ on $(u,v)$ and $(v,w)$ (in two rounds if needed to avoid overlap).
7426: For instance, a minimal cyclic-qudit message-passing gate on an edge is \eqref{eq:message_passing_gate}.
7427: This produces $\rho_1$.
7428: 
7429: \paragraph{Pooling (contraction) step.}
7430: Measure the middle qudit $v$ in the computational basis, obtaining outcome $m\in\mathbb{Z}_d$.
7431: Conditioned on $m$, apply a correction $V_{\theta,m}$ (often a simple $X_d$-shift or phase) to $u$ or $w$,
7432: and then discard $v$.
7433: The result is an effective two-site state $\rho_{uw}$ on the contracted graph $u$--$w$.
7434: 
7435: Even in this toy example one sees the RG structure:
7436: the edge-local unitary is multilinear, while the measurement-conditioned pooling creates a nonlinear effective update rule
7437: for the coarse variables (e.g.\ the measured Fourier modes at $u$ and $w$).
7438: On a hyperbolic ribbon graph with $\sim 10^5$ sites (Section~\ref{sec:hyperbolic_cyclic_hardware}),
7439: the same local primitives execute in a small number of rounds because the degree is bounded and the edge chromatic number is small.
7440: 
7441: \subsection{Relation to geodesic CNN on surfaces: from continuous patches to ribbon-graph qudits}
7442: \label{subsec:gcnn_ribbon_bridge}
7443: 
7444: Masci--Boscaini--Bronstein--Vandergheynst \cite{Masci2015GCNN} introduced \emph{geodesic convolutional neural networks} (GCNN) on manifolds by replacing the Euclidean translation used in classical CNNs with local geodesic polar coordinates.  In this subsection we recall their construction in some detail, describe the layer taxonomy of GCNN, and explain how the quanvolutional framework on ribbon graphs developed above is a natural combinatorial and quantum-hardware-ready counterpart.
7445: 
7446: \subsubsection{Geodesic convolution on a manifold}
7447: Let $X$ be a smooth Riemannian manifold (or a triangulated surface).  For each point $x\in X$, the \emph{local geodesic polar coordinate system} $\Omega(x)\colon B_{\rho_0}(x)\to [0,\rho_0]\times [0,2\pi)$ maps the geodesic ball of radius~$\rho_0$ into radial and angular coordinates $(\rho,\theta)$.  A signal $f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^P$ is \emph{interpolated into the patch} by the \textbf{patch operator}
7448: \begin{equation}
7449:   \bigl(D(x)f\bigr)(\rho,\theta)
7450:   \;=\;
7451:   \int_X v_{\rho,\theta}(x,x')\,f(x')\,dx',
7452:   \label{eq:patch_operator}
7453: \end{equation}
7454: where the interpolation weights $v_{\rho,\theta}$ are products of a radial Gaussian $v_\rho(x,x')\propto e^{-(d_X(x,x')-\rho)^2/\sigma_\rho^2}$ and an angular Gaussian $v_\theta(x,x')\propto e^{-d_X^2(\Gamma(x,\theta),x')/\sigma_\theta^2}$, with $\Gamma(x,\theta)$ the geodesic ray from~$x$ in direction~$\theta$.
7455: 
7456: Because the choice of angular origin is arbitrary, the \textbf{geodesic convolution} (GC) of the patch with a filter $a(\theta,r)$ is defined with a shift $\Delta\theta$:
7457: \begin{equation}
7458:   (f\star a)(x)
7459:   \;=\;
7460:   \sum_{\theta,\,r} a(\theta+\Delta\theta,\,r)\;\bigl(D(x)f\bigr)(r,\theta),
7461:   \label{eq:geodesic_convolution}
7462: \end{equation}
7463: and the convolution is evaluated at \emph{all} $N_\theta$ rotations $\Delta\theta = 0,\frac{2\pi}{N_\theta},\ldots,\frac{2\pi(N_\theta-1)}{N_\theta}$, producing $N_\theta$ outputs per filter.  For a bank of $P$ input channels and $Q$ output filters, each rotated by $N_\theta$ angles, the GC layer output is
7464: \begin{equation}
7465:   f^{\mathrm{out}}_{\Delta\theta,q}(x)
7466:   \;=\;
7467:   \sum_{p=1}^{P} (f_p\star a_{\Delta\theta,qp})(x),
7468:   \qquad q=1,\ldots,Q.
7469:   \label{eq:gc_layer}
7470: \end{equation}
7471: 
7472: \subsubsection{Layer taxonomy of GCNN}
7473: \label{subsubsec:gcnn_layers}
7474: The GCNN architecture of \cite{Masci2015GCNN} composes layers from the following palette:
7475: 
7476: \begin{enumerate}
7477: \item \textbf{Linear (LIN) layer.}  A pointwise linear combination of input channels, optionally followed by a nonlinearity $\xi$ (e.g.\ ReLU):
7478: \[
7479:   f_q^{\mathrm{out}}(x) = \xi\!\Bigl(\sum_{p=1}^{P} w_{qp}\,f_p^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\Bigr),
7480:   \qquad q=1,\ldots,Q.
7481: \]
7482: This adjusts input/output dimensions without altering the geometry.
7483: 
7484: \item \textbf{Geodesic Convolution (GC) layer.}  As in \eqref{eq:gc_layer}, applying all $N_\theta$ rotations of the filter bank.  This is the core layer that replaces Euclidean convolution.
7485: 
7486: \item \textbf{Angular Max-Pooling (AMP) layer.}  Eliminates the angular coordinate ambiguity by taking the maximum over all rotations:
7487: \[
7488:   f_p^{\mathrm{out}}(x) = \max_{\Delta\theta}\; f_{\Delta\theta,p}^{\mathrm{in}}(x),
7489:   \qquad p=1,\ldots,P.
7490: \]
7491: After this layer the output is rotation-invariant with respect to the angular origin.
7492: 
7493: \item \textbf{Fourier Transform Magnitude (FTM) layer.}  An alternative to AMP that first applies the patch operator (at each $(\rho,\theta)$ per input dimension), takes the Fourier transform in the angular coordinate, and returns the absolute value:
7494: \[
7495:   f_p^{\mathrm{out}}(\rho,\omega)
7496:   = \Bigl|\sum_\theta e^{-i\omega\theta}\,(D(x)f_p^{\mathrm{in}})(x)(\rho,\theta)\Bigr|,
7497: \]
7498: yielding complex-phase-invariant features.
7499: 
7500: \item \textbf{Covariance (COV) layer.}  Aggregates point-wise descriptors into a global shape descriptor
7501: $\mathbf{f}^{\mathrm{out}} = \int_X (\mathbf{f}^{\mathrm{in}}(x)-\boldsymbol\mu)(\mathbf{f}^{\mathrm{in}}(x)-\boldsymbol\mu)^\top\,dx$,
7502: used for tasks like shape retrieval.
7503: \end{enumerate}
7504: 
7505: A key insight of \cite{Masci2015GCNN} is that several classical spectral descriptors are \emph{special cases} of GCNN:
7506: \begin{itemize}
7507: \item \emph{Heat kernel signature (HKS)} is a fixed LIN layer with band-pass filters $\tau_\nu(\lambda)=\exp(-t\lambda)$.
7508: \item \emph{Wave kernel signature (WKS)} uses filters $\tau_\nu(\lambda)=\exp\!\bigl(\frac{\log\nu-\log\lambda}{2\sigma^2}\bigr)$.
7509: \item \emph{Optimal spectral descriptors (OSD)} are obtained by a \emph{learnable} LIN layer parametrized via B-splines $\tau_q(\lambda)=\sum_m a_{qm}\beta_m(\lambda)$.
7510: \item \emph{Intrinsic shape context} is a fixed LIN layer producing HKS/WKS descriptors followed by a fixed FTM layer.
7511: \end{itemize}
7512: 
7513: \subsubsection{From geodesic patches to ribbon-graph cyclic ordering: a combinatorial bridge}
7514: \label{subsubsec:gcnn_to_ribbon}
7515: 
7516: A ribbon graph $\Gamma$ embedded in a surface~$\Sigma$ provides a \emph{combinatorial discretization} of the geodesic patch construction.  The formal correspondence is:
7517: 
7518: \begin{center}
7519: \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
7520: \begin{tabular}{l@{\qquad$\longleftrightarrow$\qquad}l}
7521: \toprule
7522: \textbf{GCNN on a manifold} & \textbf{Quanvolution on a ribbon graph} \\
7523: \midrule
7524: Point $x\in X$ & Vertex $v\in V(\Gamma)$ \\
7525: Geodesic ball $B_{\rho_0}(x)$ & $r$-neighborhood $N_r(v)$ \\
7526: Angular coordinate $\theta\in[0,2\pi)$ & Cyclic order $\sigma_v$ at vertex $v$ \\
7527: $N_\theta$ angular bins & $\deg(v)$ cyclic positions \\
7528: Radial bins ($N_\rho$ levels) & Graph distance shells $\{d(v,u)=k\}$ \\
7529: Patch operator $D(x)$ & Feature encoding $\ket{\psi(h_u)}$ for $u\in N_r(v)$\\
7530: Learned filter $a(\theta+\Delta\theta,r)$ & Parameterized gate $U_\theta^{(2)}$ on edge $(v,u)$ \\
7531: Angular rotation by $\Delta\theta$ & Cyclic shift of half-edge labels at $v$ \\
7532: AMP ($\max_{\Delta\theta}$) & Max over cyclic shifts; or $X_d$-shift gate \\
7533: FTM ($|\hat f(\omega)|$) & $F_d$-basis measurement, discard phase \\
7534: ReLU / activation $\xi$ & Measurement + classical post-processing \\
7535: \bottomrule
7536: \end{tabular}
7537: \end{center}
7538: 
7539: \begin{proposition}[GCNN as a classical shadow of quanvolution on ribbon graphs]
7540: \label{prop:gcnn_ribbon}
7541: Let $\Gamma$ be a ribbon graph embedded in a surface~$\Sigma$, with $d:=\max_{v}\deg(v)$.
7542: Equip each vertex~$v$ with a cyclic qudit $\mathcal{H}_d$ (period $d$ matching the maximal degree).
7543: Define the \emph{discrete angular coordinate} at a vertex~$v$ of degree~$\delta_v$ as
7544: $\theta_v^{(j)}:= 2\pi j/\delta_v$ for $j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\delta_v}$,
7545: indexed by the cyclic ordering of half-edges at~$v$.
7546: Then:
7547: \begin{enumerate}
7548: \item The GC layer \eqref{eq:gc_layer} on $\Sigma$, restricted to $1$-neighborhoods and discretized at the vertices and cyclic positions of~$\Gamma$, reduces to a message-passing update of the form \eqref{eq:qgcn_multilinear_expansion} with the parameterized gate applied to each edge $(v,u)$ depending on the cyclic position of the half-edge $v\to u$.
7549: \item The AMP layer corresponds to taking the maximum over cyclic-shift orbits of the gate outcomes; in the quantum setting, one instead applies $X_d^j$ to the state at~$v$ before the gate and takes the maximum post-measurement, which is the cyclic-qudit analog of pooling over angular rotations.
7550: \item The FTM layer corresponds to measuring the qudit at~$v$ in the Fourier ($F_d$) basis and discarding the phase, yielding an output that depends on the absolute value of each Fourier component---precisely $|\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle|$.
7551: \end{enumerate}
7552: In this sense, the QGCN-style quanvolution on ribbon graphs (Section~\ref{subsec:qgcn_architecture}) is a quantum-hardware-native generalization of geodesic convolution on surfaces, with cyclic qudits providing a natural home for angular and Fourier-mode features.
7553: \end{proposition}
7554: 
7555: \begin{remark}[Infinite period and continuous geodesic limit]
7556: In the $d=\infty$ rotor model ($\mathcal{H}_\infty \cong L^2(S^1)$), the cyclic qudit at each vertex becomes a genuine angular degree of freedom on the circle, and the Fourier modes $(Z_\infty)^m = e^{im\theta}$ form a countable orthonormal family.
7557: The discrete-to-continuous bridge of Proposition~\ref{prop:gcnn_ribbon} then becomes exact in the limit $N_\theta\to\infty$, $N_\rho\to\infty$: the GCNN patch operator \eqref{eq:patch_operator} converges to the patch integral, and the GC filter bank \eqref{eq:gc_layer} converges to a learned filter on $L^2(S^1\times[0,\rho_0])$.
7558: In practice, truncation to a $(2N+1)$-dimensional subspace (Section~\ref{sec:hyperbolic_cyclic_hardware}) or quotienting to $\mathbb{Z}_d$ provides a finite working approximation with resolution controlled by the cutoff.
7559: \end{remark}
7560: 
7561: \begin{remark}[QGAT parameter efficiency]
7562: From a practical standpoint, the parameter overhead of the quantum approach is moderate.
7563: For instance, \cite{Ning2025QGAT} reports that a 4-head QGAT with 8~qubits and 6~strongly-entangling layers uses approximately $6.72\times 10^6$ parameters on the PPI benchmark, compared to $6.44\times 10^6$ for GAT and $12.84\times 10^6$ for GATv2, while achieving comparable F1 scores.
7564: The cyclic-qudit generalization does not change the parameter count substantially (the variational circuit has the same number of real parameters per gate, independent of~$d$), although training-time overhead from quantum simulation is significant ($5$--$6\times$ slower than classical baselines when using PennyLane-based simulators~\cite{Ning2025QGAT}).
7565: On native hardware, this overhead vanishes and the per-shot execution time is determined by circuit depth, not~$d$.
7566: \end{remark}
7567: 
7568: 
7569: \section{Renormalization via graph quantum attention (graph attention)}
7570: \label{sec:graph_qattn}
7571: 
7572: Attention differs from convolution in that the neighborhood aggregation weights are
7573: \emph{data-dependent}. This makes attention particularly natural for the present setting because
7574: dessins/fat-graphs already encode a rich combinatorial structure; attention allows the model to
7575: learn which parts of that structure are relevant for a given task.
7576: 
7577: \subsection{Graph attention as adaptive message passing}
7578: Let $h_v^{(\ell)}$ be a feature at vertex~$v$ at layer~$\ell$ (classical or quantum).
7579: A graph-attention update has the schematic form
7580: \begin{equation}
7581:   h_v^{(\ell+1)}
7582:   \;=\;
7583:   \sigma\!\left(
7584:     \sum_{u\in N(v)\cup\{v\}} \alpha_{vu}^{(\ell)}\;\mathsf{V}_\theta\!\left(h_u^{(\ell)}\right)
7585:   \right),
7586:   \qquad
7587:   \alpha_{vu}^{(\ell)} \propto
7588:   \exp\!\Bigl(\langle \mathsf{Q}_\theta(h_v^{(\ell)}),\mathsf{K}_\theta(h_u^{(\ell)})\rangle\Bigr),
7589:   \label{eq:graph_attention_classical}
7590: \end{equation}
7591: with trainable maps $\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{K},\mathsf{V}$ and a normalization over $u\in N(v)\cup\{v\}$.
7592: The nonlinearity arises from the normalization (softmax) and the activation~$\sigma$.
7593: 
7594: \subsection{Quantum attention primitives and Gaussian-projected self-attention}
7595: One practical route to quantum attention is to realize the ``query/key/value'' maps as parameterized
7596: quantum circuits and estimate the required inner products by quantum overlap tests.
7597: Another route, emphasized in the quantum-attention paper \cite{Li2022QuantumAttention}, is for a fully graph-native attention mechanism in which a single variational circuit produces many attention heads in parallel (rather than approximating the attention kernel), see the QGAT architecture \cite{Ning2025QGAT}, adapted to cyclic qudits and ribbon graphs in Section~\ref{sec:qgat_renorm}.
7598: 
7599: \emph{Gaussian-projected} approximation to the attention kernel.
7600: At a high level, one rewrites the softmax kernel in a feature-map form and approximates it by a
7601: Gaussian random projection, which yields attention weights computable from a manageable number of
7602: random features and trainable circuits.
7603: 
7604: \paragraph{A concrete Gaussian-projected scoring rule.}
7605: In \cite{Li2022QuantumAttention}, one particularly simple (and hardware-friendly) attention score is obtained by
7606: measuring a single Pauli observable on the query and key outputs and exponentiating the squared difference.
7607: In their notation, for token~$s$ attending to token~$j$,
7608: \begin{align}
7609:   \alpha_{s,j}\; &:=\;\exp\!\Bigl(-\bigl(\langle Z_q\rangle_s-\langle Z_k\rangle_j\bigr)^2\Bigr), \\ 
7610:   				 &= \;\exp\!\Bigl(-\bigl(\langle s| Z_q| s^*\rangle-\langle j| Z_k| j^* \rangle \bigr)^2\Bigr)
7611: \end{align}
7612: followed by normalization. This replaces the usual dot-product score by a Gaussian kernel on a \emph{measured}
7613: one-dimensional projection, which can drastically reduce quantum resources.
7614: 
7615: For cyclic qudits, there is an evident generalization: replace $Z$ by a chosen Fourier mode of the clock operator,
7616: e.g.\ use $\mathrm{Re}\,\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle$ or $\mathrm{Im}\,\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle$ for some small~$m$,
7617: and set
7618: \[
7619:   \alpha_{v,u}\;:=\;\exp\!\Bigl(-\bigl(\mathrm{Re}\,\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle_{q_v}-\mathrm{Re}\,\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle_{k_u}\bigr)^2\Bigr),
7620: \]
7621: with normalization over $u\in N_r(v)$. In the $d=\infty$ model (quantum rotor), $(Z_\infty)^m$ is multiplication by
7622: $e^{im\theta}$ in the angle basis, so this score compares low Fourier modes of the learned angular distributions.
7623: The key operational point is that \emph{only local, single-site measurements are needed to score edges}, making
7624: this style of attention compatible with strict locality and limited readout bandwidth.
7625: 
7626: For ribbon graphs, we restrict attention to local neighborhoods $N_r(v)$ (or to edges) so that the
7627: computation remains compatible with the local gate constraints of hardware.
7628: The resulting \emph{graph quantum attention layer} is:
7629: \begin{enumerate}
7630: \item \textbf{Local query/key/value circuits.} For each vertex~$v$, prepare quantum registers
7631: $\ket{q_v},\ket{k_v},\ket{v_v}$ by applying parameterized circuits
7632: $U_Q(\theta_Q),U_K(\theta_K),U_V(\theta_V)$ to an encoded state $\ket{\psi(h_v)}$.
7633: For cyclic qudits these circuits can be built from $X_d,Z_d,F_d$ and two-site Weyl entanglers.
7634: \item \textbf{Edge-local similarity estimation.} For each edge $(v,u)$ (or $u\in N_r(v)$),
7635: estimate a similarity score $s_{vu}$, e.g.\ via a generalized SWAP/Hadamard test giving an estimate
7636: of $|\langle q_v\mid k_u\rangle|^2$, or via a random-feature (Gaussian-projected) approximation.
7637: \item \textbf{Classical normalization.} Compute $\alpha_{vu} = \exp(s_{vu})/\sum_{w\in N_r(v)}\exp(s_{vw})$.
7638: \item \textbf{Aggregation/pooling.} Update an effective feature/state at~$v$ by a weighted mixture of
7639: value registers, and optionally pool to a coarser graph by contracting edges with large $\alpha_{vu}$.
7640: \end{enumerate}
7641: 
7642: \paragraph{Renormalization interpretation.}
7643: Attention defines a \emph{data-dependent} coarse-graining: rather than averaging uniformly over a
7644: patch, it learns which edges/vertices to keep and which to suppress. In a ribbon-graph setting this
7645: can be sharpened by enforcing that $\alpha_{vu}$ respects the cyclic order around~$v$ (e.g.\ by
7646: restricting the scoring circuit to depend on relative cyclic position), so that the learned
7647: renormalization does not destroy the fat-graph structure.
7648: 
7649: \subsection{A worked micro-example: attention on a heptagonal face}
7650: Suppose $\Gamma$ contains a heptagonal face (as in common hyperbolic tilings).
7651: Let $v$ be a vertex on that face and take $N_1(v)$ as the neighborhood.
7652: Choose a single-head attention layer in which the similarity score on an adjacent vertex~$u$ is
7653: estimated by the overlap
7654: \[
7655:   s_{vu} \approx \log\!\left(|\langle q_v\mid k_u\rangle|^2 + \varepsilon\right),
7656: \]
7657: with a small stabilizer $\varepsilon>0$ for numerical stability.
7658: A concrete cyclic-qudit circuit for preparing $q_v$ from a feature $h_v\in\mathbb{R}$ is
7659: \[
7660:   \ket{q_v} = F_d\,Z_d^{\lfloor \kappa h_v\rfloor}\,F_d^\dagger \ket{0},
7661: \]
7662: which maps $h_v$ into a localized ``momentum'' wavepacket (finite~$d$) or its truncated analog
7663: ($d=\infty$). Similar choices apply for $k_u$. The overlap test is then a short, edge-local circuit.
7664: Because hyperbolic graphs have small diameter (growing only logarithmically with~$|V|$ for fixed degree),
7665: a small number of attention layers already yields long-range sensitivity, which is exactly the kind of
7666: ``RG compression'' one exploits on negatively curved geometries.
7667: 
7668: 
7669: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
7670: \section{Renormalization via quantum graph attention networks (QGAT) on ribbon graphs}
7671: \label{sec:qgat_renorm}
7672: 
7673: The Gaussian-projected attention discussion above emphasized a hardware-friendly point:
7674: \emph{one can often score edges using only local, low-bandwidth measurements}.
7675: The recently proposed \emph{Quantum Graph Attention Network} (QGAT) of Ning--Li--Chen \cite{Ning2025QGAT}
7676: pushes in a complementary direction: it uses a variational quantum circuit to generate
7677: \emph{multi-head attention logits} directly from amplitude-encoded edge features, exploiting quantum parallelism
7678: to share parameters across heads.
7679: 
7680: In this section we transplant the QGAT mechanism into the present dessin/fat-graph setting, with two modifications:
7681: (i) we treat the underlying structure as a \emph{ribbon graph} (so cyclic order is part of the data), and
7682: (ii) we generalize from qubits to cyclic qudits of finite or infinite period.
7683: 
7684: \subsection{QGAT recap: a single circuit producing many attention heads}
7685: \label{subsec:qgat_recap}
7686: 
7687: QGAT \cite{Ning2025QGAT} starts from node features $h\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ and an edge list $E$.
7688: After a classical linear projection $W$ into a multi-head space, QGAT forms for each edge $(i,j)$ a concatenated vector
7689: $a_{ij}$ built from projected and raw node features, and compresses it to the input length required by amplitude encoding.
7690: In the notation of \cite{Ning2025QGAT}, one arrives at a vector $a'_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{2^{n_q}\cdot \lceil h/n_q\rceil}$
7691: that is amplitude-encoded into $n_q$ qubits:
7692: \[
7693:   \ket{\psi(a'_{ij})}=\sum_{t=0}^{2^{n_q}-1} (a'_{ij})_t\,\ket{t}.
7694: \]
7695: A strongly-entangling variational circuit $U(\theta)$ is applied, and \emph{each qubit} is measured in $Z$ to produce one logit:
7696: \begin{equation}
7697:   e^{(k)}_{ij}
7698:   \;:=\;
7699:   \langle \psi(a'_{ij})\mid U(\theta)^\dagger Z_k U(\theta)\mid \psi(a'_{ij})\rangle,
7700:   \qquad k=1,\dots,n_q.
7701:   \label{eq:qgat_logits_qubit}
7702: \end{equation}
7703: These logits are normalized by softmax over neighbors to obtain attention weights $\alpha^{(k)}_{ij}$, and node updates are formed
7704: by the usual attention aggregation (concat or mean) \cite{Ning2025QGAT}.
7705: A key point stressed in \cite{Ning2025QGAT} is that the same circuit evaluation yields \emph{many heads at once}
7706: by reading out different qubits, providing parameter sharing and potential efficiency advantages.
7707: 
7708: \subsection{Ribbon-graph adaptation: encoding cyclic order and respecting locality}
7709: \label{subsec:qgat_ribbon_adaptation}
7710: 
7711: On a ribbon graph, each vertex $i$ comes with a cyclic order of incident half-edges.
7712: This structure is absent in ordinary graphs but is central for dessins and for surface-embedded computation.
7713: There are (at least) two principled ways to incorporate it into attention:
7714: 
7715: \paragraph{(A) Cyclic position as an explicit feature.}
7716: For an oriented edge $i\to j$, define
7717: \[
7718:   p(i\to j)\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}_{\deg(i)}
7719: \]
7720: to be the index of the half-edge $(i\to j)$ in the cyclic ordering at $i$.
7721: This is a \emph{cyclic variable} and can be encoded naturally into a cyclic qudit or into a phase feature (Fourier mode).
7722: One can then enlarge the edge feature vector by including $p(i\to j)$ and (optionally) a small set of its Fourier features
7723: $\big(\cos(2\pi m p/\deg(i)),\sin(2\pi m p/\deg(i))\big)$.
7724: This ensures the learned attention coefficients can depend on the ribbon structure.
7725: 
7726: \paragraph{(B) Cyclic equivariance by architectural constraint.}
7727: Alternatively, one can enforce that the scoring circuit depends only on \emph{relative cyclic offsets} and share parameters across
7728: rotations around $i$, mirroring how classical CNNs share parameters across translations.
7729: This produces a ribbon-equivariant attention mechanism: rotating the half-edge order rotates intermediate activations but leaves
7730: invariants (e.g.\ pooled features) unchanged.
7731: 
7732: Both choices are compatible with strict locality: for a radius-$r$ neighborhood $N_r(i)$, attention weights are computed only for
7733: edges $(i,j)$ with $j\in N_r(i)$.
7734: 
7735: \subsection{Cyclic-qudit generalization of QGAT: Weyl measurements and qudit entangling layers}
7736: \label{subsec:qgat_qudit_generalization}
7737: 
7738: To generalize \eqref{eq:qgat_logits_qubit} from qubits ($d=2$) to cyclic qudits ($d\ge 2$ or $d=\infty$), we replace:
7739: \[
7740:   Z_k \quad\leadsto\quad (Z_d)_k,
7741: \]
7742: the generalized clock operator on the $k$-th qudit (Section~\ref{sec:cyclic_qudits_channels}), or a low Fourier mode $(Z_d)_k^m$.
7743: A direct analogue of the QGAT logit is therefore
7744: \begin{equation}
7745:   e^{(k)}_{ij}
7746:   \;:=\;
7747:   \mathrm{Tr}\!\Bigl(
7748:     (Z_d)_k\,
7749:     U(\theta)\,
7750:     \rho(a'_{ij})\,
7751:     U(\theta)^\dagger
7752:   \Bigr),
7753:   \qquad k=1,\dots,n_q,
7754:   \label{eq:qgat_logits_qudit}
7755: \end{equation}
7756: where $\rho(a'_{ij})=\ket{\psi_d(a'_{ij})}\!\bra{\psi_d(a'_{ij})}$ is the (qudit) amplitude encoding of the edge feature vector.
7757: In the infinite-period rotor model, one may instead use $e^{(k)}_{ij}=\langle e^{i\theta_k}\rangle$ or a low mode
7758: $\langle e^{im\theta_k}\rangle$; truncation/quotienting makes this numerically finite.
7759: 
7760: \paragraph{Strongly entangling layers for qudits.}
7761: QGAT uses ``strongly entangling'' blocks built from parameterized one-qubit rotations and two-qubit entanglers \cite{Ning2025QGAT}.
7762: For cyclic qudits, replace those by:
7763: \begin{itemize}
7764: \item one-qudit gates in $\mathrm{SU}(d)$ generated by $X_d$ and $Z_d$ (and, if needed for universality, a non-Clifford gate),
7765: \item two-qudit entanglers such as $\mathrm{CZ}_d$ or $\mathrm{SUM}_d$ on chosen pairs of qudits.
7766: \end{itemize}
7767: A minimal ``range-$r$'' entangling pattern suitable for graph attention is to entangle qudit $k$ with qudit $(k+r)\bmod n_q$ inside
7768: the edge register, mirroring the tunable-range pattern in \cite{Ning2025QGAT}.
7769: 
7770: \paragraph{Multilinearity and nonlinearity.}
7771: As in \eqref{eq:qgcn_multilinear_expansion}, each logit \eqref{eq:qgat_logits_qudit} is a quadratic form in the amplitude-encoded
7772: input (hence multilinear in local features when the encoding is factorized).
7773: Nonlinearity is introduced by the softmax normalization over neighbors and the subsequent activation $\sigma$ applied to aggregated
7774: values.  QGAT emphasizes that one can omit an explicit LeakyReLU on logits because the quantum circuit itself already yields a rich
7775: nonlinear feature map once measurement is included \cite{Ning2025QGAT}; the same observation holds in the cyclic-qudit setting.
7776: 
7777: \subsection{Attention-guided pooling as a data-dependent RG step on a fat-graph}
7778: \label{subsec:qgat_pooling_rg}
7779: 
7780: In a renormalization reading, attention does not merely aggregate: it \emph{selects effective degrees of freedom}.
7781: The attention weights $\alpha_{ij}^{(k)}$ define a data-dependent notion of which edges ``matter'' at the current scale.
7782: A ribbon-graph-compatible pooling rule is:
7783: 
7784: \begin{quote}
7785: \emph{contract edges that are consistently high-attention (or high mutual-attention) while preserving cyclic order, and discard low-attention
7786: edges as effectively irrelevant couplings.}
7787: \end{quote}
7788: 
7789: More formally, for each vertex $i$ choose a small set of neighbors $j$ maximizing a symmetrized score
7790: $s(i,j):=\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar\alpha_{ij}+\bar\alpha_{ji}\big)$ (averaged over heads), subject to a matching constraint to avoid overlaps,
7791: and contract those edges.  This produces a coarser ribbon graph $\Gamma\mapsto \Gamma'$ and an induced coarse state via the pooling
7792: channel \eqref{eq:qgcn_pool_channel}.  Because the contraction is guided by the learned attention, this defines a \emph{data-adaptive RG flow}
7793: rather than a fixed coarse-graining scheme.
7794: 
7795: \subsection{Scaling and explicit circuit sketches on hyperbolic cyclic-qudit hardware}
7796: \label{subsec:qgat_hyperbolic_scaling}
7797: 
7798: On a hyperbolic connectivity graph (Section~\ref{sec:hyperbolic_cyclic_hardware}), attention has a particularly clean scaling story:
7799: bounded degree implies constant-round parallel schedules for edge-local gates, while negative curvature implies logarithmic diameter, so
7800: $O(\log N)$ layers already propagate information across an $N$-site device.
7801: 
7802: The expensive step in QGAT is that logits are, in the original formulation \cite{Ning2025QGAT}, \emph{edge-specific} quantum circuit evaluations.
7803: For a device with $N\sim 10^5$ vertices and bounded degree, the number of edges is $\Theta(N)$, so a na\"ive full-edge evaluation requires
7804: $\Theta(N)$ circuit instances per layer.  There are three practical mitigations that preserve the renormalization philosophy:
7805: 
7806: \begin{enumerate}
7807: \item \textbf{Stochastic attention.} Evaluate quantum logits only on a sampled subset of edges per layer (as in large-scale classical GNN training),
7808: using classical approximations or cached scores for the remainder.
7809: \item \textbf{Node-local quantum features + classical scoring.} Use quantum circuits to compute node embeddings $q_i,k_i$ locally, then score edges
7810: classically via simple kernels of measured features (cf.\ the Gaussian-projected approach above).
7811: \item \textbf{Coarse-to-fine schedules.} Use aggressive pooling early (RG), rapidly reducing $N$, so that expensive edge-specific attention is only
7812: performed on coarser graphs.
7813: \end{enumerate}
7814: 
7815: In all three variants, the cyclic-qudit setting remains natural: attention scores can be expressed in terms of low Fourier modes of the clock
7816: operators, and truncation/quotienting determines the resolution at which ``angular'' information is retained.
7817: 
7818: 
7819: \section{Hyperbolic hardware with cyclic qudits: architecture, truncations, and qubit embeddings}
7820: \label{sec:hyperbolic_cyclic_hardware}
7821: 
7822: The attached hyperbolic-hardware document \cite{Kollar2018HyperbolicHardware} demonstrates that one can realize \emph{hyperbolic lattice}
7823: connectivity in circuit QED using networks of coplanar waveguide resonators. Conceptually, this is an
7824: excellent match to the present program: a hyperbolic (negative-curvature) connectivity graph supports
7825: rapid growth of neighborhoods, which complements hierarchical renormalization and attention.
7826: 
7827: \subsection{From hyperbolic lattices of resonators to hyperbolic graphs of cyclic qudits}
7828: In circuit QED, each resonator mode is naturally a high-dimensional (in fact infinite-dimensional)
7829: quantum system. This makes it a plausible physical substrate for the ``infinite-period'' model,
7830: provided we choose a working subspace and implement the required gates with acceptable leakage.
7831: A pragmatic hierarchy is:
7832: \begin{enumerate}
7833: \item \textbf{Infinite-period conceptual model:} $\mathcal{H}_\infty\cong \ell^2(\mathbb{Z})$ or $L^2(S^1)$.
7834: \item \textbf{Truncated implementation:} restrict to a $(2N+1)$-dimensional subspace (energy cutoff).
7835: \item \textbf{Periodized implementation:} engineer an effective $\mathbb{Z}_d$ degree of freedom
7836: (e.g.\ via a stabilizer/quotient construction), giving a finite cyclic qudit.
7837: \end{enumerate}
7838: In all cases, the connectivity graph of the device is the relevant ``spacetime'' for our graph
7839: convolution/attention layers; a hyperbolic tiling supplies high connectivity with bounded degree.
7840: 
7841: \subsection{Local gate primitives for cyclic qudits on an edge}
7842: For two neighboring cyclic qudits (sites $u$ and $v$), the following gates are sufficient to build
7843: both quanvolution and attention primitives:
7844: \begin{enumerate}
7845: \item \textbf{One-site Weyl gates} generated by $X_d$ and $Z_d$ (or their truncated analogs),
7846: together with $F_d$.
7847: \item \textbf{Two-site Weyl entanglers} such as $\mathrm{CZ}_d$ and $\mathrm{SUM}_d$.
7848: \end{enumerate}
7849: An explicit ``message passing'' two-site gate sequence for graph convolution is
7850: \begin{equation}
7851:   U_{\mathrm{msg}}^{(u\to v)}
7852:   \;=\;
7853:   \mathrm{CZ}_d^{(u,v)}\,
7854:   \bigl(F_d^{(u)}\otimes I^{(v)}\bigr)\,
7855:   \mathrm{SUM}_d^{(u,v)}\,
7856:   \bigl(F_d^{(u)\dagger}\otimes I^{(v)}\bigr),
7857:   \label{eq:message_passing_gate}
7858: \end{equation}
7859: which mixes Fourier components of~$u$ into~$v$ in a way that is canonical for cyclic degrees of freedom.
7860: For attention, an overlap-estimation primitive can be implemented by an ancilla-controlled generalized
7861: SWAP on two registers (or, in a near-term setting, by repeated randomized measurements).
7862: 
7863: \subsection{Scaling thought-experiment: $\sim 10^5$ cyclic qudits with hyperbolic connectivity}
7864: A device with $\sim 10^5$ sites is far beyond current demonstrations, but it is a useful target for
7865: algorithmic design because hyperbolic graphs have \emph{logarithmic} diameter in the number of sites
7866: for fixed degree. Concretely, for a regular degree-$q$ hyperbolic tiling one expects
7867: \[
7868:   \mathrm{radius}(N) \;\sim\; \log_{q-1}(N),
7869: \]
7870: so that $O(\log N)$ layers of local message passing (convolution or attention) suffice to propagate
7871: information across the device.
7872: This makes hierarchical renormalization and attention particularly attractive: depth stays modest even
7873: as $N$ becomes large, while the number of parallel local operations scales with the number of edges.
7874: 
7875: \subsection{Explicit circuit sketches for a \texorpdfstring{$10^5$}{1e5}-site hyperbolic cyclic-qudit processor}
7876: \label{subsec:explicit_hyperbolic_circuits}
7877: 
7878: To make the ``$\sim 10^5$ cyclic qudits'' thought-experiment concrete, fix (for definiteness) a degree-$3$
7879: hyperbolic tiling (e.g.\ a $\{7,3\}$-type local geometry). With $N\approx 10^5$ vertices, one expects
7880: $\mathrm{radius}(N)\approx \log_2(10^5)\approx 17$, so $\approx 20$ layers of strictly local operations already
7881: reach macroscopic scales.
7882: 
7883: \paragraph{Circuit A: one quanvolution+pooling (RG) step.}
7884: A single RG step can be realized by the following constant-depth schedule:
7885: \begin{enumerate}
7886: \item \textbf{Local star-entangling.} For every vertex~$v$, apply a star circuit on $\{v\}\cup N(v)$ of the form
7887: \eqref{eq:star_patch_unitary}. Since stars overlap, schedule this in a few rounds (e.g.\ by edge-coloring the
7888: graph and applying edge-local entanglers in parallel per color).
7889: \item \textbf{Readout.} Measure a fixed set of one-site observables on each~$v$ (e.g.\ $\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle$ for
7890: small~$m$), producing classical features $\phi(v)$.
7891: \item \textbf{Pooling by matching contraction.} Choose a maximal matching $M\subseteq E$ and, for each $(u,v)\in M$,
7892: apply a two-site pooling channel $W$ (unitary + trace-out) to produce an effective vertex. This reduces the number
7893: of vertices by a constant factor each RG step and yields a smaller ribbon graph.
7894: \end{enumerate}
7895: Gate counts are $O(|E|)$ two-site entanglers per layer (with high parallelism). For a bounded-degree graph,
7896: $|E|=\Theta(N)$, so the \emph{per-layer} cost scales linearly in~$N$ but the \emph{depth} remains constant.
7897: 
7898: \paragraph{Circuit B: edge-scored graph attention with single-site measurements.}
7899: Using the Gaussian-projected score above, an attention layer can be implemented as:
7900: \begin{enumerate}
7901: \item \textbf{Local Q/K/V preparation.} On each vertex~$v$, apply $U_Q,U_K,U_V$ to prepare local registers
7902: $q_v,k_v,v_v$ (these may be logical subspaces of the same physical oscillator/qudit).
7903: \item \textbf{Single-site scoring.} Measure the chosen Fourier-mode observables on $q_v$ and $k_u$.
7904: Compute $\alpha_{v,u}$ classically for edges $(v,u)$, normalize over $u\in N_r(v)$.
7905: \item \textbf{Weighted aggregation.} Either (i) keep the aggregation classical (update classical features and
7906: re-encode), or (ii) implement a quantum mixture by controlled routing of value registers using $\alpha_{v,u}$ as
7907: classical control parameters (near-term: option~(i) is dramatically simpler).
7908: \end{enumerate}
7909: The notable feature is that \emph{no two-site overlap tests are required}: the only quantum operations are local
7910: circuits, and the only measurements are single-site. The attention weights live in classical memory, which is
7911: often an acceptable hybrid compromise on near-term hardware.
7912: 
7913: 
7914: 
7915: \paragraph{Circuit C: QGCN-style edge convolution + measurement pooling on a hyperbolic ribbon graph.}
7916: To more directly mirror the QGCN blocks of Zheng--Gao--L\"u \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} in the present cyclic-qudit setting,
7917: one can implement each layer as:
7918: \begin{enumerate}
7919: \item \textbf{Edge-convolution (parameter sharing).} Fix a two-qudit unitary ansatz $U^{(2)}_\theta\in \mathrm{SU}(d^2)$ built
7920: from one-qudit Weyl rotations and entanglers (e.g.\ $\mathrm{SUM}_d$ and $\mathrm{CZ}_d$).
7921: Apply $U^{(2)}_\theta$ on every physical edge of the hyperbolic graph using an edge-coloring schedule.
7922: For bounded degree $\Delta$, at most $\Delta+1$ rounds suffice for a proper edge coloring, so depth per layer stays constant.
7923: \item \textbf{Local readout features.} Measure a small set of Fourier-mode observables per site, e.g.\ $\mathrm{Re}\langle (Z_d)^m\rangle$
7924: for a few $m$.  These play the role of hidden features.
7925: \item \textbf{Pooling by measured contraction.} Choose a maximal matching $M$ and for each matched edge $(u,v)$:
7926: measure $v$ in the computational basis, apply a simple correction (typically an $X_d$-shift) to $u$ conditioned on the outcome,
7927: and discard $v$.
7928: This realizes the pooling channel \eqref{eq:qgcn_pool_channel} and contracts $(u,v)$ in the ribbon graph.
7929: \end{enumerate}
7930: Iterating Circuit~C for $\approx \log N$ steps (with $N\approx 10^5$) reduces the device to a small effective graph while preserving
7931: ribbon structure, enabling expensive global diagnostics (holonomy, persistence) at the coarse scale.
7932: 
7933: \paragraph{Circuit D: QGAT-style multi-head quantum attention logits on cyclic qudits.}
7934: To mirror the QGAT mechanism \cite{Ning2025QGAT} more faithfully, fix an ``edge register'' of $n_q$ qudits per active edge evaluation.
7935: For each edge $(i,j)$ in a chosen subset $E_{\mathrm{att}}\subseteq E$ (possibly stochastic minibatching), perform:
7936: \begin{enumerate}
7937: \item \textbf{Edge-feature preparation.} Assemble a classical feature vector $a'_{ij}$ that includes (i) node features at $i$ and $j$,
7938: (ii) projected multi-head features, and (iii) the ribbon cyclic-position feature $p(i\to j)$ (Section~\ref{subsec:qgat_ribbon_adaptation}).
7939: Encode $a'_{ij}$ into the edge register either by amplitude encoding (deep but compact) or by phase/Fourier encoding (shallow but less expressive).
7940: \item \textbf{Strongly entangling block.} Apply a depth-$L$ variational circuit $U(\theta)$ on the edge register, using one-qudit Weyl rotations and
7941: two-qudit entanglers in a fixed pattern (e.g.\ range-$r$ couplings within the register).
7942: \item \textbf{Multi-head readout.} Measure $(Z_d)_k$ (or a low mode $(Z_d)_k^m$) on each qudit $k=1,\dots,n_q$, yielding $n_q$ logits
7943: $e^{(k)}_{ij}$ in one circuit execution.
7944: \item \textbf{Classical softmax + aggregation.} Compute $\alpha^{(k)}_{ij}$ by softmax over $j\in N_r(i)$, update node features by attention aggregation,
7945: and (optionally) pool by contracting high-attention edges.
7946: \end{enumerate}
7947: On hyperbolic connectivity graphs, the bounded degree keeps softmax neighborhoods small, while the logarithmic diameter makes a modest number of
7948: layers sufficient for long-range coupling.
7949: 
7950: 
7951: \paragraph{Failure modes at scale.}
7952: At $N\sim 10^5$, the limiting factors are not conceptual but engineering:
7953: (i) calibration drift across many nominally identical local circuits, (ii) crosstalk and frequency crowding for
7954: dense resonator networks, (iii) readout bandwidth, and (iv) leakage for truncated $d=\infty$ realizations.
7955: These considerations strongly favor designs where each layer is shallow, measurements are local, and pooling
7956: reduces active degrees of freedom rapidly.
7957: 
7958: \subsection{From qudits to qubits: a practical embedding route}
7959: 
7960: \paragraph{Why this matters for the QGCN/QGAT constructions.}
7961: Both QGCN \cite{Zheng2021QGCN} and QGAT \cite{Ning2025QGAT} are formulated in the qubit language, largely because current
7962: gate-model devices are qubit-native and because amplitude encoding is naturally phrased in a $2^n$-dimensional Hilbert space.
7963: In the present manuscript the \emph{conceptual} degrees of freedom are cyclic qudits (finite $d$ or $d=\infty$).
7964: The embedding below is therefore the concrete route that turns the qudit-level renormalization/attention layers in this chapter
7965: into implementable circuits on near-term qubit processors: first choose a truncation/quotient (if $d=\infty$), then encode
7966: $\mathcal{H}_d$ into qubits and compile the generalized Weyl gates and entanglers into modular arithmetic and controlled-phase
7967: subroutines.
7968: 
7969: Near-term devices are predominantly qubit-based. A standard embedding of a $d$-level cyclic qudit into
7970: qubits is the binary encoding
7971: \[
7972:   \mathcal{H}_d \hookrightarrow (\mathbb{C}^2)^{\otimes n},\qquad n=\lceil \log_2 d\rceil,
7973: \]
7974: by mapping $\ket{k}\mapsto \ket{\mathrm{bin}(k)}$ on the first $d$ computational basis states.
7975: Under this embedding, $\mathrm{SUM}_d$ becomes modular addition on $n$ qubits, implementable with
7976: reversible adders; $Z_d$ becomes a diagonal phase with $d$-th roots of unity, implementable by
7977: controlled-phase rotations; and $F_d$ can be approximated by the quantum Fourier transform on~$n$ qubits
7978: with appropriate truncation.
7979: For the infinite-period conceptual model, one first chooses a truncation/quotient (as in
7980: Section~\ref{sec:cyclic_qudits_channels}) and then applies the same embedding.
7981: 
7982: \paragraph{Practical caution.}
7983: The main failure modes when reducing qudits to qubits are (i) gate-count overhead growing with~$\log d$,
7984: (ii) leakage/aliasing errors introduced by truncation or quotienting, and (iii) noise amplification
7985: under deep modular arithmetic. These can often be mitigated by keeping $d$ modest (e.g.\ $d=3,4,8$),
7986: by using bosonic encodings when available, and by designing renormalization/attention layers that are
7987: shallow and strongly local on the underlying ribbon/hyperbolic graph.
7988: 
7989: 
7990: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
7991: 
7992: 
7993: \section{Renormalization as a learned quantum graph neural network: QGCNN and QGAT templates}
7994: \label{sec:renorm_qgnn_templates}
7995: 
7996: The earlier sections developed ``graph quanvolution'' and ``graph quantum attention'' intrinsically on a ribbon graph.
7997: Here we make the correspondence to standard graph neural network (GNN) language explicit, and we record the density-operator formulation that is needed when the renormalization step discards degrees of freedom.
7998: 
7999: \subsection{Classical message passing versus quantum coarse-graining}
8000: A (linear) graph convolution layer on node features $h_v\in\mathbb{R}^p$ takes the schematic form
8001: \[
8002: h_v^{(\ell+1)}=\sigma\!\Bigl(\sum_{u\in N(v)\cup\{v\}} A_{vu}\,W^{(\ell)} h_u^{(\ell)}\Bigr),
8003: \]
8004: where $\sigma$ is a nonlinearity and $A$ encodes local adjacency/normalization.
8005: 
8006: In the quantum setting the ``feature'' attached to a vertex (or patch) is not a vector but a density operator.
8007: A renormalization layer is therefore not a matrix multiplication but a CPTP map that consumes a \emph{patch state} and outputs a \emph{coarse state}:
8008: \[
8009: \rho_{S_v}^{(\ell)} \ \longmapsto\ \rho_{v}^{(\ell+1)}
8010: :=\mathcal{R}_{\theta,v}^{(\ell)}\big(\rho_{S_v}^{(\ell)}\big).
8011: \]
8012: Nonlinearity enters exactly as in measurement-based quantum computing:
8013: either by (i) measuring some degrees of freedom and classically conditioning later unitaries, or (ii) mapping $\rho_{S_v}$ to a classical feature vector via $\Tr(\rho_{S_v}O_j)$ and applying a classical nonlinearity in that feature space.
8014: 
8015: \subsection{Quantum graph convolutional networks (QGCNN) as local isometries + discarding}
8016: A particularly transparent QGCNN layer is a Stinespring-type construction:
8017: introduce an ancilla register $a_v$ in a fixed pure state $\ket{0}\bra{0}$, apply a parametric unitary $U_{\theta,v}$ on the patch plus ancilla, and then discard (trace out) the degrees of freedom that are not kept at the next scale.
8018: Concretely, choose a tensor factorization
8019: \[
8020: \mathcal{H}_{S_v}\otimes \mathcal{H}_{a_v}\ \cong\ \mathcal{H}_{v,\mathrm{keep}}\otimes \mathcal{H}_{v,\mathrm{trash}},
8021: \]
8022: and define
8023: \begin{equation}
8024: \label{eq:qgcnn_layer}
8025: \mathcal{R}_{\theta,v}^{(\ell)}(\rho_{S_v})
8026: :=
8027: \Tr_{\mathrm{trash}}\!\Bigl(
8028: U_{\theta,v}\bigl(\rho_{S_v}\otimes \ket{0}\bra{0}\bigr)U_{\theta,v}^\dagger
8029: \Bigr).
8030: \end{equation}
8031: This is a CPTP map by construction, and it is the direct quantum analogue of ``convolution + pooling'':
8032: $U_{\theta,v}$ mixes information within the patch (message passing), and the partial trace pools/discards degrees of freedom.
8033: 
8034: \begin{remark}[Equivariance to ribbon-graph symmetries]
8035: If one wants the layer to respect the cyclic order at $v$, one constrains $U_{\theta,v}$ to be equivariant under cyclic permutations of incident half-edges (or uses weight-sharing among parameter blocks labeled by cyclic positions).
8036: This is the natural fat-graph analogue of translational equivariance on grids.
8037: \end{remark}
8038: 
8039: \subsection{Quantum graph attention networks (QGAT) as state-dependent routing}
8040: In attention, edges are weighted by coefficients $\alpha_{v\leftarrow u}$ computed from ``queries'' and ``keys''.
8041: A density-operator version separates (a) the \emph{attention score extraction} (quantum-to-classical) from (b) the \emph{state update} (classical-to-quantum control).
8042: 
8043: A generic template is:
8044: \begin{enumerate}
8045: \item Choose observables $\{O_{v,u}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^M$ on the two-vertex reduced state $\rho_{vu}$ and define classical edge features
8046: \[
8047: x_{v,u}^{(m)}:=\Tr(\rho_{vu}\,O_{v,u}^{(m)}).
8048: \]
8049: \item Compute attention weights by a learnable classical map (e.g.\ a softmax)
8050: \[
8051: \alpha_{v\leftarrow u}:=\frac{\exp(f_\theta(x_{v,u}))}{\sum_{w\in N(v)}\exp(f_\theta(x_{v,w}))}.
8052: \]
8053: \item Apply an update channel whose Kraus operators depend on the weights, for example a convex mixture
8054: \[
8055: \mathcal{R}_{\theta,v}(\rho_{S_v})
8056: =\sum_{u\in N(v)} \alpha_{v\leftarrow u}\,\mathcal{E}_{\theta,(v\leftarrow u)}(\rho_{S_v}),
8057: \]
8058: or a controlled-unitary construction conditioned on a classical register storing the index $u$.
8059: \end{enumerate}
8060: 
8061: The key point is that the weights $\alpha_{v\leftarrow u}$ depend on \emph{reduced density operators} (hence on entanglement), so attention becomes a learnable, state-dependent routing rule for coarse-graining.
8062: This is precisely the renormalization intuition: ``which neighbor matters'' is itself an emergent, scale-dependent question.
8063: 
8064: \subsection{Guaranteeing physicality: a compact Stinespring lemma}
8065: Because the paper moves freely between ``learned maps'' and ``physical channels'', it is useful to recall the exact equivalence.
8066: 
8067: \begin{proposition}[Stinespring dilation in finite dimensions]
8068: \label{prop:stinespring_finite}
8069: Let $\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}')$ be a linear map between finite-dimensional operator algebras.
8070: Then $\mathcal{E}$ is CPTP iff there exist an ancilla Hilbert space $\mathcal{K}$, a state $\ket{0}\in\mathcal{K}$, and a unitary $U$ on $\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}$ such that
8071: \[
8072: \mathcal{E}(\rho)=\Tr_{\mathcal{K}}\!\Bigl(U(\rho\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0})U^\dagger\Bigr)
8073: \]
8074: for all density operators $\rho$.
8075: \end{proposition}
8076: 
8077: \begin{proof}
8078: ($\Leftarrow$) The right-hand side is a composition of (i) tensoring with a state, (ii) unitary conjugation, and (iii) partial trace; each is CPTP, hence so is their composition.
8079: 
8080: ($\Rightarrow$) If $\mathcal{E}$ is CPTP, choose Kraus operators $\{K_a\}_{a=1}^r$ as in \eqref{eq:kraus_form}.
8081: Let $\mathcal{K}\cong\mathbb{C}^r$ with basis $\{\ket{a}\}$ and define an isometry $V:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}'\otimes\mathcal{K}$ by
8082: $V\ket{\psi}=\sum_a (K_a\ket{\psi})\otimes\ket{a}$.
8083: Then $\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\Tr_{\mathcal{K}}(V\rho V^\dagger)$ and $V^\dagger V=\sum_a K_a^\dagger K_a=I$.
8084: Extend $V$ to a unitary $U$ on $\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}$ by completing an orthonormal basis.
8085: \end{proof}
8086: 
8087: \subsection{Validation checks specific to learned renormalization}
8088: Because the coarse-graining map is trainable, there are additional failure modes beyond the purely mathematical ones:
8089: \begin{itemize}
8090: \item \textbf{Nonphysical training parameterization:} an unconstrained parameterization may leave the CPTP manifold, producing $\rho'$ with negative eigenvalues or $\Tr(\rho')\neq 1$.
8091: Working with Kraus/Stinespring parameterizations or with Choi matrices with explicit semidefinite constraints avoids this.
8092: \item \textbf{Postselection bias:} if training uses postselected measurement outcomes, the effective map is not trace-preserving; diagnostics must report success probabilities and normalize appropriately.
8093: \item \textbf{Overfitting to mesh artifacts:} a learned layer may exploit discretization details of a particular embedded graph.
8094: Persistence-based stability tests (varying refinement, perturbing weights, changing cyclic order conventions) are designed to detect this.
8095: \end{itemize}
8096:
\chapter{Quantum holographic associative memories on ribbon graphs} · lines 8097–8743
8097: \chapter{Quantum holographic associative memories on ribbon graphs}
8098: \label{ch:qham}
8099: 
8100: The previous chapter developed graph convolution, graph attention, and pooling on ribbon graphs
8101: equipped with cyclic qudits.  Here we expose a deeper conceptual layer: these constructions
8102: naturally realize \emph{quantum holographic associative memories} (QHAMs).
8103: The connection is not metaphorical; it rests on precise mathematical parallels between
8104: (i) learned quanvolutional/attention layers and the retrieval dynamics of associative memories,
8105: (ii) the cyclic-qudit Fourier structure and the holographic encoding of distributed
8106: representations, and (iii) the renormalization/pooling hierarchy and the holographic principle
8107: relating boundary data to bulk geometry.
8108: 
8109: \medskip
8110: \noindent\textbf{Plan of this chapter.}
8111: Section~\ref{sec:classical_assoc_mem} reviews classical Hopfield networks and their modern
8112: continuous/attention-based generalizations.
8113: Section~\ref{sec:quanv_pattern_matching} explains how quanvolution on a ribbon graph implements
8114: quantum pattern matching.
8115: Section~\ref{sec:qgat_content_addressable} shows that graph quantum attention is a
8116: content-addressable memory with data-dependent retrieval.
8117: Section~\ref{sec:hierarchical_holographic} constructs the hierarchical holographic memory
8118: that arises from repeated pooling/RG on ribbon graphs, and connects to the
8119: emergent-gravity/holography themes of the earlier chapters.
8120: Section~\ref{sec:cyclic_holographic_encoding} explains the specific role played by the
8121: cyclic-qudit Fourier structure.
8122: Section~\ref{sec:qham_examples} provides explicit worked examples on hyperbolic hardware
8123: with $\sim 10^5$ cyclic qudits.
8124: 
8125: \section{Classical background: from Hopfield networks to attention-as-memory}
8126: \label{sec:classical_assoc_mem}
8127: 
8128: \subsection{Hopfield networks as energy-based associative memories}
8129: 
8130: A \emph{Hopfield network} stores a collection of binary patterns
8131: $\{\xi^{(\mu)}\in\{-1,+1\}^N\}_{\mu=1}^P$ in a symmetric weight matrix
8132: \begin{equation}
8133:   W_{ij}
8134:   \;=\;
8135:   \frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\xi_i^{(\mu)}\xi_j^{(\mu)},
8136:   \label{eq:hopfield_weights}
8137: \end{equation}
8138: and retrieves a stored pattern from a (possibly corrupted) probe $\sigma\in\{-1,+1\}^N$ by
8139: iterating the update rule $\sigma_i\mapsto \mathrm{sign}\bigl(\sum_j W_{ij}\sigma_j\bigr)$
8140: until convergence.  The dynamics minimize the energy
8141: $E(\sigma)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}W_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j$,
8142: and the stored patterns are (approximate) local minima.
8143: 
8144: Two features are essential for our purposes:
8145: \begin{enumerate}
8146: \item \textbf{Multilinear storage.} The weight matrix \eqref{eq:hopfield_weights}
8147: is a sum of outer products---bilinear in the stored patterns.
8148: \item \textbf{Nonlinear retrieval.} The $\mathrm{sign}(\cdot)$ activation is a hard nonlinearity
8149: that selects the basin of attraction containing the probe.
8150: \end{enumerate}
8151: This ``multilinear storage + nonlinear retrieval'' structure is precisely the pattern that recurs
8152: in the quantum graph convolution/attention layers of the previous chapter.
8153: 
8154: \subsection{Modern Hopfield networks, attention, and the exponential capacity theorem}
8155: 
8156: Ramsauer--Sch\"afl--Lehner--Seidl--Widrich--Adler--Gruber--Holzleitner--Pavlovi\'c--Sandve--Unterthiner--Brandstetter--Hochreiter~\cite{Ramsauer2020HopfieldAllYouNeed}
8157: showed that the softmax self-attention mechanism
8158: \begin{equation}
8159:   h_i^{\mathrm{out}}
8160:   \;=\;
8161:   \sum_{j}\frac{\exp\!\bigl(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d_k}\bigr)}
8162:                {\sum_{j'}\exp\!\bigl(\langle q_i,k_{j'}\rangle/\sqrt{d_k}\bigr)}
8163:   \;v_j,
8164:   \label{eq:modern_hopfield_attention}
8165: \end{equation}
8166: is equivalent to one step of a \emph{modern (continuous) Hopfield network} with an energy function of the form
8167: \[
8168:   E(\sigma)
8169:   \;=\;
8170:   -\mathrm{lse}\!\bigl(\langle \sigma, K\rangle\bigr)
8171:   \;+\;
8172:   \tfrac{1}{2}\|\sigma\|^2
8173:   \;+\;\text{const},
8174: \]
8175: where $\mathrm{lse}$ is the log-sum-exp function.
8176: This yields \emph{exponential} storage capacity ($P\sim e^{d_k/2}$ patterns), far beyond the
8177: classical $P\sim 0.14\,N$ regime.
8178: 
8179: \begin{remark}[Why this matters for us]
8180: \label{rem:attention_hopfield_why}
8181: The graph attention mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:graph_qattn}---and its quantum version
8182: (Section~\ref{sec:qgat_renorm})---inherits this associative-memory interpretation.
8183: When we restrict attention to graph neighborhoods $N_r(v)$ on a ribbon graph, we obtain a
8184: \emph{local associative memory at each vertex}, whose stored patterns are the key vectors of
8185: neighbors and whose retrieval is governed by the quantum overlap-estimation or
8186: Gaussian-projected scoring of Sections~\ref{subsec:qgat_qudit_generalization}--\ref{subsec:qgat_pooling_rg}.
8187: \end{remark}
8188: 
8189: \subsection{Holographic reduced representations and circular convolution}
8190: \label{subsec:hrr}
8191: 
8192: A related classical construction is \emph{holographic reduced representations} (HRR,
8193: Plate~\cite{Plate1995HolographicReducedRepresentations}).
8194: In HRR, objects are represented by $d$-dimensional vectors and associations are formed by
8195: \emph{circular convolution}:
8196: \begin{equation}
8197:   (\mathbf{a}\circledast \mathbf{b})_k
8198:   \;:=\;
8199:   \sum_{j=0}^{d-1} a_j\,b_{k-j\;\mathrm{mod}\;d},
8200:   \label{eq:circular_convolution}
8201: \end{equation}
8202: which in the Fourier domain becomes componentwise multiplication:
8203: $\widehat{\mathbf{a}\circledast\mathbf{b}} = \hat{\mathbf{a}}\odot\hat{\mathbf{b}}$.
8204: Retrieval of~$\mathbf{b}$ from the bound pair $\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{a}\circledast\mathbf{b}$ is
8205: accomplished by circular correlation (inverse convolution) with~$\mathbf{a}$.
8206: 
8207: The ``holographic'' property is that the bound representation~$\mathbf{c}$ has the same
8208: dimensionality as the constituents, and information about both~$\mathbf{a}$ and~$\mathbf{b}$
8209: is distributed across all components of~$\mathbf{c}$---just as in a physical hologram.
8210: 
8211: \begin{remark}[Cyclic qudits and circular convolution]
8212: \label{rem:qudit_hrr}
8213: A cyclic qudit of period~$d$ is the natural quantum substrate for circular convolution:
8214: the shift operator $X_d$ implements cyclic permutation, and the clock $Z_d$ diagonalizes to the
