8215: Fourier domain where circular convolution becomes pointwise multiplication.
8216: In particular, the quanvolutional feature $\phi_a(v)=\mathrm{Tr}(O_a\,U_\theta\,\rho_{S_v}\,U_\theta^\dagger)$ of
8217: \eqref{eq:quanv_feature} can be interpreted as the projection of a
8218: quantum-holographic bound representation (the post-entanglement state) onto a readout basis.
8219: This observation is the starting point for the quantum holographic associative memory
8220: developed in Section~\ref{sec:quanv_pattern_matching}.
8221: \end{remark}
8222: 
8223: 
8224: \section{Quanvolution as quantum pattern matching: the storage/retrieval interpretation}
8225: \label{sec:quanv_pattern_matching}
8226: 
8227: \subsection{The storage phase: parameterized unitaries encode patterns}
8228: 
8229: Consider a quanvolutional layer on a ribbon graph $\Gamma$ with cyclic qudits of period~$d$
8230: (Section~\ref{sec:graph_quanv}).
8231: The trainable parameters~$\theta$ of the patch unitary $U_\theta$ are learned by minimizing a
8232: task-specific loss.  After training, $U_\theta$ \emph{implicitly stores} a collection of input
8233: patterns to which it is maximally sensitive.
8234: 
8235: To make this precise, fix a patch $S_v$ and an encoding $x\mapsto\rho_{S_v}(x)$.
8236: The quanvolutional feature map is
8237: \[
8238:   \Phi_\theta(x)
8239:   \;=\;
8240:   \bigl(\mathrm{Tr}(O_1\,U_\theta\,\rho_{S_v}(x)\,U_\theta^\dagger),\;\ldots,\;
8241:          \mathrm{Tr}(O_p\,U_\theta\,\rho_{S_v}(x)\,U_\theta^\dagger)\bigr)
8242:   \;\in\;\mathbb{R}^p.
8243: \]
8244: This is a \emph{multilinear} function of the encoded state $\rho_{S_v}(x)$ (since the trace is
8245: bilinear in $\rho$ and $U_\theta O_a U_\theta^\dagger$), and becomes nonlinear in~$x$ once the
8246: encoding is nonlinear or post-processing is applied.
8247: 
8248: \begin{definition}[Quantum pattern templates]
8249: \label{def:pattern_templates}
8250: The \emph{pattern templates} stored by a trained quanvolutional layer with parameters~$\theta$
8251: are the states
8252: \[
8253:   \ket{\xi_a^\theta}
8254:   \;:=\;
8255:   U_\theta^\dagger\, O_a^{1/2}\, \ket{0},
8256:   \qquad a=1,\ldots,p,
8257: \]
8258: where $O_a^{1/2}$ denotes a (generalized) square root of the readout observable~$O_a$.
8259: The feature $\phi_a(v)$ then has the interpretation
8260: \[
8261:   \phi_a(v)
8262:   \;\approx\;
8263:   \bigl|\langle\xi_a^\theta\mid\psi(x_v)\rangle\bigr|^2
8264:   \;+\;\text{cross terms},
8265: \]
8266: where the cross terms involve off-diagonal elements of~$\rho$ and vanish for pure product
8267: encodings with $O_a$ rank-one.
8268: \end{definition}
8269: 
8270: In other words, each readout observable~$O_a$, conjugated by the trained unitary, defines a
8271: ``stored pattern'' $\ket{\xi_a^\theta}$, and the feature $\phi_a(v)$ measures the overlap of the
8272: encoded input with that pattern.  This is precisely the quantum analog of a Hopfield-type
8273: associative memory: \emph{the unitary stores the patterns, and measurement retrieves the best
8274: match}.
8275: 
8276: \subsection{Retrieval dynamics: measurement as winner-take-all}
8277: 
8278: In a classical Hopfield network, iterative sign activations implement winner-take-all dynamics:
8279: the probe converges to the nearest stored pattern.
8280: In the quantum quanvolutional setting, the analog is as follows.
8281: 
8282: \begin{proposition}[Quantum associative retrieval on a ribbon-graph patch]
8283: \label{prop:quantum_assoc_retrieval}
8284: Let $U_\theta$ be a trained patch unitary on the star $S_v=\{v\}\cup N(v)$ of a ribbon graph,
8285: and let $\{O_a=\ketbra{\xi_a^\theta}{\xi_a^\theta}\}_{a=1}^p$ be rank-one readout observables
8286: (projectors onto stored patterns in the $U_\theta$-rotated frame).
8287: Then for a product-state encoding $\rho_{S_v}(x)=\ketbra{\psi(x)}{\psi(x)}$:
8288: \begin{enumerate}
8289: \item The feature vector $\Phi_\theta(x)\in\mathbb{R}^p$ satisfies $\Phi_\theta(x)_a=|\braket{\xi_a^\theta}{\psi(x)}|^2\ge 0$
8290: and $\sum_a \Phi_\theta(x)_a\le 1$.
8291: \item Applying a classical $\mathrm{argmax}$ over~$a$ (hard retrieval) returns the index of the
8292: stored pattern most compatible with the input---this is winner-take-all.
8293: \item Applying a classical softmax over~$a$ (soft retrieval) returns a probability vector
8294: $\alpha_a(x)\propto \exp(\beta\,\Phi_\theta(x)_a)$ that implements modern Hopfield retrieval
8295: with inverse temperature~$\beta$.
8296: \end{enumerate}
8297: \end{proposition}
8298: 
8299: \begin{proof}
8300: (1) follows from $O_a$ being projectors and $\rho$ a pure state.
8301: (2) and (3) are classical post-processing of the overlap vector, using the correspondence
8302: between Hopfield energy minima and softmax attention documented in~\cite{Ramsauer2020HopfieldAllYouNeed}.
8303: \end{proof}
8304: 
8305: \subsection{Circular convolution structure from cyclic qudits}
8306: \label{subsec:circ_conv_qudits}
8307: 
8308: The holographic character of this memory becomes manifest when the readout observables are
8309: chosen to be \emph{Fourier modes of the clock operator}:
8310: \[
8311:   O_m \;:=\; (Z_d^{(v)})^m,\qquad m=0,1,\ldots,d-1.
8312: \]
8313: Then the feature vector at vertex~$v$ is
8314: \begin{equation}
8315:   \phi_m(v)
8316:   \;=\;
8317:   \mathrm{Tr}\bigl((Z_d^{(v)})^m\, U_\theta\,\rho_{S_v}(x)\,U_\theta^\dagger\bigr)
8318:   \;=\;
8319:   \langle (Z_d)^m\rangle_{\rho'_v},
8320:   \label{eq:fourier_features}
8321: \end{equation}
8322: where $\rho'_v$ is the reduced state at~$v$ after entangling with neighbors via $U_\theta$.
8323: 
8324: \begin{proposition}[Fourier features as holographic encoding]
8325: \label{prop:fourier_holographic}
8326: The $d$-component feature vector $(\phi_0(v),\phi_1(v),\ldots,\phi_{d-1}(v))$ is the
8327: \emph{discrete Fourier transform of the diagonal of $\rho'_v$ in the computational basis}:
8328: \[
8329:   \phi_m(v)
8330:   \;=\;
8331:   \sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\omega^{mk}\,\rho'_{v,kk},
8332:   \qquad\omega = e^{2\pi i/d},
8333: \]
8334: and is therefore a complete description of the populations (diagonal entries) of the post-interaction
8335: reduced state.
8336: 
8337: Moreover, the storage/retrieval process has an explicit circular convolution structure:
8338: if the input encoding is $Z_d^{\lfloor\kappa x_u\rfloor}\ket{0}$ for each neighbor~$u$, then the
8339: entangling gate $\mathrm{CZ}_d^{(v,u)}$ of \eqref{eq:star_patch_unitary} induces the map
8340: \[
8341:   \phi_m(v)
8342:   \;\sim\;
8343:   \sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_d}\hat\rho_{v,m-j}\;\cdot\;\hat\eta_j,
8344: \]
8345: where $\hat\rho_{v,\cdot}$ encodes the post-rotation state of the central qudit and $\hat\eta_j$
8346: encodes the Fourier transform of the combined neighbor-encoding contributions.
8347: This is precisely a \emph{circular convolution} in the Fourier domain of the cyclic qudit,
8348: i.e.\ the quantum analog of the holographic reduced representation \eqref{eq:circular_convolution}.
8349: \end{proposition}
8350: 
8351: \begin{proof}
8352: The first identity is immediate from $\langle k|Z_d^m|k\rangle = \omega^{mk}$
8353: and linearity of trace.
8354: For the second, expand the $\mathrm{CZ}_d$ action:
8355: $\mathrm{CZ}_d\ket{a,b}=\omega^{ab}\ket{a,b}$.
8356: After the star circuit \eqref{eq:star_patch_unitary}, the central qudit's state depends
8357: on the neighbors through phases $\omega^{a_v\cdot a_u}$ for each neighbor~$u$.
8358: Tracing over all neighbors and reading out $(Z_d)^m$ yields the stated convolution structure
8359: (a detailed computation parallels the Fourier analysis of controlled-phase gates in quantum
8360: signal processing; we omit the routine but lengthy calculation).
8361: \end{proof}
8362: 
8363: \begin{remark}[Infinite period: the continuous holographic limit]
8364: In the $d=\infty$ rotor model, $\phi_m(v)=\langle e^{im\theta}\rangle$ for $m\in\mathbb{Z}$,
8365: and the circular convolution becomes an ordinary convolution on $L^2(S^1)$.
8366: The Fourier modes then form a complete orthonormal system, and the stored holographic
8367: representation is a genuine infinite-dimensional encoding.
8368: Truncation to $d<\infty$ introduces finite angular resolution but preserves the holographic
8369: distribution property: each Fourier mode $\phi_m$ contains information about all stored patterns.
8370: \end{remark}
8371: 
8372: 
8373: \section{Graph quantum attention as content-addressable memory}
8374: \label{sec:qgat_content_addressable}
8375: 
8376: The attention mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:graph_qattn} admits an even more direct
8377: associative-memory interpretation than quanvolution.
8378: 
8379: \subsection{Query/key/value as address/content/data}
8380: 
8381: In a content-addressable memory (CAM), one provides an \emph{address} (query) and retrieves the
8382: \emph{data} (value) associated with the stored \emph{content} (key) that best matches the address.
8383: The graph quantum attention layer (Section~\ref{sec:qgat_renorm}) implements exactly this:
8384: 
8385: \begin{center}
8386: \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
8387: \begin{tabular}{l@{\qquad$\longleftrightarrow$\qquad}l}
8388: \toprule
8389: \textbf{CAM concept} & \textbf{QGAT on ribbon graph} \\
8390: \midrule
8391: Address (query) & $\ket{q_v}=U_Q(\theta_Q)\ket{\psi(h_v)}$ \\
8392: Content (key) & $\ket{k_u}=U_K(\theta_K)\ket{\psi(h_u)}$ for $u\in N_r(v)$\\
8393: Data (value) & $\ket{v_u}=U_V(\theta_V)\ket{\psi(h_u)}$ \\
8394: Match score & $\alpha_{vu}\propto\exp\!\bigl(-(\langle Z_d^m\rangle_{q_v}-\langle Z_d^m\rangle_{k_u})^2\bigr)$ \\
8395: Retrieved value & $h_v^{\mathrm{out}}=\sigma\!\bigl(\sum_u \alpha_{vu}\,\mathsf{V}_\theta(h_u)\bigr)$ \\
8396: \bottomrule
8397: \end{tabular}
8398: \end{center}
8399: 
8400: The ribbon-graph locality constraint means that each vertex~$v$ can only ``look up'' keys
8401: in its $r$-neighborhood $N_r(v)$.  This is a \emph{local content-addressable memory}, with the
8402: connectivity of the ribbon graph defining the memory's addressing topology.
8403: 
8404: \subsection{Multi-head attention as multi-pattern storage}
8405: 
8406: QGAT \cite{Ning2025QGAT} produces $n_q$ attention heads from a single circuit execution
8407: (Section~\ref{subsec:qgat_recap}): each qudit $k$ in the edge register yields one head's logit
8408: $e_{ij}^{(k)}$.  In the associative-memory interpretation, each head stores and retrieves
8409: \emph{different aspects} of the patterns at each vertex, analogous to multi-slot associative
8410: memory where different features are stored in parallel channels.
8411: 
8412: \begin{proposition}[Capacity of multi-head QGAT as local associative memory]
8413: \label{prop:qgat_capacity}
8414: Let~$v$ be a vertex of degree~$\delta_v$ in a ribbon graph equipped with $n_q$-qudit QGAT.
8415: Interpreting attention as modern Hopfield retrieval (Remark~\ref{rem:attention_hopfield_why}),
8416: the effective number of stored patterns per head is $\delta_v$ (the number of neighbors),
8417: and the retrieval is exponentially accurate in the hidden dimension
8418: $d_k=d^{n_q}$ of the edge-register Hilbert space.
8419: The total memory capacity at vertex~$v$ across all heads is therefore
8420: $\sim n_q\cdot\delta_v\cdot e^{d^{n_q}/2}$ (in the modern Hopfield sense of distinguishable
8421: patterns for a given accuracy).
8422: On a bounded-degree hyperbolic graph with $\delta_v\le\Delta$, this yields a per-vertex
8423: capacity exponential in the qudit Hilbert-space dimension, regardless of graph size.
8424: \end{proposition}
8425: 
8426: \begin{remark}[Why cyclic equivariance matters]
8427: When the attention mechanism respects the cyclic order at each vertex
8428: (Section~\ref{subsec:qgat_ribbon_adaptation}, option~B), the stored patterns inherit a
8429: \emph{cyclic symmetry}: rotating the half-edge labels at~$v$ permutes the stored patterns
8430: but does not change the set of retrievable associations.
8431: This is a rotational equivariance property of the local holographic memory, directly
8432: analogous to the rotational invariance of a physical hologram.
8433: \end{remark}
8434: 
8435: 
8436: \section{Hierarchical holographic memory via renormalization on ribbon graphs}
8437: \label{sec:hierarchical_holographic}
8438: 
8439: \subsection{Pooling as memory compression: from fine to coarse patterns}
8440: 
8441: The iterative pooling/RG process of Sections~\ref{subsec:qgcn_pooling_channel}
8442: and~\ref{subsec:qgat_pooling_rg} produces a hierarchy of ribbon graphs
8443: $\Gamma=\Gamma_0\to\Gamma_1\to\cdots\to\Gamma_L$
8444: with decreasing numbers of vertices.
8445: At each level~$\ell$, the quanvolutional or attention layer implements a local associative
8446: memory with its own set of stored patterns.
8447: The pooling step \emph{compresses} the stored information:
8448: \begin{itemize}
8449: \item \textbf{fine patterns} (many vertices, local features) are stored at level~$\ell$,
8450: \item \textbf{coarse patterns} (fewer vertices, aggregate features) are stored at level~$\ell+1$,
8451: \item the pooling channel $\mathcal{R}_\theta$ maps fine-level stored patterns to coarse-level
8452: stored patterns by tracing out/measuring selected degrees of freedom.
8453: \end{itemize}
8454: 
8455: This is a \emph{hierarchical holographic memory}:
8456: information about fine-scale patterns is not lost but is \emph{encoded} in the effective
8457: parameters and residual entanglement at the coarse scale.
8458: The encoding is ``holographic'' in the precise sense that:
8459: \begin{enumerate}
8460: \item each coarse-level feature $\phi_a(v')$ (where $v'$ is an effective vertex at level $\ell+1$)
8461: depends on a \emph{neighborhood} of fine-level vertices that were pooled together;
8462: \item the information is distributed: no single coarse feature fully determines the fine-level
8463: input, but the collection of coarse features (across all vertices and readout observables)
8464: allows reconstruction (up to the information lost by the pooling channel).
8465: \end{enumerate}
8466: 
8467: \subsection{Connection to AdS/CFT and the holographic principle}
8468: \label{subsec:ads_cft_holographic}
8469: 
8470: The hierarchical holographic memory has a natural interpretation in the language of AdS/CFT
8471: and the tensor-network models of holography (cf.\ the emergent gravity discussion in
8472: Section~\ref{ch:gravity}).
8473: 
8474: In tensor-network models of AdS/CFT
8475: (e.g.\ MERA~\cite{Vidal2007MERA}, HaPPY~\cite{Pastawski2015HAPPY}),
8476: the RG direction (coarse-graining) is identified with the \emph{radial (bulk)} direction in anti-de
8477: Sitter space, and the boundary CFT data are encoded in the fine-scale (UV) degrees of freedom.
8478: The key property of holographic codes is that bulk operators at depth~$\ell$ can be reconstructed
8479: from boundary data on any sufficiently large subregion---this is the \emph{entanglement wedge
8480: reconstruction} property.
8481: 
8482: On a hyperbolic ribbon graph with $\sim 10^5$ cyclic qudits
8483: (Section~\ref{subsec:explicit_hyperbolic_circuits}), the same structure emerges naturally:
8484: 
8485: \begin{center}
8486: \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
8487: \begin{tabular}{l@{\qquad$\longleftrightarrow$\qquad}l}
8488: \toprule
8489: \textbf{AdS/CFT concept} & \textbf{QHAM on hyperbolic ribbon graph} \\
8490: \midrule
8491: Boundary CFT data & Fine-scale features $\phi_a(v)$ at level~$0$ \\
8492: Radial (bulk) direction & RG/pooling direction $\ell=0,1,\ldots,L$ \\
8493: Bulk operators & Coarse-scale features $\phi_a(v')$ at level~$\ell>0$ \\
8494: Entanglement wedge & Support of the pooling channel at vertex $v'$ \\
8495: Ryu--Takayanagi surface & Minimal cut in the ribbon graph separating $v'$ from boundary\\
8496: Holographic code & The full RG hierarchy of quanvolution + pooling channels\\
8497: \bottomrule
8498: \end{tabular}
8499: \end{center}
8500: 
8501: \begin{proposition}[Holographic encoding property of the QHAM hierarchy]
8502: \label{prop:holographic_encoding}
8503: Let $\Gamma$ be a degree-$\Delta$ hyperbolic ribbon graph with $N$ vertices, and let
8504: $\Gamma_0\to\Gamma_1\to\cdots\to\Gamma_L$ be the hierarchy produced by $L=O(\log N)$
8505: pooling/RG steps, each contracting a maximal matching.
8506: Then:
8507: \begin{enumerate}
8508: \item $|\Gamma_\ell|=\Theta(N/2^\ell)$, so $|\Gamma_L|=O(1)$ after $L\sim\log_2 N$ steps.
8509: \item The effective stored patterns at level~$\ell$ depend on a ``causal cone'' of
8510: $\Theta(\Delta^\ell)$ fine-scale vertices---i.e., the memory is local in the bulk but
8511: nonlocal on the boundary, with the degree of nonlocality growing exponentially with depth.
8512: \item The total information stored across all levels is bounded by
8513: $\sum_\ell |\Gamma_\ell|\cdot p_\ell\cdot\log d$, where $p_\ell$ is the number of readout
8514: features at level~$\ell$.  For constant $p_\ell$ and $d$, this is $O(N\log d)$, matching the
8515: ``boundary area law'' scaling of holographic codes.
8516: \end{enumerate}
8517: \end{proposition}
8518: 
8519: \begin{proof}
8520: (1) follows from the matching contraction reducing vertex count by at least a factor of~$2$ per step.
8521: (2) follows from the $r$-neighborhood support of each convolution/attention layer: after $\ell$
8522: layers, the effective receptive field at a coarse vertex covers an $\ell$-hop ball whose size
8523: grows as $\Delta^\ell$ on a regular tree (and slower on a hyperbolic graph, but still exponentially).
8524: (3) is an information-theoretic counting argument: each qudit contributes $\log_2 d$ bits of
8525: information, and $p_\ell$ measurements at each of $|\Gamma_\ell|$ vertices yield the stated bound.
8526: \end{proof}
8527: 
8528: 
8529: \section{The cyclic-qudit Fourier structure as holographic encoding}
8530: \label{sec:cyclic_holographic_encoding}
8531: 
8532: The term ``holographic'' is used in two senses in this chapter:
8533: (i) the information-theoretic sense of HRR (Section~\ref{subsec:hrr}), where information is
8534: distributed across all components, and
8535: (ii) the gravitational/AdS-CFT sense of boundary-to-bulk encoding (Section~\ref{subsec:ads_cft_holographic}).
8536: The cyclic-qudit Fourier structure connects both.
8537: 
8538: \subsection{Fourier modes as distributed features}
8539: 
8540: Let $\ket{\psi_v}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}c_k\ket{k}$ be the state of a cyclic qudit at vertex~$v$
8541: after a quanvolutional layer.
8542: The readout features $\phi_m(v)=\langle(Z_d)^m\rangle=\sum_k |c_k|^2\omega^{mk}$ are the
8543: Fourier modes of the population distribution $\{|c_k|^2\}$.
8544: Each feature~$\phi_m$ depends on \emph{all} $d$ amplitudes, so the information about the
8545: input is distributed---no single Fourier mode determines the input, but the collection
8546: $(\phi_0,\ldots,\phi_{d-1})$ does (up to phase ambiguity).
8547: 
8548: This is the quantum analog of the HRR property: the ``hologram'' is the set of Fourier-mode
8549: readouts, and each mode contains a global imprint of the stored pattern.
8550: 
8551: \subsection{Angular resolution and the truncation/quotient hierarchy}
8552: 
8553: The infinite-period model ($d=\infty$) has an infinite tower of Fourier modes
8554: $\langle e^{im\theta}\rangle$, $m\in\mathbb{Z}$, providing unlimited angular resolution.
8555: Truncation to $d<\infty$ introduces a \emph{Nyquist-type} angular resolution limit:
8556: patterns differing by less than $2\pi/d$ in the angular coordinate at a vertex become
8557: indistinguishable.
8558: 
8559: For the hierarchical holographic memory, this resolution limit interacts with the RG hierarchy:
8560: \begin{itemize}
8561: \item at the fine scale (level~$0$), high angular resolution ($d$ large) captures detailed
8562: local structure;
8563: \item at the coarse scale (level~$L$), effective patterns are sums over many fine-scale inputs,
8564: and only low Fourier modes survive the averaging/pooling---a natural ``low-pass filter''
8565: in the angular direction.
8566: \end{itemize}
8567: This is exactly the behavior expected of a holographic encoding: the UV (fine) data carry high
8568: Fourier modes while the IR (coarse) data are dominated by low modes, paralleling the
8569: high-energy/low-energy split in the holographic renormalization group of AdS/CFT.
8570: 
8571: 
8572: \section{Explicit examples: quantum holographic associative memory on hyperbolic hardware}
8573: \label{sec:qham_examples}
8574: 
8575: \subsection{Example 1: pattern storage and retrieval on a $\{7,3\}$ hyperbolic tiling}
8576: \label{subsec:qham_example_73}
8577: 
8578: Fix a degree-3 hyperbolic tiling ($\{7,3\}$-type) with $N=100$ vertices (a small
8579: patch for illustration) and cyclic qudits of period $d=7$ at each vertex.
8580: 
8581: \paragraph{Storage phase.}
8582: Suppose we wish to store $P=3$ ``patterns'' $\xi^{(\mu)}\in\mathbb{Z}_7^{100}$, $\mu=1,2,3$.
8583: We train a quanvolutional layer (star-patch unitary~\eqref{eq:star_patch_unitary}) by minimizing
8584: a contrastive loss:
8585: \[
8586:   \mathcal{L}(\theta)
8587:   \;=\;
8588:   \sum_{\mu=1}^{3}\sum_v
8589:   \bigl\|
8590:     \Phi_\theta(\xi^{(\mu)})_v - \mathbf{e}_\mu
8591:   \bigr\|^2
8592:   \;+\;
8593:   \lambda\sum_{\mu\neq\nu}\bigl(
8594:     \langle\Phi_\theta(\xi^{(\mu)}),\Phi_\theta(\xi^{(\nu)})\rangle
8595:   \bigr)^2,
8596: \]
8597: where $\mathbf{e}_\mu$ is a one-hot target and the second term encourages orthogonality of stored
8598: representations.
8599: 
8600: \paragraph{Retrieval phase.}
8601: Given a corrupted input $\tilde\xi=\xi^{(1)}+\text{noise}$, evaluate $\Phi_\theta(\tilde\xi)$
8602: on each vertex, aggregate by global average pooling, and apply $\mathrm{argmax}$.
8603: Because the $\{7,3\}$ tiling has logarithmic diameter ($\sim\log_2(100)\approx 7$),
8604: a stack of $\sim 4$ quanvolutional layers (each with pooling) reduces the 100 vertices to
8605: $\sim 6$ effective vertices, after which a single classical readout determines the retrieved
8606: pattern.
8607: 
8608: \paragraph{Circuit cost.}
8609: Each quanvolutional layer requires $\Theta(|E|)=\Theta(N)$ two-qudit gates (with bounded degree,
8610: $|E|=\frac{3}{2}N$).  For $d=7$, each generalized $\mathrm{CZ}_7$ decomposes into
8611: $O(\log_2 7)\approx 3$ two-qubit gates under binary encoding
8612: (Section~\ref{sec:hyperbolic_cyclic_hardware}).
8613: Total circuit depth per layer: $\sim 3\times 3 = 9$ two-qubit gate layers (edge-coloring with
8614: 3 colors $\times$ 3-qubit decomposition depth).
8615: For 4 layers on 100 vertices: $\sim 36$ depth, well within near-term coherence budgets.
8616: 
8617: \subsection{Example 2: hierarchical holographic memory on $\sim 10^5$ cyclic qudits}
8618: \label{subsec:qham_example_1e5}
8619: 
8620: Now consider the full-scale thought experiment: $N=10^5$ vertices on a $\{7,3\}$ hyperbolic
8621: tiling with cyclic qudits of period $d=8$ ($=2^3$, so that binary encoding is clean).
8622: 
8623: \paragraph{RG hierarchy.}
8624: With $\log_2(10^5)\approx 17$, we need $\sim 17$ pooling steps to reach $O(1)$ effective vertices.
8625: In practice, one would stop earlier (say after $L=10$ steps, reaching $\sim 100$ effective vertices)
8626: and apply a few attention layers on the coarse graph before a final readout.
8627: 
8628: \paragraph{Memory organization.}
8629: \begin{itemize}
8630: \item \textbf{Level 0} (fine): $10^5$ vertices, each with a $d=8$ qudit.
8631: Local features: $\phi_m(v)$ for $m=0,\ldots,7$ (8 Fourier modes per vertex).
8632: Total features: $8\times 10^5 = 8\times 10^5$.
8633: \item \textbf{Level 5}: $\sim 3\times 10^3$ effective vertices.
8634: Coarse features capture patterns at the $\sim 2^5=32$-vertex scale.
8635: \item \textbf{Level 10}: $\sim 100$ effective vertices.
8636: Attention layers on this small graph implement a \emph{global associative memory}
8637: with per-vertex capacity exponential in $d^{n_q}=8^{n_q}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:qgat_capacity}).
8638: \item \textbf{Level 17}: $O(1)$ effective vertices.  A single classical readout determines
8639: the global pattern.
8640: \end{itemize}
8641: 
8642: \paragraph{Holographic property at scale.}
8643: By Proposition~\ref{prop:holographic_encoding}, the total information in the hierarchy is
8644: $O(N\log d)=O(10^5\times 3)\approx 3\times 10^5$ bits,
8645: matching boundary-area scaling.
8646: The effective ``bulk'' patterns at level $\ell=10$ encode nonlocal boundary correlations
8647: spanning $\sim\Delta^{10}=3^{10}\approx 6\times 10^4$ fine-scale vertices---more than half
8648: the device.
8649: 
8650: \paragraph{Circuit implementation.}
8651: Using Circuit~A (Section~\ref{subsec:explicit_hyperbolic_circuits}) for each RG step:
8652: \begin{itemize}
8653: \item star-entangling: $\sim 3N/2$ two-qudit gates per step, parallelized in 4 rounds (edge-coloring).
8654: \item each $\mathrm{CZ}_8$ gate decomposes into $3$ two-qubit gates (binary encoding of $d=8=2^3$).
8655: \item per-step depth: $4\times 3=12$ two-qubit gate layers.
8656: \item 10 RG steps: total depth $\sim 120$ two-qubit layers.
8657: \item readout at each step: single-site $Z$ measurements (negligible depth).
8658: \end{itemize}
8659: This is aggressive but not absurd for a device with $3\times 10^5$ physical qubits
8660: (binary encoding of $10^5$ qudits at $\lceil\log_2 8\rceil=3$ qubits each)
8661: and coherence times supporting $\sim 200$ gate layers.
8662: 
8663: \subsection{Example 3: attention-based pattern retrieval with cyclic equivariance}
8664: \label{subsec:qham_example_attention}
8665: 
8666: On a smaller hyperbolic patch ($N=49$ vertices, $\{7,3\}$ tiling, $d=7$), implement a
8667: QGAT-style attention layer with $n_q=4$ qudits in the edge register.
8668: 
8669: \paragraph{Storage.}
8670: Each vertex stores $n_q=4$ attention heads.  With $\delta_v=3$ neighbors, each head addresses
8671: 3 keys.  The Hilbert-space dimension of the edge register is $d^{n_q}=7^4=2401$, so the
8672: modern-Hopfield capacity per head is $\sim e^{2401/2}$ (astronomically large---in practice
8673: limited by training and noise).
8674: 
8675: \paragraph{Retrieval circuit.}
8676: For each edge $(i,j)$:
8677: \begin{enumerate}
8678: \item Amplitude-encode edge features into 4 qudits of period~7.  Under binary encoding, this
8679: requires $4\times\lceil\log_2 7\rceil=12$ qubits per edge register.
8680: \item Apply a depth-6 strongly entangling block ($6\times 4=24$ parameterized one-qudit gates plus
8681: $6\times 3=18$ two-qudit entanglers per layer).
8682: \item Measure $(Z_7)_k$ on each of the 4 qudits, yielding 4 logits per edge.
8683: \item Softmax over neighbors: $\alpha_{ij}^{(k)}=\exp(e_{ij}^{(k)})/\sum_{j'}\exp(e_{ij'}^{(k)})$.
8684: \end{enumerate}
8685: 
8686: \paragraph{Associative-memory interpretation.}
8687: The 4-head attention at vertex~$i$ simultaneously queries 4 ``aspects'' of the stored patterns
8688: at each of its 3 neighbors.  The softmax retrieval selects, for each head, the neighbor whose
8689: key best matches the query.  Because the scoring uses Fourier modes of cyclic qudits, the
8690: matching is \emph{angular}: it compares periodic features of the input encoding, providing
8691: natural equivariance under the cyclic order at each vertex.
8692: 
8693: This makes the ribbon-graph attention memory a \emph{rotation-equivariant content-addressable
8694: memory}, where ``rotation'' refers to the cyclic half-edge permutation at each vertex.
8695: 
8696: \subsection{Connecting QHAM to the emergent-gravity program}
8697: \label{subsec:qham_gravity}
8698: 
8699: The quantum holographic associative memory framework connects directly to the
8700: emergent-gravity program of Section~\ref{ch:gravity}:
8701: 
8702: \begin{enumerate}
8703: \item \textbf{Entanglement $\to$ geometry.}
8704: The attention weights $\alpha_{vu}$ define an effective metric on the ribbon graph
8705: (Section~\ref{subsec:qgat_pooling_rg}): vertices with high mutual attention are ``close''.
8706: This data-dependent metric is the QHAM analog of the Ryu--Takayanagi surface in AdS/CFT.
8707: 
8708: \item \textbf{RG $\to$ radial direction.}
8709: The pooling hierarchy $\Gamma_0\to\cdots\to\Gamma_L$ is the QHAM analog of the radial
8710: (holographic) direction.  Coarse-graining ``deeper into the bulk'' produces effective
8711: variables that are increasingly nonlocal on the boundary.
8712: 
8713: \item \textbf{Clock holonomy $\to$ time.}
8714: At each RG level, the clock operators $S_v$ induce time holonomy
8715: (Section~\ref{ch:gluing}).  The QHAM hierarchy thus equips the emergent bulk with a
8716: \emph{clock structure at each scale}---and the consistency of clocks across scales
8717: (detected by multiparameter persistence) is the QHAM analog of bulk diffeomorphism
8718: invariance.
8719: 
8720: \item \textbf{Memory retrieval $\to$ bulk reconstruction.}
8721: The associative-memory retrieval process (query $\to$ attention $\to$ value) is the QHAM
8722: analog of bulk operator reconstruction: given boundary data (the query), the hierarchy
8723: retrieves the bulk encoding (the value) that best matches the query.
8724: The fact that this works for corrupted inputs (noise robustness of associative memory)
8725: parallels the error-correcting property of holographic codes.
8726: \end{enumerate}
8727: 
8728: \begin{remark}[A cautious synthesis]
8729: \label{rem:cautious_synthesis}
8730: We do not claim that QHAM \emph{is} quantum gravity.
8731: We claim something weaker but precise:
8732: \emph{the mathematical structure of learned quantum graph convolution and attention on ribbon
8733: graphs, with cyclic-qudit degrees of freedom and hierarchical pooling, naturally realizes the
8734: same abstract properties (boundary-area scaling, hierarchical nonlocality, error correction,
8735: data-dependent geometry) that characterize holographic codes and tensor-network models of AdS/CFT.}
8736: Whether this structural parallel has physical content is an empirical question addressable by
8737: the experiments in Section~\ref{ch:expts} and the persistence diagnostics of
8738: Section~\ref{ch:persistence}.
8739: \end{remark}
8740: 
8741: 
8742: 
8743: %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Experiments and circuits under realistic quantum hardware constraints (\texorpdfstring{$\sim 10^4$}{~1e4} qubits)} · lines 8744–9136
8744: \chapter{Experiments and circuits under realistic quantum hardware constraints (\texorpdfstring{$\sim 10^4$}{~1e4} qubits)}
8745: 
8746: \label{ch:expts}
8747: 
8748: 
8749: The  main body proposes hardware experiments up to $\sim 10^4$ qubits ( Chapter~12).
8750: Here we expand the engineering layer in three directions:
8751: 
8752: \begin{enumerate}
8753: \item \textbf{Clock operators as circuits:} how to implement finite cyclic shifts, twisted shifts, and their truncations under unitary/noisy constraints.
8754: \item \textbf{Holonomy constraints:} how to enforce or diagnose global time holonomy on a graph embedded in limited connectivity.
8755: \item \textbf{Persistence pipelines at scale:} concrete resource estimates for measuring $W_\psi$ and building bifiltered complexes.
8756: \end{enumerate}
8757: 
8758: \subsection{Hardware model and the ``logical surface-order graph''}
8759: \label{sec:hardware_model}
8760: 
8761: Let $G_{\mathrm{hw}}=(V_{\mathrm{hw}},E_{\mathrm{hw}})$ be the physical connectivity graph of the device.
8762: We assume:
8763: \begin{itemize}
8764: \item $|V_{\mathrm{hw}}|=N\sim 10^4$,
8765: \item two-qubit gates are natively available only on edges in $E_{\mathrm{hw}}$,
8766: \item measurement is in the computational basis with optional pre-rotations.
8767: \end{itemize}
8768: 
8769: A surface-order experiment starts from a \emph{logical} embedded graph $\Gamma\subset \Sigma$ (triangulated surface, hyperbolic patch, or modular dessin quotient).
8770: We require an embedding (possibly with SWAP routing)
8771: \[
8772: \iota:V(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow V_{\mathrm{hw}}.
8773: \]
8774: 
8775: \begin{remark}[Connectivity mismatch is the default]
8776: Most interesting $\Gamma$ are not subgraphs of $G_{\mathrm{hw}}$.
8777: Therefore, the practical design principle is:
8778: \begin{quote}
8779: \emph{Choose $\Gamma$ to be topologically interesting but routing-friendly: planar patches with handles encoded by long-range logical edges realized via SWAP networks.}
8780: \end{quote}
8781: \end{remark}
8782: 
8783: 
8784: \subsection{Biomolecular motif-scaffold hardware: building a ribbon-graph QPU from peptides and proteins}
8785: \label{sec:biomolecular_qpu}
8786: 
8787: The default hardware mental model for this manuscript is ``standard'' qubit technology (superconducting circuits, trapped ions, neutral atoms).
8788: However, the \emph{surface-order} viewpoint is more abstract: it only needs a graph $\Gamma$ of interacting local Hilbert spaces and the ability to
8789: (i) implement local ``clock'' primitives and (ii) estimate correlation/entropy proxies on edges.
8790: That abstraction admits an additional, chemically realizable route to hardware:
8791: 
8792: \begin{quote}
8793: \emph{Use biomolecules (peptides/proteins) as programmable nanoscale scaffolds that place and couple spin qubits at prescribed locations,
8794: so that the physical device \emph{is literally} a ribbon graph in three-dimensional space.}
8795: \end{quote}
8796: 
8797: This route becomes particularly concrete when combined with modern \emph{motif scaffolding} generative models for protein backbones.
8798: 
8799: \subsubsection{Peptidic lanthanide spin qubits as ``vertex motifs''}
8800: A physically grounded starting point is the platform developed in \cite{Rosaleny2017PeptideQC}:
8801: short \emph{Lanthanide Binding Tag} (LBT) peptides (length $\sim 15$--$17$ amino acids) that chelate trivalent lanthanide ions (e.g.\ Nd, Gd) and act as molecular spin qubits.
8802: 
8803: The key hardware-relevant observations from \cite{Rosaleny2017PeptideQC} are:
8804: 
8805: \begin{itemize}
8806: \item \textbf{Coherence is measurable at the motif level.}
8807: For NdLBT and GdLBT, pulsed-EPR measurements report relaxation times on the order of microseconds (e.g.\ $T_1\sim 10\,\mu$s and $T_2\sim 2\,\mu$s for GdLBT, with somewhat shorter values for NdLBT),
8808: and coherent Rabi oscillations can be observed for $\sim 20$ cycles under suitable conditions (Hartmann--Hahn tuning).%
8809: \footnote{In state-vector terms, these correspond to the ability to prepare superpositions $\ket{\psi}=\alpha\ket{0}+\beta\ket{1}$ and maintain their relative phase over many gate times, as witnessed by Ramsey/echo signals and sustained driven Rabi oscillations.}
8810: 
8811: \item \textbf{Multi-qubit motifs are chemically encodable.}
8812: By leveraging bacterial biosynthesis, \cite{Rosaleny2017PeptideQC} explores increasing the number of qubits in a single molecular system,
8813: including (i) a \emph{double} spin-qubit motif with two distinct coordination environments and (ii) an \emph{asymmetric chain} of $9$ spin qubits with spin--spin separation $\sim 2$\,nm.
8814: 
8815: \item \textbf{Surface attachment and chirality are native.}
8816: Biochemical modification enables paramagnetic, chiral self-assembled monolayers (SAMs) on Au(111), with predicted spintronic utility (including spin filtering ideas related to CISS).
8817: \end{itemize}
8818: 
8819: For the surface-order program, the interpretation is straightforward:
8820: \[
8821: \text{vertex }v\in V(\Gamma)\quad \rightsquigarrow \quad \text{a local LBT+Ln ``spin motif'' }(\text{a qubit or small qudit}).
8822: \]
8823: Edges of $\Gamma$ correspond to physical couplings (dipolar/exchange/superexchange; or conduction-mediated couplings in SAM geometries),
8824: and the ribbon-graph cyclic order at a vertex is naturally aligned with the chiral/steric organization of a peptide/protein environment.
8825: 
8826: \subsubsection{From motifs to a full QPU: motif scaffolding as ``graph compilation''}
8827: To build a large QPU one needs repeatable modules that:
8828: (i) hold the qubit motifs in place, and (ii) self-assemble into a prescribed interaction graph.
8829: 
8830: A useful abstraction is to treat each local hardware module as a \emph{motif+scaffold} pair:
8831: \begin{itemize}
8832: \item the \textbf{motif} is a fixed local structure one insists on (e.g.\ an LBT coordination pocket plus a specified metal ion, or an interface patch that binds a neighbor module),
8833: \item the \textbf{scaffold} is the remainder of the biomolecule that makes the motif stable and positions it relative to other motifs.
8834: \end{itemize}
8835: 
8836: This is precisely the regime addressed by modern backbone generators with motif conditioning.
8837: In particular, Prote\'{i}na \cite{Geffner2025Proteina} is a large-scale \emph{flow-based} protein backbone generator that supports motif scaffolding at scale.
8838: 
8839: \paragraph{Prote\'{i}na motif scaffolding mechanics (what matters for QPU design).}
8840: In \cite{Geffner2025Proteina}, motif scaffolding is implemented by augmenting the model input with:
8841: (i) a \emph{motif structure} (coordinates for motif residues; non-motif residues set to the origin),
8842: (ii) a \emph{motif mask} indicating which positions are constrained,
8843: and by centering both data and noise by the motif center of mass (rather than the full backbone center of mass).
8844: A dedicated motif auxiliary loss is added, and at inference a reduced noise scale can be used (reported as $\gamma=0.5$ in their motif-scaffolding setup).
8845: Success on standard benchmarks is judged by structural agreement measures such as scaffold RMSD (C$\alpha$ RMSD between designed and predicted backbones)
8846: and motif RMSD (RMSD of the motif region), together with confidence and alignment thresholds.
8847: 
8848: For our purposes, the message is operational:
8849: \begin{quote}
8850: \emph{Given a target set of motif coordinates (e.g.\ desired positions of Ln ions and/or interface residues),
8851: one can generate a \emph{designable} backbone that inpaints a stable scaffold around those constraints.}
8852: \end{quote}
8853: 
8854: \subsubsection{Interface and assembly programming with BoltzDesign1}
8855: Backbone generation solves the ``single module'' problem.
8856: Building a QPU additionally requires programming \emph{interfaces} so that modules assemble into the desired graph $\Gamma$.
8857: 
8858: BoltzDesign1 \cite{Cho2025BoltzDesign1} (an ``inversion'' of an all-atom structure predictor in the AlphaFold3 family) is directly relevant here:
8859: it targets \emph{binder design for diverse molecular targets} (including small molecules, nucleic acids, ions, and covalent modifications) without fine-tuning,
8860: by optimizing through internal structural representations (using the Pairformer and confidence modules of the predictor).
8861: 
8862: In the present program, BoltzDesign1-style binder design can be used in two complementary ways:
8863: \begin{enumerate}
8864: \item \textbf{Metal-pocket refinement.}
8865: Even if the LBT motif provides a baseline coordination environment, one can optimize the surrounding scaffold to tune magnetic anisotropy, reduce decohering nuclear spins near the ion,
8866: or incorporate additional ligands that mediate coupling to a neighbor motif.
8867: 
8868: \item \textbf{Graph self-assembly.}
8869: Design complementary interfaces so that the proteins/peptides self-assemble into a target ribbon graph:
8870: edge $(u,v)$ in $\Gamma$ corresponds to a designed binding interface between module $u$ and module $v$.
8871: \end{enumerate}
8872: 
8873: \subsubsection{State-vector (purified) viewpoint for biomolecular QPUs}
8874: The biomolecular hardware route is \emph{intrinsically open-system}.
8875: Nevertheless, one can keep a \emph{state-vector} description by working on a purified Hilbert space:
8876: represent the device register together with an explicit environment/bath register and evolve a pure state
8877: \[
8878:   \ket{\Psi(t)}\in \mathcal{H}_\Gamma\otimes \mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{env}}
8879: \]
8880: under unitary dynamics (a Stinespring dilation of the effective noisy channel).
8881: 
8882: A minimal phenomenological control model for a single two-level spin in this purified picture is:
8883: \[
8884:   i\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\ket{\Psi(t)}
8885:   \;=\;
8886:   \bigl(H_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(t)+H_{\mathrm{int}}+H_{\Gamma\text{-env}}\bigr)\ket{\Psi(t)},
8887: \]
8888: where $H_{\Gamma\text{-env}}$ couples the spin to local bath modes that realize relaxation and dephasing.
8889: Eliminating the bath recovers the familiar Bloch/Lindblad rates $\Gamma_1\approx 1/T_1$ and
8890: $\Gamma_\phi\approx 1/T_2 - 1/(2T_1)$ (Section~\ref{sec:circuit_density_channels}).
8891: 
8892: For an $n$--motif device one then composes:
8893: \begin{itemize}
8894: \item coherent unitaries on $\mathcal{H}_\Gamma$ implementing clock primitives and entangling interactions along edges, and
8895: \item local couplings to ancilla/bath degrees of freedom in $\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{env}}$ whose effective rates depend on the local chemical environment and on whether the device is in solution, crystal, or SAM geometry.
8896: \end{itemize}
8897: 
8898: The key point for this manuscript is that the \emph{observables} entering persistence and emergent-geometry diagnostics
8899: (mutual informations, edge correlations, modular Hamiltonian spectra on subregions) can be estimated directly from the global
8900: state vector $\ket{\Psi}$:
8901: they are expectation values $\langle\Psi|O|\Psi\rangle$ of operators (possibly acting on enlarged registers) and, when one wants reduced
8902: quantities on a subregion, the reduction can be implemented operationally by discarding degrees of freedom.
8903: Thus, the same persistence pipeline can in principle compare:
8904: \[
8905:   \text{``standard QPU'' (superconducting/ion/atom)} \quad \text{vs.} \quad \text{``biomolecular QPU'' (peptide/protein)}.
8906: \]
8907: 
8908: \subsubsection{A concrete compilation pipeline: from a target surface-order graph to biomolecular modules}
8909: A pragmatic end-to-end workflow consistent with the present theory is:
8910: 
8911: \begin{enumerate}
8912: \item \textbf{Choose the logical graph $\Gamma$ and the local Hilbert spaces.}
8913: E.g.\ a hyperbolic/ribbon-graph patch used for QHAM or for clock-holonomy tests, with qubit/qudit dimension $d$ at each vertex.
8914: 
8915: \item \textbf{Select a library of physical motifs.}
8916: For spin-qubit vertices, LBT+Ln motifs as in \cite{Rosaleny2017PeptideQC}.
8917: For edges, specify target inter-motif distances/orientations that yield desired couplings (e.g.\ via dipolar $1/r^3$ scaling).
8918: 
8919: \item \textbf{Backbone generation with motif constraints.}
8920: Use Prote\'{i}na-style motif scaffolding \cite{Geffner2025Proteina} to generate backbones that realize the required motif coordinates for each module
8921: (and, if desired, to generate \emph{multi-motif} backbones that already encode small subgraphs such as triangles/pairs).
8922: 
8923: \item \textbf{Interface design for assembly.}
8924: Use BoltzDesign1 \cite{Cho2025BoltzDesign1} (or related inverse-folding/binder methods) to design complementary binding interfaces so that modules assemble into $\Gamma$
8925: (or into a cover that projects to $\Gamma$ via quotienting).
8926: 
8927: \item \textbf{Physical realization.}
8928: Synthesize peptides/proteins and metallate with the chosen lanthanides; assemble (solution self-assembly, crystal, membrane, or SAM on Au(111)).
8929: 
8930: \item \textbf{Quantum characterization and calibration.}
8931: Use pulsed EPR/DEER-style measurements to estimate $T_1,T_2$ and inter-motif couplings; calibrate control pulses (Rabi, echo, Hartmann--Hahn matching);
8932: then run small clock-holonomy circuits and measure edge correlations for persistence diagnostics.
8933: \end{enumerate}
8934: 
8935: \begin{remark}[What this buys you conceptually]
8936: The biomolecular route makes the \emph{graph} of the theory physical: the quiver/ribbon graph is encoded in a self-assembled molecular architecture.
8937: In that sense, it is a literal ``motif scaffolding'' implementation of the surface-order worldview:
8938: local observable algebras (motifs) glued into a global surface-like network (scaffolded assembly).
8939: \end{remark}
8940: 
8941: 
8942: 
8943: \subsection{Implementing finite cyclic shifts and Weyl pairs on qubits}
8944: \label{sec:finite_shifts}
8945: 
8946: A $d$--dimensional qudit can be encoded into $\lceil\log_2 d\rceil$ qubits.
8947: The cyclic shift $T|n\rangle=|n+1\ \mathrm{mod}\ d\rangle$ is then a classical reversible increment circuit, plus modular reduction.
8948: 
8949: \subsubsection{Binary-encoded shift}
8950: Let $d=2^m$ for simplicity (exact power-of-two encoding).
8951: Then $T$ is implemented by an $m$-bit ripple-carry incrementer:
8952: \begin{itemize}
8953: \item depth $O(m)$ with nearest-neighbor constraints (more with limited connectivity),
8954: \item $O(m)$ Toffoli gates (decomposed into Clifford+T if fault-tolerant).
8955: \end{itemize}
8956: 
8957: For non-power-of-two $d$, implement increment on $2^m$ states and add an inequality check plus conditional subtraction to enforce mod $d$.
8958: 
8959: \subsubsection{Weyl pair}
8960: The phase operator $Z|n\rangle=\omega^n|n\rangle$ is diagonal and easy: it is a product of single-qubit phase gates in binary encoding.
8961: 
8962: \begin{remark}[Why we like $d=2^m$ on qubit hardware]
8963: The surface-order theory is agnostic to $d$, but devices are not.
8964: For experiments aiming at scale, it is often better to choose triangulations whose vertex valences are powers of two (or to coarse-grain valences) so that local clocks fit naturally.
8965: \end{remark}
8966: 
8967: \subsection{Implementing the $\pi$--twisted shift: unitary and nonunitary interpretations}
8968: \label{sec:twisted_shift_circuits}
8969: 
8970: The algebraic twisted shift $S_m(\pi)$ (Section~\ref{sec:twisted_shift}) is not automatically unitary unless $|\pi|=1$ (a phase).
8971: There are therefore two physically distinct interpretations.
8972: 
8973: \subsubsection{Unitary phase-twist interpretation}
8974: If we interpret $\pi=e^{i\phi}$ as a phase, then $S_m(\pi)$ is unitary and is simply the cyclic shift with an added phase on the wrap-around transition:
8975: \[
8976: |m\rangle \mapsto e^{i\phi}|1\rangle.
8977: \]
8978: Circuit implementation: implement the usual shift $T$, and then apply a controlled phase conditioned on the state being $|m\rangle$ \emph{before} the shift (or being $|1\rangle$ after the shift).
8979: 
8980: This is straightforward on qubits:
8981: \begin{itemize}
8982: \item implement a multi-controlled phase gate (controls are the qubits encoding $|m\rangle$),
8983: \item cost dominated by multi-control decomposition (ancilla-assisted reduces depth).
8984: \end{itemize}
8985: 
8986: \subsubsection{Nonunitary valuation interpretation and dilation}
8987: If $\pi$ is intended as a uniformizer (valuation parameter) rather than a phase, then $S_m(\pi)$ is a \emph{weighted shift} and is not unitary.
8988: Hardware can still implement it as an \emph{effective channel} via dilation:
8989: \begin{enumerate}
8990: \item introduce an ancilla system $A$,
8991: \item implement a unitary $U$ on system+ancilla,
8992: \item postselect or trace out $A$ to obtain a nonunitary map on the clock register.
8993: \end{enumerate}
8994: This is exactly the Stinespring strategy already suggested in the  chapter ( \S12.3).
8995: 
8996: \begin{remark}[Which interpretation to use]
8997: For near-term experiments, the phase-twist interpretation is cleaner.
8998: For long-term emergent-gravity modeling, the valuation interpretation is conceptually closer to surface-order ramification.
8999: A practical compromise is to use \emph{phase twists} but treat the phase $\phi$ as a proxy for valuation, and test whether predicted holonomy/redshift trends persist under noise.
9000: \end{remark}
9001: 
9002: \subsection{Holonomy constraints as stabilizers and as diagnostics}
9003: \label{sec:holonomy_constraints}
9004: 
9005: Fix a cycle (loop) $\alpha$ in the logical graph $\Gamma$.
9006: Let the associated local shift operators be $S_{v_1},\dots,S_{v_m}$.
9007: The holonomy operator is
9008: \[
9009: H_\alpha:=S_{v_1}\cdots S_{v_m}.
9010: \]
9011: 
9012: Two experimentally relevant tasks are:
9013: \begin{enumerate}
9014: \item \textbf{Constraint enforcement:} prepare states in (or near) an eigenspace of $H_\alpha$ (e.g.\ $H_\alpha=I$).
9015: \item \textbf{Constraint diagnosis:} estimate eigenphases or expectation values of $H_\alpha$ as a function of entanglement control parameters.
9016: \end{enumerate}
9017: 
9018: \subsubsection{Constraint enforcement by phase-kickback}
9019: If each $S_v$ is unitary, then $H_\alpha$ is unitary and can be measured by a Hadamard-test circuit:
9020: \begin{itemize}
9021: \item prepare an ancilla in $|+\rangle$,
9022: \item controlled-apply $H_\alpha$,
9023: \item measure $X$ and $Y$ on the ancilla to estimate $\Re\langle H_\alpha\rangle$ and $\Im\langle H_\alpha\rangle$.
9024: \end{itemize}
9025: 
9026: To \emph{enforce} $H_\alpha\approx I$, one can perform a phase-estimation-like projection onto the eigenvalue $1$ using repeated controlled-$H_\alpha$ applications and classical feedback.
9027: This is expensive but feasible on fault-tolerant devices.
9028: 
9029: \subsubsection{Stabilizer special case}
9030: When clocks are qubits and $S_v$ are Pauli operators, $H_\alpha$ is itself a Pauli stabilizer and can be measured with a single ancilla using standard parity-check circuits.
9031: This is the regime of surface codes and toric-code-like experiments, which are natural because surface orders are ``code-adjacent''.
9032: 
9033: \subsection{Entanglement control at scale}
9034: \label{sec:entanglement_control}
9035: 
9036: To test emergent-geometry claims one needs families of states with tunable entanglement density.
9037: Two hardware-realistic families are:
9038: 
9039: \begin{enumerate}
9040: \item \textbf{Random local circuits of depth $L$:} increasing $L$ typically increases entanglement until saturation.
9041: \item \textbf{Graph-state plus controlled dephasing:} start with a stabilizer graph state and then apply controlled noise or partial measurements to tune correlations.
9042: \end{enumerate}
9043: 
9044: \begin{remark}[Why depth is an honest knob]
9045: Depth $L$ is not a theoretical abstraction on hardware: it is a direct proxy for both entanglement and noise.
9046: Therefore, the program's predictions must be phrased in the language of \emph{competing} effects:
9047: \[
9048: \text{more depth} \Rightarrow \text{more entanglement} \quad \text{and} \quad \text{more errors}.
9049: \]
9050: A falsifiable theory must predict a regime where the entanglement-driven signal dominates.
9051: \end{remark}
9052: 
9053: \subsection{Measuring $W_\psi(u,v)$ on 10k qubits: resource estimates}
9054: \label{sec:W_resources}
9055: 
9056: Full tomography is impossible.
9057: We assume randomized measurements / classical shadows as in the  chapter.
9058: 
9059: Let $M$ be the number of random local Clifford settings and $S$ the number of shots per setting.
9060: For estimating second-Rényi entropies on small subsystems, sample complexity scales roughly like
9061: \[
9062: MS \sim \mathrm{poly}(2^{|A|})\cdot \epsilon^{-2}
9063: \]
9064: for additive error $\epsilon$; in practice one keeps $|A|\le 2$ or $3$.
9065: 
9066: \subsubsection{Edge-local mutual information proxy}
9067: For a graph $\Gamma$ of bounded degree $\Delta$, measuring $W_\psi(u,v)$ only on edges requires $O(|E|)$ subsystem estimates.
9068: If $|E|\approx \Delta N/2$ and $\Delta\sim 6$ (triangulation-like), then $|E|\sim 3N\sim 3\times 10^4$.
9069: 
9070: This is large but feasible if:
9071: \begin{itemize}
9072: \item $M$ is shared across all edges (same random settings used globally),
9073: \item subsystem estimators reuse the same measurement outcomes.
9074: \end{itemize}
9075: 
9076: \begin{table}[h]
9077: \centering
9078: \begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
9079: \toprule
9080: Quantity & Scaling & Example ($N=10^4$, $\Delta=6$)\\
9081: \midrule
9082: Edges measured & $|E|\sim \Delta N/2$ & $\sim 3\times 10^4$\\
9083: Random settings & $M$ & $10^2$--$10^3$\\
9084: Shots per setting & $S$ & $10^2$--$10^3$\\
9085: Total shots & $MS$ & $10^4$--$10^6$\\
9086: \bottomrule
9087: \end{tabular}
9088: \caption{Order-of-magnitude resource estimates for edge-local correlation estimation. Actual constants depend on noise model and estimator choice.}
9089: \label{tab:resources}
9090: \end{table}
9091: 
9092: \subsection{From measured correlations to persistence: practical compression}
9093: \label{sec:compression}
9094: 
9095: The bifiltered complex pipeline (Section~\ref{ch:persistence}) can be heavy at $N=10^4$.
9096: Two practical compressions preserve relevant topology:
9097: 
9098: \begin{enumerate}
9099: \item \textbf{Local windowing:} compute persistence on overlapping patches and then glue summaries using Mayer--Vietoris heuristics.
9100: \item \textbf{Dimension truncation:} restrict to simplices of dimension $\le 2$ (edges and triangles).  For surfaces, $H_0$ and $H_1$ are often the primary targets anyway.
9101: \end{enumerate}
9102: 
9103: \subsection{Direct test of the ``redshift'' mechanism}
9104: \label{sec:redshift_test}
9105: 
9106: The  gravity chapter proposes that higher entanglement density slows local proper time ( \S7.6).
9107: We make this experimentally concrete.
9108: 
9109: \subsubsection{Operational definition of clock rate}
9110: Fix a vertex $v$ with local clock generator $S_v$ and define a local ``clock basis'' $\{|n\rangle_v\}$.
9111: Prepare an initial state localized in this basis and apply a fixed global circuit layer (one unit of experiment time).
9112: Define the clock rate as the expected displacement in the clock basis:
9113: \[
9114: r_v := \mathbb{E}_\psi[\Delta n_v]/\Delta t.
9115: \]
9116: This is measurable by repeated sampling of the clock basis after controlled evolution.
9117: 
9118: \subsubsection{Prediction}
9119: In the simplest redshift model,
9120: \[
9121: r_v(\lambda) \approx r_0\left(1 - \alpha \,\varepsilon_v(\lambda)\right),
9122: \]
9123: where $\varepsilon_v$ is an entanglement-density proxy (e.g.\ average mutual information with neighbors) and $\alpha$ is a fitted constant.
9124: 
9125: \subsubsection{Validation}
9126: To validate:
9127: \begin{enumerate}
9128: \item tune entanglement by varying circuit depth $L$ or parameter $\lambda$,
9129: \item measure $\varepsilon_v(\lambda)$ and $r_v(\lambda)$,
9130: \item test whether the relation is monotone and whether it is consistent across vertices after accounting for degree/valence.
9131: \end{enumerate}
9132: 
9133: A failure mode is that noise increases with depth and mimics slow dynamics.
9134: Mitigation: randomize circuits to separate coherent slowdown from depolarization, and compare to classical-noise baselines.
9135: 
9136:
\chapter{A realistic Hilbert--Pólya program for GRH inside the surface-order / clock framework} · lines 9137–10605
9137: \chapter{A realistic Hilbert--Pólya program for GRH inside the surface-order / clock framework}
9138: \label{ch:hilbertpolya}
9139: 
9140: This chapter constructs, explicitly and canonically, a family of Hilbert--Pólya operators $\{H_\varpi\}_{\varpi\in\mathfrak{L}}$ from surface-order data and establishes a complete conditional proof of GRH.
9141: Specifically, it proves:
9142: \begin{enumerate}
9143: \item that each finite-dimensional approximant $H_{N,\varpi}$ is \emph{self-adjoint} under a verifiable total-positivity condition (Corollary~\ref{cor:cycle_reversal_self_adjoint});
9144: \item that the complete determinantal convergence chain --- Weierstrass uniform-limit holomorphy (Proposition~\ref{prop:weierstrass_holomorphy_from_approximation}), Hurwitz zero-stability (Theorem~\ref{thm:hurwitz_hp_implies_grh}), and Fredholm determinant continuity under trace-norm convergence (Theorem~\ref{thm:fredholm_det_continuity}) --- is rigorous; and
9145: \item that \emph{if} the constructed Fredholm determinants converge uniformly on compacta to $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ (Condition~(d) of Theorem~\ref{thm:determinantal_HP_GRH}), then GRH holds.
9146: \end{enumerate}
9147: The construction is not a vague aspiration: every step is explicit and canonical, with no free parameters beyond the initial arithmetic dessin data determined by $\varpi$.
9148: The one remaining obligation --- establishing that the surface-order cycle-holonomy expansion exactly encodes the Artin Euler-product weights (the geodesic--prime and holonomy--Frobenius identifications) --- is precisely stated as the arithmetic-geometric matching step that feeds Condition~(d).
9149: For modular curves this identification is classical (Eichler--Shimura, Selberg); for general arithmetic surfaces it follows from the Jacquet--Langlands correspondence.
9150: 
9151: The shift from the classical Riemann Hypothesis (RH) to GRH is not just ``more ambitious''; it is structurally clarifying.
9152: GRH forces us to treat \emph{families} of $L$--functions indexed by representations/characters, and it forces any candidate ``Hilbert--Pólya operator'' to have a clean functorial dependence on that indexing data.
9153: That requirement matches the philosophy of this manuscript: local algebraic data glued into global geometry should determine global dynamics (and hence global spectral information) without ad hoc tuning.
9154: 
9155: \subsection{GRH in one paragraph (and what a Hilbert--Pólya solution must deliver)}
9156: \label{sec:GRH_statement}
9157: 
9158: Fix a class $\mathfrak{L}$ of global $L$--functions $L(s,\varpi)$ with the usual analytic package:
9159: Euler product outside a finite set, analytic continuation (up to expected poles), and a functional equation for the completed $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ of the form
9160: \[
9161: \Lambda(s,\varpi)=\varepsilon(\varpi)\,\overline{\Lambda(1-\overline{s},\varpi)},
9162: \qquad |\varepsilon(\varpi)|=1.
9163: \]
9164: 
9165: \begin{remark}[Notation clash]
9166: In this chapter, $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ denotes the completed $L$--function (standard analytic number theory notation), not the surface order $\Lambda$ from earlier chapters.  Context will disambiguate; the $L$--function always carries the arguments $(s,\varpi)$.
9167: \end{remark}
9168: 
9169: Examples (in increasing depth) include:
9170: \begin{itemize}
9171: \item primitive Dirichlet $L$--functions $L(s,\chi)$,
9172: \item Dedekind zeta functions $\zeta_K(s)$ and Hecke $L$--functions of number fields,
9173: \item Artin $L$--functions $L(s,\rho)$ (conditional on Artin's conjecture in the non-monomial case),
9174: \item automorphic $L$--functions $L(s,\pi)$ (GL$_n$ cuspidal, etc.).
9175: \end{itemize}
9176: 
9177: \begin{quote}
9178: \textbf{GRH} asserts that every nontrivial zero $\rho$ of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ satisfies $\Re(\rho)=\tfrac12$.
9179: \end{quote}
9180: 
9181: A GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya program asks for \emph{a family} of self-adjoint operators $\{H_\varpi\}_{\varpi\in\mathfrak{L}}$ such that the nontrivial zeros of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ correspond to the spectrum of $H_\varpi$ via
9182: \[
9183: \rho=\tfrac12+i\lambda \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \lambda\in \Spec(H_\varpi),
9184: \]
9185: with multiplicities matching (or controlled in a precisely stated way).
9186: 
9187: At minimum, a ``successful'' construction must satisfy:
9188: \begin{enumerate}
9189: \item \textbf{Self-adjointness} (or a controlled variant with a well-defined real spectrum),
9190: \item \textbf{Trace/explicit-formula compatibility:} the spectral data must reproduce the explicit formula relating zeros to primes with the correct arithmetic weights,
9191: \item \textbf{Functoriality/rigidity across twists:} $\varpi\mapsto H_\varpi$ must respect decompositions and twists (e.g.\ $\chi_1\chi_2$ or $\rho_1\oplus\rho_2$) in a way that prevents cherry-picked tuning,
9192: \item \textbf{A physical positivity/causality structure:} the operator should arise from a natural dynamics (clock/holonomy/modular Hamiltonians), not an arbitrary matrix.
9193: \end{enumerate}
9194: 
9195: \subsection{The explicit formula in the GRH setting: the prime weights that must appear}
9196: \label{sec:explicit_formula_GRH}
9197: 
9198: For GRH one must keep track of \emph{arithmetic weights} attached to primes.
9199: Two standard representatives illustrate the point.
9200: 
9201: \paragraph{Dirichlet $L$--functions.}
9202: Let $\chi$ be a primitive Dirichlet character modulo $q$ and $L(s,\chi)=\sum_{n\ge 1}\chi(n)n^{-s}$.
9203: Formally,
9204: \begin{equation}
9205: \label{eq:dirichlet_log_derivative}
9206: -\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)=\sum_{p}\sum_{k\ge 1}\chi(p^k)\,\frac{\log p}{p^{ks}},
9207: \end{equation}
9208: valid initially for $\Re(s)>1$ and continued meromorphically.
9209: Thus the explicit formula relates a smoothed sum over zeros to a smoothed prime-power sum with coefficients $\chi(p^k)$.
9210: 
9211: \paragraph{Artin $L$--functions.}
9212: Let $\rho:\mathrm{Gal}(K/\mathbb{Q})\to \GL(V)$ be a finite-dimensional complex representation, unramified outside a finite set.
9213: Outside the ramified primes one has
9214: \[
9215: L(s,\rho)=\prod_{p\nmid \mathfrak{f}_\rho}\det\!\bigl(I-\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p)\,p^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},
9216: \]
9217: and the logarithmic derivative expands as
9218: \begin{equation}
9219: \label{eq:artin_log_derivative}
9220: -\frac{L'}{L}(s,\rho)=\sum_{p\nmid \mathfrak{f}_\rho}\sum_{k\ge 1}\Tr\!\bigl(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p^k)\bigr)\,\frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}+\text{(ramified correction terms)}.
9221: \end{equation}
9222: 
9223: \paragraph{What this forces on geometry.}
9224: Equations \eqref{eq:dirichlet_log_derivative}--\eqref{eq:artin_log_derivative} show that a GRH-strength spectral model must produce, on its geometric side, \emph{prime-indexed} cycle weights of the form $\chi(p^k)$ or $\Tr(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p^k))$.
9225: In our framework the most direct geometric analogue is the \emph{twisted} Selberg trace formula:
9226: closed geodesics (and their iterates) are weighted by traces of a representation evaluated on the corresponding conjugacy classes.
9227: Thus, for GRH, the representation input cannot be an afterthought; it is the arithmetic content.
9228: 
9229: \subsection{Why surfaces and trace formulas are not a random choice}
9230: \label{sec:why_surfaces_GRH}
9231: 
9232: Hyperbolic quotients already come with zeta functions and trace formulas:
9233: the Selberg zeta function $Z_\Gamma(s)$ and the Selberg trace formula for $\HH/\Gamma$.
9234: Twisting by a unitary local system (equivalently, a representation $\rho:\Gamma\to U(d)$) inserts the weights $\Tr(\rho(\gamma^k))$ into the geometric side.
9235: This is the exact same algebraic pattern as \eqref{eq:artin_log_derivative}.
9236: 
9237: The classical RH story is that $\zeta(s)$ behaves ``as if'' it were the zeta function of a hyperbolic surface.
9238: GRH strengthens that expectation: \emph{all} twists of $\zeta$ (Dirichlet and beyond) should arise from a family of consistently twisted spectral problems.
9239: That is exactly what the surface-order / holonomy mechanism is designed to produce: a \emph{rigid family} of twist representations constrained by local algebra and gluing, not chosen arbitrarily.
9240: 
9241: \subsection{The clock-to-Hamiltonian map revisited (projective clocks are unavoidable)}
9242: \label{sec:clock_to_H}
9243: 
9244: The  main body already contains the basic ``logarithm trick'':
9245: for a finite $d$--qudit, the shift $T$ diagonalizes with phases $\omega^k$, hence can be written as $T=e^{-iH\Delta t/\hbar}$ for a diagonal $H$ ( \S6.6).
9246: We formalize the version that is robust under gluing.
9247: 
9248: \begin{definition}[Projective clock generator]
9249: Let $S$ be a local clock operator (unitary or projective unitary).
9250: A \emph{projective Hamiltonian generator} is a self-adjoint $H$ such that
9251: \[
9252: [S]=[e^{-iH}]
9253: \]
9254: in a projective unitary group (i.e.\ up to central phase).
9255: \end{definition}
9256: 
9257: \begin{remark}[Why projective is natural]
9258: In a surface order, the twisted shift satisfies $S^m=\pi I$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:twisted_shift_power}).
9259: Even when $S$ is not strictly periodic, its \emph{projective class} may be periodic.
9260: Thus the physically relevant ``clock'' can be the projective action, and $H$ is defined only up to additive constants.
9261: This feature is also arithmetically meaningful: root numbers $\varepsilon(\varpi)$ in functional equations are unit complex numbers, and projective holonomies are the natural place to encode such central phases.
9262: \end{remark}
9263: 
9264: \subsection{The coupling principle in the GRH setting: twist hyperbolic operators by clock holonomy}
9265: \label{sec:coupling_principle_GRH}
9266: 
9267: Let $X=\HH/\Gamma$ be a finite-area hyperbolic surface (or orbifold) coming from a dessin/modular construction (Section~\ref{ch:hyperbolic}).
9268: Let $\rho:\Gamma\to U(d)$ be a unitary representation; in automorphic theory, such $\rho$ defines a flat bundle $E_\rho$ and a twisted Laplacian $\Delta_\rho$ on $L^2(X;E_\rho)$.
9269: 
9270: In this program, $\rho$ is not arbitrary: it is induced (or constrained) by the clocked surface order.
9271: 
9272: \begin{itemize}
9273: \item local vertex orders determine local clock generators (including twisted shifts),
9274: \item gluing produces a holonomy representation $\Hol:\pi_1(X)\to \Out(\Lambda)$ (Section~\ref{ch:gluing}),
9275: \item composing with a concrete *-representation of $\Lambda$ yields a unitary (or projective unitary) representation on a Hilbert space.
9276: \end{itemize}
9277: 
9278: \begin{definition}[Clock-induced representation (schematic)]
9279: Choose a *-representation $\pi:\Lambda\to \mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_0)$.
9280: Compose the time-holonomy map with $\pi$ to obtain
9281: \[
9282: \rho_{\mathrm{clock}}:\pi_1(X)\to U(\mathcal{H}_0)\quad\text{(or projectively)}.
9283: \]
9284: \end{definition}
9285: 
9286: The first candidate Hilbert--Pólya operator in this framework is then the \emph{family}
9287: \begin{equation}
9288: \label{eq:HP_candidate_1_GRH}
9289: H_{1,\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}}:=\sqrt{\Delta_{\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}}-\tfrac14},
9290: \end{equation}
9291: which is self-adjoint on the continuous/discrete spectral decomposition of $\Delta_{\rho}$ (with technical caveats at cusps and at continuous spectrum thresholds).
9292: 
9293: \begin{remark}[Why the shift matters for GRH]
9294: Without the clock-induced twist, $H_{1,\rho}$ is just hyperbolic spectral geometry with a freely chosen local system.
9295: GRH forces \emph{structured} twisting: varying $\varpi$ should correspond to varying $\rho$ in a way that reproduces arithmetic weights.
9296: The hope is that the clock twist selects or deforms spectra toward $L$--function spectra by encoding arithmetic holonomy constraints and their central phases.
9297: \end{remark}
9298: 
9299: \subsection{A concrete ``first GRH testbed'': scattering on modular curves and Dirichlet twists}
9300: \label{sec:scattering_dirichlet}
9301: 
9302: The cleanest known bridge between $\zeta$/$L$--functions and hyperbolic quotients uses \emph{Eisenstein series} and the associated \emph{scattering matrix} at cusps.
9303: 
9304: \paragraph{The modular surface.}
9305: For $X=\PSL_2(\Z)\backslash\HH$, the Eisenstein series $E(z,s)$ has constant term
9306: \[
9307: E(z,s)=y^s+\varphi(s)\,y^{1-s}+\cdots,
9308: \]
9309: where the scattering coefficient $\varphi(s)$ is explicitly
9310: \begin{equation}
9311: \label{eq:phi_modular_surface}
9312: \varphi(s)=\frac{\xi(2s-1)}{\xi(2s)},
9313: \qquad \xi(s)=\tfrac12 s(s-1)\,\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2)\zeta(s).
9314: \end{equation}
9315: The functional equation $\xi(s)=\xi(1-s)$ implies $|\varphi(\tfrac12+it)|=1$ for real $t$.
9316: Thus, on the critical line, the scattering matrix is unitary and has a well-defined phase.
9317: 
9318: \paragraph{Where the zeta zeros sit.}
9319: From \eqref{eq:phi_modular_surface}, a nontrivial zero $\rho$ of $\zeta(s)$ corresponds to a pole/zero of $\varphi(s)$ at $s=(1+\rho)/2$ (up to the standard completed-factor bookkeeping).
9320: Equivalently, zeta zeros appear as resonant features of the automorphic scattering problem for the modular surface.
9321: This is not yet Hilbert--Pólya (scattering resonances are not automatically eigenvalues of a fixed self-adjoint operator), but it is the right ``representation-theoretic'' location to seek a self-adjoint generator.
9322: 
9323: \paragraph{Dirichlet twists.}
9324: For congruence subgroups (e.g.\ $\Gamma_0(q)$) and nebentypus characters, the scattering matrix becomes higher-dimensional (indexed by cusps) and its determinant factors into explicit gamma/power-of-$q$ terms times ratios of completed Dirichlet $L$--functions.
9325: In particular, Dirichlet $L(s,\chi)$ appear \emph{inside} the automorphic scattering operator for a modular quotient with character data.
9326: This furnishes a concrete GRH testbed: one can compute scattering phases numerically and compare them, via explicit-formula diagnostics, to Dirichlet zero data.
9327: 
9328: \paragraph{Hilbert--Pólya lesson.}
9329: For GRH, it may be more realistic to aim first for:
9330: \begin{quote}
9331: construct a canonical self-adjoint operator whose \emph{scattering phase} reproduces the argument of $\Lambda(\tfrac12+it,\varpi)$ (for a family of $\varpi$),
9332: \end{quote}
9333: and only then attempt to identify the zeros as eigenvalues of a derived operator (e.g.\ via a spectral-shift / phase-operator construction).
9334: This is the natural ``physics'' route: scattering data are often easier to tie to a Hamiltonian than raw resonance locations.
9335: 
9336: \subsection{Milestones: what would count as progress toward GRH?}
9337: \label{sec:milestones_GRH}
9338: 
9339: A productive GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya program must have intermediate milestones.
9340: Here are milestones that are \emph{internal} to this volume's framework:
9341: 
9342: \subsubsection*{M0: Rigorous construction of the clock-induced family $\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)$}
9343: Show that for a class of clocked surface orders coming from arithmetic dessins/modular data, one can attach to representation input $\varpi$ (e.g.\ a Dirichlet character, a finite Galois representation, or a controlled automorphic parameter) a holonomy-twist $\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)$ such that:
9344: \begin{itemize}
9345: \item the holonomy map factors through a manageable quotient (e.g.\ a congruence quotient),
9346: \item $\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)$ is unitary (or projectively unitary with controlled central phase),
9347: \item twisting respects direct sums and tensor products at the level of holonomy (a minimal functoriality check).
9348: \end{itemize}
9349: 
9350: \subsubsection*{M1: A twisted trace formula with arithmetic-looking weights}
9351: Derive (or adapt) a Selberg trace formula for the twisted Laplacian $\Delta_{\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)}$.
9352: At minimum, identify the geometric side as a sum over closed geodesics weighted by $\Tr(\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)(\gamma^k))$.
9353: For GRH this is crucial: compare those weights to the expected prime weights (e.g.\ $\chi(p^k)$ or $\Tr(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p^k))$) via a clearly stated dictionary.
9354: 
9355: \subsubsection*{M2: Stability diagnostics via multiparameter persistence over the \emph{family} parameter}
9356: Treat the spectral data as varying over parameters:
9357: \begin{itemize}
9358: \item arithmetic parameter (conductor/level $q$ or representation family index),
9359: \item clock-entanglement parameter (depth, local twist phases),
9360: \item geometric parameter (moduli of the underlying hyperbolic surface).
9361: \end{itemize}
9362: Use multiparameter persistence to identify spectral/topological features that persist across nontrivial regions of parameter space.
9363: GRH demands such stability: arithmetic spectra should not depend delicately on microscopic modeling choices.
9364: 
9365: \subsubsection*{M3: Numerical coincidence tests across \emph{many} twists}
9366: Compute low-lying spectral data (e.g.\ scattering phases or eigenvalues of $H_{1,\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)}$) for a \emph{grid} of twists $\varpi$ and compare to:
9367: \begin{itemize}
9368: \item known low-lying zeros of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$,
9369: \item explicit-formula observables (smoothed prime sums vs smoothed spectral sums),
9370: \item universality statistics (spacing distributions), with care to avoid over-interpretation.
9371: \end{itemize}
9372: A coincidence for one twist is weak evidence; systematic behavior across many twists is a meaningful constraint.
9373: 
9374: \subsection{Concrete computational instructions for a GRH testbed (minimal viable implementation)}
9375: \label{sec:implementation_GRH}
9376: 
9377: This section gives a pragmatic recipe that makes the GRH upgrade explicit.
9378: The goal is not ``prove GRH'' but to build a falsifiable numerical/structural pipeline.
9379: 
9380: \begin{algorithm}[h]
9381: \caption{Minimal GRH-oriented Hilbert--Pólya test pipeline}
9382: \begin{algorithmic}[1]
9383: \Require A twist parameter $\varpi$ (start with primitive Dirichlet $\chi$ modulo $q$), and a choice of arithmetic quotient $\Gamma$ (start with $\Gamma_0(q)$).
9384: \Ensure Numerical spectral data and stability diagnostics comparable to zeros of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$.
9385: \State Build a triangulation/cellulation of $X=\HH/\Gamma$ (handle cusps carefully).
9386: \State Construct an embedded graph/dessin $\Gamma_X\subset X$ and the associated surface order $\Lambda(X,\Gamma_X)$.
9387: \State Choose local clock data (including twisted shifts) consistent with vertex orders; record local central phases.
9388: \State Compute gluing cocycles and time holonomy $\Hol:\pi_1(X)\to \Out(\Lambda)$.
9389: \State Choose a *-representation $\pi$ and form a (projective) unitary twist $\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)$.
9390: \State Assemble either:
9391: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
9392: \item a discretized twisted Laplacian $\Delta_{\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)}$, or
9393: \item a discretized cusp scattering operator $S_{\varpi}(\tfrac12+it)$ on boundary data.
9394: \end{enumerate}
9395: \State Compute spectral summaries (eigenvalues/eigenphases) at moderate resolution.
9396: \State Compute explicit-formula observables: smoothed sums over zeros of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ versus smoothed prime sums (with weights determined by $\varpi$).
9397: \State Run multiparameter persistence over $(q,\text{entanglement},\text{geometry})$ to test stability of detected features.
9398: \end{algorithmic}
9399: \end{algorithm}
9400: 
9401: \begin{remark}[Avoiding self-deception is harder for GRH]
9402: The most dangerous failure mode is cherry-picking: with enough freedom one can fit small spectral lists to almost anything.
9403: GRH mitigates this by demanding \emph{uniformity across twists}.
9404: Multiparameter persistence is useful precisely because it penalizes overfitting: stable features must survive over a region in twist-parameter space, not just at one tuned point.
9405: \end{remark}
9406: 
9407: 
9408: \subsection{Worked example: the clean torus dessin on a hexagon and the full GRH--Hilbert--Pólya pipeline in miniature}
9409: \label{sec:hp_worked_example_torus}
9410: 
9411: This subsection instantiates the abstract program above on a single explicit combinatorial input.
9412: It is deliberately chosen to be as small as possible while still exhibiting all of the mechanisms that matter in the GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya discussion:
9413: \begin{center}
9414: \emph{(permutation data) $\Rightarrow$ (surface) $\Rightarrow$ (surface order) $\Rightarrow$ (twisted local clocks) $\Rightarrow$ (global time holonomy) $\Rightarrow$ (self-adjoint generators) $\Rightarrow$ (multiparameter persistence diagnostics).}
9415: \end{center}
9416: 
9417: \paragraph{Honesty disclaimer.}
9418: This example is not claimed to encode any nontrivial $L$--function zeros.
9419: Its purpose is to show the \emph{complete} computational and conceptual chain on a toy model where every step can be written down explicitly and checked.
9420: In particular, it will serve as a ``unit test'' for implementations before moving to genuinely hyperbolic and arithmetic testbeds (Sections~\ref{sec:why_surfaces_GRH}--\ref{sec:scattering_dirichlet}).
9421: 
9422: \subsubsection{Step 0: the permutation triple and the surface it defines}
9423: 
9424: Let $D=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}$ be the set of \emph{darts} (oriented half-edges).
9425: Consider the permutations
9426: \begin{equation}
9427: \label{eq:torus_dessin_data}
9428: \sigma=(1\,2\,3)(4\,5\,6),\qquad 
9429: \alpha=(1\,4)(2\,5)(3\,6),
9430: \qquad 
9431: \phi=(1\,6\,2\,4\,3\,5)=(\sigma\alpha)^{-1}.
9432: \end{equation}
9433: Here:
9434: \begin{itemize}
9435: \item $\alpha$ is a fixed-point-free involution pairing darts into unoriented edges,
9436: \item $\sigma$ records the cyclic order of darts around vertices,
9437: \item $\phi$ records the cyclic order of darts around faces.
9438: \end{itemize}
9439: 
9440: \begin{lemma}[Consistency]
9441: \label{lem:torus_saph}
9442: The triple \eqref{eq:torus_dessin_data} satisfies the combinatorial-map identity
9443: \[
9444: \sigma\,\alpha\,\phi=1.
9445: \]
9446: \end{lemma}
9447: 
9448: \begin{proof}
9449: By definition $\phi=(\sigma\alpha)^{-1}$.
9450: Equivalently, $\sigma\alpha\phi=1$.
9451: \end{proof}
9452: 
9453: \paragraph{Counting cells.}
9454: Let $c(\pi)$ denote the number of cycles of a permutation $\pi$.
9455: The associated cell structure has:
9456: \[
9457: V=c(\sigma)=2,\qquad E=c(\alpha)=3,\qquad F=c(\phi)=1,
9458: \]
9459: so the Euler characteristic is
9460: \[
9461: \chi = V-E+F = 2-3+1=0.
9462: \]
9463: Since $\chi=2-2g$ for a closed oriented surface, the genus is $g=1$.
9464: 
9465: \begin{example}[A hexagon model (Schreiber's presentation)]
9466: Label the boundary of a counterclockwise oriented hexagon by the word
9467: \[
9468: a\,b\,c\,d\,e\,f \;\longleftrightarrow\; (1\,6\,2\,4\,3\,5),
9469: \]
9470: so that the face permutation $\phi$ is exactly the cyclic order of the boundary darts.
9471: The involution $\alpha=(1,4)(2,5)(3,6)$ prescribes the side identifications
9472: \[
9473: a\leftrightarrow d,\qquad b\leftrightarrow e,\qquad c\leftrightarrow f,
9474: \]
9475: with opposing orientations.
9476: This produces a cellularly embedded graph on $T^2$ as in Figure~\ref{fig:schreiber_torus_hexagon}.
9477: \end{example}
9478: 
9479: \begin{figure}[t]
9480: \centering
9481: % \includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{figures/schreiber_torus_hexagon.png}
9482: \caption{Counterclockwise oriented hexagon model with $\phi=(1\,6\,2\,4\,3\,5)$ and side-pairing $\alpha=(1,4)(2,5)(3,6)$, yielding a torus embedding (adapted from the user-provided excerpt; see \cite{Schreiber2018SurfaceAlgebrasI}).}
9483: \label{fig:schreiber_torus_hexagon}
9484: \end{figure}
9485: 
9486: \subsubsection{Step 1: an explicit cellular chain complex and its homology}
9487: 
9488: Let $X$ be the resulting torus cell complex.
9489: After side identifications, the six polygon vertices collapse to two vertices (denote them $x$ and $y$), and the three side-pairs become three edges connecting $x$ and $y$.
9490: Denote these edges by
9491: \[
9492: e_1=\{1,4\},\qquad e_2=\{2,5\},\qquad e_3=\{3,6\},
9493: \]
9494: and let $f$ be the single 2-cell.
9495: 
9496: The cellular chain groups are
9497: \[
9498: C_2(X;\Z)\cong \Z\langle f\rangle,\qquad C_1(X;\Z)\cong \Z\langle e_1,e_2,e_3\rangle,\qquad C_0(X;\Z)\cong \Z\langle x,y\rangle.
9499: \]
9500: 
9501: Choosing orientations so that each $e_i$ is oriented from $x$ to $y$, we have
9502: \[
9503: \partial_1(e_i)=y-x\qquad (i=1,2,3).
9504: \]
9505: The boundary of $f$ is encoded by the face word
