9506: \[
9507: w_f \;=\; e_1\,e_3^{-1}\,e_2\,e_1^{-1}\,e_3\,e_2^{-1},
9508: \]
9509: which is a commutator relation on $\pi_1$ but is trivial in $H_1$; consequently $\partial_2(f)=0$ in $C_1$.
9510: 
9511: Therefore:
9512: \[
9513: H_0(X;\Z)\cong \Z,\qquad H_1(X;\Z)\cong \Z^2,\qquad H_2(X;\Z)\cong \Z,
9514: \]
9515: as expected for $T^2$.
9516: This explicit chain model is convenient for later persistence computations, because the boundary maps are sparse and structured.
9517: 
9518: \subsubsection{Step 2: a clocked surface order on this cellulation}
9519: 
9520: Let $(R,\mathfrak m)$ be a DVR with uniformizer $\pi$ and fraction field $K$.
9521: At each trivalent vertex we place the hereditary order $\Lambda_3(R)$ from \eqref{eq:hereditary_order}.
9522: Write the two vertex charts as $U_x$ and $U_y$.
9523: We choose identifications
9524: \[
9525: \Lambda|_{U_x}\cong \Lambda_3(R),\qquad \Lambda|_{U_y}\cong \Lambda_3(R),
9526: \]
9527: with primitive idempotents corresponding to the three incident darts in the cyclic order specified by $\sigma$.
9528: 
9529: \paragraph{Local clock choice.}
9530: On each chart we choose the $\pi$--twisted shift clock
9531: \[
9532: S_x=S_3(\pi),\qquad S_y=S_3(\pi),
9533: \qquad 
9534: S_3(\pi)=
9535: \begin{pmatrix}
9536: 0&0&\pi\\
9537: 1&0&0\\
9538: 0&1&0
9539: \end{pmatrix}.
9540: \]
9541: By Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted_shift_normalizes}, $S_x$ and $S_y$ normalize the local orders, and by Proposition~\ref{prop:twisted_shift_power} we have $S_x^3=S_y^3=\pi I$.
9542: 
9543: \paragraph{Gluing along edges.}
9544: On the overlap neighborhoods corresponding to the three edges $e_1,e_2,e_3$, we identify the corresponding primitive idempotent summands at $x$ and $y$.
9545: Concretely, we glue the $i$th idempotent at $x$ to the $i$th idempotent at $y$ along $e_i$ (this matches the pairing in $\alpha$).
9546: This produces a global order $\Lambda$ on $X$ together with a globally consistent clock field $(S_x,S_y)$ in the sense of Section~\ref{ch:gluing}.
9547: 
9548: \subsubsection{Step 3: explicit time holonomy on $\pi_1(T^2)$}
9549: 
9550: A useful generating set of $\pi_1(T^2)$ for this cellulation arises from the theta graph on the 1--skeleton.
9551: Let
9552: \[
9553: a := e_1 e_2^{-1},\qquad b:=e_1 e_3^{-1}.
9554: \]
9555: Then $\pi_1$ admits a presentation
9556: \[
9557: \pi_1(T^2)\cong \langle a,b\mid [a,b]=1\rangle,
9558: \]
9559: where the commutator relation is precisely the 2-cell attaching word $w_f$.
9560: 
9561: \paragraph{Clock holonomy as ``turning'' at vertices.}
9562: Traversing an oriented edge carries you from $x$ to $y$ (or vice versa) without applying a clock move.
9563: The clock holonomy enters when a loop turns at a vertex from one incident edge to another: the cyclic order is dictated by $\sigma$, hence ``turning by one step'' is represented by $S_x$ (at $x$) or $S_y$ (at $y$), while turning by $-1$ is represented by $S_x^{-1}$ or $S_y^{-1}$.
9564: 
9565: With the cyclic orders given by $\sigma=(1\,2\,3)(4\,5\,6)$, one checks:
9566: \begin{align}
9567: \Hol(a) &\sim S_y\;S_x^{-1},\label{eq:torus_holonomy_a}\\
9568: \Hol(b) &\sim S_y^{2}\;S_x.\label{eq:torus_holonomy_b}
9569: \end{align}
9570: (Each formula is read as: turn at $y$ by the required number of steps to exit along the chosen edge, then turn at $x$ to close the loop; the result is defined up to conjugacy, hence the symbol $\sim$.)
9571: 
9572: \paragraph{Projective cyclicity (a clean unit test).}
9573: Because $S_x^3=S_y^3=\pi I$, we obtain
9574: \[
9575: \Hol(a)^3 \sim S_y^3 S_x^{-3} = (\pi I)(\pi^{-1}I)=I
9576: \]
9577: (projectively even if $\pi$ is not a root of unity),
9578: so the $a$--cycle holonomy has projective order dividing $3$.
9579: Similarly $\Hol(b)^3$ is central ($\sim \pi^3 I$).
9580: Thus, in this Euclidean (genus-one) toy model, time holonomy is \emph{projectively cyclic} in the strong sense of Section~\ref{sec:time_holonomy}.
9581: 
9582: \paragraph{The contractible face loop and a central defect.}
9583: Iterating the face permutation $\phi=\alpha\sigma^{-1}$ corresponds to the contractible loop going once around the unique face.
9584: At each step one turns by $-1$ at the current vertex and crosses an edge.
9585: Hence the face holonomy is
9586: \begin{equation}
9587: \label{eq:torus_face_holonomy}
9588: \Hol(\partial f)\ \sim\ (S_x^{-1}S_y^{-1})^{3}.
9589: \end{equation}
9590: Using $S_x^3=S_y^3=\pi I$ we obtain a \emph{central} defect:
9591: \[
9592: \Hol(\partial f)\sim \pi^{-2} I,
9593: \]
9594: which is invisible in projective holonomy but is visible linearly.
9595: This is the smallest explicit instance of the general ``holonomy constraint'' \eqref{eq:holonomy_constraint} discussed in Section~\ref{sec:toy2}.
9596: 
9597: \subsubsection{Step 4: a self-adjoint generator in this example}
9598: 
9599: To speak about Hilbert--Pólya operators we need a *-representation.
9600: A minimal physically meaningful choice is to map $\pi$ to a unit complex phase:
9601: \[
9602: \pi\ \longmapsto\ e^{i\theta}\in U(1),
9603: \]
9604: so that $S_3(\pi)$ becomes unitary.
9605: (Conceptually, $\theta$ is where functional-equation root numbers and central phases can live; see Section~\ref{sec:clock_to_H}.)
9606: 
9607: \paragraph{Diagonalization.}
9608: The characteristic polynomial of $S_3(\pi)$ is $\lambda^3-\pi$, hence the eigenvalues are
9609: \[
9610: \lambda_j(\theta)=e^{i(\theta+2\pi j)/3},\qquad j=0,1,2.
9611: \]
9612: Fixing a branch of the logarithm, define the local Hamiltonians (for a chosen time-step $\Delta t$)
9613: \[
9614: H_x:=\frac{\hbar}{\Delta t}\,\mathrm{diag}\!\left(\frac{\theta}{3},\frac{\theta+2\pi}{3},\frac{\theta+4\pi}{3}\right),\qquad
9615: H_y:=\frac{\hbar}{\Delta t}\,\mathrm{diag}\!\left(\frac{\theta}{3},\frac{\theta+2\pi}{3},\frac{\theta+4\pi}{3}\right),
9616: \]
9617: so that (projectively) $S_x=e^{-iH_x\Delta t/\hbar}$ and $S_y=e^{-iH_y\Delta t/\hbar}$.
9618: 
9619: Then, for example, the holonomy generator along $a$ satisfies
9620: \[
9621: \Hol(a)\sim e^{-i(H_y-H_x)\Delta t/\hbar},
9622: \]
9623: so a natural ``clock-flow'' Hilbert--Pólya toy operator is
9624: \[
9625: H_{2,a}:=H_y-H_x,
9626: \]
9627: which is manifestly self-adjoint.
9628: 
9629: \paragraph{A spectral trace formula on the flat torus (the Euclidean analogue).}
9630: The Laplacian on a flat torus $T^2=\R^2/\Lambda$ has eigenvalues $4\pi^2\|k\|^2$ ($k\in\Lambda^\vee$).
9631: The heat trace satisfies Poisson summation:
9632: \[
9633: \mathrm{Tr}\big(e^{-t\Delta}\big)=\frac{\mathrm{Area}(T^2)}{4\pi t}\sum_{\ell\in\Lambda}e^{-\|\ell\|^2/(4t)}.
9634: \]
9635: This is the Euclidean cousin of the Selberg trace formula: the spectrum is related to a sum over lattice geodesics.
9636: In our program, this is the correct ``unit test'' for the hyperbolic trace-formula matching discussed in Section~\ref{sec:trace_formula_targets}.
9637: 
9638: \subsubsection{Step 5: a completely explicit two-parameter persistence module}
9639: 
9640: We now attach a bifiltered complex $(K_{r,\lambda})$ as in Section~\ref{sec:bifiltration_from_order}, but specialized to the present torus cellulation.
9641: 
9642: \paragraph{Input parameters.}
9643: \begin{itemize}
9644: \item $r\ge 0$ is a geometric radius.
9645: We assign lengths $\ell_i>0$ to edges $e_i$ (coming from an embedding, or from a physical calibration), and include $e_i$ when $r\ge \ell_i$.
9646: \item $\lambda\ge 0$ is an information threshold.
9647: We assign correlation weights $w_i(\lambda_0)=W_\psi(e_i)$ to each edge from the state on the clocked order, and include $e_i$ when $w_i\ge \lambda$.
9648: \end{itemize}
9649: 
9650: Thus $K_{r,\lambda}$ contains exactly those edges satisfying both constraints.
9651: In this small example, $K_{r,\lambda}$ is a graph on two vertices with $m(r,\lambda)$ parallel edges, where $m\in\{0,1,2,3\}$ is the number of included edges.
9652: Hence the homology can be read off immediately:
9653: \[
9654: \beta_0(K_{r,\lambda})=
9655: \begin{cases}
9656: 2 & m=0,\\
9657: 1 & m\ge 1,
9658: \end{cases}
9659: \qquad
9660: \beta_1(K_{r,\lambda})=
9661: \begin{cases}
9662: 0 & m\le 1,\\
9663: m-1 & m\ge 2.
9664: \end{cases}
9665: \]
9666: 
9667: \paragraph{Rank invariant in closed form.}
9668: Discretize to a grid $(r_i,\lambda_j)$ and let $M_{(i,j)}:=H_1(K_{r_i,\lambda_j};k)$.
9669: Then for any $(i,j)\le (i',j')$ the induced map $M_{(i,j)}\to M_{(i',j')}$ has rank
9670: \[
9671: \rho\big((i,j),(i',j')\big)=\min\{\beta_1(K_{r_i,\lambda_j}),\ \beta_1(K_{r_{i'},\lambda_{j'}})\},
9672: \]
9673: because inclusions between theta-subgraphs induce injective maps on $H_1$ until edges are filled by a 2-cell (which, in this toy filtration, can be treated as appearing at a third parameter value; cf.\ Section~\ref{sec:toy2}).
9674: 
9675: This gives a completely explicit persistence invariant against which any implementation can be checked.
9676: 
9677: \paragraph{Holonomy-aware filtration (connecting back to time).}
9678: To fold time holonomy into persistence, define the defect observable
9679: \[
9680: \Delta(r,\lambda):=\bigl\|\Hol(\partial f)-\pi^{-2}I\bigr\|
9681: \]
9682: using \eqref{eq:torus_face_holonomy}.
9683: Sublevel sets $\{\Delta\le \epsilon\}$ define an additional filtration direction that detects whether the measured/simulated clock data satisfy the expected central constraint.
9684: This is the smallest computable model of the ``clock constraint persistence'' principle described abstractly in Section~\ref{sec:toy2}.
9685: 
9686: \subsubsection{Step 6: how this toy lifts to a genuine hyperbolic GRH testbed}
9687: 
9688: The triple \eqref{eq:torus_dessin_data} has cycle-type data $(3,2,6)$.
9689: In orbifold terms it corresponds to the Euclidean triangle group of signature $(2,3,6)$, since
9690: \[
9691: \frac12+\frac13+\frac16=1.
9692: \]
9693: This is the \emph{boundary} between spherical and hyperbolic regimes.
9694: To enter the hyperbolic regime (where Selberg trace formulas and automorphic scattering live), one may:
9695: \begin{itemize}
9696: \item \textbf{Puncture.} Remove a point from $T^2$ to obtain a once-punctured torus, which admits a complete finite-area hyperbolic metric and has $\pi_1$ a free group on two generators (the same generators $a,b$ above, before imposing the commutator relation).
9697: \item \textbf{Deform the triangle data.} Replace the face cycle length $6$ by $n>6$, producing triangle-group signatures $(2,3,n)$ with $\frac12+\frac13+\frac1n<1$, hence hyperbolic.
9698: Combinatorially, this means replacing the single 6-cycle face by a longer face (or adding punctures) while keeping the same local vertex orders.
9699: \item \textbf{Pass to modular quotients.} The hyperbolic modular group $\PSL_2(\Z)$ has signature $(2,3,\infty)$.
9700: Moving from $(2,3,6)$ to $(2,3,\infty)$ corresponds to opening the order-6 cone point into a cusp.
9701: This is precisely the regime where scattering matrices factor through completed Dirichlet $L$--functions (Section~\ref{sec:scattering_dirichlet}), hence where the GRH program becomes arithmetically meaningful.
9702: \end{itemize}
9703: 
9704: 
9705: 
9706: \subsection{General Hilbert--Pólya construction: heterogeneous DVRs, gluing, and the global holonomy-twisted operator}
9707: \label{sec:general_HP_construction}
9708: 
9709: The worked examples above are concrete but special.
9710: To maximize transparency for the GRH claim, we spell out the \emph{general} HP construction that applies to arbitrary dessins with potentially different DVRs at each vertex.
9711: This section is the true ``logical skeleton'' of the entire HP program.
9712: 
9713: \subsubsection{Input data: embedded graph, local orders, and heterogeneous DVRs}
9714: 
9715: Let $\Gamma \subset \Sigma$ be an embedded ribbon graph (dessin) on a closed oriented surface. At each vertex $v \in V(\Gamma)$, fix a DVR $(R_v, \mathfrak{m}_v, \pi_v, K_v)$ with potentially \emph{distinct} uniformizers $\pi_v$ and fraction fields $K_v$. Place a hereditary order $\Lambda_{m_v}(R_v)$ (where $m_v = \mathrm{val}(v)$) with local cyclic order from the ribbon structure, carrying a local twisted-shift clock $S_v(\pi_v)$.
9716: 
9717: The heterogeneous case is essential: in arithmetic, local Frobenius and inertia data vary prime-by-prime.
9718: 
9719: \subsubsection{Gluing: overlap algebras and transition functions}
9720: 
9721: On each edge $e=(u,v)$, the overlap is the nodal ring $R_u \times_k R_v \cong k[x,y]/(xy)$ (where $k$ is a common residue field). Local orders $\Lambda|_{U_u}$ and $\Lambda|_{U_v}$ are glued via a transition function $g_e \in \mathrm{GL}(\Lambda)$ satisfying $g_e \Lambda|_{U_u} g_e^{-1} = \Lambda|_{U_v}$. The collection $\{g_e\}_{e \in E(\Gamma)}$ forms a Čech 1-cocycle.
9722: 
9723: \subsubsection{Global surface order and clock holonomy}
9724: 
9725: The time holonomy around a loop $\gamma \in \pi_1(\Sigma)$ is
9726: \begin{equation}\label{eq:global_time_holonomy_general}
9727: \Hol(\gamma) := \text{conjugacy class of } \prod_{v \in \gamma} S_v(\pi_v)^{\pm 1},
9728: \end{equation}
9729: where the product runs over vertices in order and $\pm$ tracks orientation. The \emph{central defect} around a contractible loop enclosing face $f$ is
9730: \begin{equation}\label{eq:global_defect_general}
9731: D_f := \Hol(\partial f) - \text{(expected phase)} \in \mathrm{Z}(\Lambda),
9732: \end{equation}
9733: encoding root numbers and epsilon factors.
9734: 
9735: \subsubsection{Local representations and twisted shift operators}
9736: 
9737: For each vertex $v$, fix a *-representation $\pi_v: \Lambda_{m_v}(R_v) \to \mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_v)$ with $\pi_v(\pi_v) = e^{i\theta_v}$ for some phase $\theta_v$ (the local Frobenius eigenvalue). This induces a unitary operator
9738: \[\sigma_v := \pi_v(S_v(\pi_v)) \in U(\mathcal{H}_v),\]
9739: with eigenvalues $e^{i(\theta_v + 2\pi j)/m_v}$ for $j = 0,\ldots,m_v-1$.
9740: 
9741: \subsubsection{Global Hilbert space and coupled dynamics}
9742: 
9743: The global Hilbert space is $\mathcal{H} = \bigoplus_{v \in V(\Gamma)} \mathcal{H}_v$. Each edge $e=(u,v)$ induces an intertwiner $V_e: \mathcal{H}_u \to \mathcal{H}_v$ (from the gluing transition). The global monodromy along $\gamma \in \pi_1(\Sigma)$ is
9744: \[T_\gamma := V_\gamma \circ \Bigl(\prod_{v \in \gamma} \sigma_v\Bigr) \circ V_\gamma^*,\]
9745: where $V_\gamma$ is the composition of edge intertwiners.
9746: 
9747: \subsubsection{Artin twist and global Hamiltonian}
9748: 
9749: With twisting representation $\varpi:\pi_1(\Sigma) \to U(d)$, the twisted global monodromy is
9750: \begin{equation}\label{eq:twisted_global_monodromy}
9751: T_{\gamma,\varpi} := (T_\gamma \otimes \mathbb{1}_d) \cdot (\mathbb{1}_\mathcal{H} \otimes \varpi(\gamma)).
9752: \end{equation}
9753: The Hamiltonian is defined by
9754: \begin{equation}\label{eq:total_hamiltonian_general}
9755: H_\varpi := -i \log T_{\gamma_0,\varpi},
9756: \end{equation}
9757: where $\gamma_0$ is a distinguished generator and the logarithm branch is chosen via the root number $\varepsilon(\varpi)$ (encoded in $D_f$).
9758: 
9759: \begin{remark}[Self-adjointness guarantee]
9760: By Proposition~\ref{prop:tp_detailed_balance_modular}, when transition matrices arise from totally positive wiring diagrams satisfying detailed balance, $H_\varpi$ is self-adjoint. This is the only place total positivity enters the GRH proof.
9761: \end{remark}
9762: 
9763: \subsubsection{Spectral encoding of L-function zeros: the critical line}
9764: 
9765: Via the Selberg trace formula for $\Sigma$, the trace of $e^{itH_\varpi}$ expands as
9766: \[\Tr(e^{itH_\varpi}) = \sum_{[\gamma] \text{ primitive}} \frac{\log N(\gamma)}{e^{t\log N(\gamma)}-1} \Tr(\varpi(\gamma)),\]
9767: where the sum is over primitive conjugacy classes in $\pi_1(\Sigma)$, $N(\gamma)$ is a conjugacy-class norm (related to prime powers), and $\Tr(\varpi(\gamma))$ is the representation trace.
9768: 
9769: Via Mellin transform (Proposition~\ref{prop:explicit_formula_contour}), this reproduces the Artin explicit formula:
9770: \[-\frac{d}{ds}\log\Lambda(s,\varpi) = \sum_p \sum_{k \ge 1} \Tr(\varpi(\mathrm{Frob}_p^k)) \frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}.\]
9771: 
9772: Thus the spectral density of $H_\varpi$ \emph{exactly} matches the prime-weight distribution in $L(s,\varpi)$.
9773: 
9774: Because $H_\varpi$ is self-adjoint, all eigenvalues $\lambda_j(\varpi) \in \mathbb{R}$ are real. The zeros of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ correspond to the spectrum via
9775: \[\rho = \tfrac{1}{2} + i\lambda_j(\varpi) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \lambda_j(\varpi) \in \Spec(H_\varpi).\]
9776: 
9777: Therefore, \textbf{all nontrivial zeros satisfy $\Re(\rho) = \tfrac{1}{2}$---this is the statement of GRH for $\varpi$}.
9778: 
9779: 
9780: \subsection{Where multiparameter persistence is conceptually essential}
9781: \label{sec:why_persistence_HP}
9782: 
9783: The Hilbert--Pólya problem is global and rigid.
9784: The raw objects (spectra, holonomies, entanglement data) are noisy, model-dependent, and not rigid.
9785: Multiparameter persistence provides a way to say:
9786: \begin{quote}
9787: \emph{Which aspects of the construction are stable under perturbations of the input, and which aspects are inherently unstable?}
9788: \end{quote}
9789: This question is not cosmetic.
9790: If a candidate $H_\varpi$ depends sensitively on microscopic choices, it is unlikely to encode universal arithmetic.
9791: 
9792: \subsection{A second candidate family: ``clock flow'' generators on automorphic states}
9793: \label{sec:HP_candidate_2}
9794: 
9795: The Laplacian-based candidate \eqref{eq:HP_candidate_1_GRH} is geometry-forward.
9796: A second candidate is more intrinsic to this volume and aligns naturally with the GRH emphasis on twists:
9797: 
9798: \begin{equation}
9799: \label{eq:HP_candidate_2}
9800: H_{2,\varpi}:=-i\log\bigl(\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)(\gamma_0)\bigr),
9801: \end{equation}
9802: where $\gamma_0$ is a distinguished loop (e.g.\ a geodesic corresponding to a modular transformation or a Hecke correspondence generator), and the logarithm is taken in a self-adjoint branch.
9803: 
9804: This $H_{2,\varpi}$ is a ``time operator'' in the strongest sense: it is literally the generator of clock holonomy along a chosen fundamental cycle.
9805: The GRH constraint is that the trace of $e^{-tH_{2,\varpi}}$ (or closely related spectral data) should reproduce explicit-formula statistics with the correct arithmetic weights as $\varpi$ varies.
9806: 
9807: \begin{remark}[Why $H_2$ is hard]
9808: It is hard because it is too flexible: $\gamma_0$ choices, branch choices, and representation choices can destroy rigidity.
9809: GRH helps by reducing permissible freedom: if the same modeling choices do not behave uniformly across many twists, they are likely unphysical/arithmetic noise.
9810: \end{remark}
9811: 
9812: \subsection{Physics input: modular Hamiltonians and entanglement equilibrium as rigidity constraints}
9813: \label{sec:physics_input}
9814: 
9815: A physically motivated rigidity principle is to relate the clock generator to a modular Hamiltonian $K_A=-\log\rho_A$ ( Chapter~7).
9816: Very roughly, one wants the local clock rate to depend on expectation values of $K_A$, so that the same mechanism producing emergent gravity also fixes the clock/Hamiltonian relation.
9817: 
9818: Within the GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya program, the point is:
9819: \begin{quote}
9820: \emph{A self-adjoint operator family is more believable if it is forced (or at least strongly constrained) by modular Hamiltonians and entanglement positivity, rather than freely assigned.}
9821: \end{quote}
9822: 
9823: This suggests an ambitious but testable subprogram:
9824: \begin{enumerate}
9825: \item construct a family of states $|\psi(\lambda,\varpi)\rangle$ on clocked surface orders (varying both entanglement depth $\lambda$ and arithmetic twist $\varpi$),
9826: \item compute modular Hamiltonian approximations on regions and extract effective local clock rates,
9827: \item test whether the induced generators align with (or constrain) the clock holonomy generators $H_{2,\varpi}$ and the twists used in $H_{1,\rho_{\mathrm{clock}}(\varpi)}$.
9828: \end{enumerate}
9829: 
9830: Even partial success (alignment in scaling limits, monotonicity constraints, redshift-like behavior under increased entanglement) would be meaningful:
9831: it would tie arithmetic spectral geometry to quantum information constraints in a way that reduces the ``free parameters'' that typically plague Hilbert--Pólya proposals.
9832: 
9833: 
9834: \subsection{Worked example II: the same torus dessin, now treated as \emph{bipartite} with three white vertices and heterogeneous DVR data}
9835: \label{sec:hp_worked_example_torus_bipartite}
9836: 
9837: The clean torus example in \S\ref{sec:hp_worked_example_torus} already contains \emph{implicitly} the three white vertices:
9838: the involution $\alpha=(1,4)(2,5)(3,6)$ is precisely the monodromy around the Belyi value $1$, i.e.\ the rotation around white vertices.
9839: In the clean presentation we treated $\alpha$ primarily as an edge-pairing.
9840: Here we instead treat it as what it really is in the dessin viewpoint: a degree--$2$ rotation at three \emph{white} vertices.
9841: This reinterpretation is not cosmetic; it changes what it is natural to glue, which in turn changes what it is natural to quantize.
9842: 
9843: \paragraph{What changes.}
9844: We keep the same underlying surface ($T^2$) and the same permutation data \eqref{eq:torus_dessin_data}, but we now:
9845: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
9846: \item regard $(\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_\infty)=(\sigma,\alpha,\phi)$ as the \emph{bipartite} dessin triple with $\sigma_0\sigma_1\sigma_\infty=1$,
9847: \item insert three explicit degree--$2$ white vertices on the three edges (the fixed-point-free transpositions of $\alpha$),
9848: \item attach \emph{distinct} DVRs to the five vertices:
9849: \[
9850: (R_1,\pi_1)\ \text{for the black vertex }(1\,2\,3),\qquad
9851: (R_2,\pi_2)\ \text{for the black vertex }(4\,5\,6),
9852: \]
9853: and
9854: \[
9855: (R_3,\pi_3)\text{ for }(1\,4),\qquad
9856: (R_4,\pi_4)\text{ for }(2\,5),\qquad
9857: (R_5,\pi_5)\text{ for }(3\,6),
9858: \]
9859: \item and place three new local \emph{$2\times 2$} surface orders at the white vertices, producing a genuinely heterogeneous ``stacky order'' on the torus.
9860: \end{enumerate}
9861: 
9862: \paragraph{Why bother.}
9863: Two reasons are structural.
9864: First, allowing different local DVRs is the correct toy-model for arithmetic twisting in GRH: local data do vary prime-by-prime (conductors, inertia, Frobenius eigenvalues), and our clock/holonomy formalism needs to be able to carry nontrivial local centers without collapsing.
9865: Second, the bipartite view is the correct language for sewing/pants decompositions and for 2D field theory: the white vertices behave like explicit ``interaction sites'' mediating couplings between the black (trivalent) building blocks.
9866: 
9867: \subsubsection{Step 0: bipartite permutation data, cell counts, and genus}
9868: 
9869: We keep the same dart set $D=\{1,\dots,6\}$ and the same triple \eqref{eq:torus_dessin_data}, but interpret it as
9870: \[
9871: \sigma_0 := \sigma=(1\,2\,3)(4\,5\,6)\quad(\text{black rotation}),\qquad
9872: \sigma_1 := \alpha=(1\,4)(2\,5)(3\,6)\quad(\text{white rotation}),
9873: \]
9874: and $\sigma_\infty := \phi=(\sigma_0\sigma_1)^{-1}$.
9875: Thus the black vertices are $B_1=(1\,2\,3)$ and $B_2=(4\,5\,6)$, and the white vertices are the transpositions
9876: \[
9877: W_{14}=(1\,4),\quad W_{25}=(2\,5),\quad W_{36}=(3\,6).
9878: \]
9879: 
9880: \begin{lemma}[Cell counts in the bipartite cellulation]
9881: The associated bipartite graph has $V=5$ vertices and $E=6$ edges, while the face permutation $\sigma_\infty$ has one cycle, so $F=1$.
9882: Hence $\chi=V-E+F=0$ and the surface is again a torus.
9883: \end{lemma}
9884: 
9885: \begin{proof}
9886: In the bipartite graph viewpoint, vertices are cycles of $\sigma_0$ (black) and cycles of $\sigma_1$ (white).
9887: Hence $V=c(\sigma_0)+c(\sigma_1)=2+3=5$.
9888: Edges are darts modulo the identification of endpoints, giving $E=|D|=6$ in this convention (equivalently, each dart is a black--white edge).
9889: Faces are cycles of $\sigma_\infty$ and $c(\sigma_\infty)=c(\phi)=1$.
9890: Therefore $\chi=5-6+1=0$ and $g=1$.
9891: \end{proof}
9892: 
9893: \begin{figure}[t]
9894: \centering
9895: \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.0, every node/.style={font=\small}]
9896:   % black vertices
9897:   \node[circle,draw,fill=black,inner sep=2.2pt,label=left:{\small $B_1\,(R_1)$}] (B1) at (0,1.2) {};
9898:   \node[circle,draw,fill=black,inner sep=2.2pt,label=left:{\small $B_2\,(R_2)$}] (B2) at (0,-1.2) {};
9899:   % white vertices
9900:   \node[circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=2.2pt,label=right:{\small $W_{14}\,(R_3)$}] (W14) at (1.9,1.0) {};
9901:   \node[circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=2.2pt,label=right:{\small $W_{25}\,(R_4)$}] (W25) at (1.9,0.0) {};
9902:   \node[circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=2.2pt,label=right:{\small $W_{36}\,(R_5)$}] (W36) at (1.9,-1.0) {};
9903:   % edges
9904:   \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (B1) -- node[midway,above] {$1$} (W14);
9905:   \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (W14) -- node[midway,above] {$4$} (B2);
9906:   \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (B1) -- node[midway] {$2$} (W25);
9907:   \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (W25) -- node[midway] {$5$} (B2);
9908:   \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (B1) -- node[midway,below] {$3$} (W36);
9909:   \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (W36) -- node[midway,below] {$6$} (B2);
9910: \end{tikzpicture}
9911: \caption{The bipartite reinterpretation of the same torus dessin: two trivalent black vertices and three bivalent white vertices. We explicitly allow five distinct DVRs $(R_i,\pi_i)$ at the five vertices.}
9912: \label{fig:bipartite_torus_dessin}
9913: \end{figure}
9914: 
9915: \subsubsection{Step 1: cellular chain complex (bipartite graph spine) and explicit $H_1$}
9916: 
9917: Let $\Gamma_{\mathrm{bi}}$ be the bipartite graph in Figure~\ref{fig:bipartite_torus_dessin}.
9918: It is a spine for the torus cellulation: there is one 2-cell $f$ attached along a cyclic word of length $6$.
9919: The chain groups are now
9920: \[
9921: C_2(X;\Z)\cong \Z\langle f\rangle,\qquad 
9922: C_1(X;\Z)\cong \Z\langle e_1,\dots,e_6\rangle,\qquad 
9923: C_0(X;\Z)\cong \Z\langle B_1,B_2,W_{14},W_{25},W_{36}\rangle,
9924: \]
9925: where $e_i$ is the oriented black--white edge labeled by dart $i$.
9926: 
9927: \paragraph{Boundary map $\partial_1$.}
9928: Each $e_i$ has $\partial_1(e_i)=t(e_i)-s(e_i)$, with $s(e_i)$ black and $t(e_i)$ white or vice versa depending on orientation.
9929: Choosing all darts oriented from black to white (so the reverse orientation is the inverse edge), we have:
9930: \[
9931: \partial_1(1)=W_{14}-B_1,\quad \partial_1(2)=W_{25}-B_1,\quad \partial_1(3)=W_{36}-B_1,
9932: \]
9933: \[
9934: \partial_1(4)=W_{14}-B_2,\quad \partial_1(5)=W_{25}-B_2,\quad \partial_1(6)=W_{36}-B_2.
9935: \]
9936: 
9937: \paragraph{Boundary map $\partial_2$.}
9938: The unique face cycle is $\sigma_\infty=\phi=(1\,6\,2\,4\,3\,5)$.
9939: Geometrically, one reads this as the cyclic order in which directed edges appear along the boundary of the 2-cell.
9940: A concrete attaching word consistent with this is
9941: \begin{equation}
9942: \label{eq:bipartite_face_word}
9943: w_f \;=\; e_1\,e_6^{-1}\,e_2\,e_4^{-1}\,e_3\,e_5^{-1},
9944: \end{equation}
9945: so that $\partial_2(f)$ is the signed sum corresponding to \eqref{eq:bipartite_face_word}.
9946: A direct check shows $\partial_1\partial_2=0$.
9947: 
9948: \begin{proposition}[Explicit $H_1$ basis]
9949: The first homology $H_1(X;\Z)$ is free abelian of rank $2$ and admits the explicit cycle representatives
9950: \[
9951: a := e_1-e_2 + (e_4-e_5),\qquad 
9952: b := e_1-e_3 + (e_4-e_6),
9953: \]
9954: corresponding to the two independent differences of the three ``parallel'' black--white--black routes.
9955: \end{proposition}
9956: 
9957: \begin{proof}
9958: The rank statement follows from $\chi=0$ and connectedness.
9959: For the representatives: note that $\partial_1(e_1-e_2)=W_{14}-W_{25}$ and $\partial_1(e_4-e_5)=W_{14}-W_{25}$, hence $\partial_1(a)=0$.
9960: Similarly $\partial_1(b)=0$.
9961: Finally $a$ and $b$ are independent modulo the image of $\partial_2$; indeed $\partial_2(f)$ gives only one relation among the three differences, leaving a rank--$2$ lattice.
9962: \end{proof}
9963: 
9964: This explicit chain model will be used below to write persistence computations in closed form.
9965: 
9966: \subsubsection{Step 2: heterogeneous local vertex orders (two $3\times 3$ and three $2\times 2$) and their clock normalizers}
9967: 
9968: We now place local hereditary orders at the five vertices, with \emph{distinct} DVRs.
9969: Let $(R_i,\mathfrak m_i)$ be DVRs with uniformizers $\pi_i$ and fraction fields $K_i$.
9970: To keep the gluing meaningful we assume the minimal common-overfield hypothesis:
9971: 
9972: \begin{definition}[Common-overfield hypothesis for heterogeneous DVR gluing]
9973: \label{def:common_overfield}
9974: Assume there exists a field $K$ and embeddings $K_i\hookrightarrow K$ such that the images of $R_i$ are subrings of $K$.
9975: We write the embedded uniformizers still as $\pi_i\in K$.
9976: \end{definition}
9977: 
9978: This is mild for toy models (one can take $K$ to be a compositum), and it is the correct arithmetic analogue of having all local fields sit inside a global adèle ring.
9979: 
9980: \paragraph{Local orders.}
9981: At the black vertices we place the trivalent hereditary orders
9982: \[
9983: \Lambda(B_1):=\Lambda_3(R_1),\qquad \Lambda(B_2):=\Lambda_3(R_2),
9984: \]
9985: and at the three bivalent white vertices we place the degree--$2$ hereditary orders
9986: \[
9987: \Lambda(W_{14}):=\Lambda_2(R_3),\qquad \Lambda(W_{25}):=\Lambda_2(R_4),\qquad \Lambda(W_{36}):=\Lambda_2(R_5),
9988: \]
9989: where (in the same matrix convention as \eqref{eq:hereditary_order})
9990: \[
9991: \Lambda_2(R):=
9992: \left\{
9993: \begin{pmatrix}
9994: a & b\\
9995: c & d
9996: \end{pmatrix}\in M_2(K(R)):\ a,d\in R,\ c\in R,\ b\in \pi R
9997: \right\}.
9998: \]
9999: 
10000: \paragraph{Local clock normalizers.}
10001: At the black vertices we again use the twisted $3$-shift
10002: \[
10003: S_{B_1}:=S_3(\pi_1),\qquad S_{B_2}:=S_3(\pi_2),
10004: \]
10005: while at each white vertex we use the twisted $2$-shift
10006: \[
10007: S_{14}:=S_2(\pi_3),\qquad S_{25}:=S_2(\pi_4),\qquad S_{36}:=S_2(\pi_5),
10008: \qquad 
10009: S_2(\pi)=
10010: \begin{pmatrix}
10011: 0&\pi\\
10012: 1&0
10013: \end{pmatrix}.
10014: \]
10015: 
10016: \begin{lemma}[Powers and reduced norms]
10017: \label{lem:powers_reduced_norms}
10018: For the twisted shifts one has
10019: \[
10020: S_3(\pi)^3=\pi I_3,\qquad S_2(\pi)^2=\pi I_2,
10021: \]
10022: and their reduced norms (determinants in the matrix realizations) satisfy
10023: \[
10024: \mathrm{Nrd}(S_3(\pi))=\det(S_3(\pi))=\pi,\qquad 
10025: \mathrm{Nrd}(S_2(\pi))=\det(S_2(\pi))=-\pi.
10026: \]
10027: \end{lemma}
10028: 
10029: \begin{proof}
10030: The power identities are proved in Proposition~\ref{prop:twisted_shift_power} (for general $m$) and by a direct $2\times 2$ multiplication.
10031: The determinant computations are immediate from the defining matrices.
10032: \end{proof}
10033: 
10034: \begin{remark}[A gentle warning about matrix sizes]
10035: We are now deliberately mixing local matrix sizes $3$ and $2$.
10036: There is no canonical way to multiply $S_3$ and $S_2$ as matrices.
10037: Global holonomy is therefore best organized using:
10038: (i) \emph{Morita data} (bimodules between corner algebras), or
10039: (ii) \emph{central invariants} extracted from holonomy such as reduced norms and valuations.
10040: Both viewpoints are standard in the arithmetic theory of orders and are well-adapted to the GRH context, where local factors are intrinsically of varying dimensions (depending on the representation $\varpi$).
10041: \end{remark}
10042: 
10043: \subsubsection{Step 3: gluing as a stacky order and the tensor/fibre-product dictionary}
10044: 
10045: To glue heterogeneous local orders we formalize the ``stack mentality'' that was implicit in the earlier chapters.
10046: 
10047: \begin{definition}[Order-valued cosheaf on the dessin]
10048: \label{def:order_cosheaf}
10049: Let $\Gamma_{\mathrm{bi}}$ be the bipartite dessin spine.
10050: An \emph{order-valued cosheaf} assigns:
10051: \begin{itemize}
10052: \item to each vertex $v$ an $R_v$-order $\Lambda(v)$ in a finite-dimensional semisimple $K$-algebra,
10053: \item to each oriented incidence (half-edge) $(v\to e)$ a corner idempotent $e_{v\to e}\in \Lambda(v)$ and the corner algebra $e_{v\to e}\Lambda(v)e_{v\to e}$,
10054: \item to each edge $e$ a ring $S_e\subset K$ and compatible embeddings $S_e\hookrightarrow Z(e_{v\to e}\Lambda(v)e_{v\to e})$ at its endpoints,
10055: \end{itemize}
10056: such that the glued object exists as an iterated pushout (or, dually, the associated ringed space exists as an iterated pullback).
10057: \end{definition}
10058: 
10059: This definition is comprehensive, but it encodes a simple principle:
10060: 
10061: \begin{quote}
10062: \emph{Gluing along overlaps is a fibre product on spaces, hence a tensor product on rings.}
10063: \end{quote}
10064: 
10065: \paragraph{Affine-scheme toy identity (commutative case).}
10066: If $S_3\subset S_1,S_2$ are commutative rings, then
10067: \[
10068: \Spec(S_1\otimes_{S_3}S_2)\cong \Spec(S_1)\times_{\Spec(S_3)}\Spec(S_2).
10069: \]
10070: The noncommutative order case is subtler (pushouts need not exist in the naive category), but \emph{Morita contexts} and \emph{amalgamated free products} provide workable substitutes in practice.
10071: For this torus toy model, the most robust computational strategy is to track the induced gluing on:
10072: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
10073: \item centers and reduced norms (commutative invariants),
10074: \item module categories (stack viewpoint),
10075: \item and, for quantum simulation, the induced block structure on a global Hilbert space (operator-algebra viewpoint).
10076: \end{enumerate}
10077: 
10078: \paragraph{Operator-algebra duality reminder.}
10079: If $A$ is a commutative unital $C^\ast$-algebra, Gelfand duality identifies $A\cong C(X)$ for a compact Hausdorff space $X$.
10080: Noncommutative $C^\ast$-algebras are then interpreted as ``noncommutative spaces'' in the sense of Connes; gluing and tensoring become operator-algebraic operations rather than literal set-theoretic gluings.
10081: We will use this viewpoint below when we discuss spectral triples and regularized determinants.
10082: 
10083: \subsubsection{Step 4: time holonomy invariants with heterogeneous centres (a computation you can actually do)}
10084: 
10085: Because matrix sizes vary, we work with a \emph{central holonomy character} extracted from the local turning operators.
10086: 
10087: \begin{definition}[Reduced-norm holonomy character]
10088: \label{def:nrd_holonomy}
10089: Let $\gamma$ be a closed loop in the dessin spine.
10090: Define $\Hol_{\mathrm{turn}}(\gamma)$ as the ordered product of turning operators encountered along $\gamma$:
10091: at a trivalent black vertex use $S_{B_i}^{\pm 1}$ depending on the turn direction, and at a bivalent white vertex use $S_{jk}^{\pm 1}$.
10092: The \emph{reduced-norm holonomy} is
10093: \[
10094: \chi_{\mathrm{clock}}(\gamma)\ :=\ \prod_{v\in \gamma} \mathrm{Nrd}\big(S_v^{\epsilon_v(\gamma)}\big)\ \in\ K^\times,
10095: \]
10096: where $\epsilon_v(\gamma)\in\Z$ records the net number of $+1$ turns at $v$ minus the net number of $-1$ turns at $v$.
10097: \end{definition}
10098: 
10099: \begin{remark}
10100: This definition is deliberately low-tech: it produces an invariant that (i) survives heterogeneous sizes, (ii) lands in a commutative group, and (iii) is precisely the kind of object that can carry GRH-relevant ``local signs'' (root numbers, tame phases) once one couples to arithmetic twists.
10101: \end{remark}
10102: 
10103: \paragraph{Face loop computation.}
10104: For the unique face, the boundary step is $i\mapsto \sigma_\infty(i)=\sigma_1\sigma_0^{-1}(i)$ (since $\sigma_\infty=(\sigma_0\sigma_1)^{-1}$ and $\sigma_1$ is an involution).
10105: Thus each boundary step contributes one black turn ($\sigma_0^{-1}$) and one white turn ($\sigma_1$).
10106: Tracing the cycle $\sigma_\infty=(1\,6\,2\,4\,3\,5)$ one finds:
10107: \begin{itemize}
10108: \item black turns: $B_1$ appears $3$ times and $B_2$ appears $3$ times,
10109: \item white turns: each of $W_{14},W_{25},W_{36}$ appears exactly $2$ times.
10110: \end{itemize}
10111: Hence, using Lemma~\ref{lem:powers_reduced_norms},
10112: \begin{equation}
10113: \label{eq:bipartite_face_nrd}
10114: \chi_{\mathrm{clock}}(\partial f)\ =\ \pi_1^{-3}\,\pi_2^{-3}\,(\!-\pi_3)^{2}\,(\!-\pi_4)^{2}\,(\!-\pi_5)^{2}
10115: \ =\ \frac{\pi_3^2\pi_4^2\pi_5^2}{\pi_1^3\pi_2^3}\ \in K^\times.
10116: \end{equation}
10117: 
10118: \begin{remark}[Central defects as ``arithmetic bookkeeping'']
10119: In the clean example, the face holonomy defect was a single central factor $\pi^{-2}$.
10120: Here the defect keeps track of \emph{which} local centers were traversed, and \emph{how often}.
10121: In GRH-strength applications, the analogue of \eqref{eq:bipartite_face_nrd} is where conductors and local epsilon factors naturally want to live.
10122: \end{remark}
10123: 
10124: \subsubsection{Step 5: entanglement weights, induced distances, and how persistence sees the mismatch of metrics}
10125: 
10126: We now connect three different notions of distance on the same underlying torus:
10127: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
10128: \item combinatorial distance on the dessin graph (edge-count or weighted by local data),
10129: \item geometric distance from a punctured-hyperbolic structure on the once-punctured torus,
10130: \item and an entanglement-induced distance derived from a quantum state on the vertex Hilbert spaces.
10131: \end{enumerate}
10132: 
10133: \paragraph{Hilbert spaces and a concrete state family.}
10134: Attach Hilbert spaces of dimensions matching vertex valences:
10135: \[
10136: \cH_{B_1}\cong \C^3,\quad \cH_{B_2}\cong \C^3,\quad 
10137: \cH_{14}\cong \C^2,\quad \cH_{25}\cong \C^2,\quad \cH_{36}\cong \C^2.
10138: \]
10139: Consider a family of states $|\psi(\lambda)\rangle$ built as follows:
10140: each black--white edge carries an entangled pair of strength $\lambda\ge 0$ (e.g.\ via a two-qubit or qubit--qutrit entangler implemented by an isometry into the larger space), and then one applies shallow local unitaries at each vertex to ``spread'' correlations across incident edges.
10141: This is a deliberately hardware-friendly analogue of a PEPS-like ansatz.
10142: 
10143: \paragraph{Entanglement-derived edge lengths.}
10144: Let $I_{uv}(\lambda)$ denote the mutual information between vertex subsystems $u$ and $v$ in the state $|\psi(\lambda)\rangle$.
10145: Define an entanglement length on edges by
10146: \[
10147: \ell_{uv}(\lambda):= -\log\!\left(\frac{I_{uv}(\lambda)+\varepsilon}{I_0+\varepsilon}\right),
10148: \]
10149: with a small $\varepsilon>0$ to avoid numerical blowups.
10150: Then $\ell_{uv}$ decreases as entanglement increases, and it is additive along paths (as a path-length metric).
10151: The induced \emph{entanglement distance} is the shortest-path metric
10152: \[
10153: d_{\mathrm{ent},\lambda}(u,v):=\inf_{\text{paths }u\rightsquigarrow v}\ \sum_{(x,y)\in \text{path}}\ell_{xy}(\lambda).
10154: \]
10155: 
10156: \paragraph{Comparison to hyperbolic distance.}
10157: The once-punctured torus admits a complete finite-area hyperbolic metric; one model is $X=\HH/\Gamma$ for a suitable $\Gamma<\PSL_2(\Z)$.
10158: Let $d_{\mathrm{hyp}}$ denote the hyperbolic distance on the thick part, and note that near the cusp it diverges.
10159: In contrast, $d_{\mathrm{ent},\lambda}$ is bounded on the finite graph, so the cusp is necessarily \emph{collapsed} at finite discretization.
10160: This is not a bug: it is exactly what coarse-graining does.
10161: 
10162: \begin{proposition}[Quasi-isometry on the thick part (informal but checkable)]
10163: Fix a hyperbolic punctured torus structure and embed $\Gamma_{\mathrm{bi}}$ as a spine in the thick part.
10164: Then there exist constants $A\ge 1,B\ge 0$ (depending on the embedding) such that for vertices $u,v$ in the thick part,
10165: \[
10166: A^{-1}d_{\Gamma}(u,v)-B\ \le\ d_{\mathrm{hyp}}(u,v)\ \le\ A\,d_{\Gamma}(u,v)+B,
10167: \]
10168: where $d_\Gamma$ is combinatorial distance on $\Gamma_{\mathrm{bi}}$.
10169: \end{proposition}
10170: 
10171: \begin{remark}
10172: This is the standard ``spine quasi-isometry'' fact: graphs embedded as spines in thick parts of finite-area hyperbolic surfaces coarsely capture the geometry.
10173: In practice it is verified numerically by sampling geodesic distances between embedded vertices.
10174: The key point for us is that \emph{entanglement distance} can violate this quasi-isometry if entanglement concentrates on certain edges, and persistence detects exactly where and when that violation happens.
10175: \end{remark}
10176: 
10177: \paragraph{How multiparameter persistence enters.}
10178: We now define a two-parameter filtration that simultaneously tracks (i) metric reachability and (ii) holonomy defect.
10179: 
10180: Let $r\ge 0$ and $\kappa\in\R$.
10181: Define a subcomplex $K_{r,\kappa}\subset X$ by:
10182: \begin{itemize}
10183: \item include all vertices,
10184: \item include an edge $(u,v)$ if $d_{\mathrm{ent},\lambda}(u,v)\le r$ (equivalently, $\ell_{uv}(\lambda)\le r$ for direct edges),
10185: \item include the face $f$ if the \emph{defect valuation} satisfies
10186: \[
10187: v\!\left(\chi_{\mathrm{clock}}(\partial f)\right)\ge \kappa,
10188: \]
10189: where $v$ is a chosen valuation on $K$ (e.g.\ an additive valuation extending the $v_i$ along the embeddings).
10190: \end{itemize}
10191: 
10192: As $(r,\kappa)$ increases, edges and then the face appear.
10193: The resulting bifiltration produces a bigraded persistence module
10194: \[
10195: M_{p}=\{H_p(K_{r,\kappa})\}_{(r,\kappa)\in\R^2}
10196: \]
10197: over the poset $\R^2$ (equivalently a finitely presented $k[x,y]$-module after discretization).
10198: 
10199: \begin{example}[Closed-form $H_1$ behavior in this toy bifiltration]
10200: Because $\Gamma_{\mathrm{bi}}$ has $\beta_1=2$ and there is a single 2-cell, the only way $H_1$ can decrease is when the face attaches.
10201: Thus for any $(r,\kappa)$ such that the 1-skeleton is present but the face is absent, one has $H_1\cong \Z^2$.
10202: Once the face is included, one relation kills one rank and $H_1\cong \Z$ if the attaching map is nontrivial on $H_1$ (which it is here).
10203: Therefore the rank invariant of $H_1$ has the schematic form
10204: \[
10205: \rank H_1(K_{r,\kappa})=
10206: \begin{cases}
10207: 0,& r<r_{\mathrm{conn}},\\
10208: 2,& r\ge r_{\mathrm{conn}}\ \text{and}\ \kappa<\kappa_{\mathrm{face}},\\
10209: 1,& r\ge r_{\mathrm{conn}}\ \text{and}\ \kappa\ge \kappa_{\mathrm{face}},
10210: \end{cases}
10211: \]
10212: where $r_{\mathrm{conn}}$ is the threshold at which the 1-skeleton becomes connected under $d_{\mathrm{ent},\lambda}$ and $\kappa_{\mathrm{face}}:=v(\chi_{\mathrm{clock}}(\partial f))$ is the holonomy defect threshold.
10213: 
10214: The point is not the triviality of the arithmetic here, but the \emph{logic}:
10215: a holonomy defect provides an \emph{independent} persistence axis that can be compared against entanglement-derived connectivity.
10216: \end{example}
10217: 
10218: \subsubsection{Step 6: pants decompositions, 2D CFT sewing, and a holography-shaped hint}
10219: 
10220: The punctured torus is the smallest surface where ``pants gluing'' is nontrivial yet completely explicit.
10221: A once-punctured torus can be obtained by taking a pair of pants $P$ (a sphere with three boundary components) and gluing two of its boundaries together.
10222: On the CFT side, this is precisely where conformal blocks, sewing, and modular transformations start to bite.
10223: 
10224: \paragraph{Pants gluing as a categorical operation.}
10225: In a 2D CFT (or more generally a modular functor / 2D TQFT), the pair of pants is the fundamental multiplication:
10226: it provides a multilinear map on state spaces assigned to boundary circles.
10227: Gluing boundaries corresponds to taking an inner product / trace, and the torus partition function becomes a graded trace.
10228: Symbolically,
10229: \[
10230: Z_{T^2}(q,\bar q)=\mathrm{Tr}_{\cH}\!\left(q^{L_0-c/24}\,\bar q^{\bar L_0-c/24}\right),
10231: \]
10232: with the modular parameter $\tau$ entering via $q=e^{2\pi i\tau}$.
10233: 
10234: \paragraph{Where our orders fit.}
10235: Surface orders provide a concrete algebraic model for ``local pieces'' that can be glued:
10236: trivalent black vertices behave like pants junctions, while bivalent white vertices behave like explicit ``propagators'' (degree--2 sewing sites).
10237: The heterogeneous DVRs then act as local deformation parameters (conductors / local scales).
10238: In that language, the gluing in Definition~\ref{def:order_cosheaf} is the algebraic shadow of CFT sewing:
10239: tensor products (quantum composition) mirror fibre products (geometric gluing), with Morita bimodules playing the role of boundary conditions.
10240: 
10241: \paragraph{A holography-shaped hint (carefully labelled as a hint).}
10242: If one further interprets entanglement distance as a discrete bulk metric, then changing $\lambda$ changes the effective ``bulk geometry'' seen by the graph.
10243: In AdS/CFT heuristics, entanglement controls geometry; here, persistence provides a way to test whether that control is \emph{stable} under changes of discretization and twist.
10244: No miracle is claimed, but the methodological link is real:
10245: \emph{sewing axioms constrain operator families, and operator families are what Hilbert--Pólya needs.}
10246: 
10247: \subsubsection{Step 7: the noncommutative-geometric lens (spectral triples) and why Connes's recent ``zeta spectral triples'' matter here}
10248: 
10249: A persistent theme in this volume is that we need an operator-theoretic home where:
10250: \begin{itemize}
10251: \item the algebraic gluing data live as an algebra $A$,
10252: \item the quantum/entanglement data live as a representation on a Hilbert space $H$,
10253: \item and the geometric/time data live as an (unbounded) self-adjoint operator $D$.
10254: \end{itemize}
10255: This is precisely the format of a \emph{spectral triple} $(A,H,D)$.
10256: 
10257: Recent work of Connes--Consani--Moscovici proposes ``zeta spectral triples'' designed so that spectra of certain self-adjoint operators approximate the zeros of $\zeta(\tfrac12+is)$, together with regularized determinants converging toward the $\Xi$-function \cite{ConnesConsaniMoscovici2025ZetaSpectralTriples}.
10258: We do \emph{not} import their construction wholesale here, but we take it seriously as a template:
10259: it is a concrete, operator-theoretic attempt at Hilbert--Pólya with explicit determinants.
10260: 
10261: \paragraph{Our toy torus version.}
10262: For the bipartite torus order, one can form:
10263: \begin{itemize}
10264: \item $A$ as a $C^\ast$-completion of the (block) algebra generated by local orders and edge bimodules,
10265: \item $H$ as the direct sum of vertex Hilbert spaces (or, better, the completion of a module of sections),
10266: \item $D$ as a combination of a geometric Dirac/Laplacian discretization and the clock generator(s) extracted from the $S_v$ via a logarithm branch.
10267: \end{itemize}
10268: The regularized determinant $\det_{\mathrm{reg}}(D-z)$ is then an honest object one can compute numerically and compare across twists.
10269: In this volume we keep this in the ``proposal'' box, but we emphasize: it is the right kind of box.
10270: 
10271: \paragraph{A small wink.}
10272: If you are a non-believer, the correct attitude here is not faith; it is version control.
10273: Build the toy $D$, compute its determinant, perturb it, and see what is stable.
10274: Persistence is the debugger.
10275: 
10276: \subsubsection{Step 8: a fully explicit discretized two-parameter persistence module (a ``unit test'' you can hand to your code)}
10277: \label{sec:bipartite_step8_persistence}
10278: 
10279: The previous steps described the bifiltration $K_{r,\kappa}$ conceptually.
10280: Here we freeze a tiny discretization and write the resulting $k[x,y]$--module presentation explicitly.
10281: The point is not sophistication; it is reproducibility.
10282: 
10283: \paragraph{Discretization.}
10284: Fix a field $k$ (for computations, take $k=\F_2$).
10285: Choose two $r$--levels and two defect levels:
10286: \[
10287: r_0<r_1,\qquad \kappa_0<\kappa_1,
10288: \]
10289: where $r_0$ is below the connectivity threshold (so the graph is disconnected at $r_0$) and $r_1$ is above it (so the full 1--skeleton is present at $r_1$),
10290: and where $\kappa_0< v(\chi_{\mathrm{clock}}(\partial f))\le \kappa_1$ so that the face is absent at $\kappa_0$ and present at $\kappa_1$.
10291: This yields a $2\times 2$ grid of complexes:
10292: \[
10293: K_{r_0,\kappa_0}\subset K_{r_1,\kappa_0},\qquad
10294: K_{r_0,\kappa_1}\subset K_{r_1,\kappa_1},
10295: \]
10296: with the obvious inclusions in the $\kappa$ direction as well.
10297: 
10298: \paragraph{Homology on the grid.}
10299: In this discretization one checks:
10300: \[
10301: H_1(K_{r_0,\kappa_0};k)=0,\qquad H_1(K_{r_1,\kappa_0};k)\cong k^2,
10302: \]
10303: and
10304: \[
10305: H_1(K_{r_0,\kappa_1};k)=0,\qquad H_1(K_{r_1,\kappa_1};k)\cong k,
10306: \]
10307: because the only difference between $\kappa_0$ and $\kappa_1$ is the attachment of the single 2--cell $f$.
10308: 
10309: \begin{proposition}[Minimal bigraded presentation in the $2\times 2$ discretization]
10310: \label{prop:2x2_persistence_presentation}
10311: Let $M$ be the resulting bigraded persistence module $M(r_i,\kappa_j)=H_1(K_{r_i,\kappa_j};k)$.
10312: Then $M$ admits a minimal presentation as a $k[x,y]$--module with:
10313: \begin{itemize}
10314: \item two generators $g_1,g_2$ in bidegree $(r_1,\kappa_0)$,
10315: \item one relation in bidegree $(r_1,\kappa_1)$ killing exactly one $k$--linear combination of $g_1,g_2$ under the action of $y$.
10316: \end{itemize}
10317: Equivalently, after choosing a basis so that $y g_1=h$ and $y g_2=0$ for a generator $h$ in bidegree $(r_1,\kappa_1)$, one may write
10318: \[
10319: M \ \cong\  \frac{k[x,y]\langle g_1,g_2\rangle}{\langle y g_2,\ y^2 g_1\rangle},
10320: \]
10321: with grading understood in the obvious truncated sense.
10322: \end{proposition}
10323: 
10324: \begin{proof}
10325: At $(r_1,\kappa_0)$ there are precisely two independent 1-cycles (Proposition above), hence two generators.
10326: At $(r_1,\kappa_1)$ the face imposes exactly one independent relation on $H_1$, hence the image of $y:M(r_1,\kappa_0)\to M(r_1,\kappa_1)$ has rank $1$.
10327: Choose $g_1$ mapping to a nonzero class $h$ and choose $g_2$ spanning the kernel of $y$.
10328: The stated relations encode exactly this kernel/image structure.
10329: \end{proof}
10330: 
10331: \paragraph{What this buys you in practice.}
10332: If you implement multiparameter persistence for surface-order filtrations, Proposition~\ref{prop:2x2_persistence_presentation} is a sanity check:
10333: your code should recover (up to isomorphism) a module with two generators and one independent ``$y$--killing'' relation.
10334: 
10335: If it does not, you have either:
10336: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
10337: \item a bug in the filtration construction (common),
10338: \item a convention mismatch in the boundary orientation (also common),
10339: \item or a genuine modeling difference (rare, but interesting).
10340: \end{enumerate}
10341: Either way, you learn something without appealing to numerical coincidence with any $L$--function.
10342: 
10343: 
10344: 
10345: 
10346: \subsection{Summary: a ``wise'' conclusion (now in GRH form)}
10347: \label{sec:HP_summary}
10348: 
10349: A GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya program demands \emph{uniform rigidity across twists}.
10350: This volume's contribution is to propose a rigidifying architecture:
10351: \begin{itemize}
10352: \item hyperbolic quotient geometry supplies trace-formula and scattering structure,
10353: \item surface orders supply local-to-global gluing constraints and a controlled source of twists,
10354: \item twisted shifts supply nontrivial holonomy with arithmetic-looking central factors (a plausible home for root numbers and local signs),
10355: \item multiparameter persistence supplies stability diagnostics to keep the program honest across families,
10356: \item quantum hardware experiments supply a sandbox where the time/holonomy mechanism can be tested at scale without claiming number theory miracles.
10357: \end{itemize}
10358: 
10359: If this program fails, it should fail \emph{informatively}:
10360: by isolating exactly which coupling assumptions break trace-formula compatibility, destroy twist-uniformity, or violate stability.
10361: If it succeeds, it will likely do so by revealing that the ``right'' operator family was not invented, but \emph{forced} by consistent gluing of local algebra, hyperbolic geometry, and information-theoretic positivity.
10362: 
10363: \subsection{Trace-formula alignment: explicit targets one can actually compare}
10364: \label{sec:trace_formula_targets}
10365: 
10366: A recurring ambiguity in Hilbert--Pólya discussions is that ``matching zeros'' is treated as a single monolithic target.
10367: In practice one must decide \emph{what observable} of the spectrum is being matched, and at which scale.
10368: 
10369: Two reference formulas organize the comparison:
10370: 
10371: \paragraph{(1) The explicit formula (general $L$--function).}
10372: For a suitable test function $f$, the explicit formula relates a smoothed sum over nontrivial zeros $\rho=\tfrac12+i\gamma$ to a sum over prime powers with arithmetic weights depending on $\varpi$.
10373: Schematically,
10374: \[
10375: \sum_{\rho} \widehat{f}(\gamma)
10376: \;=\;
10377: \text{(main terms from poles and gamma factors)}\;+\;\sum_{p^k}\frac{\log p}{p^{k/2}}\, a_\varpi(p^k)\, f(k\log p),
10378: \]
10379: where $a_\varpi(p^k)$ is $\chi(p^k)$ for Dirichlet twists and $\Tr(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p^k))$ for Artin twists, etc.
10380: 
10381: \paragraph{(2) The Selberg trace formula (hyperbolic quotient, twisted).}
10382: For a compact hyperbolic surface $X=\HH/\Gamma$ and a unitary twist $\rho:\Gamma\to U(d)$, a twisted trace formula relates a smoothed sum over Laplace eigenvalues $\lambda_n=\tfrac14+r_n^2$ to a sum over primitive closed geodesics $\{\gamma\}$ weighted by $\Tr(\rho(\gamma^k))$.
10383: In schematic form,
10384: \[
10385: \sum_{n} h(r_n)\,\dim(\text{twist space})
10386: \;=\;
10387: \text{(identity / area term)}\;+\;
10388: \sum_{\{\gamma\}}\sum_{k\ge 1} \Tr(\rho(\gamma^k))\,W(\gamma,k;g),
10389: \]
10390: where $W(\gamma,k;g)$ is the standard hyperbolic weight built from $\ell(\gamma)$ and a transform $g$ of $h$.
10391: 
10392: \paragraph{What can be matched.}
10393: The analogy is structural:
10394: \begin{itemize}
10395: \item prime powers $p^k$ correspond to iterates $k\ell(\gamma)$ of primitive closed geodesics,
10396: \item the arithmetic coefficients $a_\varpi(p^k)$ correspond to trace weights $\Tr(\rho(\gamma^k))$,
10397: \item and the test function is the dial that selects the comparison scale.
10398: \end{itemize}
10399: 
10400: In our program, the operator is not \emph{just} the Laplacian: it is a \emph{clock/holonomy-twisted} object.
10401: The concrete target is therefore a \emph{twisted trace identity} whose geometric side naturally carries arithmetic weights and whose spectral side is manifestly self-adjoint (or has a controlled scattering interpretation).
10402: 
10403: \subsection{Numerical milestones and resource estimates (classical and quantum) --- now across twists}
10404: \label{sec:hp_milestones}
10405: 
10406: A pragmatic GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya program should be organized by milestones that can be reached with today's tools.
10407: A reasonable staging is:
10408: 
10409: \begin{enumerate}
10410: \item \textbf{Twist-family selection.}
10411: Choose a family $\{\varpi\}$ where zero data are accessible and where analytic continuation is established.
10412: The minimal reliable choice is primitive Dirichlet characters up to conductor $q\le q_{\max}$.
10413: 
10414: \item \textbf{Arithmetic surface selection.}
10415: Choose $X=\HH/\Gamma$ where $\Gamma$ is arithmetic and where both Laplace/scattering data and geodesic data are computationally accessible.
10416: Congruence subgroups of $\PSL_2(\Z)$ are natural for Dirichlet twists.
10417: 
10418: \item \textbf{Order/clock data on $X$.}
10419: Pick an embedded graph $\Gamma_X\subset X$ (e.g.\ a spine) and build a surface order whose local data reflect the graph valences.
10420: Choose twisted shift representations and record the induced holonomy class $[g]\in \check{H}^1(X;\Aut(\Lambda))$.
10421: 
10422: \item \textbf{Define a self-adjoint candidate family.}
10423: Construct a family of operators of the form
10424: \[
10425: H_\varpi(\theta)=\Phi(\Delta_X)\;+\;\Psi_\varpi\!\big(U_\theta\big),
10426: \]
10427: where $\Delta_X$ is (a suitable self-adjoint extension of) the Laplacian and $U_\theta$ is a unitary holonomy operator built from the clock/gluing data.
10428: The map $\varpi\mapsto \Psi_\varpi$ must be rigid (e.g.\ induced from representation theory), otherwise the construction has no hope of addressing GRH.
10429: 
10430: \item \textbf{Spectral/scattering computation.}
10431: On the classical side: compute eigenvalues and/or scattering phases for a grid of $(\varpi,\theta)$.
10432: On the quantum side (longer term): implement block-encodings or Hamiltonian simulation for discretized $H_\varpi(\theta)$ and use phase estimation to sample eigenphases.
10433: The resource bottleneck is not just qubits but \emph{coherent depth} and conditioning.
10434: 
10435: \item \textbf{Trace-statistic comparison.}
10436: Choose test functions and compare smoothed spectral sums to geometric sums over closed geodesics (or graph-cycle proxies) with twisted weights.
10437: For GRH, do this \emph{uniformly over twists} and record which observables are stable.
10438: 
10439: \item \textbf{Persistence diagnostic layer.}
10440: Run multiparameter persistence diagnostics on the same data to detect regime changes (as $\varpi$ or $\theta$ varies) and to separate numerical artifacts from structural effects.
10441: \end{enumerate}
10442: 
10443: The point of this milestone plan is that failure is informative:
10444: if trace statistics refuse to align uniformly over twists, the obstruction can often be localized to one of the construction steps above.
10445: 
10446: 
10447: \subsection{A GRH ``completion step'': from toy twists to genuine GRH families (with emphasis on Artin $L$--functions)}
10448: \label{sec:GRH_artin_completion}
10449: 
10450: The worked examples above were intentionally small: they were meant to make the pipeline executable end-to-end, not to solve analytic number theory.
10451: This final subsection records the \emph{full-strength target} for the theory when the GRH indexing parameter $\varpi$ is taken seriously.
10452: We focus on Artin $L$--functions, because they are the cleanest setting where:
10453: (i) local factors are naturally matrix-valued, and
10454: (ii) functoriality constraints are not optional.
10455: 
10456: \subsubsection{Artin $L$--functions: the exact data GRH forces you to carry}
10457: 
10458: Fix a finite Galois extension $L/\Q$ with Galois group $G=\mathrm{Gal}(L/\Q)$ and a complex representation
10459: \[
10460: \varpi=\rho:\ G\to \GL_n(\C).
10461: \]
10462: For each unramified prime $p$, let $\mathrm{Frob}_p\in G$ be a Frobenius conjugacy class.
10463: The Artin $L$--function is (initially for $\Re(s)>1$) the Euler product
10464: \begin{equation}
10465: \label{eq:artin_L_euler}
10466: L(s,\rho)\ :=\ \prod_{p\ \mathrm{unram.}}\ \det\!\big(I_n-\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p)\,p^{-s}\big)^{-1}\ \cdot\ \prod_{p\ \mathrm{ram.}}L_p(s,\rho),
10467: \end{equation}
10468: where the finitely many ramified local factors $L_p(s,\rho)$ are defined using inertia invariants.
10469: The \emph{completed} $L$--function has the form
10470: \begin{equation}
10471: \label{eq:completed_artin}
10472: \Lambda(s,\rho)\ :=\ N(\rho)^{s/2}\,L_\infty(s,\rho)\,L(s,\rho),
10473: \end{equation}
10474: where $N(\rho)$ is the Artin conductor and $L_\infty$ is a product of $\Gamma$--factors determined by the archimedean type of $\rho$.
10475: Assuming Artin conjectures as needed for analytic continuation, $\Lambda(s,\rho)$ satisfies a functional equation
10476: \begin{equation}
10477: \label{eq:artin_FE}
10478: \Lambda(s,\rho)\ =\ \varepsilon(\rho)\,\Lambda(1-s,\rho^\vee),
10479: \qquad |\varepsilon(\rho)|=1.
10480: \end{equation}
10481: 
10482: \begin{definition}[GRH for Artin $L$--functions]
10483: GRH for $\Lambda(s,\rho)$ asserts that all nontrivial zeros satisfy $\Re(s)=\tfrac12$.
10484: \end{definition}
10485: 
10486: \paragraph{What this forces on Hilbert--Pólya.}
10487: A GRH-strength Hilbert--Pólya realization must produce, for \emph{each} $\rho$ in the family of interest, a self-adjoint operator $H_\rho$ such that
10488: \[
10489: \rho=\tfrac12+i\gamma\ \text{is a nontrivial zero of }\Lambda(s,\rho)\quad \Longleftrightarrow\quad \gamma\in \Spec(H_\rho),
10490: \]
10491: and it must do so \emph{functorially} in $\rho$:
10492: isomorphic representations must yield unitarily equivalent operators, induction/restriction should have a controlled effect, and local ramification must be visible in the construction (otherwise one is just fitting points).
10493: 
10494: \subsubsection{Where surface orders and heterogeneous DVRs match Artin data}
10495: 
10496: The bipartite torus example in \S\ref{sec:hp_worked_example_torus_bipartite} was designed to mimic two unavoidable features of Artin data:
10497: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
10498: \item \textbf{multiple local centres:} ramified primes behave differently from unramified primes, and local conductors vary;
10499: \item \textbf{matrix-valued local factors:} $\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p)$ is an $n\times n$ matrix, so local weights are traces/determinants of matrices, not scalars.
10500: \end{enumerate}
10501: 
10502: This suggests a general schematic dictionary:
10503: 
10504: \begin{center}
10505: \begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
10506: \toprule
10507: Artin/GRH object & Surface-order/clock object \\
10508: \midrule
10509: prime $p$ and local field $\Q_p$ & a local chart with DVR centre $R_p$ \\
10510: ramification/inertia at $p$ & degeneration of the local order / modified vertex type \\
10511: $\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p)$ eigenvalues & local clock spectrum / local twisted shift phases \\
10512: Artin conductor exponent at $p$ & valuation of the local holonomy defect \\
10513: root number $\varepsilon(\rho)$ & global central phase of time holonomy \\
10514: \bottomrule
10515: \end{tabular}
10516: \end{center}
10517: 
10518: \begin{remark}[This is a dictionary, not a theorem]
10519: Each row indicates \emph{where} the arithmetic datum could live in our framework.
10520: Turning this dictionary into a theorem requires a trace formula or explicit-formula alignment that pins the construction down.
10521: \end{remark}
10522: 
10523: \subsubsection{Tensor products vs gluing (a precise toy lemma and why ``stack thinking'' matters)}
10524: 
10525: Because you explicitly asked for a clean comparison, we record the basic commutative identity and then explain the noncommutative analogue we actually use.
10526: 
10527: \begin{lemma}[Affine fibre product duality]
10528: Let $S_3\subseteq S_1,S_2$ be commutative rings.
10529: Then there is a natural isomorphism of affine schemes
10530: \[
10531: \Spec(S_1\otimes_{S_3}S_2)\ \cong\ \Spec(S_1)\times_{\Spec(S_3)}\Spec(S_2).
10532: \]
10533: \end{lemma}
10534: 
10535: \begin{remark}[Noncommutative replacement]
10536: Orders are not commutative, so one cannot literally apply the lemma.
10537: However:
10538: \begin{itemize}
10539: \item on centres (which \emph{are} commutative), the lemma applies exactly;
10540: \item on module categories, gluing is governed by bimodules, and tensoring over the overlap algebra is the correct operation;
10541: \item on the $C^\ast$ side, the minimal tensor product $A\otimes_{\min}B$ corresponds to the product of noncommutative spaces in Connes' sense.
10542: \end{itemize}
10543: This is why we repeatedly insist on the stack/categorical viewpoint:
10544: it is the right level where heterogeneous local centres and matrix sizes can be glued without cheating.
10545: \end{remark}
10546: 
10547: \subsubsection{Connes-style spectral triples and regularized determinants: what can be generalized (and what cannot)}
10548: 
10549: A spectral triple $(A,H,D)$ packages a noncommutative space $A$ together with a representation on $H$ and a Dirac-type operator $D$.
10550: The regularized determinants $\det_{\mathrm{reg}}(D-z)$ that appear in spectral geometry are analytic analogues of Fredholm determinants.
10551: They are the natural home for $\Xi$-type entire functions.
10552: 
10553: Recent work of Connes--Consani--Moscovici (arXiv:2511.22755~\cite{ConnesConsaniMoscovici2025ZetaSpectralTriples}, ``Zeta Spectral Triples'') constructs self-adjoint operators $D_{\log}^{(\lambda,N)}$ by rank-one perturbations of a spectral triple built from the scaling operator on $[\lambda^{-1},\lambda]$, and reports striking numerical convergence of spectra toward zeta zeros, together with determinant comparisons to $\Xi$.
10554: This is an RH-oriented result/proposal; GRH is not claimed there.
10555: Still, it provides an unusually concrete template for what a Hilbert--Pólya program can look like when written in operator-algebra language.
10556: 
10557: \begin{remark}[A conservative generalization path toward GRH]
10558: A plausible GRH upgrade would proceed in three strictly separated layers:
10559: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
10560: \item \textbf{Twist layer:} modify the arithmetic coefficients in the construction by inserting the correct local weights $a_\rho(p^k)=\Tr(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_p^k))$ (unramified) together with inertia-corrected ramified factors.
10561: \item \textbf{Geometry layer:} ensure the modified operator remains self-adjoint by a robust positivity/self-adjointness theorem (the hard part).
10562: \item \textbf{Determinant layer:} show that suitably normalized $\det_{\mathrm{reg}}(D_\rho-z)$ converges to $\Xi_\rho(z)$, the completed function on the critical line.
10563: \end{enumerate}
10564: Our surface-order framework is designed to help with (i) and with parts of (ii) by restricting how twists can enter: twists must come from gluing/holonomy of local orders, not from arbitrary coefficient hacking.
10565: \end{remark}
10566: 
10567: \subsubsection{How the bipartite torus example suggests an Artin-ready ``clock twist''}
10568: 
10569: Return to the heterogeneous DVR torus.
10570: The face defect \eqref{eq:bipartite_face_nrd} was a product of local uniformizers with explicit exponents.
10571: In an Artin setting, the analogue is that the global phase $\varepsilon(\rho)$ and the conductor $N(\rho)$ factor into local contributions.
10572: This suggests an operator ansatz of the form
10573: \[
10574: H_\rho\ \approx\ \Phi(\Delta_{\mathrm{hyp}})\ +\ \sum_{v\in \Gamma_X} \Psi_v\!\big(\log S_v(\rho)\big),
10575: \]
10576: where $S_v(\rho)$ is a local clock operator whose spectrum encodes local Frobenius data (at least after coarse-graining), and $\Phi$ is a functional calculus term providing the trace-formula backbone.
10577: The ``$\approx$'' is intentional: the analytic meaning of the sum and the domain of $H_\rho$ is where most false Hilbert--Pólya programs die.
10578: 
10579: \paragraph{Why persistence matters even more for Artin families.}
10580: Artin twists vary in dimension, conductor, and local ramification structure.
10581: A naive numerical match for a single $\rho$ is meaningless.
10582: Multiparameter persistence offers a falsifiable stability demand:
10583: \begin{quote}
10584: \emph{features that pretend to be arithmetic should persist under controlled variations of $\rho$ within a family, under mesh refinement, and under modest model perturbations.}
10585: \end{quote}
10586: If they do not, the construction is almost certainly fitting noise.
10587: 
10588: \subsubsection{Closing ``auditor's checklist'' for GRH-strength claims}
10589: 
10590: Any serious GRH/Hilbert--Pólya claim in this framework should explicitly address:
10591: 
10592: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
10593: \item \textbf{Functoriality in $\rho$:} isomorphic $\rho$ give unitarily equivalent $H_\rho$; induction/restriction behave predictably.
10594: \item \textbf{Local-global factorization:} conductor and epsilon factor appear as products of local contributions (holonomy defects).
10595: \item \textbf{Trace-formula alignment:} a precise identity comparing spectral sums to geometric/arithmetic sums, uniformly over a family of twists.
10596: \item \textbf{Self-adjointness:} domains, closures, and perturbations are controlled (no ``it looks Hermitian in finite precision'' shortcuts).
10597: \item \textbf{Stability:} multiparameter persistence diagnostics demonstrate robustness under parameter variation and discretization.
10598: \end{enumerate}
10599: 
10600: If any of these are missing, the correct reaction is not despair; it is gratitude:
10601: the framework has done its job by telling you \emph{exactly} what is still unproven.
10602: 
10603: \subsection*{Selected references for the Hilbert--Pólya / GRH discussion}
10604: 
10605:
\chapter{Hilbert--Pólya for GRH in density-operator language: conditional theorems and a proof skeleton} · lines 10606–13313
10606: \chapter{Hilbert--Pólya for GRH in density-operator language: conditional theorems and a proof skeleton}
10607: \label{ch:hilbertpolya_density}
10608: 
10609: This chapter provides a \emph{complete conditional proof of GRH} in density-operator language.
10610: Theorem~\ref{thm:determinantal_HP_GRH} proves that if a sequence of finite-dimensional self-adjoint HP approximants built from totally positive surface-order transfer matrices satisfies Condition~(d) (Fredholm determinant convergence to the completed $L$-function $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$), then GRH holds.
10611: Theorem~\ref{thm:hp_convergence_implies_holomorphy_and_grh} additionally establishes Artin holomorphy in the critical strip under the same hypothesis.
10612: The content here is not a sketch: it is a rigorous operator-algebraic proof framework in which all analysis --- Weierstrass holomorphy preservation (Proposition~\ref{prop:weierstrass_holomorphy_from_approximation}), Fredholm determinant continuity (Theorem~\ref{thm:fredholm_det_continuity}), Hurwitz zero-stability with full argument-principle proof (Theorem~\ref{thm:hurwitz_hp_implies_grh}) --- is fully proved.
10613: The sole remaining obligation is Condition~(d): the arithmetic-geometric identification establishing that the surface-order Fredholm determinants converge to $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$, which reduces to matching geodesic lengths to $\log p$ and holonomy traces to Frobenius traces.
10614: The emphasis is on two themes already present in this volume:
10615: \begin{enumerate}
10616: \item spectral data should be extracted from \emph{self-adjoint generators} (Hamiltonians or modular Hamiltonians), and
10617: \item coarse-graining, noise, and functorial families are most naturally phrased in terms of \emph{density operators and CPTP maps}.
10618: \end{enumerate}
10619: 
10620: \section{The Hilbert--Pólya implication: self-adjoint spectrum forces the critical line}
10621: \label{sec:hp_implication_grh}
10622: 
10623: Fix an $L$--function family $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ as in Section~\ref{sec:GRH_statement}.
10624: Write its multiset of nontrivial zeros as
10625: \[
10626: Z(\varpi)=\{\rho\in\mathbb{C}:\Lambda(\rho,\varpi)=0,\ 0<\Re(\rho)<1\},
10627: \]
10628: counted with multiplicity.
10629: The functional equation forces the symmetry $\rho\in Z(\varpi)\Rightarrow 1-\overline{\rho}\in Z(\varpi)$.
10630: 
10631: \begin{remark}[Notation: $Z(\varpi)$ vs $Z(\beta)$]
10632: In Section~\ref{sec:explicit_formula_GRH}, $Z(\varpi)$ denotes the multiset of nontrivial zeros of $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$.  Earlier in Section~\ref{sec:trace_to_explicit_formula}, $Z(\beta)$ denotes the partition function $\Tr(e^{-\beta H})$. These notations are distinguished by their arguments and context; no confusion arises.
10633: \end{remark}
10634: 
10635: \begin{definition}[Spectral realization at the critical line]
10636: \label{def:spectral_realization}
10637: A \emph{Hilbert--Pólya spectral realization} for $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ is a self-adjoint operator $H_\varpi$ on a separable Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}_\varpi$ with real spectrum $\Spec(H_\varpi)\subset\mathbb{R}$, together with a multiplicity assignment, such that
10638: \[
10639: \rho\in Z(\varpi)
10640: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
10641: \rho=\tfrac12+i\lambda\ \text{ for some }\ \lambda\in\Spec(H_\varpi),
10642: \]
10643: and the multiplicity of the zero at $\tfrac12+i\lambda$ equals the spectral multiplicity of $\lambda$.
10644: \end{definition}
10645: 
10646: \begin{theorem}[Hilbert--Pólya $\Rightarrow$ GRH (tautological but essential)]
10647: \label{thm:hp_implies_grh}
10648: If $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ admits a spectral realization in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:spectral_realization}, then $\Lambda(s,\varpi)$ satisfies GRH, i.e.\ every nontrivial zero $\rho$ has $\Re(\rho)=\tfrac12$.
10649: \end{theorem}
10650: 
10651: \begin{proof}
10652: By Definition~\ref{def:spectral_realization}, each nontrivial zero has the form $\rho=\tfrac12+i\lambda$ with $\lambda\in\Spec(H_\varpi)$.
10653: Self-adjointness of $H_\varpi$ implies $\Spec(H_\varpi)\subset\mathbb{R}$.
10654: Therefore $\Re(\rho)=\tfrac12$ for all nontrivial zeros.
10655: \end{proof}
10656: 
10657: The content of Hilbert--Pólya is thus \emph{not} the implication ``self-adjoint $\Rightarrow$ critical line'' (which is immediate), but the existence of a functorial family $\varpi\mapsto H_\varpi$ whose trace data reproduces the explicit formula.
10658: 
10659: \section{Functional equation symmetry as a chiral pairing of eigenvalues}
10660: \label{sec:functional_equation_chiral}
10661: 
10662: The functional equation symmetry $\rho\mapsto 1-\overline{\rho}$ becomes $\lambda\mapsto -\lambda$ on the critical line.
10663: On the operator side this is implemented by a grading or ``chiral'' symmetry.
10664: 
10665: \begin{definition}[Chiral symmetry]
10666: A self-adjoint operator $H$ on $\mathcal{H}$ has \emph{chiral symmetry} if there exists a unitary involution $\Gamma$ ($\Gamma^\dagger=\Gamma$, $\Gamma^2=I$) such that
10667: \begin{equation}
10668: \label{eq:chiral_anticommute}
10669: \Gamma H \Gamma = -H.
10670: \end{equation}
10671: \end{definition}
10672: 
10673: \begin{proposition}[Chiral symmetry $\Rightarrow$ $\pm$-paired spectrum]
10674: \label{prop:chiral_pairs}
10675: If $H$ is self-adjoint and satisfies \eqref{eq:chiral_anticommute}, then $\lambda\in\Spec(H)$ iff $-\lambda\in\Spec(H)$ with equal multiplicity.
10676: \end{proposition}
10677: 
10678: \begin{proof}
10679: If $H\ket{\psi}=\lambda\ket{\psi}$ then
10680: \[
10681: H(\Gamma\ket{\psi}) = -\Gamma H\ket{\psi} = -\lambda(\Gamma\ket{\psi}),
10682: \]
10683: so $-\lambda$ is an eigenvalue with the same multiplicity (the map $\ket{\psi}\mapsto\Gamma\ket{\psi}$ is invertible).
10684: For continuous spectrum the same argument applies to the spectral measure via conjugation by $\Gamma$.
10685: \end{proof}
10686: 
10687: In the surface-order/clock framework, the most natural source of such a symmetry is an orientation reversal or time-reversal operation on the underlying fat-graph dynamics, implemented by an involution on the clock field.
10688: 
10689: \section{Density operators, modular Hamiltonians, and why they belong in Hilbert--Pólya}
10690: \label{sec:density_modular_hp}
10691: 
10692: A Hilbert--Pólya operator is a \emph{Hamiltonian-like} generator.
10693: There are two closely related operator-theoretic ways to package such a generator.
10694: 
10695: \subsection{Gibbs density operators from a candidate Hilbert--Pólya Hamiltonian}
10696: Assume for the moment that $H_\varpi$ is bounded below and has discrete spectrum growing fast enough that $e^{-\beta H_\varpi}$ is trace class for $\beta>0$ (this is automatic in finite-dimensional toy models and in many geometric Laplacian settings after regularization).
10697: Define the (normalized) Gibbs state
10698: \begin{equation}
10699: \label{eq:gibbs_state}
10700: \rho_{\beta,\varpi}:=\frac{e^{-\beta H_\varpi}}{Z_\varpi(\beta)},\qquad
10701: Z_\varpi(\beta):=\Tr\bigl(e^{-\beta H_\varpi}\bigr).
10702: \end{equation}
10703: Then $Z_\varpi(\beta)$ is the heat-kernel trace/partition function, and the associated \emph{modular Hamiltonian} is
10704: \[
10705: K_{\beta,\varpi}:=-\log\rho_{\beta,\varpi}=\beta H_\varpi + \log Z_\varpi(\beta)\,I.
10706: \]
10707: Thus, for Gibbs states, modular flow and physical flow agree up to a constant shift:
10708: \[
10709: \sigma_{\beta,\varpi}^t(O)=e^{itK_{\beta,\varpi}}Oe^{-itK_{\beta,\varpi}}
10710: =
10711: e^{it\beta H_\varpi}Oe^{-it\beta H_\varpi}.
10712: \]
10713: This is the cleanest bridge between the ``modular Hamiltonian'' discussion in Chapter~\ref{ch:gravity} and the Hilbert--Pólya operator: the latter can be viewed as a modular Hamiltonian of an appropriate thermal state.
10714: 
10715: \subsection{Why density operators matter even if you ultimately want a spectrum}
10716: There are three practical reasons to elevate density operators from a technicality to a primary object:
10717: \begin{enumerate}
10718: \item \textbf{Trace formulas are traces of density operators.}
10719: Both Selberg-type trace formulas and explicit-formula identities are statements about traces of functions of an operator.
10720: The heat kernel $e^{-\beta H}$ is literally an (unnormalized) density operator, and $Z(\beta)=\Tr(e^{-\beta H})$ is its trace.
10721: \item \textbf{Families and functoriality are naturally channel-theoretic.}
10722: A GRH-strength program requires a functorial dependence $\varpi\mapsto H_\varpi$.
10723: In practice one often constructs \emph{transfer operators} or \emph{quantum channels} whose fixed points or modular generators encode the desired spectrum.
10724: \item \textbf{Noise/regularization are unavoidable.}
10725: Any physical implementation (or any numerical test) produces mixed states.
10726: A density-operator formulation ensures that each intermediate diagnostic (entropy, mutual information, persistence) remains well-defined.
10727: \end{enumerate}
10728: 
10729: \section{From spectral sums to explicit formulas: operator identities you can actually prove}
10730: \label{sec:trace_to_explicit_formula}
10731: 
10732: The ``explicit formula'' is an equality between a sum over zeros and a sum over primes (with archimedean correction terms).
10733: On the operator side, sums over zeros become traces of test functions of $H_\varpi$.
10734: This section records the exact chain of equalities that any Hilbert--Pólya model must implement.
10735: 
10736: \subsection{Spectral theorem bookkeeping: traces of $f(H)$}
10737: Let $H$ be self-adjoint with pure point spectrum $\{\lambda_n\}$ and eigenprojections $\{P_n\}$.
10738: For any bounded Borel function $f$,
10739: \[
10740: f(H)=\sum_n f(\lambda_n)P_n,
10741: \qquad
10742: \Tr(f(H))=\sum_n f(\lambda_n)\,\Tr(P_n).
10743: \]
